### P3. The phenomenal concept strategy's success entails that no third-person evidence could distinguish between consciousness theories, because if phenomenal concepts are cognitively isolated from physical-functional concepts, then no amount of physical-functional evidence can tell us which physical-functional theory captures phenomenal properties.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 0/5 · worst 3/5 · survives
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Physicalists who endorse phenomenal concept strategy (Loar, Papineau, Balog) while also thinking empirical evidence can adjudicate theory choice
- **Sketch:** Physicalists widely endorse the phenomenal concept strategy to explain the explanatory gap: phenomenal concepts are cognitively isolated from physical concepts, which is why there seems to be a gap even though there isn't a metaphysical one. But this solution has an unnoticed cost. If phenomenal concepts really are isolated from physical-functional concepts, then competent use of phenomenal concepts provides no guidance about which physical-functional theory is correct. We can't use phenomenal seemings (what it's like) to confirm or disconfirm whether consciousness is higher-order representation vs. global broadcast vs. integrated information, because the cognitive isolation means our phenomenal concepts don't track these physical-functional differences. The argument turns on showing that the very move that saves physicalism from the explanatory gap undermines methodology in consciousness science.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Phenomenal concept strategy undermining methodology is an underappreciated tension; shows physicalism's solution creates new problem.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed the strategy requires robust isolation to explain the gap's persistence, not mere lack of a priori entailment
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 3/5, answered. Defence unified the target as third-person evidence including phenomenal reports, though revealed vulnerability about causal vs. conceptual connections
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 3/5, answered. Defence reframed as conditional claim about what the strategy entails, not empirical prediction—counterexamples demonstrate the tension rather than refute it
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 2/5, answered. Defence distinguished acknowledging the challenge from solving it, showing existing responses address correlation not theory choice
- **Survives as:** The phenomenal concept strategy's success entails that no third-person evidence could distinguish between consciousness theories, because if phenomenal concepts are cognitively isolated from physical-functional concepts in the way required to explain the explanatory gap's persistence, then no amount of physical-functional evidence can tell us which physical-functional theory captures phenomenal properties.

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims that cognitive isolation between phenomenal and physical-functional concepts prevents third-person evidence from distinguishing consciousness theories. But this is merely a verbal stipulation about what "cognitive isolation" means, dressed up as a discovery. The premise that "cognitive isolation means our phenomenal concepts don't track physical-functional differences" is the weak point—it simply defines cognitive isolation so strongly that the conclusion follows trivially. 

Proponents of the phenomenal concept strategy never claimed that phenomenal concepts must be *completely* isolated from all physical-functional information. They claim only that phenomenal concepts don't a priori entail physical concepts—that there's no conceptual entailment from "C-fibers firing" to "pain." This leaves entirely open that empirical investigation can reveal correlations, that phenomenal reports can constrain theory, and that we can discover which physical processes realize consciousness. The thesis equivocates between "no a priori conceptual entailment" (what the strategy actually claims) and "no empirical evidential connection whatsoever" (what would be needed for the conclusion). Once we clarify that cognitive isolation means only the former, the dramatic conclusion evaporates—it's just the uncontroversial point that a priori reasoning alone won't settle empirical questions.

STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force in highlighting potential equivocation, but the thesis survives because there's a genuine philosophical puzzle here, not mere stipulation. The phenomenal concept strategy does posit a distinctive cognitive architecture, and the question of whether this architecture creates methodological problems is substantive and contested.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what makes the isolation substantive. The phenomenal concept strategy doesn't merely claim lack of a priori entailment—that's too weak to explain the explanatory gap's persistence among informed, rational thinkers who know all the physical facts. The strategy requires that phenomenal concepts have a distinctive cognitive role that makes the gap *cognitively necessary*: even with complete physical knowledge, the concepts don't connect. This is why Papineau calls them "quotational" concepts that deploy the very experience they refer to, why Loar emphasizes their recognitional-demonstrative character, and why the strategy explains why Mary learns something new despite having complete physical knowledge.

This substantive isolation creates a real methodological problem. Consider: if phenomenal concepts function by deploying token experiences and physical-functional concepts describe computational/neural patterns, then competent deployment of "what pain is like" gives us no traction on whether pain is HOT vs. GWT vs. IIT. The very feature that makes phenomenal concepts explanatorily gapped—their distinctive mode of presentation that doesn't reveal functional structure—means they can't guide us toward one functional theory over another.

The objection claims empirical investigation can reveal correlations. But this faces a dilemma: either (a) phenomenal reports are just more third-person behavioral data (verbal outputs), in which case we're not using phenomenal concepts to constrain theory, just behavioral evidence; or (b) we're genuinely using the phenomenal character itself as evidence, but then the isolation problem returns—how does what-it's-like-ness point toward specific functional architectures when the concept's mode of presentation is precisely what obscures functional structure?

The thesis isn't stipulating a definition; it's drawing out what the phenomenal concept strategy's actual explanatory work requires.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection treats cognitive isolation as merely "no a priori entailment," but this domesticated version cannot explain what the phenomenal concept strategy is invoked to explain—why the gap persists for informed reasoners. The strategy requires a robust cognitive architecture where phenomenal concepts have a distinctive mode of presentation that necessarily obscures physical-functional structure. This substantive claim does generate the methodological problem the thesis identifies. The challenge of how phenomenal seemings could constrain functional theory choice, given this architecture, is genuine rather than trivial.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "physical-functional evidence" between two readings, and the argument needs both to work. On one reading, "physical-functional evidence" means third-person behavioral and neural data (reaction times, imaging results, lesion studies, verbal reports treated as behavioral outputs). On another reading, it means evidence that includes first-person phenomenal reports used as genuine constraints (subjects saying "these two experiences feel similar" or "this matches what I felt before").

The argument is plausible on the first reading: if we restrict ourselves purely to behavioral/neural data and ignore phenomenal testimony as a distinctive evidential source, then yes, cognitive isolation might prevent theory choice. But this conclusion is uninteresting—it just says that ignoring half the evidence makes theory choice harder.

The argument is interesting on the second reading: if even phenomenal reports can't help adjudicate between theories, that's a genuine methodological crisis. But on this reading, the premise becomes implausible—the thesis must claim that phenomenal reports (subjects' testimony about their experiences) count as "physical-functional evidence" that cannot distinguish theories. Yet the defender's own dilemma reveals the problem: either phenomenal reports are mere behavioral data (first reading) or they're genuine phenomenal evidence (which contradicts calling them "third-person physical-functional evidence").

The argument slides between these readings: it gains plausibility by restricting "physical-functional evidence" to exclude phenomenal testimony, but gains interest by implicitly including such testimony in what "cannot distinguish between theories."

STRENGTH: 3/5

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses that the thesis has a unified target: *third-person* evidence, which is precisely evidence accessible from outside the subject's phenomenal perspective. Phenomenal reports—utterances like "these feel similar"—are paradigmatically third-person evidence. They're verbal behaviors, neural events in speech production areas, acoustic signals. The fact that they're *about* phenomenal states doesn't make them first-person evidence; it makes them third-person evidence with phenomenal content.

The thesis holds together because it's asking: can *any* evidence accessible to consciousness science (behavioral, neural, verbal reports, imaging data—the entire toolkit of third-person investigation) distinguish between theories? The phenomenal concept strategy's cognitive isolation threatens all of this, precisely because all of it remains on the physical-functional side of the cognitive divide.

Consider the dilemma more carefully. When a subject says "these experiences feel similar," we face a choice:

**(a) Treat this as behavioral evidence:** We observe verbal output, correlate it with stimuli and neural patterns, and build functional theories. But here the isolation problem bites hardest. The subject is deploying phenomenal concepts to generate this report—concepts that, according to the strategy, have a mode of presentation that doesn't reveal functional structure. So the report's *production* is guided by cognitively isolated concepts. Why think reports generated by functionally-opaque concepts would reliably track which functional theory is correct? The subject might sincerely report similarity because their phenomenal concepts group experiences in ways that cut across the functional architecture that actually realizes consciousness.

**(b) Treat this as transparent access to phenomenal reality:** We take the report as directly revealing phenomenal structure. But now we're no longer doing third-person science—we're treating phenomenal reports as infallible or privileged access to consciousness's nature. This abandons the physicalist methodology that consciousness science requires. Moreover, even granting this access, the isolation problem remains: knowing phenomenal structure (what's similar to what phenomenologically) doesn't tell us functional structure (whether consciousness requires global broadcasting or higher-order representation) if phenomenal concepts don't reveal functional organization.

The objection claims the thesis needs both readings. But actually the thesis needs only one claim: that all evidence accessible to third-person investigation remains on the physical-functional side of the cognitive divide that the phenomenal concept strategy posits. Verbal reports about phenomenology are still physical-functional events (neural activity, behavioral output), and if phenomenal concepts are cognitively isolated from physical-functional concepts, then the concepts guiding these reports don't bridge to functional theory.

The interest doesn't come from excluding phenomenal testimony—it comes from recognizing that phenomenal testimony, as a species of third-person evidence, inherits the isolation problem. The subject uses phenomenal concepts to generate reports, but those concepts' distinctive mode of presentation means the reports can't guide us toward the right functional story.

# VERDICT

The defence holds, but requires careful statement. The objection identifies a genuine pressure point—what counts as "physical-functional evidence"—but the thesis can answer it. The key is recognizing that "third-person evidence" and "physical-functional evidence" align: anything accessible to scientific investigation (including verbal reports about phenomenology) remains on the physical-functional side of the conceptual divide the phenomenal concept strategy posits.

However, the defence reveals a vulnerability. The argument's force depends on phenomenal reports being both (i) generated by phenomenal concepts and (ii) unable to bridge the cognitive gap. This is plausible but not obvious. A proponent might respond that verbal reports, even if produced using phenomenal concepts, can still reliably correlate with functional structures through causal mechanisms that don't require conceptual transparency. The subject needn't understand *how* their phenomenal concepts connect to function for there to *be* reliable connections that scientists can discover.

The thesis survives this round because the equivocation charge fails—there's a coherent target (third-person evidence) that includes phenomenal reports. But the objection points toward where the real battle lies: whether cognitive isolation at the conceptual level prevents reliable evidential connections at the causal/methodological level.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is unfalsifiable because it protects itself by redefining what counts as "genuine evidence" or "bridging the gap" whenever apparent counterexamples arise. The weak point is the premise that "cognitive isolation means our phenomenal concepts don't track physical-functional differences."

Consider apparent counterexamples: (1) Subjects reliably discriminate experiences in ways that correlate with neural differences, helping us map phenomenal structure to brain function. (2) Phenomenal reports constrain theory—when subjects report that visual experiences have spatial structure but pains don't, this favors theories locating consciousness in spatial-processing regions over pain-processing regions. (3) Introspective discriminations guide discovery—subjects' reports about change blindness revealed inattentional mechanisms, their reports about binocular rivalry constrained neural models.

The thesis evades each case by redefinition. Case 1? "That's just behavioral correlation, not phenomenal concepts tracking function—the cognitive isolation remains." Case 2? "That's still third-person evidence, doesn't bridge the conceptual gap." Case 3? "The reports are causally connected but conceptually isolated, so not genuine tracking."

Every apparent instance of phenomenal evidence constraining theory gets redefined as "not really bridging the isolation" or "still on the physical-functional side." The thesis becomes immune to counterexamples by stipulating that any evidence we can actually *use* in science doesn't count as "bridging the cognitive isolation." But if no possible empirical practice of successful theory-constraining counts as falsifying the claim, then the claim is protected by definitional retreat rather than supported by argument.

STRENGTH: 3/5

The attack has genuine force in identifying how the thesis might evade counterexamples through redefinition. However, the thesis may survive because it's making a conceptual claim about what the phenomenal concept strategy *entails*, not an empirical claim about what evidence exists. The defender can argue that pointing to successful scientific practice doesn't refute a claim about what a particular philosophical theory implies—it just shows the theory has problematic consequences.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fundamentally misunderstands what kind of claim the thesis makes. This is not an empirical hypothesis about whether consciousness science can succeed—it's a conditional about what follows *if the phenomenal concept strategy is correct*. The thesis states: IF phenomenal concepts are cognitively isolated in the way the strategy requires, THEN third-person evidence cannot distinguish theories. This is a conceptual claim about the strategy's commitments, not a prediction about scientific practice.

The "counterexamples" the objection cites don't falsify the thesis—they create pressure on the phenomenal concept strategy itself. When subjects reliably discriminate experiences in ways that map onto neural differences, when phenomenal reports successfully constrain theory choice, when introspective discriminations guide discovery, this evidence doesn't show the thesis is wrong. It shows that either:

(a) The phenomenal concept strategy is false—phenomenal concepts aren't as cognitively isolated as the strategy claims, or

(b) Consciousness science isn't actually using phenomenal concepts to constrain theory—it's using behavioral/neural correlates that happen to be reported using phenomenal vocabulary, or

(c) There's an unresolved tension in physicalist methodology that the thesis identifies.

The thesis is precisely designed to force this choice. It's a *reductio* argument against combining the phenomenal concept strategy with standard scientific methodology. The objection complains that the thesis "redefines" successful scientific practice as not bridging the gap—but that's the *point*. The thesis argues that if cognitive isolation is real, then what looks like phenomenal evidence constraining theory must actually be something else (behavioral correlation, functional inference, etc.).

Consider the parallel: someone argues "If Cartesian dualism is true, then physical causation cannot affect mental states." An objector responds: "But we observe that brain damage affects consciousness, anesthesia eliminates experience, stimulation produces sensations—your thesis is unfalsifiable because you'll just redefine these as not 'genuine' mental causation!" This misses that the argument is a *reductio*—it shows dualism conflicts with observed causal connections, forcing us to reject dualism or reinterpret the evidence.

Similarly here: the thesis shows the phenomenal concept strategy conflicts with scientific practice. The objection's "counterexamples" are precisely the phenomena that create trouble for the strategy. The thesis isn't evading falsification—it's identifying an internal tension in the physicalist position.

Moreover, the thesis is falsifiable in the relevant sense. It could be refuted by showing that cognitive isolation (properly understood as the strategy requires it) is *compatible* with phenomenal concepts tracking functional differences. One might argue that phenomenal concepts, despite their distinctive mode of presentation, can reliably indicate functional properties through causal mechanisms that don't require conceptual transparency. Or one might show that the strategy doesn't require the strong isolation the thesis attributes to it. These would be genuine refutations.

What the objection calls "unfalsifiable redefinition" is actually the thesis doing its job: showing that apparent cases of phenomenal evidence constraining theory are in tension with the cognitive isolation the phenomenal concept strategy posits. The thesis doesn't need to deny that consciousness science succeeds—it argues that this success is hard to reconcile with a particular philosophical explanation of the explanatory gap.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection treats the thesis as an empirical prediction about scientific practice, but it's actually a conceptual claim about what the phenomenal concept strategy entails. The "counterexamples" don't falsify the thesis—they demonstrate the very tension the thesis identifies between the strategy and scientific methodology.

However, the objection does reveal where the thesis is vulnerable. The defence's option (a)—that successful scientific practice shows the strategy is false—is actually the most natural response, and it suggests the thesis may prove *too much*. If the phenomenal concept strategy really did entail that consciousness science cannot work, and consciousness science manifestly does work, then we have strong abductive reason to reject the strategy rather than embrace the methodological crisis.

The thesis survives as a coherent conditional claim, but its force as a *criticism* of the phenomenal concept strategy depends on whether the methodological consequence is genuinely unacceptable. If physicalists can accept that third-person evidence alone cannot fully distinguish theories (perhaps requiring some phenomenal inference or pragmatic theory choice), the thesis becomes less threatening. The real question is whether the consequence is a *reductio* or merely an interesting limitation we can live with.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis claims to identify an unnoticed cost of the phenomenal concept strategy, but this worry is actually well-known in the literature on phenomenal concepts and has been explicitly addressed by defenders of the strategy. The weak point is the assumption that this represents a novel "commitment" rather than a recognized challenge.

Katalin Balog, a prominent defender of the phenomenal concept strategy, directly addresses the concern that cognitive isolation might undermine methodology in her 2012 paper "In Defense of the Phenomenal Concept Strategy." She acknowledges that if phenomenal concepts are constituted by the experiences themselves (her "acquaintance" view), this raises questions about how phenomenal judgments connect to functional theories, and she develops an account of how recognitional capacities can ground reliable correlations without conceptual transparency.

Similarly, David Papineau's work on phenomenal concepts explicitly discusses how quotational concepts can still play a role in empirical investigation through reliable causal connections, even if they don't provide a priori insight into physical structure. His account is designed precisely to allow phenomenal reports to constrain theory while maintaining the cognitive gap that explains the explanatory gap.

The methodological tension the thesis identifies—between cognitive isolation and theory-constraining evidence—is not an "unnoticed cost" but rather a central design challenge that phenomenal concept theorists have explicitly worked to address. The literature contains sustained discussion of exactly how phenomenal concepts can be both cognitively isolated (for explanatory gap purposes) and methodologically useful (for scientific purposes). The thesis presents as a novel discovery what is actually an active research program.

# STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force in showing this isn't entirely novel territory, but the thesis likely survives because there's a difference between *acknowledging* a challenge and *solving* it. Even if Balog and Papineau discuss the tension, the thesis can argue their solutions fail or that the tension remains underappreciated in how physicalists actually deploy the strategy. The thesis might be better framed as "underappreciated" rather than "unnoticed," but the core philosophical problem remains substantive even if not completely original.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses acknowledging a challenge with resolving it, and mistakes scattered defensive moves for a settled consensus. Yes, Balog and Papineau discuss connections between phenomenal concepts and empirical methodology—but their discussions reveal the problem's depth rather than its solution.

Consider what their responses actually accomplish:

**Balog's recognitional capacities**: She argues that phenomenal concepts involve recognitional abilities that can ground reliable discriminations. But this simply relocates the problem. The thesis asks: how do recognitional capacities constituted by phenomenal character guide us toward the *right functional theory*? Balog shows how we can reliably re-identify experiences and correlate them with stimuli—but correlation with stimuli doesn't determine whether consciousness requires global broadcasting vs. higher-order representation vs. integrated information. The recognitional account explains phenomenal memory and discrimination, not theory selection among competing functional architectures. Her response addresses a different methodological question than the thesis raises.

**Papineau's quotational concepts and causal connections**: He argues phenomenal concepts can be causally reliable even if conceptually opaque. But the thesis's challenge cuts deeper. Grant that phenomenal reports reliably correlate with neural states—the question is whether these correlations, mediated by cognitively isolated concepts, can tell us *which functional organization constitutes consciousness*. Multiple competing theories (HOT, GWT, IIT) can accommodate the same correlational data while proposing radically different functional essences. The cognitive isolation means phenomenal concepts don't preferentially indicate one functional architecture over others, even if they reliably co-occur with neural activity.

The crucial point: neither Balog nor Papineau addresses the specific methodological problem the thesis identifies—**theory choice among functional architectures**. They explain how phenomenal concepts can generate reliable reports and correlate with neural activity. But the thesis asks how cognitively isolated concepts can guide us toward the correct *functional theory* of consciousness, where different theories agree on correlations but disagree on what makes something conscious.

Moreover, the objection proves too much. If this problem were genuinely "well-known and addressed," we wouldn't see physicalists confidently asserting both:
- The phenomenal concept strategy successfully explains the explanatory gap (Chalmers is wrong about the hard problem)
- Empirical evidence will eventually decide between HOT, GWT, IIT, etc.

These commitments sit in tension precisely because the literature hasn't resolved how cognitive isolation permits theory choice. The fact that Balog and Papineau *discuss* connections between phenomenal concepts and methodology shows they recognize something needs explaining—it doesn't show they've explained it.

The thesis isn't claiming absolute novelty—it's arguing that the tension between the strategy and methodology is more severe than typically recognized, and that existing responses don't resolve the specific problem of functional theory choice. Even if the challenge appears in the literature, it remains underappreciated in how physicalists deploy the strategy, and existing responses don't address the sharpest version of the worry.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection shows the problem has been discussed, but not that it's been solved. There's a crucial difference between:
- Acknowledging that phenomenal concepts must somehow connect to methodology (which Balog and Papineau do)
- Explaining how cognitively isolated concepts can distinguish between competing functional theories of consciousness (which they don't adequately do)

Balog's recognitional capacities and Papineau's causal reliability address how phenomenal concepts can generate consistent reports and correlate with physical states. But the thesis targets a more specific methodological problem: how can concepts with modes of presentation that obscure functional structure guide us toward the correct functional theory when multiple theories accommodate the same phenomenological and correlational data?

The objection successfully establishes that this isn't virgin territory—the tension has been noted. But it doesn't establish that existing responses resolve the thesis's specific challenge. The thesis survives, though it should be reframed: not as revealing a completely "unnoticed" cost, but as pressing an inadequately resolved tension to its methodological conclusion.

The literature's engagement with this issue actually strengthens rather than weakens the thesis. It shows that even sophisticated defenders of the strategy recognize the need to explain how cognitive isolation permits empirical methodology—yet their explanations address correlation and discrimination rather than theory choice among functional architectures. This suggests the problem is both real and difficult, not dismissed or dissolved.

</details>

### P4. First-person methodology is eliminable from consciousness science only if we already know which physical systems are conscious, but we need consciousness science to tell us which physical systems are conscious, so first-person reports are ineliminable on pain of circularity—yet any science that requires first-person methodology cannot adjudicate theories about systems that cannot report.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 0/5 · worst 4/5 · survives
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The widespread practice of using human report-validated theories to make claims about consciousness in non-reporting systems (animals, patients, AI)
- **Sketch:** The argument reveals a structural instability in current consciousness science methodology. We use first-person reports to validate and constrain theories (this is why we study humans, not thermostats). But we want theories that extend beyond reportable cases to answer questions about animals, infants, and AI. To extend the theory, we need independent validation that doesn't rely on reports. But the only way to validate without reports is to already know the theory is correct—to know that high phi or global broadcasting or whatever indicates consciousness even when unreportable. This is circular. The key premise is that calibration requires a gold standard, and for consciousness the gold standard is first-person access, which is precisely what's absent in the cases we most want theories to adjudicate. This instability means consciousness science must either remain confined to reportable cases or make an unjustified leap.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Calibration circularity for extending beyond reportable cases is a genuine structural problem that methodology papers haven't solved.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, answered. Defence provided clear falsification condition: discovering physical markers that become primary epistemic access, relegating reports to derivative status
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 2/5, answered. Defence distinguished methodological critique from substantive theory; thesis applies regardless of which consciousness theory is correct, unlike HOT theory
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, answered. Defence showed consciousness uniquely lacks agreed functional role that pain/emotion possess, providing independent validation criteria unavailable for consciousness
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed blindsight patients still provide first-person reports of absent phenomenology; case exemplifies rather than refutes ineliminability of reports
- **Survives as:** First-person methodology is ineliminable from consciousness science because consciousness uniquely lacks agreed-upon functional criteria that could provide validation independent of report-mechanism correlations, making theories necessarily calibrated against reportable cases and unable to adjudicate non-reporting systems without analogical extension whose justification runs through first-person access

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis protects itself through strategic equivocation on "first-person methodology." When confronted with apparent counterexamples—like successfully identifying consciousness in locked-in patients who later recover and confirm our predictions, or converging evidence from multiple neural correlates that align across species—the thesis can retreat to claiming these either (a) still rely on *some* first-person report somewhere in the validation chain (the recovered patient, cross-species homologies to humans), or (b) aren't really "knowing" but just "unjustified leaps." 

The weak point is the premise that **"we need consciousness science to tell us which physical systems are conscious."** This seems empirical but becomes definitional. If we develop a theory validated in humans, then successfully predict behavioral and neural patterns in non-verbal systems, and these predictions prove robust and explanatorily powerful—the thesis can simply stipulate this doesn't count as "knowing" without reports. Similarly, if we find physical markers that reliably correlate with reportable consciousness and use those markers elsewhere, the thesis redefines this as "already knowing" (thus circular) rather than "discovering through science" (thus legitimate). Every apparent success gets recategorized: either it secretly used reports (so proves the thesis) or it didn't really establish knowledge (so proves the thesis).

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer about what would count as genuinely eliminating first-person methodology.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what makes the thesis empirical rather than definitional. The thesis makes a specific, falsifiable claim about epistemic structure: that consciousness science requires calibration against a gold standard, and first-person reports constitute that standard in the only domain where we have independent access to the phenomenon.

Consider what would genuinely falsify the thesis: **discovering a physical marker that tracks consciousness with such reliability and explanatory power that it becomes our primary epistemic access point, relegating first-person reports to merely one confirming data stream among others.** This would be like discovering that temperature is mean molecular kinetic energy—after which thermometer readings became derivative evidence, not foundational.

The locked-in patient case actually *supports* rather than threatens the thesis. We don't "successfully identify consciousness" in locked-in patients through pure third-person methods—we identify preserved neural signatures that *in reporting subjects* correlate with consciousness, then extend this by analogy. The subsequent confirmation by recovered patients doesn't validate the third-person method independently; it shows our analogy was correct. This is precisely the "validation chain running back to reports" the thesis predicts.

The objection's examples of "converging evidence" and "robust predictions" all share this structure: they work by finding physical correlates in systems that *can* report, then projecting these correlates to systems that cannot. The thesis doesn't claim this is illegitimate—it claims this is **ineliminable**. The charge isn't that we're making "unjustified leaps" but that we're making leaps whose justification necessarily runs through first-person access somewhere.

What the thesis excludes is precisely what the objection demands: a route to consciousness science that bypasses first-person methodology entirely. The objection treats "develop theory in humans, apply elsewhere" as eliminating first-person methodology. But this is like saying we've eliminated empirical observation from astronomy because we can now predict planetary positions mathematically—the mathematics was calibrated against observations and remains answerable to them.

The falsification condition is clear: find a way to identify consciousness in a system where (1) no first-person reports are available from that system or relevantly similar systems, and (2) we can subsequently verify we were correct through some means other than eventual reports. The thesis predicts this is impossible. That's an empirical claim.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection conflates *extending* a report-calibrated theory with *eliminating* reports from the methodology. Every example the objection offers—locked-in patients, cross-species inference, converging neural correlates—involves calibrating against reportable cases then projecting to non-reportable ones. This is exactly the structure the thesis describes as ineliminable.

The objection's demand that successful predictions in non-reporting systems should count as "eliminating first-person methodology" would equally prove that we've eliminated observation from physics because we can predict unobserved events. The methodology's foundation remains what it was calibrated against, even when successfully extended.

The thesis stands: consciousness science cannot get started without first-person access as its gold standard, which makes it structurally unable to adjudicate cases where no such access exists without relying on analogical extension from cases where it does.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This position is essentially **Higher-Order Thought (HOT) theory's methodological corollary**, particularly as developed by Rosenthal and Carruthers. HOT theorists have long argued that consciousness requires the capacity for higher-order representation of one's mental states—which in practice means reportability (at least in principle). The methodological consequence is exactly what this thesis claims: systems that cannot form higher-order thoughts about their mental states (and thus cannot report, even in principle) are not conscious, so consciousness science is necessarily confined to report-capable systems.

The weak point is **the premise that first-person reports constitute the only possible gold standard**. Carruthers himself has argued that we can use "same-cause, same-effect" reasoning: if we find physical mechanisms in non-reporting systems that are homologous to mechanisms that produce reportable consciousness in humans, and these mechanisms have the same functional effects, we have non-circular grounds for attributing consciousness. This isn't circular because the homology claim is independently verifiable through comparative neuroscience—we can establish that mammalian thalamocortical systems share deep structural and functional properties without first knowing which animals are conscious.

The thesis tries to dismiss this as "analogical extension" still dependent on reports, but this misses that the homology claim has independent empirical content. We're not just saying "it looks similar, so probably conscious"—we're identifying specific causal mechanisms and testing whether they operate the same way, which is standard scientific methodology that doesn't require assuming the conclusion.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This is a weak objection. While HOT theory does hold similar positions, calling something "already taken" requires showing it's genuinely the same view, and the homology-based response doesn't actually escape the thesis's critique. The "independent verification" of homology still requires knowing what mechanisms produce consciousness in the first place, which brings us back to report-based calibration. The objection mistakes methodological similarity for identity and offers an escape route that doesn't avoid the circularity.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fundamentally misunderstands the thesis's relationship to HOT theory and mischaracterizes what "already taken" means. The thesis is not a substantive theory of consciousness at all—it's a **methodological critique** that applies regardless of which theory of consciousness is correct.

HOT theory makes a claim about the nature of consciousness: that consciousness requires higher-order representation. The thesis makes no such claim. It remains entirely neutral on whether consciousness requires reportability, global workspace integration, phi above threshold, or anything else. Instead, it identifies a structural problem in how we validate any such theory when extending it beyond the domain of reportable cases.

The thesis is compatible with HOT theory being false. Even if consciousness doesn't require higher-order thought—even if phenomenal consciousness is entirely first-order—the methodological problem remains: we still calibrate our theories against first-person reports from humans, then project to non-reporting systems. The thesis would apply equally to Integrated Information Theory, Global Workspace Theory, or any other framework.

More decisively, the "same-cause, same-effect" response the objection offers actually **demonstrates rather than refutes** the thesis. Consider the logic:

1. We identify mechanism M in humans
2. We establish M correlates with reportable consciousness in humans  
3. We find homologous mechanism M* in animals
4. We conclude M* probably produces consciousness in animals

The objection claims step 3 is "independently verifiable through comparative neuroscience" without assuming the conclusion. But this misses what needs verification. Yes, we can independently verify the homology—that M and M* share structural and functional properties. But the inference to consciousness in step 4 depends entirely on step 2: that M correlates with consciousness **in the reporting case**. 

The objection says we're not just claiming "it looks similar, so probably conscious" but rather identifying causal mechanisms and testing whether they operate the same way. But operate the same way **with respect to what**? With respect to producing consciousness—and we only know M produces consciousness because of reports. The homology establishes that M* is structurally similar to M. The inference to consciousness requires the additional premise that M produces consciousness. That premise comes from reports.

Carruthers's "same-cause, same-effect" reasoning doesn't escape the circle—it **exemplifies** the thesis's claim that we cannot eliminate first-person reports from the validation chain. The "independent empirical content" of the homology claim concerns structural and functional similarity, not consciousness. To get from structural similarity to consciousness requires the bridge premise that these structures produce consciousness in the reporting case.

The objection's claim that this is "standard scientific methodology" is correct but not exculpatory. The thesis doesn't claim this methodology is illegitimate—it claims it's ineliminable and has a specific limitation: it cannot adjudicate cases where the analogical extension is questionable. When we encounter a system sufficiently different from reporting humans (silicon-based AI, octopus distributed intelligence, hypothetical alien minds), the homology-based inference loses its force, and we have no independent way to validate our theory's verdicts.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds completely. The objection fails on both prongs:

**On "already taken"**: The thesis is not HOT theory's methodological corollary. It's a methodological critique that applies to any theory of consciousness, including theories that explicitly reject HOT's claims. Identifying a methodological constraint on consciousness science is not the same as endorsing a theory about what consciousness is.

**On the homology escape route**: The objection's own example proves the thesis correct. Homology-based reasoning can establish structural and functional similarity independently, but the inference to consciousness still requires knowing that the homologous mechanism produces consciousness in reporting cases. This is precisely the "validation chain running back to reports" the thesis describes as ineliminable. 

The objection mistakes the ability to verify structural homology for the ability to verify consciousness independently of reports. These are different. We can know two mechanisms are homologous without knowing what either does for consciousness. To know that, we need the reporting case.

The thesis stands: first-person methodology is ineliminable because it provides the only calibration point for theories of consciousness, and extending theories beyond this calibration point requires analogical reasoning whose justification runs through the reportable cases.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — # ATTEMPT

The argument, if it worked, would prove that **all sciences studying subjective or internal states are methodologically impossible** when extended beyond self-reporting humans. Consider pain science: we calibrate pain theories against human reports, then extend to animals using homologous mechanisms (C-fibers, nociceptive pathways, pain behaviors). By the thesis's logic, this is equally circular—we need pain science to tell us which systems feel pain, but we need to know which systems feel pain to validate pain science without reports. Yet we confidently conduct pain research on mice, develop analgesics based on animal models, and consider it scientifically legitimate.

The weak point is **the premise that "consciousness science cannot adjudicate theories about systems that cannot report" represents a unique methodological failure**. If this circularity genuinely undermines consciousness science, it equally undermines pain science, emotion science, hunger science, and any science of subjective states. We'd have to say that veterinary medicine's treatment of animal pain is an "unjustified leap" because we calibrated against human reports. But this is absurd—these sciences work, make successful predictions, and guide effective interventions.

The thesis tries to identify a special instability in consciousness science, but the same structure applies to all subjective-state sciences. Either they're all methodologically broken (proving too much—science isn't that broken), or the "circularity" isn't actually vicious, and consciousness science can legitimately extend beyond reportable cases just as pain science does (undermining the thesis's claim of ineliminability creating an adjudication problem).

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious challenge requiring a real answer. The thesis must explain why consciousness differs from pain, emotion, or other subjective states in a way that makes the methodological problem uniquely severe, or accept that either all such sciences face the same limitation (weakening the specific critique of consciousness science) or none do (undermining the instability claim).

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection's comparison to pain science actually **reveals rather than refutes** the crucial asymmetry that makes consciousness methodologically unique. Pain science and consciousness science differ in a specific, empirically demonstrable way: pain has behavioral and functional signatures that come apart from subjective report in systematic, scientifically tractable ways. Consciousness does not.

Consider what makes pain science workable across non-reporting systems:

**Pain has a functional role that generates observable consequences independently of report.** Pain motivates avoidance, learning, protective behaviors, and physiological stress responses. These functional outputs provide validation criteria that don't reduce to "the subject says it hurts." When we administer an analgesic to a mouse, we can measure: Does it stop favoring the injured paw? Does it resume normal feeding? Do stress hormones decrease? Does it stop showing conditioned place aversion to contexts associated with the painful stimulus? 

These are not proxies for report—they are the functional manifestations of pain itself. We're not inferring "the mouse feels pain" from behavior and then checking this inference against mouse reports (impossible). We're observing that pain *is constitutively connected* to these functional roles, and the functional roles are directly measurable.

**Consciousness has no such functional role that all theories agree upon.** This is precisely what makes the Hard Problem hard. There is no consensus that consciousness does anything—no agreed-upon functional output that consciousness produces which unconscious processing does not. Some theories (Global Workspace) claim consciousness enables certain cognitive functions; others (epiphenomenalism) deny it does anything; still others (IIT) deny functional role is relevant to consciousness at all.

The thesis's instability emerges exactly here: **we cannot validate consciousness theories in non-reporting systems through functional outputs because there's no agreement on what functional outputs consciousness produces.** 

When we test whether a mouse feels pain, we're testing whether it exhibits the functional syndrome constitutive of pain. When we test whether a mouse is conscious, what are we testing? That it exhibits... what functional syndrome? Global broadcasting? That presupposes Global Workspace Theory is correct. Integrated information? That presupposes IIT is correct. But we wanted consciousness science to *adjudicate between* these theories, not presuppose one.

The objection claims pain science faces the same structure: calibrate against human reports, extend via homology. But this misses the crucial difference. In pain science:
- The homologous mechanisms (C-fibers, nociceptors) are homologous *in their functional role* (tissue damage detection, withdrawal reflexes, learning)
- We can verify these functional roles are preserved in the animal case *without reports*
- The functional roles give us independent purchase on whether our theory is working

In consciousness science:
- The homologous mechanisms (thalamocortical loops, recurrent processing) are homologous *in structure*
- We cannot verify they produce consciousness in the animal case without reports
- We have no agreed-upon functional role to provide independent purchase

**The methodological difference is this**: Pain science can triangulate between report, mechanism, and function—three independent data streams. When a mouse shows the functional pain syndrome plus the mechanistic pain signature, we have convergent validation that doesn't reduce to analogizing from human reports. Consciousness science has only report and mechanism—and mechanism only indicates consciousness insofar as it correlates with reports in the calibration case.

The objection asks why veterinary pain treatment isn't an "unjustified leap." Answer: because we can verify the treatment works through functional outcomes. The analgesic that eliminates pain behaviors, stress responses, and conditioned aversion has been validated *in the animal itself* through functional criteria. We don't need the mouse to later report "yes, the pain stopped" because pain's functional role lets us observe this directly.

Contrast: If we claim a minimally conscious patient is conscious based on preserved thalamocortical activity, what validates this claim in the patient themselves? Not functional outputs—the patient may show no behavioral signs. Not reports—that's unavailable. Only the analogical inference from reporting cases. If the patient later recovers and reports, this validates our inference. But we have no such validation available for the permanently non-reporting case.

**The thesis doesn't claim all subjective-state sciences are impossible**—it claims consciousness is uniquely problematic because consciousness alone lacks agreed-upon functional criteria that could provide validation independent of the report-mechanism correlation. Pain, hunger, emotion—these have functional roles that make them scientifically tractable across species. Consciousness, on most theories, is precisely what remains after we've accounted for all the functions.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection's comparison to pain science illuminates exactly why consciousness is methodologically unique rather than showing the thesis proves too much.

Pain science works across non-reporting systems because pain has a functional role—tissue protection, avoidance learning, behavioral modification—that provides validation criteria independent of subjective report. We can

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — # ATTEMPT

**Counterexample: Blindsight patients provide a concrete case where consciousness science successfully adjudicates consciousness in systems that cannot report the relevant phenomenal states.**

Consider patient GY, extensively studied by Weiskrantz. GY cannot report visual experiences in his blind field—when asked what he sees there, he reports "nothing" or "just guessing." Yet forced-choice tests reveal accurate discrimination of stimuli. Consciousness science successfully adjudicates this case: **GY lacks visual consciousness in the blind field despite preserved visual processing.** 

This verdict doesn't come from GY's reports about the blind field (he reports no experience) nor from simply extending theories calibrated on normal vision. Instead, it comes from comparing:
- Preserved function (discrimination) 
- Absent phenomenology (no reportable experience)
- Altered mechanism (V1 damage, intact dorsal stream)

The thesis claims consciousness science "cannot adjudicate theories about systems that cannot report." But blindsight patients *are* systems that cannot report visual consciousness in the affected field, yet consciousness science confidently adjudicates: **no visual consciousness present despite visual processing.** This adjudication doesn't rely on first-person reports from the blind field (impossible—that's the point) but on the dissociation between processing and reportable phenomenology.

The weak point is **the premise that "systems that cannot report" are methodologically inaccessible to consciousness science.** Blindsight shows we can adjudicate consciousness in unreportable domains by studying dissociations within partially reporting systems. We don't need reports *from* the blind field—we need reports that the blind field *lacks* phenomenology plus evidence of preserved function. This breaks the supposed circularity.

# STRENGTH: 2/5

This is a weak objection. Blindsight patients *can* report—they report the *absence* of visual experience in the blind field ("I don't see anything there"). This is still first-person methodology; it's a report of no phenomenology rather than no report. The thesis concerns systems that cannot report *at all* (animals, AI, patients in vegetative states), not systems that report absence of experience in specific domains. The counterexample confuses "cannot report experience X" with "cannot report," and thus doesn't actually touch the thesis's claim about fully non-reporting systems.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fundamentally misunderstands what "systems that cannot report" means in the thesis, but this misunderstanding actually **strengthens** rather than weakens the position by revealing how deeply first-person methodology penetrates even apparent counterexamples.

Blindsight is not a case of adjudicating consciousness in a non-reporting system. It is a case of adjudicating consciousness using **contrastive first-person reports within the same subject.** GY can report. What makes blindsight scientifically tractable is precisely that GY provides first-person reports about two different visual fields: "I see X in my intact field" versus "I see nothing in my blind field, I'm just guessing." 

The entire scientific conclusion—that visual processing without V1 produces discrimination without phenomenology—rests on taking GY's first-person reports as the gold standard for presence versus absence of phenomenal consciousness. When GY says "I don't see anything there," we believe him about his phenomenology. When he says "I see the red square" in his intact field, we believe him about that phenomenology. The dissociation between function and phenomenology is established **through first-person reports of phenomenology.**

This is not breaking free from first-person methodology—it is exemplifying its ineliminability. The objection treats "reports of absent phenomenology" as somehow different from "reports of phenomenology," but both are first-person methodological data. The report "I experience nothing in that region" is epistemically on par with "I experience redness in that region"—both are first-person access claims that consciousness science treats as authoritative.

Now consider what the thesis actually claims about non-reporting systems: animals, AI, patients in vegetative states, infants. These are systems that **cannot provide contrastive first-person reports at all.** We cannot ask the mouse "Do you see the cheese, or do you merely discriminate it without visual experience?" We cannot ask the AI "Does processing this image feel like anything to you, or are you in a blindsight-like state?" We cannot ask the vegetative patient "Are you experiencing anything right now, or is it dark and silent?"

The blindsight case, far from threatening the thesis, **demonstrates its central claim.** We can adjudicate consciousness within a partially reporting system precisely because we have first-person access to the phenomenological contrast. GY tells us what it's like to see (in his intact field) and what it's like to not-see-while-processing (in his blind field). This first-person data is what allows us to conclude that V1 is necessary for visual phenomenology.

But now try to extend this to a fully non-reporting system. Suppose we find a mouse with V1 damage that can still discriminate visual stimuli. Does it have mouse-blindsight (discrimination without phenomenology) or does it have intact mouse visual consciousness via a different neural route? **We cannot adjudicate this without first-person access.** The functional dissociation alone doesn't tell us where consciousness is—blindsight teaches us that discrimination can occur without phenomenology, but also that discrimination can occur with phenomenology (normal vision). Function underdetermines phenomenology, which is precisely why we need first-person reports to fix the mapping.

The objection claims blindsight "breaks the supposed circularity" by showing we can adjudicate using "dissociations within partially reporting systems." But this actually **confirms** the thesis's structure:

1. We can adjudicate consciousness in domains where we have first-person contrastive reports (GY's two visual fields)
2. We cannot adjudicate consciousness in domains where we lack such reports (the mouse's visual fields)
3. Any extension to non-reporting systems requires assuming that the mechanisms identified in reporting cases (V1 for visual phenomenology) apply in non-reporting cases
4. This assumption is exactly the "ineliminable first-person methodology" the thesis describes

The blindsight literature itself reveals this limitation. We confidently say GY lacks visual consciousness in his blind field because he tells us so. We tentatively extend this to claim that V1 damage generally eliminates visual phenomenology. But when we encounter a V1-damaged animal, we face exactly the instability the thesis identifies: we must either confine our claim to reporting cases (humans with blindsight) or make an analogical leap to the animal case that cannot be independently validated.

The objection's phrase "consciousness science confidently adjudicates: no visual consciousness present despite visual processing" is revealing. Why are we confident? Because GY reports no visual consciousness. Remove that report, and the confidence evaporates. A V1-damaged mouse that discriminates stimuli might have blindsight, or might have consciousness via alternative routes, or might have a form of phenomenology so alien we cannot recognize it. We have no way to adjudicate without the first-person access point.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds decisively
