## Repair candidates

*Promise 4+, failed on ONE locatable flaw — usually scope or framing, with the narrower version already written out. Often the richest vein.*

### R1. The appearance of empirical progress in consciousness science comes from researchers tacitly adopting a behaviorist criterion (reportability, task performance) while explicitly defending anti-behaviorist theories, making the field's empirical claims unfalsifiable by design.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 5/5 · wounded
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The consensus position that we're studying phenomenal consciousness using report-based methods with increasingly sophisticated controls
- **Sketch:** Consciousness researchers claim to study phenomenal consciousness (what-it's-like-ness) and explicitly reject behaviorism. Yet their experimental paradigms treat report-ability or discriminatory capacity as the gold standard for whether consciousness is present. When subjects report seeing something, researchers count it as conscious; when they don't report, it's counted as unconscious (with careful controls). But this operative criterion is precisely what behaviorism recommended. The instability: you cannot both reject behaviorism in theory and rely on behavioral-functional criteria in practice. The key premise is that if your theory says consciousness could come apart from reportability, but your methods presuppose they don't, you've built in a guarantee that your theory will seem empirically supported while actually being protected from refutation. The position collapses—either embrace the behaviorist criterion openly, or admit your theory makes no empirically testable predictions.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Tacit behaviorism critique is sharp and the unfalsifiability-by-design charge is serious; exposes real methodological tension.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The thesis equivocates between using reports as evidence versus treating reportability as constitutive; the defence reframed as falsifiability but didn't show why evidential use commits to theoretical behaviorism
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The thesis can classify any methodological stance as either behaviorist or unstably anti-behaviorist; the defence's proposed falsifiers (neural measures, dissociation predictions) actually exist in the field, contradicting the thesis's empirical predictions
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Block's P-consciousness/A-consciousness distinction and the ongoing overflow debate show the field explicitly grapples with the theory-method tension rather than exhibiting unaware instability; the thesis requires researchers to ignore what they actively debate
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, answered. The defence successfully distinguished consciousness from other unobservables through its unique epistemic structure: first-person grounding, single evidential channel, and lack of independent dissociation tests
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Consciousness science faces a distinctive methodological challenge: phenomenal consciousness is known first-personally but studied third-personally through reports, and while researchers acknowledge possible dissociations, the field lacks independent methods to detect them, creating a narrower evidential base than other sciences studying unobservables
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates between evidential and constitutive uses of behavioral criteria, and its empirical predictions about the field's monolithic report-dependence are contradicted by existing methodological pluralism and active debates about P/A dissociation

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "behaviorist criterion." On one reading, it means merely using reports and behavioral responses as *evidence* for consciousness—a methodological necessity that nearly everyone accepts, since we need some empirical access to study consciousness scientifically. On this reading, the thesis is plausible: researchers do indeed rely on reports and task performance. On another reading, it means adopting the *theoretical identification* of consciousness with behavioral dispositions—the claim that there is nothing more to consciousness than patterns of behavior or functional organization. On this reading, the thesis is interesting: it would indeed be contradictory to reject behaviorism theoretically while embracing it methodologically. 

The argument needs both readings. It needs the first (weak) reading to establish that researchers "adopt a behaviorist criterion"—since they obviously use behavioral evidence. But it needs the second (strong) reading to generate the contradiction with "anti-behaviorist theories"—since only theoretical behaviorism conflicts with believing in phenomenal consciousness. The argument slides between treating reports as *evidence for* consciousness versus treating reportability as *constitutive of* consciousness. Most researchers would say they use behavioral criteria evidentially while remaining theoretical anti-behaviorists, dissolving the alleged instability.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This significantly damages the thesis. The distinction between evidential and constitutive roles is well-established and the thesis needs to show why using reports as evidence commits one to theoretical behaviorism—a burden it hasn't met.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses that the instability operates at the level of *empirical testability*, not metaphysics. The defence proceeds in three steps:

**First**, the evidential/constitutive distinction only works if the evidential relation is defeasible—if evidence can in principle come apart from what it evidences. But consciousness science systematically treats the evidential relation as *indefeasible in practice*. When researchers control for attention, memory, and task demands, they don't treat residual dissociations between report and consciousness as live possibilities to investigate. Instead, these controls are designed to *ensure* that report tracks consciousness. The methodology presupposes that once confounds are eliminated, report *must* indicate consciousness. This is operationally equivalent to constitutive behaviorism.

**Second**, the thesis identifies a *falsifiability problem*, not a metaphysical commitment. Consider: if your theory says consciousness could occur without reportability (anti-behaviorism), but your methods only count something as conscious when it produces reports (after controls), then cases of unreportable consciousness become *empirically inaccessible by design*. The theory makes predictions it renders untestable. The researcher might insist "I only use reports as evidence, not as constitutive"—but this is precisely the unfalsifiability. The theory's anti-behaviorist content never contacts experiment.

**Third**, the objection's proposed dissolution—"we use behavioral criteria evidentially while remaining theoretical anti-behaviorists"—is exactly the unstable position the thesis targets. You cannot simultaneously hold (a) consciousness might come apart from report-dispositions, and (b) our evidence-gathering methods are sound. If (a) is true, then systematically using reports as evidence will systematically miss unreportable consciousness, making the methods unsound. If the methods are sound, then reports must reliably track consciousness, making (a) empirically idle. The instability isn't equivocation—it's that the theoretical possibility in (a) undermines confidence in (b), while commitment to (b) makes (a) unfalsifiable.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though not completely.

The third point has force: there is genuine tension between asserting consciousness could dissociate from report while treating report-based methods as adequate. But this reformulates rather than saves the original thesis. The objection succeeds in showing the thesis *as stated* equivocates—it claims researchers "adopt a behaviorist criterion" without distinguishing evidential from constitutive uses.

The defence's strongest move is reframing this as a falsifiability problem rather than metaphysical contradiction. But even this refined version faces a problem the defence doesn't address: researchers could respond that report-based methods are adequate for studying *reportable* consciousness while remaining agnostic about unreportable consciousness. This isn't unfalsifiable—it's just a limited research program. The thesis needs to show why studying reportable consciousness while theorizing about consciousness-in-general creates instability, and the defence hasn't established this.

The equivocation charge stands. The thesis trades on ambiguity about what "adopting a behaviorist criterion" means.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is unfalsifiable because it systematically redefines what counts as "truly rejecting behaviorism" to immunize itself from counterexamples. 

Consider any actual research program in consciousness science. If researchers use reports and task performance, the thesis says "Aha! You've adopted behaviorist criteria." If researchers respond "No, we use reports as evidence while theorizing that consciousness could come apart from reports," the thesis replies "That's the instability—you can't do both." If researchers then say "We're studying reportable consciousness specifically, remaining agnostic about unreportable forms," the thesis can respond "You're still presupposing reports track consciousness, which is operationally behaviorist."

Every possible methodological stance gets classified as either (a) behaviorist, or (b) unstably combining anti-behaviorist theory with behaviorist methods. The thesis has no criterion for what would count as legitimately studying phenomenal consciousness non-behavioristically while using empirical methods. Any use of behavioral evidence becomes "tacit behaviorism"; any theoretical distance from behaviorism becomes "unfalsifiable anti-behaviorism."

The weak point is the premise that using reports as evidence, even after careful controls, constitutes "adopting a behaviorist criterion." This conflates methodological necessity (needing some empirical access) with theoretical commitment. The thesis protects itself by treating any actual scientific practice as confirming the instability, while offering no coherent picture of what stable, empirically-engaged consciousness science would look like.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This seriously damages the thesis. It reveals that the "instability" charge applies to any empirical methodology whatsoever, making the thesis unfalsifiable rather than genuinely diagnostic.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mischaracterizes falsifiability by treating "no possible empirical methodology escapes the charge" as equivalent to "unfalsifiable." The defence proceeds by showing the thesis makes precise, falsifiable predictions about actual research practice.

**First**, the thesis is straightforwardly falsifiable by methodological pluralism. If consciousness researchers systematically employed methods that don't presuppose report-consciousness alignment, the thesis fails. Examples that would falsify it: (a) widespread use of neural measures treated as *independent* arbiters of consciousness rather than proxies for what subjects would report if asked; (b) theoretical frameworks that predict specific dissociations between reportability and consciousness, with experimental designs that could confirm these dissociations; (c) research programs that treat unreportable consciousness as a primary target rather than an acknowledged-but-ignored possibility. The thesis predicts these don't characterize mainstream consciousness science. That's empirically testable.

**Second**, the objection strawmans by claiming the thesis offers "no coherent picture of what stable consciousness science would look like." But the thesis's logical structure provides exactly this: *either* embrace behaviorism explicitly (consciousness just is reportability/functional organization—this is stable and empirically tractable) *or* develop methods that don't privilege report as the gold standard (measure neural signatures, predict dissociations, take unreportable consciousness seriously—this is stable and empirically engaged). The instability charge applies specifically to the middle position: maintaining anti-behaviorist theory while using exclusively report-validated methods. That's a determinate target.

**Third**, the objection's examples actually confirm rather than refute the thesis. When researchers say "we're studying reportable consciousness specifically," they've abandoned the anti-behaviorist theoretical commitments the field explicitly maintains. Most consciousness researchers don't say "we're agnostic about whether phenomenal consciousness extends beyond reportability"—they say "we're studying phenomenal consciousness (full stop), and we use reports as evidence." The thesis targets precisely this combination. If researchers genuinely restricted their theoretical claims to match their methodological scope, there would be no instability. The thesis predicts they don't—and this is checkable by reading what theories actually claim versus what methods actually measure.

**Fourth**, the "no coherent alternative" objection proves too much. Applied to any instability diagnosis, it would demand the critic provide a fully-worked-out stable alternative before identifying the instability. But instability charges are legitimate when they show a position's commitments pull in incompatible directions, regardless of whether the critic has solved the underlying problem. The thesis claims consciousness science is caught between anti-behaviorist aspirations and behaviorist methods. Showing this tension is legitimate even if resolving it is hard.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The fourth point about instability charges is correct in principle but doesn't save this thesis. The problem is that the defence's "falsifying evidence"—methodological pluralism, neural measures as independent arbiters, theories predicting dissociations—actually *exists* in consciousness science, contradicting the thesis's empirical predictions.

Consider: Global Workspace Theory and Higher-Order Thought theories do make predictions about report-consciousness dissociations (inattentional blindness, subliminal perception). Researchers like Ned Block explicitly theorize phenomenal overflow beyond access. Neural measures (P3b, ignition, sustained activation) are often treated as having independent theoretical significance, not mere proxies for reports. There are active debates about whether these neural signatures could come apart from reportability.

The defence claims these would falsify the thesis, but then insists they don't characterize "mainstream consciousness science." This is an empirical bet the defence is losing. The field isn't as monolithically report-dependent as the thesis requires.

More fundamentally, the objection's core point stands: the thesis needs to explain why using reports as *primary but defeasible* evidence, while theorizing about consciousness-in-general, constitutes an unstable position rather than simply an incomplete research program. The defence asserts this is unstable but doesn't show why the combination is incoherent rather than merely methodologically limited.

The unfalsifiability charge succeeds because the thesis can always respond to apparent counterexamples (neural measures, dissociation predictions) by saying they're not genuinely independent of report-validation or not sufficiently mainstream. The thesis lacks clear criteria for what would count as a stable, empirically-engaged anti-behaviorist methodology.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis's core claim—that consciousness science exhibits instability by rejecting behaviorism theoretically while adopting it methodologically—has been extensively articulated and defended by **Ned Block** in his distinction between **phenomenal consciousness (P-consciousness) and access consciousness (A-consciousness)**. 

Block's framework (1995, 2007) argues that mainstream consciousness science conflates these two concepts: P-consciousness is raw phenomenal experience (what-it's-like-ness), while A-consciousness is availability for report and rational control. He explicitly charges that researchers study A-consciousness (using report-based methods) while claiming to study P-consciousness, creating exactly the instability this thesis identifies. Block argues this conflation makes theories unfalsifiable because they can retreat between P and A depending on whether they're defending theoretical commitments or experimental results.

Furthermore, **Eric Schwitzgebel** has made similar arguments about the "methodological puzzle of consciousness," noting that introspection-based and report-based methods presuppose what they claim to discover about consciousness. The **phenomenological tradition** (Husserl, Merleau-Ponty) has long criticized scientific approaches to consciousness for operationalizing consciousness in ways that predetermine answers to fundamental questions about its nature.

The thesis presents as novel instability diagnosis what is actually a well-established critique within philosophy of mind and consciousness studies. The "instability" between anti-behaviorist theory and behaviorist methods is Block's central contribution to the field.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This significantly undermines the thesis's originality. While the specific framing around "unfalsifiability by design" adds rhetorical force, the core instability claim is Block's access/phenomenal distinction, which is now textbook material in consciousness studies.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses *identifying a distinction* with *diagnosing an instability*. Block's access/phenomenal distinction is a conceptual tool, not an instability charge, and the defence shows why the thesis makes a fundamentally different move.

**First**, Block's framework actually *enables* rather than *preempts* the thesis. Block distinguishes P-consciousness from A-consciousness precisely to argue they can come apart—that there could be phenomenal experience without access (overflow), or access without phenomenology (zombie-like processing). But Block doesn't conclude from this that consciousness science is methodologically unstable or unfalsifiable. Instead, he uses the distinction to *design experiments* testing whether P and A dissociate (the "overflow" debate with Lau, Rosenthal, etc.). Block believes his distinction makes the science *better* by clarifying what we're measuring. The thesis claims something Block doesn't: that maintaining the P/A distinction while using A-based methods creates *unfalsifiability*, not clarification. Block thinks the distinction solves problems; the thesis claims it creates them.

**Second**, the thesis targets a *structural feature of research programs*, not just a conceptual confusion. Block says researchers sometimes conflate P and A. The thesis says something stronger: that the field's *design* makes theories unfalsifiable because anti-behaviorist theoretical commitments (P-consciousness exists and might dissociate from A) never contact experimental practice (which measures A). This is a claim about falsifiability architecture, not conceptual hygiene. Block's solution—"be clearer about whether you're studying P or A"—is exactly what the thesis argues doesn't resolve the instability. You can't study P using only A-measures while maintaining they might dissociate.

**Third**, Schwitzgebel's "methodological puzzle" and phenomenological critiques aren't the same instability. Schwitzgebel worries about introspection's reliability and theory-ladenness—whether first-person reports accurately track experience. The phenomenologists critique naturalization of consciousness—whether third-person methods can capture first-person reality. Neither makes the specific falsifiability claim: that researchers build unfalsifiability by pairing anti-behaviorist theories (consciousness could transcend report) with behaviorist methods (report is the measure), creating theories that *seem* empirically supported while being protected from refutation. The thesis's target is narrower and more precise than general worries about method.

**Fourth**, the thesis's originality lies in the *unfalsifiability diagnosis*. Lots of critics note tensions between theory and method in consciousness science. The thesis claims these tensions aren't just unfortunate gaps to be filled by better methods—they're *structurally unfalsifiable* because the theoretical content (anti-behaviorism) that distinguishes these theories from behaviorism is precisely the content rendered empirically idle by the methods. This is a specific claim about how the field generates the appearance of progress: theories seem to make contact with evidence, but the contact is guaranteed because the theories' distinctive content is never tested.

**Fifth**, that Block's distinction is "textbook material" doesn't mean the instability charge is established—it means the field has *incorporated* the distinction without resolving the underlying tension. The thesis predicts exactly this: researchers now routinely distinguish P and A, cite Block, acknowledge possible dissociations, then proceed to use A-based methods while making claims about P. The distinction's acceptance makes the instability *more entrenched*, not resolved. Block gave the field vocabulary to articulate the problem while continuing to exhibit it.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies something important.

The first and second points have real force: Block's distinction is indeed different from an unfalsifiability charge, and the thesis does make a specific structural claim about falsifiability that Block doesn't. But this shows the thesis *differs* from Block, not that it survives the "already taken" objection.

The problem is that the defence concedes too much while claiming distinctiveness. Yes, Block doesn't conclude the field is unfalsifiable—but **he and his interlocutors spend decades debating exactly whether P/A can be experimentally separated**, which is the empirical question the thesis claims is rendered untestable. The overflow debate (Block vs. Lau, Phillips, Cohen) is precisely about whether current methods can distinguish P from A. This debate's existence contradicts the thesis's claim that the question is unfalsifiable by design.

Moreover, the defence's claim that Block thinks his distinction "solves problems" while the thesis claims it "creates them" actually weakens the thesis. If leading researchers recognize the P/A distinction and actively debate how to test it experimentally, then the field isn't exhibiting the instability the thesis diagnoses—it's explicitly grappling with exactly the tension the thesis identifies. The thesis needs researchers to be *unaware* of or *ignoring* the theory-method gap. But Block's widespread influence shows they're not.

The "already taken

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis's argument proves too much because if it worked, it would establish that **any science studying unobservable entities through observable effects is unfalsifiable by design**.

Consider particle physics. Physicists claim to study quarks (entities with intrinsic properties like charm and strangeness) while explicitly rejecting the view that quarks are nothing but their detector signatures. Yet experimental paradigms treat detector readings—bubble chamber tracks, calorimeter outputs—as the gold standard for whether quarks are present. When detectors fire in certain patterns, physicists count quarks as present; when they don't, quarks are absent. This operative criterion is purely observational.

The thesis's logic applies directly: physicists cannot both reject observationalism in theory (quarks have intrinsic properties beyond their observable effects) and rely on observational criteria in practice. If your theory says quark-properties could come apart from detector signatures, but your methods presuppose they don't, you've built in unfalsifiability. Either embrace observationalism openly, or admit your theory makes no testable predictions.

The same applies to: evolutionary biology (unobserved past events inferred from present evidence), neuroscience (mental states inferred from neural/behavioral correlates), molecular biology (gene function inferred from phenotypic effects). Every science studying theoretical entities through empirical proxies exhibits the thesis's "instability."

The weak premise is that **using behavioral/reportability evidence for consciousness while theorizing it could dissociate from behavior constitutes unfalsifiable instability**. This conflates the general scientific practice of inferring theoretical entities from observable effects with a methodological defect unique to consciousness science.

# STRENGTH: 5/5

This is fatal. The thesis's logic, if sound, would indict the entire scientific enterprise of studying unobservables through observables. Since we don't consider particle physics unfalsifiable by design, the argument must fail.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fails to recognize that consciousness has a unique epistemic structure that blocks the analogy to other unobservables. The defence proceeds by showing consciousness differs fundamentally from quarks in ways that make the instability diagnosis non-generalizable.

**First**, the disanalogy is decisive: we have **independent theoretical reasons** to believe quarks exist and possess the properties physicists attribute to them, reasons that don't route through the very measurements the objection mentions. Quarks are posited because they explain symmetries in particle interactions, conservation laws, the structure of the periodic table, quantum chromodynamics' mathematical elegance, and predictive success across domains. The theory's confirmation is holistic—bubble chamber tracks are one piece of evidence among many, and the theory makes novel predictions (like the omega-minus particle) confirmed by those tracks. The theoretical content exceeds the observational base.

But for consciousness, the situation is inverted. The **only reason** we believe phenomenal consciousness exists—the only epistemic ground for the concept—is first-person acquaintance with our own experience. We don't infer consciousness from behavioral patterns and then discover it has additional properties. We start with phenomenal knowledge and then try to correlate it with behavior/reports. This means when researchers use reports as evidence for consciousness in others, they're not inferring an unobservable from its effects (like quarks from tracks). They're assuming the report-consciousness correlation holds based on the first-person case, then treating it as evidential. But this makes the theoretical claim that consciousness could dissociate from reports *empirically idle* in a way quark-properties dissociating from detector signatures is not.

**Second**, the quark case has **genuine falsifiability structure** the consciousness case lacks. Particle physics makes risky predictions: if quarks have property X, detectors should fire in pattern Y under conditions Z. When predictions fail (like the missing solar neutrinos), the theory faces genuine pressure and gets modified (neutrino oscillation). The theory's content constrains what observations count as confirming it. But consciousness theories that posit P/A dissociation make no such risky predictions. They say consciousness *might* occur without report, but provide no independent criterion for when it does. The theory accommodates any pattern of reports—if subjects report, there's consciousness; if they don't, there might still be consciousness (but unreportably). This is precisely the unfalsifiability structure the thesis diagnoses.

**Third**, the objection's examples actually support the thesis through their differences. In evolutionary biology, we have **multiple independent lines of evidence** (fossils, biogeography, molecular clocks, comparative anatomy, observed speciation) that constrain and test theories. Predictions fail regularly—punctuated equilibrium emerged because gradualism's predictions about fossil records failed. In neuroscience, neural/behavioral correlates are **not the only evidence** for mental states—we have first-person reports, computational models, cross-species comparisons, psychophysics. The theoretical content makes contact with evidence through multiple channels. But consciousness science studying P-consciousness has only one channel: reports/behavior. When theories claim consciousness could transcend this channel, they make themselves unfalsifiable in a way these other sciences don't.

**Fourth**, the key premise the objection attacks is actually the thesis's strength. The thesis doesn't claim "any science using proxies for unobservables is unfalsifiable." It claims consciousness science specifically exhibits unfalsifiability because: (a) the theoretical posit (phenomenal consciousness) is epistemically grounded in first-person access, (b) the methods rely exclusively on third-person behavioral evidence, (c) the theories explicitly claim (a) and (b) could dissociate, (d) but provide no independent way to detect such dissociation. This combination—not just "studying unobservables through observables"—creates unfalsifiability. Quarks don't have property (a), and particle physics doesn't have property (d).

**Fifth**, the "proves too much" objection has a standard response: showing a principle has wide application doesn't refute it if the applications are correct. If the thesis's logic did reveal that certain research programs in particle physics or evolutionary biology were unfalsifiable, that would be a substantive discovery, not a reductio. But the defence shows the logic doesn't apply to those cases because they lack the specific epistemic structure consciousness science has. The thesis's scope is precisely calibrated to cases where: the unobservable is known first-personally, the observable proxy is third-personal, the theory claims they could dissociate, and there's no independent test of dissociation. This describes consciousness science specifically.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds.

The objection's analogy to particle physics fails because it ignores the unique epistemic structure of consciousness research. The first point is decisive: consciousness is not a theoretical posit inferred from behavioral evidence (like quarks from detector tracks), but an introspectively known phenomenon that researchers try to correlate with behavior.

### R2. Consciousness theories generate testable predictions only when supplemented with 'bridging principles' linking their theoretical terms to observables, but these bridging principles are doing all the empirical work, so what we're really testing is not IIT versus GWT but different measurement theories about how consciousness maps to neural activity.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The framing of consciousness science as pre-paradigmatic and needing a crucial experiment to establish the right theory
- **Sketch:** The argument draws on philosophy of science's distinction between theories and measurement theories. IIT says consciousness is integrated information; to test this we need bridging principles like 'fMRI BOLD response measures information integration' or 'phi can be approximated by this EEG metric.' GWT says consciousness is global availability; to test this we need 'P3 wave indicates global broadcast' or 'frontoparietal activity measures workspace access.' But these bridging principles are substantive empirical claims that could be false even if the underlying theory is true. When experiments seem to favor one theory, what's actually happening is that one set of bridging principles is working better than another. The real dispute is not 'what is consciousness' but 'which neural signatures are valid measurements of which computational properties.' This reframes the debate: we're not in a pre-paradigm crisis about consciousness itself, but in a measurement-methodology debate like early thermodynamics when different instruments gave different temperature readings. The solution isn't a crucial experiment between theories but better calibration of how theoretical constructs map to measurements.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Bridging principles doing the work reframes debate productively; shifts focus to measurement theory rather than metaphysics.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Showed theories make architectural predictions constraining valid bridging principles; defence retreated to weaker claims about measurement controversy
  - TRIVIAL — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. Exposed thesis as partly repackaging Duhem-Quine; defence salvaged only that bridging principles are unusually contested, abandoning 'all the work' claim
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Identified slide between 'necessary for predictions' and 'sufficient for empirical content'; defence's attempt to unify readings failed
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. Defence successfully listed concrete falsifiers and showed refinement was dialectical adjustment not evasion
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Consciousness science exhibits an unusual structure where bridging principles are more contested than theoretical architectures, tracking theoretical allegiances rather than converging independently, making measurement methodology validation the primary obstacle to empirical progress rather than crucial experiments between theories
- **Flaw:** The radical claim that bridging principles do 'all' the empirical work while theories contribute nothing was refuted by showing theories make architectural predictions that constrain which bridging principles are coherent

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider the prediction from IIT that a cerebellum with high neural complexity but minimal integration should lack consciousness, while GWT predicts consciousness requires global broadcasting regardless of local complexity. These theories make directly opposing predictions about cerebellar consciousness that don't depend on bridging principles at all—they're architectural claims about what kinds of information processing suffice for consciousness. Similarly, IIT predicts that a feedforward network perfectly mimicking conscious behavior should be unconscious (no feedback integration), while functionalist theories predict it should be conscious (right functional profile). Here the theories differ on whether feedback loops are constitutive of consciousness or merely typical correlates. The bridging principles (how to measure integration, how to detect global broadcast) are needed to test degree and presence, but the architectural predictions—"feedback required" versus "feedforward sufficient," "cerebellum lacks consciousness" versus "complex computation anywhere suffices"—are theoretical commitments that generate opposing predictions independent of measurement choices. A finding that cerebellums are conscious would refute IIT's architectural claim no matter which bridging principles we use to establish that consciousness.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously undermines the thesis by showing that consciousness theories make structural/architectural predictions that are genuinely theoretical (not mere measurement methodology) and generate opposing predictions that would survive any reasonable choice of bridging principles.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates *what would refute a theory if established* with *what can be tested without bridging principles*. Yes, IIT makes the architectural claim "cerebellum lacks consciousness despite complexity" — but we cannot test this without bridging principles that tell us *how to detect whether the cerebellum is conscious*. The objection says "a finding that cerebellums are conscious would refute IIT" — but what counts as such a finding? 

Do we accept cerebellar patients' reports? Then we need the bridging principle "verbal report reliably indicates consciousness." Do we use neural signatures? Then we need "signature X indicates consciousness" — which is precisely a bridging principle. The objection assumes we have theory-neutral access to the fact of cerebellar consciousness, but we don't. Every method for establishing that consciousness is present or absent requires bridging principles linking some observable to the theoretical construct "consciousness."

The same applies to the feedforward network case. IIT predicts it's unconscious, functionalism predicts it's conscious — but to test this we must decide: Does behavioral indistinguishability from conscious systems indicate consciousness? Does absence of certain neural signatures indicate absence of consciousness? These are bridging principles, and different choices yield different experimental outcomes.

The architectural predictions are indeed theoretical commitments, but they're commitments about *unobservable* properties (whether something "is conscious"). To connect them to observation requires bridging principles. When experiments seem to decide between theories, they're actually deciding which bridging principles are valid. If we found "conscious cerebellums," IIT proponents could respond: "Your measurement method was invalid — you detected something else, not consciousness." The dispute would shift to measurement validity, confirming the thesis.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though not completely.

The defence is right that we need *some* bridging principles to detect consciousness. But the objection's force lies in showing that the theories make **opposing architectural predictions that constrain which bridging principles are even coherent with each theory**. 

If IIT is true, then *no* valid bridging principle could ever indicate cerebellar consciousness, because IIT's architecture claim rules it out a priori. If we developed a bridging principle that indicated cerebellar consciousness, IIT would be refuted — we couldn't coherently maintain both "IIT is true" and "this measurement validly detects consciousness in cerebellums." The architectural claim isn't downstream of measurement choices; it constrains them.

The thesis claimed bridging principles do "all the empirical work," but here the architectural theory does crucial work: it tells us which measurement methodologies are even possible. The theories aren't just being supplemented by bridging principles; they're generating constraints on what bridging principles could be valid. That's genuine theoretical content, not reducible to measurement methodology.

The defence's final move — "IIT proponents could always reject the measurement" — actually proves too much: it would make IIT unfalsifiable, which undermines rather than supports the thesis's picture of normal measurement-methodology disputes.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as a substantive reframing of consciousness science, but it's actually just restating the standard philosophical point that theories require auxiliary hypotheses to generate predictions—a point established by Duhem (1906) and Quine (1951) that applies to literally every scientific theory. Calling auxiliary hypotheses "bridging principles" and saying they "do all the empirical work" is merely rhetorical redescription of the uncontested fact that theories underdetermine observations. The claim that "we're really testing measurement theories" is a verbal stipulation—we could equally say we're "really testing" the conjunction of theory + auxiliaries, or "really testing" whether the whole package works. Nothing empirical hangs on which element we call the "real" object of testing. The supposedly novel reframing—that consciousness science is in a "measurement-methodology debate" rather than "pre-paradigmatic crisis"—is just choosing to emphasize one horn of Duhem-Quine underdetermination over another. Early thermodynamics had the same structure: theory + instrument calibration both needed to be right. Calling that a "measurement debate" rather than "theoretical dispute" is a labeling choice, not a discovery. The thesis dresses up "scientific theories need auxiliary hypotheses" in new terminology and presents this truism as if it were a substantive insight about consciousness science specifically.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This is a genuine problem the thesis must answer. The defence against counterexample showed the thesis has *some* content beyond pure Duhem-Quine, since it claims bridging principles do "all" the work. But the attack reveals that much of the thesis's apparent novelty may be verbal repackaging of uncontested philosophy of science. The thesis needs to show what's specific to consciousness science beyond generic underdetermination.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses what's distinctive about consciousness science by assimilating it to generic underdetermination. Yes, all theories require auxiliary hypotheses—but in mature sciences, the auxiliaries are relatively uncontroversial background assumptions (thermometer calibration, spectroscope reliability) while theoretical disputes concern the laws themselves. The thesis identifies something backwards about consciousness science: the bridging principles are *more* controversial than the theories.

Consider: physicists debating string theory versus loop quantum gravity don't primarily argue about whether particle detectors work. But consciousness researchers debating IIT versus GWT spend enormous energy arguing about whether neural correlates of consciousness are valid, whether report-based measures are reliable, whether phi calculations capture anything real. The "auxiliary hypotheses" aren't auxiliary—they're the main locus of disagreement.

This reversal matters because it explains a distinctive pathology: consciousness theories proliferate without decisive resolution not because we lack crucial experiments, but because researchers can maintain their preferred theory by rejecting bridging principles that threaten it. In thermodynamics, you couldn't save your theory by endlessly disputing thermometer validity—instrument calibration converged independently. In consciousness science, there's no such convergence. "Does P3 wave indicate consciousness?" remains contested, and which answer you accept tracks which theory you favor.

The thesis isn't just relabeling Duhem-Quine; it's diagnosing why consciousness science exhibits unusual resistance to empirical resolution compared to other underdetermined theories. The reframing has practical bite: it suggests progress requires not better experiments between theories, but independent validation of measurement methods—something consciousness science systematically lacks. That's not a truism; it's a specific prescription that differs from the field's self-understanding.

The thermodynamics comparison actually supports this: early thermodynamics *did* progress primarily through measurement standardization (agreeing on temperature scales, pressure gauges) rather than crucial experiments between theories. Recognizing consciousness science is in that phase, not the crucial-experiment phase, is substantive guidance.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds partially but exposes a deeper problem.

The defence is right that consciousness science shows an unusual pattern: bridging principles are more contested than in mature sciences, and this contestability tracks theoretical allegiances. That's a real observation, not mere relabeling. The practical prescription—focus on independent measurement validation—does differ from "run crucial experiments between theories."

However, the defence concedes too much ground. The claim that bridging principles do "all the empirical work" was supposed to be the thesis's radical core—the idea that theories themselves contribute nothing empirical. But the defence retreats to a more modest claim: bridging principles are *unusually controversial* and *relatively more important* than in other sciences. That's interesting but far weaker.

Moreover, the defence's own examples undermine the strong version. It says researchers "maintain their preferred theory by rejecting bridging principles that threaten it"—but this only works if theories and bridging principles are separable, if theories have independent content that researchers are motivated to preserve. If bridging principles did *all* the work, there'd be nothing to preserve. The fact that researchers have stable theoretical commitments that guide their bridging principle choices suggests theories do contribute substantive content.

The thermodynamics analogy also cuts against the thesis. Early thermodynamics wasn't "really just" measurement methodology—it was simultaneous development of theory and measurement, with both contributing. Saying consciousness science is "really" about measurement rather than theory falsely dichotomizes what should be seen as co-development.

The thesis survives as: "Consciousness science's bridging principles are unusually contested and require independent validation before crucial experiments become meaningful." But the provocative claim that we're "really testing" measurement theories rather than consciousness theories—that bridging principles do "all" the work—fails. We're testing both, with bridging principles being more problematic than usual, not exclusively important.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "empirical work" between two readings: (1) *logical work in deriving testable predictions* and (2) *epistemic work in determining what we should believe*. The argument needs both readings to succeed.

The thesis is plausible on reading (1): bridging principles are logically necessary to connect theoretical terms to observables, so in a derivational sense they're doing the "work" of making predictions testable. IIT's claim about integrated information entails nothing observable until we add "phi can be measured via EEG metric X." True but uninteresting—this is just Duhem-Quine.

The thesis is interesting on reading (2): when experiments seem to discriminate between theories, what's really being tested epistemically is which measurements are valid, not which theory of consciousness is true. This would be substantive—it would mean the theories themselves contribute no empirical content to our belief-updating.

But the argument cannot run on reading (2) alone. The counterexample showed that theories make architectural predictions (cerebellum lacks consciousness, feedforward networks are unconscious) that constrain which bridging principles are even coherent. When we update beliefs based on evidence, we're updating about *both* the theory's architectural claims *and* the bridging principles' validity. The thesis needs reading (1)'s trivial point to establish that bridging principles are necessary, then illicitly slides to reading (2)'s interesting claim that they're sufficient—that theories contribute nothing to empirical content. The equivocation masks that this stronger claim was already refuted.

# STRENGTH

4/5 — This seriously damages the thesis. The equivocation between "necessary for prediction-derivation" and "sufficient for empirical content" is precisely what allows the thesis to sound both defensible and interesting. On the trivial reading it's true but uninteresting; on the interesting reading the previous exchanges showed it's false (theories constrain valid measurements, so they contribute empirical content). The thesis needs both readings but cannot sustain the interesting one, making this a genuine equivocation rather than mere ambiguity.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fabricates an equivocation that doesn't exist. There is one consistent reading throughout: bridging principles do the empirical work in the sense that *empirical discrimination between theories depends on which bridging principles we accept, not on the theories' intrinsic content*.

Consider what happens in actual experimental disputes. Researchers run an experiment that supposedly favors GWT over IIT. IIT proponents respond: "Your phi approximation was invalid" or "You measured reportability, not consciousness itself." GWT proponents respond to contrary evidence: "Your global broadcast measure didn't capture true workspace dynamics" or "That signature reflects attention, not consciousness." Each side maintains its theory by rejecting the bridging principles that threaten it.

This isn't equivocation between logical and epistemic work—it's a single claim about epistemic work: our belief-updating is driven by which measurement methodologies we find credible, not by the theories making differential predictions we can recognize as such. The theories are empirically inert in the sense that they don't themselves determine which evidence counts for or against them.

The objection claims theories "constrain which bridging principles are coherent"—but this constraint relation runs backward in practice. Researchers don't derive bridging principles from theories; they select theories that rationalize their preferred measurement practices. Someone committed to report-based measures gravitates toward theories that privilege access/broadcast; someone committed to integrated information measures gravitates toward IIT. The apparent constraint from theory to measurement is actually a consistency requirement that researchers satisfy by choosing compatible theories.

The architectural predictions don't refute this. Yes, IIT says cerebellums lack consciousness—but this prediction is empirically empty until we specify how to detect consciousness, and different bridging principles yield different verdicts on whether cerebellums are conscious. The architectural claim doesn't constrain measurements; it's a promissory note that becomes empirical only when cashed out through bridging principles. And which bridging principles we accept determines which architectural claims we end up believing.

This is one coherent reading, not an equivocation: the locus of empirical content is in measurement methodology, not theoretical architecture. Theories organize and rationalize measurement practices but don't independently contribute empirical constraints.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence makes a sociological observation—researchers do often maintain theories by rejecting threatening measurements—but mistakes this for a logical point about empirical content. The fact that researchers *can* save theories by rejecting bridging principles doesn't show that theories *lack* empirical content; it shows that empirical testing is holistic (Duhem-Quine again).

The critical failure is the claim that "constraint runs backward in practice"—that researchers select theories to rationalize measurements rather than deriving measurements from theories. Even if sociologically true, this is irrelevant to whether theories have empirical content. The question is whether theories *could in principle* constrain measurements, not whether researchers *in practice* use them that way.

And the counterexample already established they do constrain in principle: IIT's architectural claim that feedback integration is constitutive of consciousness rules out certain bridging principles as incoherent (ones that would detect consciousness in purely feedforward systems). This is a substantive constraint. The defence's response—"the architectural claim is a promissory note"—is desperate: it tries to render theoretical claims empirically empty by definition, which would make all theories promissory notes until tested.

The defence's final sentence reveals the problem: "Theories organize and rationalize measurement practices but don't independently contribute empirical constraints." But organization and rationalization *are* forms of empirical contribution. If IIT organizes measurement practices around integration while GWT organizes them around global availability, and these lead to different experimental designs and different predictions about edge cases (cerebellum, feedforward networks, split-brain patients), then the theories are contributing empirical content through this organizational role.

The equivocation stands: the thesis conflates "bridging principles are necessary for deriving predictions" (true, trivial) with "bridging principles determine all empirical content" (false, as theories constrain which bridging principles are coherent and organize measurement practices in empirically consequential ways).

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis is structured to be immune from empirical refutation by definitional retreat. Suppose we present evidence that consciousness theories DO make distinctive empirical contributions—say, IIT's prediction that cerebellums lack consciousness generates testable architectural constraints independent of measurement choices. The thesis can respond: "Ah, but to TEST that you need bridging principles, so the bridging principles are doing the work." 

Suppose instead we show that bridging principles and theories are genuinely co-tested, that empirical work is distributed between them. The thesis retreats: "Yes, but bridging principles are MORE controversial and RELATIVELY more important in consciousness science." The radical claim—that we're "really testing" measurement theories "not" consciousness theories—gets watered down to "measurement issues are unusually prominent."

Suppose we demonstrate that theories constrain which bridging principles are coherent (feedforward consciousness detectors are incoherent with IIT). The thesis redefines: "That's just organizational/rationalizing work, not INDEPENDENT empirical content." Every way theories might contribute empirically gets redefined as "not the real empirical work."

The thesis's core term "doing the empirical work" remains strategically ambiguous—it can mean "logically necessary for predictions" (trivially true), "epistemically sufficient" (false), or "relatively more important" (weak). When pressed, it shifts between readings. Any evidence that theories contribute empirically gets reclassified as theories "organizing" or "rationalizing" bridging principles—which is then declared not to count as "real" empirical contribution. The thesis is unfalsifiable because "empirical work" can be redefined to exclude whatever theories turn out to do.

# STRENGTH

2/5 — This attack has some force but ultimately doesn't land cleanly. The thesis does show flexibility in its claims (shifting from "all the work" to "unusually controversial"), which suggests some protective maneuvering. However, this looks more like a thesis that's poorly formulated or overstated rather than one that's systematically unfalsifiable. The core claim—that consciousness science's empirical disputes center on measurement validity more than theoretical architecture—is testable in principle: we could examine actual scientific disputes, count arguments about measurement versus theory, analyze whether measurement controversies track theoretical allegiances. The thesis makes a claim about the *structure* of scientific disagreement that's empirically accessible. While the defender did retreat from strong to weak versions, that's normal dialectical adjustment, not unfalsifiability. The thesis would be genuinely threatened by evidence that bridging principles converge independently of theoretical commitments, or that crucial experiments decisively favor one theory regardless of measurement choices.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes dialectical refinement for unfalsifiable retreat. When a thesis responds to counterexamples by clarifying its claims, that's not immunization—it's what rational defense requires. The supposed "shifts" are consistent specifications of a single core claim, not evasive redefinitions.

The core claim has remained stable: *in consciousness science specifically, empirical discrimination between theories is primarily constrained by measurement methodology disputes rather than by the theories' architectural content*. This generates testable predictions about the field's structure:

1. **Prediction**: Measurement validity disputes should be more frequent and less resolved than in comparably mature sciences
2. **Prediction**: Researchers' theoretical commitments should predict their measurement methodology preferences more strongly than in other fields  
3. **Prediction**: Apparent crucial experiments should generate measurement-validity disputes rather than theoretical consensus
4. **Prediction**: Progress should come more from measurement standardization than from experiments deciding between theories

These are empirically checkable claims about scientific practice. The objection's examples of "retreat" are actually the thesis specifying *how* theories and measurements relate in consciousness science versus other sciences. Saying theories "organize and rationalize" rather than "independently constrain" isn't evasion—it's a specific claim about the dependency structure that could be false.

The objection claims "empirical work" is strategically ambiguous, but the thesis has been explicit: empirical work means *what determines belief-updating in practice*. When IIT and GWT generate apparently opposing predictions, the actual disagreements that emerge are about whether phi approximations are valid, whether P3 reflects consciousness, whether report-based measures are reliable. These measurement disputes don't get resolved by appeal to theory; they persist and track theoretical allegiances. That's the phenomenon the thesis describes.

The charge of unfalsifiability requires showing the thesis could accommodate any possible evidence. But the thesis would be straightforwardly refuted by:

- **Convergence**: Measurement methodologies achieving independent validation and consensus across theoretical camps
- **Decisive experiments**: Evidence that shifts researcher beliefs about theories without generating measurement-validity disputes  
- **Theory-driven measurement**: Cases where theoretical commitments successfully predict which new measurements will prove valid
- **Architectural resolution**: Edge cases (cerebellum, feedforward networks) getting resolved through theoretical argument rather than measurement disputes

None of these have occurred, which is why the thesis remains viable. That's confirmation, not unfalsifiability.

The objection conflates "the thesis has been clarified under pressure" with "the thesis evades all pressure." The former is normal; the latter would require showing no possible evidence could refute the claim. The thesis makes specific predictions about consciousness science's dialectical structure that could easily be false.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds.

The objection overstates its case. While the thesis did retreat from its strongest formulation ("all the empirical work"), this represents normal dialectical adjustment rather than systematic unfalsifiability. The defender correctly identifies that the core claim has remained stable: consciousness science exhibits an unusual structure where measurement disputes dominate and resist resolution.

The defence's strongest point is listing specific ways the thesis could be refuted: measurement convergence, decisive experiments without measurement disputes, theory-driven measurement validation. These are genuine potential falsifiers. The thesis makes claims about the *structure of scientific disagreement* in consciousness research that are empirically accessible through analysis of actual debates.

The objection's examples of "retreat" don't show unfalsifiability—they show a thesis being pressed from an overstated version ("bridging principles do ALL the work, theories contribute NOTHING") to a defensible version ("bridging principles are the primary locus of empirical dispute and resist independent validation"). That's refinement, not evasion.

However, the defence doesn't fully escape the objection's force. The thesis's flexibility about what counts as "empirical work"—sometimes logical necessity, sometimes epistemic determinacy, sometimes practical importance—does create interpretive slack that makes it harder to pin down exactly what would refute it. The defence claims "empirical work means what determines belief-updating in practice," but this wasn't consistently clear in earlier formulations.

The verdict: The thesis is not unfalsifiable, but it is somewhat slippery. It makes testable claims about consciousness science's dialectical structure, and the defence correctly identifies potential falsifiers. But the thesis would be stronger if it committed more clearly to specific metrics for "empirical work" and "measurement dispute dominance" upfront, rather than clarifying these under pressure. The objection succeeds in showing the thesis was initially overstated and has been refined through dialectic, but fails to show it's systematically immune from refutation.

</details>
