### R3. The demand that consciousness theories make different empirical predictions commits you to the view that consciousness is causally efficacious, which excludes all epiphenomenalist and illusionist positions from the space of testable theories.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Consciousness scientists and philosophers who demand empirical testability while remaining neutral on whether consciousness is causally efficacious
- **Sketch:** If consciousness theories must be empirically distinguishable, they must make different predictions about what we observe—brain states, behaviors, reports, etc. But observable differences require consciousness to play some causal role in producing those observables. If consciousness were epiphenomenal (causally inert) or illusory (nonexistent), it couldn't produce any observable differences; all observations would be explained by the underlying physical/functional facts. Therefore, anyone insisting we need empirical evidence to choose between consciousness theories is committed to rejecting epiphenomenalism and illusionism before looking at any evidence. This reveals that the methodological principle 'theories must be empirically testable' is not neutral but already substantively excludes major positions. The seemingly procedural requirement of empirical distinguishability is actually a substantive metaphysical commitment.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Reveals that methodological requirements smuggle in substantive metaphysical commitments, which could expose hidden assumptions in research programs.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed the thesis targets an unacknowledged performative contradiction, not just definitional consequences
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 3/5, answered. Defence established a single stable reading: predictions about the consciousness-implicated observables that constitute the actual evidence
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence retreated to claiming the target position is incoherent rather than occupied, making the thesis's critical force against actual practice unclear
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence distinguished meta-methodological from object-level claims but provided no evidence the supposed confusion exists in actual practitioners
- ⚠️ **Defender folded without defending** — treat this row's scores as unreliable.
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Demanding empirical testability for consciousness theories constitutes a substantive metaphysical commitment to causal efficacy, not a neutral methodological principle, because only causally efficacious consciousness can produce the observable differences (reports, behaviors, neural correlates) that empirical testing requires
- **Flaw:** The thesis cannot establish that its target position—demanding testability while claiming metaphysical neutrality—is actually occupied by researchers who are unaware of the commitment, making it unclear whether it reveals hidden confusion or merely restates known tensions

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis is trivial because it merely restates what "epiphenomenalism" and "illusionism" already mean by definition. Epiphenomenalism is *defined* as the view that consciousness has no causal effects on the physical world. Illusionism is *defined* as the view that phenomenal consciousness doesn't really exist as we think it does. The thesis claims that demanding empirical testability "excludes" these positions—but this is just pointing out that theories explicitly denying causal efficacy are incompatible with methods requiring causal efficacy. This is like saying "demanding that a theory make predictions about physical objects commits you to excluding the view that physical objects don't exist." Yes, obviously. The thesis dresses up this definitional truth as if it were a discovery about hidden commitments. But there's no hidden commitment here: anyone familiar with epiphenomenalism knows it faces the testability problem, and this is openly discussed in the literature. The thesis simply labels as "substantive metaphysical commitment" what is actually a straightforward logical consequence of combining definitions. It's a verbal observation masquerading as philosophical insight—pointing out that methodologies assuming X are incompatible with theories denying X.

STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force because the incompatibility between epiphenomenalism and empirical testability is indeed well-known. However, the thesis isn't entirely trivial because it makes a substantive point about methodology: that the demand for empirical testability isn't metaphysically neutral but already takes sides in live debates about consciousness. This meta-level claim—that a seemingly procedural requirement smuggles in substantive commitments—has genuine philosophical content beyond mere definition-restating.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The triviality charge misidentifies what the thesis claims to reveal. The thesis is not announcing the discovery that epiphenomenalism faces testability problems—that's textbook material. Rather, it exposes a *performative contradiction* in a widespread methodological stance.

The target is explicit: consciousness scientists and philosophers who simultaneously (1) demand empirical testability as the arbiter between theories, and (2) claim to remain neutral on whether consciousness is causally efficacious. The thesis shows these commitments are incompatible. This is not trivial because the target position is actually occupied—many researchers insist on empirical methods while professing agnosticism about the metaphysics of causation, treating it as a separate question to be settled later or elsewhere.

The philosophical work lies in showing that what presents as methodological neutrality ("let's just follow the empirical evidence wherever it leads") is actually a substantive metaphysical filter operating at the level of admissible theories. The person who says "I don't take a stand on epiphenomenalism; I just require empirical testability" is like someone saying "I don't take a stand on whether God exists; I just require all explanations to be naturalistic." The commitment is real even if unacknowledged.

Consider the dialectical context: illusionists like Frankish and Dennett often present their view as the *most* scientifically respectable precisely because it's most amenable to empirical methods. They don't typically say "empirical testability rules us out." Similarly, some epiphenomenalists have tried to argue their view is empirically tractable. The thesis cuts through these moves by showing the methodological demand already settles the question against them, regardless of their protests.

The objector's analogy fails. Saying "methods assuming X exclude theories denying X" is only trivial when the assumption is explicit and acknowledged. The thesis targets cases where the assumption is *implicit* and the exclusion *unacknowledged*. That's why it has critical bite against the target position.

VERDICT.

The defence holds. The thesis is not trivial because it identifies a real tension in an actually-occupied methodological position, not merely a logical consequence of definitions everyone already accepts. The philosophical content lies in the meta-level claim about hidden commitments in methodological stances, which has genuine critical force against researchers who profess metaphysical neutrality while enforcing empirically-driven constraints.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis equivocates on "empirical predictions" between two readings: (1) predictions about any observable phenomena whatsoever, and (2) predictions specifically about consciousness-dependent observables. The argument needs both readings to work.

The thesis is plausible on reading (1): If we interpret "empirical predictions" broadly as predictions about brain states, behavior, and reports, then yes, epiphenomenal consciousness would make no differential predictions since it causally does nothing. This reading makes the exclusion claim work.

But the thesis is interesting only on reading (2): The provocative claim is that demanding empirical testability is a *substantive metaphysical commitment* about consciousness itself being causally efficacious. This seems to reveal something important about consciousness science specifically.

However, the argument cannot run on (2) alone. On that reading, the demand would be "consciousness theories must make different predictions about consciousness-dependent observables"—but this is trivially question-begging, already assuming consciousness is the kind of thing that has observable effects. The force of the thesis depends on starting with the innocent-seeming (1)—just asking for empirical predictions generally—and then showing this excludes certain positions.

Yet on (1) alone, the thesis loses its interest. Demanding that theories make predictions about *any* observables is just standard scientific methodology, not a specific commitment about consciousness. The appearance of revealing a hidden metaphysical commitment about consciousness depends on sliding between these readings.

STRENGTH: 3/5

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The equivocation charge fails because it misidentifies where the philosophical work happens. The thesis runs on a single, stable reading throughout: empirical predictions about **the observables that consciousness theories actually purport to explain**—namely, reports, judgments, behavioral discriminations, and neural correlates that constitute our evidence that consciousness exists at all.

There is no equivocation because these observables are **already consciousness-implicated** by the very nature of the debate. When we study consciousness empirically, we don't study consciousness directly (we can't—it's subjective). We study reports of experiences, discriminations between stimuli, verbal and behavioral indicators. These are the data. The thesis's point is that if consciousness makes no difference to *these specific observables*—the ones that constitute our only empirical access to consciousness—then theories about it cannot be empirically distinguished.

The objector's reading (1) vs. (2) distinction is artificial. There aren't "observables in general" versus "consciousness-dependent observables" as separate domains. There are just the observables we use to study consciousness: reports, discriminations, neural activity during conscious versus unconscious processing. The question is whether consciousness itself plays a causal role in producing these, or whether they'd occur exactly the same way without consciousness (epiphenomenalism) or without phenomenal consciousness as we conceive it (illusionism).

The thesis isn't sliding between readings—it's pointing out that the standard empirical observables in consciousness science (what the objector calls "any observable phenomena") **are precisely the ones whose explanation requires consciousness to be causally efficacious**. When Integrated Information Theory predicts different phi values than Global Workspace Theory, and we test this by looking at neural activity patterns during reported conscious vs. unconscious perception, we're assuming consciousness affects those reports and that neural activity. That's not an equivocation; that's the methodology.

The objector claims demanding predictions about "any observables is just standard scientific methodology, not a specific commitment about consciousness." But this proves the thesis's point! Standard scientific methodology, when applied to consciousness, **imports** the assumption that consciousness is causally efficacious. The methodology isn't neutral—it works only for causally efficacious phenomena. Applying it to consciousness commits you to consciousness being that kind of thing.

# VERDICT

The defence holds. There is no equivocation. The thesis uses a single, consistent notion of empirical predictions throughout: predictions about the observable evidence (reports, behaviors, neural correlates) that consciousness science actually studies. The appearance of equivocation comes from the objector's attempt to separate "observables in general" from "consciousness-dependent observables," but this distinction doesn't map onto the actual practice of consciousness science, where the observables we study are already the ones whose explanation requires taking a stand on consciousness's causal role. The thesis successfully shows that applying standard empirical methodology to consciousness is not metaphysically neutral.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis is unfalsifiable because any apparent counterexample gets redefined away through strategic reinterpretation of what counts as "demanding empirical testability" or "remaining neutral." 

Consider the natural counterexample: a researcher who says "I demand empirical testability AND I'm genuinely agnostic about epiphenomenalism—I'll let the evidence decide." The thesis must claim this person is *really* committed to causal efficacy despite their protestations. But how could we ever falsify this? If the researcher insists they're neutral, the thesis replies "you have a hidden commitment you don't acknowledge." If they accept they're committed to causal efficacy, that confirms the thesis. If they abandon empirical testability to remain neutral, that also confirms it (they had to choose). 

The thesis protects itself by making the "commitment" invisible to the agent themselves—it's always a "hidden" or "implicit" commitment that the thesis reveals. Any consciousness scientist who claims to be the counterexample (neutral + empiricist) gets told they're confused about their own position. The thesis becomes immune to testimony: "I occupy the target position and I don't have that commitment" gets answered with "Yes you do, you just don't realize it."

The weak point is the claim that the target position is "actually occupied"—but the thesis can always reinterpret anyone who seems to occupy it as either not really demanding testability (they allow untestable theories sometimes) or not really neutral (they implicitly assume causal efficacy). Every apparent instance gets explained away.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This attack has serious force. The thesis does seem to make the target position disappear on contact—anyone who claims to occupy it gets reinterpreted as confused or self-deceived about their own commitments, making it very difficult to produce a genuine counterexample that the thesis would accept.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The unfalsifiability charge mistakes the nature of philosophical claims about commitment. The thesis doesn't make an empirical prediction about what people will say or believe—it makes a *logical* claim about what commitments are entailed by certain methodological stances. This is falsifiable in the way philosophical arguments are: by showing the logical entailment fails.

Here's how to falsify it: Show that demanding empirical testability for consciousness theories does NOT entail commitment to causal efficacy. Specifically, demonstrate how an epiphenomenal or illusionist theory could make differential empirical predictions despite consciousness being causally inert or nonexistent. If someone could produce this, the thesis would be falsified.

The objector complains that when researchers claim to occupy the target position (neutral + empiricist), the thesis says they're confused. But this isn't unfalsifiable evasion—it's the *point*. The thesis claims the position is *incoherent*, not merely unoccupied. Compare: "You can't consistently believe both that all bachelors are unmarried and that your bachelor friend John is married." If John insists "I hold both views," the response "you're confused" isn't unfalsifiable—it's pointing out a logical inconsistency.

The thesis can be falsified by counterexample at the level of principle, not testimony. Show a way that:
- Consciousness makes zero causal difference to any observable (brain states, reports, behavior), AND
- Two theories about consciousness nonetheless make different empirical predictions

If this is possible, the thesis fails. The objector hasn't shown it's possible—they've only complained that people who claim to do this get told they're not actually doing it. But that's because they're not! When pressed, supposed counterexamples always smuggle in causal efficacy somewhere—in the reports, in the access mechanisms, in the functional organization. The thesis's job is precisely to expose this smuggling.

The falsification condition is clear: produce a worked-out case where consciousness is causally idle yet theories about it are empirically distinguishable. No hand-waving about "letting evidence decide"—show the actual mechanism. Until then, the thesis stands as a claim about what's logically possible, not what people happen to say about their views.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though not completely.

The defence is right that philosophical theses about logical entailment aren't falsified by testimony and that demanding a worked-out counterexample is legitimate. However, it doesn't adequately address the deeper problem: the thesis's scope becomes unclear when the "target position" can always be reinterpreted.

The real issue is this: The thesis claims to target "consciousness scientists and philosophers who demand empirical testability while remaining neutral on whether consciousness is causally efficacious"—but if this position is *incoherent* (as the defence now emphasizes), then it's not actually occupied, and the thesis becomes a claim about an impossible position rather than a revelation about real practitioners' hidden commitments.

This creates a dilemma: Either (1) the target position is genuinely occupied by real researchers, in which case we should be able to identify them and they should recognize themselves, or (2) it's incoherent and therefore empty, in which case the thesis's critical force against actual practice evaporates—it's just pointing out that an impossible combination is impossible.

The defence tries to have it both ways: the position is occupied enough to be worth targeting, but incoherent enough that occupants don't realize their confusion. This makes it very difficult to tell whether any given researcher is a genuine instance of the target or has been mischaracterized. The thesis needs some way to distinguish "researcher who genuinely holds the incoherent position (unknowingly)" from "researcher who the thesis mistakenly thinks holds it"—but the defence provides no such criterion beyond "try to work out the details and you'll see the incoherence." That's not quite unfalsifiable, but it's close enough to make the charge stick.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis is already taken — it's the standard objection to epiphenomenalism that has been central to the debate since the position was first articulated. The claim that epiphenomenalism cannot be empirically tested because causally inert consciousness makes no observable difference is not a novel insight about hidden methodological commitments — it's **the** classical argument against epiphenomenalism, discussed extensively by William James, C.D. Broad, and virtually every subsequent philosopher who has written on the topic.

The weak point is the claim to be revealing something unacknowledged. The thesis presents as exposing a "hidden" or "implicit" commitment in empirical methodology, but this is actually the most well-rehearsed point in the epiphenomenalism literature. James famously argued that epiphenomenal consciousness would be "unverifiable and unfruitful" for precisely this reason. Contemporary philosophers like David Chalmers explicitly discuss how epiphenomenalism faces "the problem of other minds" in an acute form because it severs the normal evidential connections. The testability problem for epiphenomenalism is not a secret commitment that needs exposing — it's Philosophy of Mind 101.

Even the extension to illusionism isn't novel. Illusionists like Frankish and Dennett are fully aware that their view must explain why we have the reports and judgments we do through purely physical/functional means, and critics have long pointed out the resulting constraints. The thesis repackages these familiar dialectical moves as a meta-level discovery about methodology, but the "discovery" is just the explicit content of decades of published debate.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This attack does serious damage. The thesis's claim to reveal hidden commitments is significantly undermined if the supposed revelation is actually standard textbook material that all parties to the debate already acknowledge and discuss openly.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The "already taken" charge misidentifies the thesis's contribution by conflating two different claims: (1) that epiphenomenalism faces testability problems, and (2) that demanding empirical testability as a methodological requirement **itself constitutes a substantive metaphysical commitment** that excludes positions before examining evidence.

Yes, the testability problem for epiphenomenalism is well-known. But the thesis makes a distinct meta-methodological point: that consciousness scientists who present empirical testability as a **neutral procedural requirement** are actually smuggling in substantive metaphysics. This is not the same as the classical objection to epiphenomenalism.

Consider the dialectical difference:

**Classical objection**: "Epiphenomenalism is false/problematic because it makes consciousness empirically inaccessible." This is an argument **against epiphenomenalism** from within a debate where empirical testability is already accepted.

**This thesis**: "If you demand empirical testability, you've already ruled out epiphenomenalism **before** looking at evidence, which means your methodological principle is not metaphysically neutral but is itself a substantive position in the debate." This is an argument **about the status of methodological requirements** in consciousness science.

The thesis targets a specific contemporary phenomenon: the widespread rhetoric in consciousness science that we should "let the empirical evidence decide" between theories, as if the methodology itself takes no stand on metaphysical questions. Researchers regularly present Global Workspace Theory vs. Integrated Information Theory vs. Higher-Order Theory as an empirical contest, claiming methodological neutrality while implicitly excluding entire classes of positions (epiphenomenalism, illusionism) through the very demand for empirical distinguishability.

The objector cites Chalmers discussing how epiphenomenalism faces the problem of other minds. But Chalmers doesn't thereby conclude that **demanding empirical testability is itself a substantive metaphysical commitment**. He treats it as a problem **for** epiphenomenalism, not as evidence that the methodological framework is non-neutral. The thesis makes the stronger claim: the methodology **is** the metaphysics, at least in part.

The novelty lies in the reversal of explanatory priority. The standard view: "We have this neutral method (empiricism), and unfortunately some positions (epiphenomenalism) don't fit it." The thesis: "The method itself encodes substantive commitments, so positions that don't fit it aren't 'unfortunately' excluded—they're excluded by design, because the method was never neutral."

This matters for contemporary practice. When consciousness scientists dismiss certain theories as "not empirically tractable" while maintaining they're being methodologically rigorous rather than metaphysically opinionated, they're enacting exactly the confusion the thesis targets. The thesis reveals that "not empirically tractable" is not a neutral methodological verdict—it's a substantive metaphysical judgment dressed in methodological clothing.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds, but only partially.

The defence correctly identifies a genuine distinction between the classical objection to epiphenomenalism and the meta-methodological claim about what demanding testability commits you to. These are indeed different claims, and the meta-level point has some independent content.

However, the defence overstates the novelty. The recognition that methodological requirements encode substantive commitments is itself a familiar point in philosophy of science, going back at least to logical positivism's critics. The specific application to consciousness science—that empirical methodology isn't metaphysically neutral—has been made by philosophers like Galen Strawson and others who argue that physicalist/functionalist assumptions are built into standard cognitive scientific methodology.

More importantly, the defence doesn't fully rescue the thesis from the "already taken" charge because even the meta-level point has been explicitly made in the literature. When philosophers debate whether consciousness science should be "theory-neutral" or whether certain metaphysical commitments are unavoidable, they're already having the conversation the thesis claims to initiate. The thesis may articulate the point crisply, but it's not revealing something previously hidden.

The defence's strongest move is pointing to actual contemporary practice—researchers who genuinely seem unaware they're making metaphysical commitments through methodological choices. If such researchers exist in significant numbers, the thesis has critical work to do. But the defence doesn't provide concrete examples of this confusion, relying instead on general claims about "widespread rhetoric." Without evidence that the target position is actually occupied by people who don't already know what the thesis claims to reveal, the "already taken" charge substantially holds.

The thesis survives as a useful articulation and reminder, but not as a novel discovery.

</details>

---

### R4. Treating 'theory choice in consciousness science' as analogous to theory choice in physics systematically ignores that consciousness theories also function as conceptual reforms—they're not just explaining consciousness but reconstituting what counts as an explanation.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Methodologists who model consciousness science on natural science theory choice without recognizing the conceptual dimension
- **Sketch:** When physicists debate string theory versus loop quantum gravity, they share standards for what would count as explaining quantum gravity. But IIT, GWT, and HOT don't just differ in their proposed mechanisms—they differ in what makes something an explanation of consciousness at all. IIT treats mathematical structure as explanatory; GWT treats access and broadcast as explanatory; HOT treats representation as explanatory. These are competing normative frameworks for what 'explaining consciousness' means. The epistemological question therefore isn't just evidential—it's partly conceptual and evaluative. Resolving it requires not just looking at data but philosophical argument about explanatory standards themselves. Current methodological discussions miss this by treating all theories as playing the same game.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Genuinely identifies that theories differ in explanatory standards, not just mechanisms—a real meta-theoretical insight that could reframe methodology debates.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defender's distinction between empirically-forced versus constitutive conceptual shifts was itself shown to prove too much—early-stage theories routinely exhibit this structure—and the thesis became merely a claim about field maturity rather than about consciousness explanation itself.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The vegetative state case demonstrated competing empirical predictions, not conceptual reform; the defender's claim that theories individuate the phenomenon differently was shown to make them either empirically equivalent (thus unscientific) or to collapse into standard theory competition.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defender's attempt to mark a principled boundary via 'shared pre-theoretical explanandum' was shown to merely identify pre-paradigmatic science generally, and the claim that consciousness lacks such an explanandum was shown false—phenomenal experience is the shared target.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defender's attempt to connect the two readings via 'identity claims' backfired—it either made the theories definitional stipulations rather than empirical claims, or revealed them as bad scientific theories rather than distinctively conceptual ones.
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Current consciousness science exhibits pre-paradigmatic features—competing individuation schemes for the explanandum and lack of consensus on adequacy standards—that make philosophical argument about explanatory frameworks a necessary complement to empirical work, though this reflects the field's developmental stage rather than something distinctive about consciousness explanation as such.
- **Flaw:** The thesis cannot distinguish consciousness theories from either (1) standard competing scientific theories that happen to use different explanatory primitives, or (2) pre-paradigmatic sciences that haven't achieved consensus on their explanandum—it either trivializes into redescription or reduces to a sociological observation about field maturity.

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would apply to virtually any theoretical dispute in science, making all theory choice partly conceptual rather than empirical. Consider: quantum mechanics versus classical mechanics don't just differ in mechanisms—they differ fundamentally in what counts as explanation (deterministic trajectories versus probabilistic wavefunctions). Relativity versus Newtonian physics differ in whether absolute simultaneity is explanatory. Evolutionary theory versus typological biology differ in whether population-level variation or ideal types are explanatory. Even within physics, interpretations of quantum mechanics (Copenhagen, many-worlds, Bohmian) differ precisely in what makes something an explanation—whether collapse is real, whether observation is special, what counts as a complete description. Yet we don't conclude these disputes are "partly conceptual and evaluative" in a way that exempts them from standard scientific methodology. The weak point is the premise that "differing in what makes something an explanation" indicates conceptual reform rather than standard theoretical disagreement. If accepted, this would make nearly all major scientific revolutions into exercises in conceptual reform, rendering the distinction between empirical and conceptual questions meaningless and making standard scientific methodology inapplicable to most important theoretical disputes.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The thesis needs to explain what makes consciousness theories special beyond features shared by many paradigmatic cases of scientific theory choice.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection treats all shifts in explanatory standards as equivalent, but there's a crucial difference between cases where the shift is *downstream* of empirical discoveries versus cases where it's *upstream* and constitutive.

When quantum mechanics displaced classical mechanics, the shift to probabilistic explanation was *forced by recalcitrant phenomena*—blackbody radiation, photoelectric effect, atomic spectra. Scientists didn't choose to reconceptualize explanation; the world made deterministic trajectories empirically untenable. The conceptual shift was *answerable to* empirical constraint. Same with relativity: Michelson-Morley results and electromagnetism anomalies drove the reconceptualization. The conceptual reform was *responsive to* observational pressure.

Consciousness theories differ structurally. IIT's commitment to integrated information as explanatory, GWT's commitment to access/broadcast, HOT's commitment to higher-order representation—these aren't forced by recalcitrant data in the same way. Rather, they constitute *different ways of carving up the same phenomena*. A reportable experience can be redescribed as: high phi (IIT), globally broadcast (GWT), or higher-order represented (HOT). The theories aren't competing to accommodate anomalies; they're offering competing conceptual frameworks that each accommodate the data differently.

The key is *empirical constraint on the conceptual shift itself*. In paradigmatic scientific revolutions, anomalies force conceptual change. In consciousness science, the conceptual frameworks determine what counts as an anomaly. That's what makes it distinctively conceptual rather than merely theoretical disagreement that happens to involve concepts.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies something real.

The problem is that even this refined version proves too much. Many theoretical disputes in their *early stages* exhibit exactly this structure—competing frameworks that each accommodate existing data differently, without decisive anomalies forcing one framework. Pre-Einsteinian debates about ether had this character. Early evolutionary theory versus natural theology had this character. Competing research programs often begin by offering different conceptualizations of the same phenomena before empirical differentiation becomes possible.

More fatally: the defence concedes that *eventually* empirical constraint could force conceptual change in consciousness science too—it just hasn't yet. But that makes the thesis temporally contingent rather than principled. The claim becomes "consciousness science is currently in a conceptually underdetermined phase" rather than "consciousness theories essentially function as conceptual reforms." That's a much weaker, less interesting thesis about the current state of the field rather than about the nature of consciousness explanation itself.

The objection stands: the thesis hasn't identified what makes consciousness theories *distinctively* conceptual in a way that other major theoretical disputes aren't.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider Global Workspace Theory (GWT) and Integrated Information Theory (IIT) applied to a concrete case: a patient in a vegetative state showing complex thalamo-cortical activity but no reportable awareness.

GWT predicts: no consciousness (no global broadcast to workspace).
IIT predicts: possibly high consciousness (integrated information phi may be very high in thalamo-cortical loops).

The thesis claims these theories "differ in what makes something an explanation of consciousness at all"—that this is a conceptual dispute about explanatory standards, not just empirical disagreement.

But this is straightforwardly empirical. We can ask: Is there something it is like to be this patient? This is a fact about the world, not about our concepts. The theories make different predictions about this fact. One is right, one is wrong (or both are wrong).

The thesis's weak point is treating "theories use different explanatory primitives" as evidence they're engaged in conceptual reform rather than empirical explanation. But competing empirical theories *always* use different explanatory primitives—that's what makes them different theories. Phlogiston theory and oxygen theory used different primitives (phlogiston versus oxygen). That didn't make their dispute "conceptual and evaluative" rather than empirical. The vegetative state patient either is or isn't conscious—a fact that will vindicate one explanatory framework over another, just as combustion facts vindicated oxygen over phlogiston.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously undermines the thesis by showing a concrete case where apparent "conceptual reform" is actually just competing empirical predictions with different theoretical vocabularies.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection assumes we can ask "Is there something it is like to be this patient?" as a theory-neutral empirical question that adjudicates between frameworks. But this is precisely what the thesis denies—and the vegetative state case actually *demonstrates* rather than refutes the conceptual dimension.

The question "Is there consciousness here?" doesn't have a theory-neutral answer because the theories disagree about what consciousness *is*. For IIT, consciousness just *is* integrated information—if phi is high, consciousness is present by definition, whether or not there's report, access, or any functional role. For GWT, consciousness just *is* global availability—if there's no broadcast, there's no consciousness by definition, regardless of integration measures.

This isn't like phlogiston versus oxygen, where both theories agreed on what combustion *is* (that observable process of burning) and disagreed about its mechanism. Here, the theories disagree about the very phenomenon being explained. They're not offering competing mechanisms for the same explanandum—they're offering competing specifications of what the explanandum is.

The objection says "one is right, one is wrong" about whether the patient is conscious. But *right by which standard*? If we check for global broadcast and find none, IIT theorists will say "that's irrelevant—consciousness isn't about broadcast, it's about integration." If we measure high phi, GWT theorists will say "that's irrelevant—consciousness isn't about integration, it's about access." Each theory has internal coherence; neither is making a mistake within its own framework.

The crucial disanalogy: phlogiston theory made predictions (like mass loss during combustion) that turned out empirically false *within its own framework*. But IIT and GWT don't fail within their own frameworks when applied to vegetative states—they simply deliver different verdicts because they're measuring different things, each of which they identify with consciousness.

This is why it's conceptual reform: the theories aren't just explaining consciousness differently; they're *individuating* the phenomenon differently. And you cannot empirically test competing individuation schemes without presupposing one of them.

VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence's last move is fatal to itself: "you cannot empirically test competing individuation schemes without presupposing one of them." If this were true, the theories would be empirically equivalent—they'd be untestable metaphysical positions, not scientific theories. But they're not empirically equivalent.

Here's why: Both theories make predictions beyond their definitional cores. IIT predicts that certain integrated systems (like cerebellum, despite its massive neural activity) have low phi and thus low/no consciousness, while certain simple systems might have surprisingly high phi. GWT predicts consciousness tracks with specific functional capacities—report, memory encoding, rational action guidance. These generate different predictions about animal consciousness, about which brain lesions eliminate consciousness, about whether consciousness could exist in systems without broadcast architecture.

More decisively: the defence's claim that "each theory has internal coherence; neither is making a mistake within its own framework" would equally apply to phlogiston versus oxygen *if we described them as the objection does*. A phlogiston theorist could say: "Phlogiston theory isn't making predictions about mass that turn out false—it's offering a different individuation of what combustion IS. You're measuring mass change; I'm measuring phlogiston release. We're measuring different things." But this would be sophistry. The world constrains which individuation schemes are scientifically fruitful.

The vegetative state case remains a genuine counterexample: it's a case where theories using different "explanatory primitives" are making competing empirical predictions about a matter of fact, not engaging in conceptual reform. The thesis hasn't shown why this is different from standard theory competition.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims consciousness theories "function as conceptual reforms" that "reconstitute what counts as an explanation," but this is merely a redescription of what all competing scientific theories do, dressed up as a distinctive discovery. Every theoretical dispute involves different explanatory frameworks—that's what makes them different theories. Calling this "conceptual reform" versus "theoretical disagreement" is a verbal stipulation, not a substantive insight.

The weak point is the thesis's core distinction between "explaining consciousness" and "reconstituting what counts as an explanation." This distinction collapses on examination. When IIT treats mathematical structure as explanatory, GWT treats access as explanatory, and HOT treats representation as explanatory, they're simply offering different theories—different explanatory models using different theoretical primitives. Labeling this "conceptual reform" adds nothing to the observation that they're different theories with different explanatory resources.

The thesis's claim that "resolving it requires philosophical argument about explanatory standards" is equally empty. All theory choice involves judgments about explanatory adequacy—parsimony, scope, precision, unification. Pointing out that consciousness theories involve such judgments doesn't distinguish them from any other scientific theories. The thesis has simply taken the truism that "different theories explain things differently" and relabeled it "conceptual reform," creating the false impression of a discovery about consciousness science specifically.

What's presented as insight—that consciousness theories differ in explanatory standards—is uncontested: of course different theories have different explanatory approaches. What's presented as distinctive—that this makes consciousness science special—is unsupported verbal stipulation.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The thesis appears to be stipulatively defining "conceptual reform" to mean "theories with different explanatory frameworks," which makes the claim true but trivial, or maintaining a substantive distinction that hasn't been established.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates *degree* with *kind*. Yes, all theories involve explanatory frameworks, but consciousness theories exhibit an extreme version that crosses into conceptual reform, and there's a principled way to mark the boundary.

The key is *what the theory takes as its primary explanandum*. Standard scientific theories take a pre-theoretically identifiable phenomenon and explain it: combustion (that observable burning), planetary motion (those wandering lights), inheritance (offspring resembling parents). Theoretical dispute concerns the *mechanism* behind the shared explanandum.

Consciousness theories differ structurally. There is no pre-theoretically stable explanandum called "consciousness" that all theories are explaining. What IIT calls consciousness (integrated information) and what GWT calls consciousness (globally broadcast information) are *different phenomena*. A system can have high phi with no broadcast, or broadcast with low phi. These aren't two explanations of the same thing—they're explanations of different things that happen to use the same word.

This generates a distinctive methodological situation. In standard theory choice, you can test theories against the shared explanandum: does this theory correctly predict/explain the phenomenon we all agree we're studying? But consciousness theories can't be tested this way because there's no theory-neutral specification of what we're studying. Each theory *stipulates* what consciousness is, then explains that stipulation.

The mark of conceptual reform versus mere theoretical disagreement: **Can the competing theories be tested against a shared, pre-theoretical explanandum?** If yes, it's standard theory choice. If no—if the theories themselves constitute what counts as the phenomenon—it's conceptual reform.

This isn't verbal stipulation. It identifies a real methodological difference: consciousness science lacks the empirical anchor that normally constrains theory choice. That's why philosophical argument about explanatory standards becomes necessary—not as a supplement to empirical testing, but as a *substitute* for the shared explanandum that would normally guide testing.

VERDICT

The defence fails, and the failure reveals the thesis is indeed trivial.

The defence's proposed mark—"Can theories be tested against a shared, pre-theoretical explanandum?"—doesn't identify consciousness as special; it identifies *all immature or pre-paradigmatic sciences*. Before Lavoisier, there was no theory-neutral specification of combustion (was it phlogiston release? oxygen combination? caloric transfer?). Before Darwin, there was no theory-neutral specification of species (were they natural kinds? arbitrary groupings? stages of divine creation?). Before plate tectonics, there was no theory-neutral specification of what mountains *are* in a way that could adjudicate competing theories.

The defence concedes this by saying consciousness science "lacks the empirical anchor that normally constrains theory choice." But this just means consciousness science is currently pre-paradigmatic—it hasn't yet achieved consensus on its explanandum. That's a sociological observation about the field's maturity, not a principled distinction about the nature of consciousness explanation.

More fatally: the defence's claim that "there is no pre-theoretically stable explanandum called consciousness" is straightforwardly false. There absolutely is: **phenomenal experience, what-it's-like-ness, subjective character**. This is why all these theories call themselves theories of *consciousness* rather than theories of integration, broadcast, or representation. They're all trying to explain why some physical systems have subjective experience and others don't. That's the shared explanandum. They propose different mechanisms/bases for it.

The thesis has collapsed into either:
- A trivial redescription: "consciousness theories use different explanatory frameworks" (true of all competing theories)
- A sociological observation: "consciousness science hasn't achieved paradigm consensus yet" (true but uninteresting)
- A false claim: "there's no shared explanandum" (contradicted by the fact that all theories explicitly target phenomenal consciousness)

The objection stands. No substantive, non-trivial distinction has been established.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "what counts as an explanation," which does different work at different points in the argument.

**Reading 1 (makes the thesis plausible):** "What counts as an explanation" means the *explanatory resources* or *theoretical primitives* a theory employs—integration, broadcast, representation, etc. On this reading, the claim that consciousness theories "differ in what counts as an explanation" is obviously true: different theories use different explanatory machinery. This is the reading that makes premises like "IIT treats mathematical structure as explanatory; GWT treats access as explanatory" seem compelling.

**Reading 2 (makes the thesis interesting):** "What counts as an explanation" means the *standards for explanatory adequacy*—the meta-level criteria by which we judge whether any proposed explanation succeeds or fails. On this reading, the thesis claims consciousness science lacks shared methodological standards for theory evaluation, making it distinctively conceptual/philosophical rather than empirical.

**The equivocation:** The argument *needs* Reading 1 to establish its premise (that consciousness theories differ in "what counts as explanation") because on that reading the premise is clearly true. But it *needs* Reading 2 to reach its interesting conclusion (that consciousness science requires philosophical argument about explanatory standards rather than standard empirical methodology) because only on that reading does the conclusion follow about methodology being distinctively philosophical.

The argument cannot be run on either reading alone. On Reading 1, the premise is true but the conclusion doesn't follow—different explanatory resources don't entail different meta-standards for evaluation. On Reading 2, the conclusion would follow but the premise hasn't been established—nothing shows consciousness theories actually disagree about meta-level explanatory standards rather than just employing different theoretical primitives within shared standards.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis must either specify which reading it intends and reconstruct the argument accordingly, or show how the two readings connect in a way that preserves both plausibility and interest.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection presents this as equivocation, but it's actually a *constitutive connection*: in consciousness science specifically, disagreement about explanatory resources (Reading 1) necessarily generates disagreement about meta-level standards (Reading 2) in a way that doesn't occur elsewhere.

Here's why: Explanatory resources in consciousness science aren't just theoretical machinery—they're *identity claims* about what consciousness is. When IIT says integrated information is explanatory of consciousness, it's not proposing a mechanism that causes consciousness; it's claiming consciousness *is* integrated information. When GWT says broadcast is explanatory, it's claiming consciousness *is* global availability. These aren't causal hypotheses; they're constitutive identities.

This generates the connection between readings. If consciousness *is* integration, then adequate explanation of consciousness requires showing integration. If consciousness *is* broadcast, then adequate explanation requires showing broadcast. The choice of explanatory primitive (Reading 1) directly determines the standard for explanatory adequacy (Reading 2). They're not separate questions that happen to be equivocated—they're the same question at different levels of description.

Contrast with standard science: oxygen versus phlogiston disagree about explanatory resources (different substances) but share meta-standards (explain combustion, predict mass changes, account for respiration). The explanatory primitive doesn't determine the adequacy standard. But consciousness theories can't separate these because the explanandum just *is* the explanatory primitive—explaining consciousness by showing integration *is* showing that consciousness is integration.

The objection treats "explanatory resources" and "adequacy standards" as independently variable, but in consciousness science they're locked together by the identity claims. That's not equivocation; it's the distinctive structure of consciousness theorizing where ontological and methodological questions collapse into each other.

VERDICT

The defence fails badly and actually strengthens the objection.

The defence claims consciousness theories make "identity claims" (consciousness *is* integration, consciousness *is* broadcast) rather than causal/mechanistic claims. But this is either false or makes the theories non-empirical stipulations rather than scientific theories.

If IIT genuinely claims consciousness *is* integrated information as a definitional identity, then it's not making an empirical claim testable by evidence—it's stipulating a meaning for "consciousness." But IIT explicitly presents itself as an empirical theory making testable predictions. Tononi doesn't say "I hereby define consciousness as phi"; he claims phi *tracks* consciousness, that systems with high phi *are* conscious as a matter of discoverable fact. That's a substantive claim about the world, not a definition.

The defence's move actually reveals the equivocation more clearly. When the thesis needs plausibility, it describes theories as using "different explanatory resources" (which sounds empirical, testable, scientific). When it needs to connect to meta-level standards, it redescribes the same theories as making "identity claims" and "constitutive identities" (which sounds philosophical, definitional, conceptual). But these can't both be right about the same theories.

More decisively: even if we accept the defence's picture, it doesn't show consciousness science is distinctive. Early theories in many domains made identity claims. Behaviorism claimed mental states *are* dispositions to behavior. Logical positivism claimed meaning *is* verification conditions. Vitalism claimed life *is* élan vital. These identity claims did generate meta-level disputes about adequacy standards. But we don't conclude these disputes were "distinctively conceptual" in a way that exempted them from empirical methodology—we conclude they were *bad scientific theories* that eventually got replaced by better empirical theories.

The objection stands reinforced: the thesis equivocates between empirical disagreement (different explanatory resources) and meta-methodological disagreement (different adequacy standards), and the defence's attempt to connect them via "identity claims" just makes the theories sound unscientific rather than distinctively conceptual.

</details>
