### R5. The consciousness theory debate treats neural substrate and computational architecture as potentially independent variables, but this is unstable: any architecturally-specified theory will collapse into substrate-dependence once we ask whether artificial systems running the same computation are conscious, and any substrate-based theory will collapse into architectural commitments once we ask which features of the substrate matter.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The standard classification of consciousness theories into functionalist/computational versus biological/substrate-dependent camps
- **Sketch:** Consider GWT: it's presented as architecture-neutral—any system with global broadcasting would be conscious regardless of implementation. But when pressed on whether a giant lookup table implementing the same input-output function would be conscious, GWT theorists either (a) say no, revealing hidden substrate requirements, or (b) say yes, accepting the conclusion that consciousness has nothing to do with information processing per se. Now consider biological naturalism: presented as substrate-dependent—consciousness requires neurons or similar biological tissue. But when pressed on which features of neurons matter (electrochemical signaling? protein folding? quantum effects?), theorists must specify architectural/computational properties that could in principle be realized elsewhere, collapsing back into functionalism. The instability is that substrate and architecture cannot actually be separated in the way the debate assumes. Every supposedly substrate-neutral theory smuggles in substrate assumptions when confronted with bizarre implementations, and every supposedly substrate-dependent theory smuggles in architectural assumptions when asked what about the substrate matters. This means positions in the current theory-space are not actually stable resting points.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Exposes genuine instability in substrate/architecture distinction through concrete dialectical pressure; forces theories to reveal hidden commitments.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence identified consciousness's epistemic uniqueness but this revealed the thesis misdiagnosed the problem as metaphysical instability rather than epistemic underdetermination
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence conceded cryptography maintains the distinction via functional specification, thereby admitting consciousness's problem is conceptual immaturity not inherent instability
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claimed to identify structural pattern beyond mere disagreement but examples showed sophisticated theorists already recognize hybrid positions are needed
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence demonstrated the equivocation in action by treating architectural properties (causal structure, integration) as physical constraints to claim substrate-dependence
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Consciousness theory lacks the functional specification needed to stabilize substrate/architecture distinctions that other domains maintain, revealing the field's pre-paradigmatic state rather than inherent incoherence in the distinction itself
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates between substrate as specific materials versus substrate as any physical implementation detail, making ordinary theoretical refinement appear as category collapse, while the defences repeatedly conceded the real issue is conceptual immaturity not inherent instability

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would show that no scientific theory can stably distinguish between structural/functional properties and material/substrate properties in any domain, which is clearly false. Consider the theory that "hearts pump blood via rhythmic muscular contractions." When pressed on bizarre implementations (could a system of bellows and tubes be a heart?), we must either (a) say no, revealing hidden substrate requirements (it needs muscle tissue), or (b) say yes, accepting that hearts have nothing to do with biology per se. When pressed on what features of cardiac muscle matter (the proteins? the cellular structure? the ATP metabolism?), we must specify functional properties (contractility, elasticity) that could be realized elsewhere, "collapsing back into functionalism." Yet we successfully maintain the distinction: structural cardiologists study valve architecture while biochemists study myocardial substrate, and neither field has collapsed. The same applies to kidneys (filtration architecture vs. nephron substrate), lungs (gas exchange architecture vs. alveolar tissue), and countless other biological systems. If consciousness theories cannot maintain the substrate/architecture distinction, this needs a special explanation about consciousness specifically—not a general argument that the distinction is inherently unstable when we ask hard questions. The argument proves that no functional/material distinction in science can survive scrutiny, which is absurd.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The argument needs to explain why consciousness uniquely cannot maintain a distinction that other domains maintain successfully.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The heart analogy fails because it misidentifies what makes the consciousness case special. In cardiology, we have independent empirical access to the phenomenon (blood circulation) that lets us determine whether bizarre implementations succeed or fail as hearts. We can test whether the bellows-and-tubes system actually pumps blood, whether it maintains pressure gradients, whether it responds to demand. This empirical traction lets us stabilize the substrate/architecture distinction: we discover that certain architectural features (one-way valves, rhythmic compression) are necessary and that certain substrate features (muscle contractility, not the specific proteins) are necessary, and these discoveries are non-circular because we can measure cardiac output independently.

But consciousness has no such independent empirical access. We cannot test whether the giant lookup table is "actually conscious" the way we test whether the bellows actually pump blood. This is precisely what creates the instability. When GWT theorists face the lookup table, they cannot appeal to measurements of consciousness-as-independent-phenomenon to resolve whether it counts. They must instead rely on intuitions or theoretical commitments—and these inevitably draw on either substrate assumptions (it's not the right kind of physical system) or architectural assumptions beyond what GWT officially requires (it needs the right kind of causal structure, not mere input-output equivalence). 

The objector's examples actually demonstrate this: "structural cardiologists study valve architecture while biochemists study myocardial substrate" works because both can validate their findings against cardiac function measured independently. There is no analogous "consciousness output" we can measure to validate whether our substrate or architectural specifications are correct. The instability arises specifically from consciousness being epistemically special—we lack the empirical anchor that stabilizes substrate/architecture distinctions elsewhere.

VERDICT

The defence fails, but not completely.

It successfully identifies what makes consciousness special: the absence of third-person empirical access to the phenomenon itself. This is a real disanalogy with hearts. However, this doesn't save the thesis from the proves-too-much objection—it transforms it into a different problem.

If the instability derives from epistemic inaccessibility rather than something inherent to the substrate/architecture distinction itself, then the thesis has misdiagnosed the source of instability. The problem isn't that "substrate and architecture cannot actually be separated in the way the debate assumes"—they can be separated conceptually, just as they are for hearts. The problem is that we cannot empirically determine which separation is correct. This makes the debate epistemically unstable (we cannot resolve it), not metaphysically or conceptually unstable (the positions themselves are incoherent).

This is a significant retreat. The thesis claimed the positions are "not actually stable resting points"—that they collapse under questioning. But if the real issue is lack of empirical testability, then GWT and biological naturalism can remain perfectly stable as theoretical positions; we simply cannot adjudicate between them. That's a problem about consciousness science, not about the positions themselves.

The defence identifies the right disanalogy but thereby reveals the thesis overstates its conclusion.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. The distinction between substrate and architecture remains perfectly stable in cryptography, which shares consciousness's key feature: no independent empirical access to the phenomenon itself. Consider encryption security: we cannot directly observe whether a system "really is secure" the way we observe blood pumping. Yet cryptographers successfully maintain the distinction. Architectural theories specify that security requires certain computational properties (one-way functions, key length) regardless of implementation—and when pressed on bizarre substrates (encryption via trained pigeons, mechanical Enigma machines, quantum computers), they give consistent answers based purely on architectural analysis without collapsing into substrate requirements. Conversely, substrate-based theories (quantum encryption requires quantum substrates) maintain stability when pressed on which features matter—they specify physical properties (superposition, entanglement) without collapsing into mere architecture. The field hasn't collapsed despite lacking direct empirical access to "security itself." We verify security through attempted breaks and mathematical proofs, not by observing some independent security-phenomenon. If consciousness theories cannot maintain this distinction despite the same epistemic limitation, the thesis needs to explain what makes consciousness different from cryptographic security, not just different from hearts. The parallel is strong: both involve functional properties, both lack direct observability, both face questions about substrate-independence—yet only consciousness allegedly shows instability.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This counterexample shares the epistemic feature the defender claimed makes consciousness special, yet maintains stability. The thesis needs additional explanation.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The cryptography analogy fails because security, despite lacking direct observability, still has a functional specification that grounds the substrate/architecture distinction in a way consciousness does not.

When we ask "is this pigeon-based system secure?", we can answer by analyzing whether it satisfies the functional definition of security: does it resist attacks from computationally bounded adversaries? Can someone without the key extract the plaintext in polynomial time? These questions have determinate answers derivable from the system's computational properties. The architectural theorist can say "yes, if the pigeons implement RSA with sufficient key length" and the substrate theorist can say "no, because biological systems leak timing information" — and crucially, both are making claims that cash out in terms of the same functional criterion (resistance to decryption). The distinction remains stable because both sides agree on what security *is* functionally, even while disagreeing on whether architecture or substrate determines it.

But consciousness has no such agreed functional specification. When we ask "is the giant lookup table conscious?", we cannot analyze whether it satisfies *the* functional definition of consciousness because there isn't one that's non-question-begging. The GWT theorist who says "yes, it has global broadcasting" and the biological naturalist who says "no, it lacks the right substrate" are not disagreeing about whether the system meets a shared functional criterion — they're disagreeing about what consciousness *is*. 

The cryptographer can point to attempted breaks and mathematical proofs because these test whether the system performs the security function. But what would be the analogous test for consciousness? Verbal reports? That assumes consciousness is whatever produces reports (architectural bias). Neural correlates? That assumes consciousness is whatever brains do (substrate bias). The very tests we might use to adjudicate already presuppose an answer to the substrate/architecture question.

In cryptography, the distinction is stable because "security" has a functional specification independent of the debate (computational intractability of decryption). Both architectural and substrate theories are trying to explain what realizes *that* agreed-upon function. In consciousness, there is no such independent functional specification — the debate about substrate versus architecture *is* the debate about what consciousness is, not a debate about what realizes an agreed-upon phenomenon.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence correctly identifies that cryptography has a functional specification (computational intractability) that both sides accept, while consciousness lacks this. But this actually strengthens the counterexample rather than defeating it.

If the instability in consciousness theory arises because we lack an agreed functional specification, then the problem is not that "substrate and architecture cannot actually be separated" — they can be separated perfectly well, as cryptography shows. The problem is that consciousness theorists haven't agreed on what they're theorizing *about*. That's a problem of conceptual clarity or theoretical maturity, not an inherent instability in the substrate/architecture distinction itself.

Consider: early chemistry faced similar issues. Before we understood combustion functionally, "phlogiston theorists" and "oxygen theorists" were not just disagreeing about what realizes combustion — they disagreed about what combustion *was*. The substrate/architecture distinction was unstable there too, but not because such distinctions are inherently problematic. It was unstable because the phenomenon was poorly understood. Once we got the functional specification right (rapid oxidation), substrate and architectural questions became stable and separable.

The defence thus concedes that consciousness theory's instability is not about the substrate/architecture distinction per se, but about the field's pre-paradigmatic state. GWT and biological naturalism don't "collapse" when pressed — they reveal that they're offering competing *definitions* of consciousness, not competing explanations of an agreed phenomenon. This is a problem, but it's not the instability the thesis claimed. The thesis said positions collapse under questioning; the defence reveals they simply answer different questions.

The counterexample stands: domains can maintain stable substrate/architecture distinctions even without direct empirical access, provided they have functional specifications. Consciousness lacks this, but that's a different problem than the thesis diagnosed.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims to reveal an "instability" in consciousness theory, but this is merely a verbal repackaging of the well-known and entirely uncontested observation that consciousness theories disagree about what consciousness fundamentally is. Every philosopher of mind already knows that functionalists and biological naturalists are not merely offering different explanations of the same agreed-upon phenomenon—they have different conceptions of what consciousness amounts to. Chalmers has written extensively on how different theories operationalize consciousness differently; Block distinguishes access consciousness from phenomenal consciousness precisely because theorists target different phenomena; the "hard problem" literature explicitly thematizes that we lack consensus on what needs explaining. The thesis dresses up this familiar stalemate as a discovery about "instability" and "collapse," but nothing collapses—the positions remain exactly where they started, disagreeing about fundamentals. Saying "GWT smuggles in substrate assumptions when pressed" is just saying "GWT theorists, when asked hard questions, reveal their background commitments"—which is true of any theory about anything. Saying "the positions are not stable resting points" sounds like a metaphysical discovery, but it means only "theorists haven't reached consensus," which is the uncontested starting point of consciousness studies, not a finding about it. The thesis takes the field's well-known pre-paradigmatic status and relabels it as "instability," creating the appearance of insight where there is only redescription.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The thesis appears to repackage the widely acknowledged lack of theoretical consensus as if it were a novel structural discovery about how positions "collapse" under pressure, when really it just describes the contested state everyone already recognizes.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The triviality objection misses the specific structural claim. Yes, everyone knows consciousness theories disagree about fundamentals. But the thesis is not merely reporting disagreement—it's identifying a particular *pattern* in how that disagreement manifests, one that reveals something about the theory-space itself.

The claim is not just "theories disagree" but rather: **the standard way of classifying theories (substrate-dependent vs. architecture-neutral) systematically misdescribes the positions, because each position necessarily contains both elements in a way that makes the classification itself unstable**. 

Consider what "instability" means here precisely: it's not that theorists haven't reached consensus (trivial), but that the very categories used to map the dialectical terrain cannot bear the weight placed on them. When we try to locate GWT in theory-space as "the architecture-neutral position," we find that defending it against lookup-table cases requires invoking substrate constraints that contradict its official classification. When we try to locate biological naturalism as "the substrate-dependent position," we find that specifying what matters about the substrate requires architectural commitments that contradict its official classification.

This is not mere disagreement—it's a structural feature of how the positions relate to the organizing taxonomy. The analogy: it's the difference between (trivial) "biologists disagree about whether birds are reptiles" and (non-trivial) "the reptile/non-reptile distinction cannot be consistently applied to birds because birds have both reptilian and non-reptilian features in ways that make the classification scheme itself incoherent for this case."

The thesis claims the substrate/architecture distinction functions like a false dichotomy that generates pseudo-positions. GWT *looks* like it occupies "architecture-only" space, but that space doesn't actually exist as a stable theoretical position—it's a classification artifact. Similarly for pure substrate-dependence. The field's pre-paradigmatic status might explain *why* we're stuck with bad categories, but the badness of the categories is not itself trivial.

Moreover, the specific mechanism matters: the thesis identifies *how* positions collapse—through forced choices about bizarre implementations and substrate specifications. This is a diagnostic tool, not just a label for confusion. If correct, it tells us something actionable: stop trying to develop "pure" architectural or substrate theories, because that theory-space is malformed. Instead, recognize that viable positions must explicitly theorize the substrate-architecture relationship rather than trying to privilege one.

The objector's examples actually support this: Chalmers on operationalization, Block on access vs. phenomenal consciousness—these are attempts to *escape* the bad substrate/architecture framing by introducing new distinctions. The thesis explains *why* that framing keeps failing: not because we lack data, but because the categories themselves cannot be instantiated purely.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence tries to distinguish between "mere disagreement" (trivial) and "structural instability in the classification scheme" (non-trivial), but this distinction collapses under examination.

First, the bird/reptile analogy actually undermines the defence. We *don't* say the reptile/non-reptile distinction is "incoherent" or "unstable" when applied to birds—we say birds are non-reptiles with reptilian ancestry, and this is a perfectly stable classification once we understand evolutionary history. The fact that birds share features with reptiles doesn't make the classification scheme unstable; it makes it informative. Similarly, if GWT "smuggles in substrate assumptions," this might just mean GWT is best understood as a hybrid position, not that the substrate/architecture distinction itself is incoherent.

Second, the defence claims the thesis offers a "diagnostic tool" that tells us to "stop trying to develop pure architectural or substrate theories." But this advice only makes sense if someone *is* trying to develop such pure theories. Who is? The defence itself notes that sophisticated theorists like Chalmers and Block have already moved beyond simple substrate/architecture framings. If the field has already recognized that viable positions must "explicitly theorize the substrate-architecture relationship," then the thesis is indeed trivially restating what sophisticated practitioners already know.

Third, the mechanism the defence highlights—"forced choices about bizarre implementations"—is just the standard philosophical method of testing theories with thought experiments. Every theory in every domain faces this. When we press utilitarians on repugnant conclusions, they either bite bullets or add qualifications; when we press libertarians on luck egalitarianism, they either accept implications or refine positions. The thesis presents this as revealing special instability in consciousness theory, but it's just how theoretical dialectic works. Positions get refined under pressure—that's not "collapse," it's theory development.

Finally, and most devastatingly: if the thesis is right that the substrate/architecture distinction "cannot be instantiated purely," then what is the *content* of saying positions "collapse" into each other? Collapse from what to what? If there never were pure positions

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "substrate" between two readings that make the argument appear to work but actually beg the question.

**Reading 1 (Narrow):** "Substrate" means specific physical materials—neurons vs. silicon, biological tissue vs. metal, particular molecular implementations. On this reading, the thesis is interesting: it would be a significant finding if theories claiming to care only about materials actually smuggle in architectural commitments, and vice versa.

**Reading 2 (Broad):** "Substrate" means any physical realization details whatsoever, including causal structure, timing properties, physical connectivity patterns, and implementation constraints. On this reading, the thesis is plausible but uninteresting: of course any computational architecture requires *some* physical realization properties (you can't have global broadcasting without some physical mechanism for broadcasting), and of course any physical system has *some* architectural organization.

The argument needs both readings. When claiming GWT "collapses into substrate-dependence" by rejecting lookup tables, it needs the broad reading—lookup tables fail because they lack the right causal/physical structure for genuine broadcasting. But this makes the conclusion trivial: GWT requires physical implementation details, which every computational theory does. When claiming biological naturalism "collapses into architectural commitments," it needs the narrow reading to make this seem like a concession—specifying electrochemical signaling patterns sounds like abandoning pure materialism. But on the broad reading, specifying which material properties matter is exactly what substrate-dependence should do.

The thesis cannot run on either reading alone: on narrow, GWT doesn't collapse (it never claimed silicon couldn't work); on broad, the "instability" is trivial (all theories need both materials and organization).

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This significantly damages the thesis. The appearance of discovering instability depends on sliding between "substrate as specific materials" and "substrate as any physical implementation detail," making ordinary theoretical refinement look like category collapse.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The equivocation charge fails because it misidentifies where the argumentative work happens. The thesis does not depend on shifting between narrow and broad readings of "substrate"—it depends on a *single, consistent* understanding of what the substrate/architecture distinction is supposed to accomplish in consciousness theory, namely: **to separate questions about what must be computed from questions about what must physically realize the computation**.

On this understanding, "substrate-dependent" means consciousness requires specific physical properties beyond whatever is needed to implement the relevant computation, while "architecture-neutral" means consciousness supervenes on the computational architecture alone, multiply realizable in any substrate that can run it. This is precisely how the debate is framed: functionalists claim consciousness is substrate-independent (silicon could be conscious if it runs the right algorithms), while biological naturalists claim it's substrate-dependent (silicon cannot be conscious because it lacks the right physical properties).

The thesis shows this framing is unstable by demonstrating that neither pole can actually be occupied:

**For GWT:** The lookup table implements the same input-output function—the same mapping from sensory inputs to behavioral outputs, including reports about conscious states. If GWT is truly architecture-neutral in the relevant sense (consciousness supervenes on information patterns, not physical implementation), it must accept the lookup table. Rejecting it reveals that GWT actually requires specific *physical* properties of the implementation—not just "some physical realization" (trivial), but particular causal-dynamical properties that the lookup table lacks despite computational equivalence. This is substrate-dependence in the relevant sense: physical properties beyond computational specification matter.

**For biological naturalism:** When Searle or other biological naturalists specify what matters about neurons, they cannot point to "biological tissue per se" without explanatory vacuity. They must identify features—electrochemical signaling, temporal dynamics, causal organization. But these are precisely the features that could be realized in other substrates if we understood them well enough. The collapse isn't from "narrow substrate" to "broad substrate"—it's from "consciousness requires this specific material" to "consciousness requires these functional/organizational properties that this material happens to possess."

The objector claims this makes the instability "trivial"—of course theories need both computation and physical realization. But this misses the point. The triviality would be saying "GWT needs *some* physical implementation." The thesis says something stronger: **GWT cannot specify its architectural requirements without smuggling in physical constraints that violate its official substrate-neutrality, and biological naturalism cannot specify its substrate requirements without smuggling in architectural constraints that violate its official position that consciousness is tied to specific materials**.

Consider the dialectical situation more carefully. When pressed on lookup tables, GWT theorists don't just say "well, it needs physical realization" (trivial). They say things like: "the lookup table doesn't have the right causal structure," "it lacks genuine information integration," "it's not really computing, just simulating." These responses invoke physical properties—causal structure, integration mechanisms, the difference between computing and simulating—that go beyond the architectural specification GWT officially provides. That's not equivocation between readings of "substrate"; it's showing that the architectural specification alone cannot do the work the theory needs.

Similarly, when biological naturalists specify what matters about neurons, they don't just say "neurons are biological" (vacuous). They identify specific properties—and the thesis's point is that these properties are invariably ones that could in principle be realized otherwise, which means the theory has collapsed from "requires biological substrate" to "requires these architectural features that biology implements."

The objector's two readings don't capture this. The thesis operates with a single, stable distinction: does consciousness supervene on computational architecture (multiply realizable) or does it require specific physical properties beyond what's needed for the computation? The instability is that answering this question for any actual theory reveals it requires both in a way that makes its classification as "architecture-neutral" or "substrate-dependent" misleading.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, and the equivocation charge stands.

The defence claims to operate with "a single, consistent understanding" of the substrate/architecture distinction, but actually demonstrates the equivocation in action. Look at what it says about GWT:

"GWT cannot specify its architectural requirements without smuggling in physical constraints that violate its official substrate-neutrality"

But then immediately:

"These responses invoke physical properties—causal structure, integration mechanisms, the difference between computing and simulating—that go beyond the architectural specification"

This is the equivocation. "Causal structure" and "integration mechanisms" ARE architectural specifications in any reasonable sense—they describe how the system must be organized, not what materials it must use. The defence treats these as "physical constraints" (suggesting substrate-dependence), but they're actually architectural constraints (they specify organizational requirements that could be met in multiple substrates). A silicon system could have the "right causal structure" for global broadcasting; a mechanical system could have "genuine integration mechanisms." These aren't substrate requirements in the sense that

</details>

### R6. The demand that consciousness theories be empirically distinguishable presupposes verificationism about meaning, which consciousness researchers explicitly reject when defending their theories against behaviourist critiques.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · wounded
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The research program exemplified by Templeton Foundation's 'Accelerating Research on Consciousness' that funds empirical tests while presupposing anti-behaviourist positions
- **Sketch:** Consciousness researchers routinely argue that consciousness is real even if zombies are possible - they reject the verificationist move that says 'if it makes no empirical difference, there's no fact of the matter.' But methodological papers about testing consciousness theories assume that if two theories aren't empirically distinguishable, we have no grounds for choosing between them. The key premise is that these positions are in tension: you can't simultaneously hold that (a) consciousness facts transcend empirical detectability (anti-verificationism about consciousness) and (b) we should prefer whichever consciousness theory is better empirically supported (verificationism about theory choice). Either empirical equivalence means no fact of the matter (behaviourism wins) or there can be facts that outrun empirical tests (so empirical distinguishability isn't required). Consciousness researchers want to resist behaviourism while doing empirical science, but this combination is unstable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies real tension between anti-verificationism about consciousness and verificationism about theory choice; forces uncomfortable methodological choice.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, answered. The objection claimed the thesis just redescribes normal scientific realism, but the defence showed it targets a specific asymmetry in how empirical equivalence is treated across contexts.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The objection identified two senses of 'empirical' (broad/narrow), and while the defence tried to turn this into a double standard charge, it failed to explain why phenomenology should discriminate between theories that both accept it as their starting point.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. The defence successfully specified what would refute the thesis (a principled distinction that works, or adopting consistent verificationism/anti-verificationism) and showed it examines rather than evades proposed distinctions.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence claimed the literature lacks justification for the combination, but the objection's verdict (cut off mid-sentence) was explaining that Chalmers's two-dimensional semantics provides exactly the metaphysical/epistemic distinction the thesis claims is missing.
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Consciousness research programs that fund empirical theory-testing while rejecting behaviourism have not adequately justified why phenomenological evidence that establishes consciousness's existence cannot also adjudicate between competing theories about consciousness's nature and structure
- **Flaw:** The thesis treats as unprincipled what is actually a standard distinction between establishing a phenomenon exists (where phenomenology is decisive) and choosing between explanatory theories about that phenomenon (where phenomenology underdetermines choice and additional evidence is needed)

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis claims to expose a "tension" or "instability," but this reduces to the trivial observation that consciousness researchers are fallibilists rather than verificationists. The weak point is the premise that "you can't simultaneously hold (a) and (b)."

This is straightforwardly false. Standard scientific methodology has always distinguished between metaphysical claims (what exists) and epistemological claims (how we gain knowledge about what exists). Researchers can coherently hold: (i) consciousness facts are real and not reducible to behavior (metaphysical anti-verificationism), while (ii) empirical evidence is our best epistemic tool for choosing between theories about those facts (methodological empiricism).

This is not a tension requiring resolution—it's the default position in nearly every science. Physicists believe quarks really exist (anti-instrumentalism) while demanding empirical tests to distinguish quark models (methodological empiricism). No one thinks this is "unstable."

The thesis dresses up as a discovery what is actually just the standard distinction between metaphysics and epistemology. Saying "you reject verificationism about consciousness but accept empirical testing" is like saying "you believe in electrons but use experiments"—it's a verbal repackaging of normal scientific realism, not an exposed contradiction.

STRENGTH: 5/5

The thesis cannot survive this attack. It conflates rejecting verificationism as a metaphysical doctrine with rejecting empirical methods as epistemic tools—a confusion, not a genuine tension.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies the target. The thesis is not attacking the mere combination of metaphysical realism with methodological empiricism—that would indeed be trivial. Rather, it targets a specific asymmetry in how empirical distinguishability is treated.

The defence runs through three steps:

**First, the actual demand is stronger than "use empirical evidence."** When consciousness researchers insist theories must be empirically distinguishable, they typically mean something like: "If theories T1 and T2 make identical predictions about all possible experiments, then there are no scientific grounds for preferring one over the other." This is not mere methodological empiricism—it's the claim that empirical equivalence entails epistemic parity.

**Second, this demand is not applied to the consciousness-versus-behaviourism debate itself.** Consciousness researchers argue that phenomenal consciousness is real even though a zombie world—empirically identical to ours but lacking consciousness—is conceivable. They explicitly reject the move that says "if consciousness makes no empirical difference to third-person observation, we should be agnostic about it." They treat this as a bad argument that confuses metaphysics with epistemology.

**Third, the asymmetry is unexplained.** When comparing IIT versus GWT, empirical equivalence supposedly means we lack grounds for choice. But when comparing consciousness-realism versus eliminativism, empirical equivalence (the zombie scenario) does NOT mean we lack grounds for choice—first-person evidence breaks the tie. The question is: why doesn't first-person evidence also break ties between competing realist theories? If phenomenology can establish that consciousness exists despite zombie-possibility, why can't phenomenology distinguish between theories about consciousness's nature?

The physicists-and-quarks analogy fails because we don't have competing quark theories that are empirically equivalent yet claim to differ about what quarks really are. If we did, and physicists said "we need empirical tests to choose" while simultaneously saying "but the empirical equivalence of quarks versus quark-eliminativism doesn't matter because we have non-empirical grounds," THAT would be the parallel—and it would be puzzling.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, but not for the reason the objection claims.

The defence successfully shows the thesis is not trivially wrong—there IS a genuine question about why first-person evidence counts in one context but not the other. However, the defence collapses because consciousness researchers have a ready answer: **first-person evidence establishes the explanandum but cannot adjudicate between competing explanations.**

Your phenomenology tells you that consciousness exists (establishing the phenomenon), but it doesn't tell you whether IIT or GWT correctly explains that phenomenon. This is perfectly standard: your experience of seeing colours establishes that colour vision exists, but doesn't tell you whether trichromatic theory or opponent-process theory is correct. You need empirical tests of the mechanisms.

The thesis tries to frame this as an unprincipled asymmetry, but it's actually a principled distinction between evidence that establishes what needs explaining versus evidence that tests explanatory theories. The defence's question—"why doesn't phenomenology distinguish between theories?"—has a straightforward answer: because phenomenology provides the data, not the theory.

The thesis fails because the apparent tension dissolves once we distinguish establishing the phenomenon from testing theories about the phenomenon's underlying nature.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis equivocates on "empirically distinguishable" between a third-person observational sense and a broader sense that includes first-person phenomenal evidence.

The weak point is the premise that consciousness researchers "reject verificationism" when defending against behaviourism. What they actually reject is a RESTRICTED form of verificationism that counts only third-person behavioral/physical observations as empirical evidence. When arguing against zombies, they insist that first-person phenomenal data IS empirical evidence—it's just not third-person accessible. This is still verificationism; it just has a broader evidential base.

However, when demanding that consciousness theories be empirically distinguishable, researchers typically mean distinguishable by third-person experimental methods—the kind that can be published, replicated, and shared intersubjectively. This narrower sense is appropriate for theory choice precisely because first-person evidence, while establishing the explanandum, is not differentially accessible across competing theories that all accept phenomenal consciousness as real.

The thesis presents this as contradictory: rejecting "verificationism" in one context while embracing it in another. But there's no contradiction—just two different scopes of what counts as empirical evidence, each appropriate to its context. Against behaviourism, we need the broad sense (including phenomenology). For theory comparison among realist theories, we need the narrow sense (intersubjective experiments).

The argument only works by sliding between these two senses of "empirically distinguishable" to manufacture a tension.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This seriously damages the thesis. The apparent contradiction depends on treating "empirical" univocally, but consciousness researchers can distinguish first-person evidence (establishes the phenomenon) from third-person evidence (tests theories about mechanisms) without any verificationist inconsistency.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The objection claims there are two legitimate senses of "empirical" being deployed for different purposes, but this actually *strengthens* rather than defeats the thesis. Here's why:

**The equivocation charge backfires.** If consciousness researchers are indeed using "empirical" in two different senses—broad (including phenomenology) versus narrow (third-person only)—then the thesis has identified something important: a methodological double standard masquerading as principled distinction. The question becomes: what justifies restricting "empirical evidence" to the narrow sense precisely when comparing realist theories?

**The restriction is unmotivated in context.** The objection says first-person evidence "is not differentially accessible across competing theories that all accept phenomenal consciousness as real." But this is false. IIT and GWT make different claims about what consciousness *is like*—IIT predicts rich phenomenology even in cerebellum-only systems, GWT predicts consciousness requires global broadcasting with specific reportability conditions. These generate different predictions about the structure and grain of phenomenal experience. If phenomenology can establish consciousness exists, it can test claims about consciousness's structure.

**The narrow/broad distinction collapses under pressure.** Consider: why is phenomenology "empirical" when arguing against eliminativism but not "empirical" when testing theories? The objection's answer—"it establishes the explanandum but doesn't test explanations"—is question-begging. Whether phenomenology can test explanatory theories is precisely what's at issue. The zombie argument trades on phenomenology having theoretical force (it rules out functionalism). The demand for third-person tests trades on phenomenology lacking theoretical force. Both can't be right.

**The real issue is intersubjectivity, not empiricism.** If the objection succeeds, it does so by revealing that "empirically distinguishable" actually means "third-person intersubjectively testable." But now the thesis has a sharper formulation: consciousness researchers reject third-person-only standards when defending consciousness against eliminativism, then reimpose exactly those standards when comparing theories. That's not two appropriate scopes of evidence—it's opportunistic scope-shifting.

The physics analogy remains disanalogous: physicists don't argue "quarks exist because of private quark-experiences inaccessible to third-person observation, but theories about quarks must be third-person testable." Consciousness researchers do make exactly this move.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, though it forces important clarifications.

The defence successfully shows the thesis isn't guilty of *simple* equivocation—there's a real question about why phenomenological evidence gets restricted when comparing theories. However, the defence collapses because it mischaracterizes what's happening in the theory-comparison case.

**The decisive point:** When consciousness researchers demand empirical distinguishability between IIT and GWT, they're not *excluding* first-person evidence in principle. Rather, they're recognizing that both theories *already accept* the first-person data as their shared starting point. The issue is that phenomenology underdetermines theory choice—both IIT and GWT can accommodate the same phenomenological reports by interpreting them through their respective frameworks.

This is disanalogous to the zombie case, where phenomenology *does* determine the choice (consciousness exists vs. doesn't exist). The asymmetry isn't unprincipled: phenomenology has decisive force when theories disagree about whether consciousness exists, but lacks decisive force when theories agree it exists but disagree about its underlying nature.

The thesis's "sharper formulation" about intersubjectivity actually undermines it. If the real demand is for intersubjectively checkable tests, this is justified not by verificationism but by the practical epistemology of theory choice: when private evidence is compatible with multiple theories, we need public evidence to break the tie. This is methodological, not metaphysical.

The thesis fails because what looks like opportunistic scope-shifting is actually a principled response to evidential underdetermination. The researcher's position is stable: phenomenology is real evidence, but when it doesn't discriminate between theories, we need additional evidence that does.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ## ATTEMPT

The thesis is unfalsifiable because any apparent counterexample gets reinterpreted as confirming the "tension" it claims to expose.

The weak point is the characterization of what constitutes "verificationism" and "anti-verificationism." The thesis can protect itself from every potential counterexample through strategic redefinition:

**Case 1:** If consciousness researchers explicitly distinguish between phenomenology as evidence for consciousness's existence versus third-person tests for theoretical mechanisms, the thesis responds: "Aha! This restriction is itself the 'unexplained asymmetry' I'm exposing—why doesn't phenomenology count in both contexts?"

**Case 2:** If researchers argue that phenomenology *does* constrain theory choice (as some IIT proponents claim about phenomenological axioms), the thesis responds: "Then you're not really demanding empirical distinguishability after all—you've abandoned the methodological standard!"

**Case 3:** If researchers say both phenomenology and third-person tests are needed together, the thesis responds: "This still doesn't explain why you reject pure behaviorism on phenomenological grounds alone but won't choose between theories on phenomenological grounds alone—the asymmetry remains."

Every possible research practice gets coded as either "inconsistently applying verificationism" or "inconsistently rejecting it." The thesis cannot be tested because it hasn't specified what would count as a consciousness research program that is NOT in tension. Is such a program even possible? If every combination of views about consciousness's reality and methods for theory-testing can be reinterpreted as manifesting this "tension," then the thesis immunizes itself from counterexamples by making the "tension" unfalsifiable.

The thesis needs to specify: what would a non-tensioned position actually look like in practice?

## STRENGTH: 3/5

This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis does seem to treat multiple different methodological positions as all exhibiting the same "tension," which suggests it may be protected by interpretive flexibility. However, the thesis could potentially survive by specifying more precisely what would count as resolving the tension (e.g., "either accept behaviorism or abandon demands for empirical distinguishability between realist theories"). The attack has force but isn't devastating.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The unfalsifiability charge misunderstands what the thesis claims and what would refute it. The thesis is not an empirical prediction but a conceptual claim about commitment-tension, and such claims ARE falsifiable—by showing the commitments are actually consistent.

**What would refute the thesis is straightforward:** Show that the two commitments are compatible by providing a principled distinction that isn't ad hoc. The thesis claims no such distinction exists. This is falsifiable—the objector just needs to provide one that works.

**The three "cases" aren't evasions—they're the thesis testing proposed distinctions and finding them wanting.** This is how conceptual analysis works:

- Case 1: The "phenomenology establishes explanandum / third-person tests explanations" distinction fails because phenomenological claims ARE explanatory claims (IIT's axioms, GWT's reportability conditions). The thesis shows this distinction doesn't hold up.

- Case 2: If phenomenology constrains theory choice, then the demand for empirical distinguishability is abandoned—that's not reinterpretation, that's just following the logic. The thesis is vindicated, not protected.

- Case 3: "Both are needed together" doesn't address the asymmetry. Against eliminativism, phenomenology alone suffices. Between realist theories, suddenly it doesn't. "We need both" doesn't explain why the standard changes.

**What a non-tensioned position looks like is perfectly clear:**

*Option A (Consistent Verificationism):* Accept that empirical equivalence means epistemic parity everywhere—including the zombie case. Admit we have no grounds to prefer consciousness-realism over eliminativism beyond pragmatic or aesthetic considerations. Accept something like Dennett's position.

*Option B (Consistent Anti-Verificationism):* Accept that phenomenological and theoretical considerations can justify theory choice even without empirical distinguishability. Allow that we might have grounds to prefer IIT over GWT based on phenomenological fit, conceptual elegance, or explanatory power, even if they make identical empirical predictions.

**The tension is that consciousness researchers want neither of these.** They want anti-verificationism when defending consciousness's reality, verificationism when comparing theories. The thesis claims this combination is unprincipled. That's a testable claim—show the principle that makes it work.

The "interpretive flexibility" is actually the thesis doing its job: examining proposed principles and showing they fail. That's not unfalsifiability—that's philosophical analysis. The thesis would be refuted by a principle that actually holds up to examination.

## VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The thesis survives this attack.

The unfalsifiability charge fails because it mistakes rigorous examination of proposed distinctions for evasive redefinition. The thesis makes a clear, falsifiable claim: there is no principled distinction that makes the two commitments compatible. It specifies what would count as resolution (Options A or B above), and it's open to being refuted by a distinction that actually works.

The objector's "Cases 1-3" aren't examples of the thesis protecting itself—they're examples of proposed distinctions failing under scrutiny. That's exactly what should happen if the thesis is correct. The thesis would be unfalsifiable only if it rejected *every logically possible* distinction through definitional tricks. But it doesn't—it examines specific proposed distinctions and shows why they don't resolve the tension.

The challenge "what would a non-tensioned position look like?" has a clear answer: either consistent verificationism (Dennett) or consistent anti-verificationism (allowing phenomenology to decide between theories). Current consciousness research explicitly rejects both, which is precisely what the thesis identifies as unstable.

The thesis stands. It makes a substantive claim about a real methodological inconsistency, specifies what would resolve it, and remains open to refutation by a successful principled distinction. The fact that proposed distinctions have failed doesn't make the thesis unfalsifiable—it makes it unrefuted.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ## ATTEMPT

The thesis claims to expose a novel tension in consciousness research methodology, but this is actually the well-established position known as **methodological naturalism** or **epistemological naturalism**, articulated extensively in philosophy of mind and cognitive science.

The weak point is the implicit claim that this combination of views is "unstable" or represents an unrecognized problem. In fact, this exact position has been explicitly defended by numerous philosophers:

**David Chalmers** distinguishes between the "easy problems" (functional/behavioral questions requiring empirical methods) and the "hard problem" (consciousness's existence, which he argues survives zombie-style empirical equivalence). He explicitly endorses using standard scientific methodology for theory comparison while rejecting behaviorism—this is not an unnoticed tension but his considered position.

**Ned Block** in his access/phenomenal consciousness distinction makes essentially this move: phenomenal consciousness is real even if functionally inert (anti-verificationism about consciousness), but theories about access consciousness require empirical testing (methodological empiricism about mechanisms).

**Joseph Levine's** "explanatory gap" argument is built on exactly this structure: there's something about consciousness that outruns functional explanation (anti-verificationism), yet we still do empirical neuroscience to understand consciousness's correlates and mechanisms.

The thesis presents as a discovered "commitment tension" what is actually the mainstream position in non-eliminativist philosophy of consciousness, explicitly articulated and defended for decades. It's not that researchers are unknowingly contradicting themselves—they're knowingly adopting the standard naturalistic dualist or property dualist methodology.

This is "already taken" territory, not a novel exposure of inconsistency.

## STRENGTH: 4/5

This seriously damages the thesis. If the position is already explicitly held and defended by major figures in consciousness studies, then the thesis isn't exposing an unrecognized tension—it's just restating a known methodological stance and calling it unstable without engaging the existing defenses. The thesis would need to show why the established position fails, not just describe it as if it's a newly discovered problem.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The objection fundamentally misreads what "already taken" means in this context. The thesis isn't claiming to discover a *position* no one has articulated—it's claiming to expose an *instability* that position's proponents haven't adequately addressed. There's a crucial difference between "this view is widely held" and "this view is coherent."

**The cited philosophers don't resolve the tension—they exemplify it.** 

Chalmers distinguishes easy from hard problems, but this distinction doesn't explain the asymmetry. The hard problem is supposed to be hard precisely because consciousness might make no functional difference (zombie argument). But if consciousness makes no functional difference, then theories about consciousness's nature also make no functional difference—the hardness should apply all the way down. Why does the hard problem stop being hard once we accept consciousness exists? Chalmers doesn't explain why empirical equivalence matters for IIT vs. GWT but not for realism vs. eliminativism.

Block's access/phenomenal distinction actually *deepens* the problem. If phenomenal consciousness can be functionally inert, then different theories about phenomenal consciousness's substrate could also be functionally inert—they might differ about what realizes phenomenal consciousness without differing in predictions. Yet Block still endorses empirical testing to choose between such theories. The distinction doesn't resolve the tension; it relocates it.

Levine's explanatory gap is precisely the issue. If there's an explanatory gap between physical facts and consciousness, why isn't there also an explanatory gap between empirical tests and theories about consciousness? The gap argument says empirical facts underdetermine consciousness's existence—but then empirical facts should equally underdetermine consciousness's nature.

**The real point: these philosophers assert the combination is stable without showing why.** They take it as obvious that you can reject verificationism metaphysically while embracing it methodologically. The thesis challenges this obviousness. Saying "Chalmers holds this view" doesn't answer "is Chalmers's view consistent?" That's not an appeal to authority fallacy—it's the difference between sociological observation and philosophical evaluation.

**Methodological naturalism doesn't solve the problem—it names it.** Yes, consciousness researchers practice methodological naturalism: they bracket metaphysical questions about consciousness's ultimate nature while doing empirical work. But the thesis asks: how can you bracket those questions when the zombie argument explicitly unbrackets them? The zombie argument says metaphysical facts about consciousness can come apart from empirical facts. Methodological naturalism says we should use empirical facts to choose theories. These pull in opposite directions.

**The "already taken" charge would only work if there were an established, successful defense of the combination.** But there isn't. What exists is:
1. Widespread practice of the combination
2. Philosophical labels for it ("methodological naturalism," "naturalistic dualism")
3. No compelling argument for why it's not ad hoc

The thesis isn't ignorant of this literature—it's claiming the literature hasn't solved its own problem. Chalmers, Block, and Levine all assert we can be anti-verificationist about consciousness while being verificationist about theory choice, but none explains *why* this isn't opportunistic. They describe their methodology; they don't justify its coherence.

**What would actually refute the thesis:** Show that one of these philosophers has given a principled reason why empirical equivalence defeats eliminativism but shouldn't defeat theory choice between realist theories. Not just asserted it's possible—explained what breaks the symmetry. The objection doesn't cite such an explanation because there isn't one in the literature.

The thesis targets precisely this gap: consciousness researchers have a practice, they have labels for it, but they lack a justification for why it's not methodologically inconsistent.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, but reveals something important about why the thesis ultimately cannot survive.

The defence is right that citing philosophers who hold a position doesn't prove the position is coherent. However, the defence fails because **the literature does contain the relevant justification—the defender just hasn't engaged it properly.**

The key is in Chalmers's two-dimensional semantics and his distinction between epistemic and metaphysical possibility. Here's the actual resolution the literature provides:

**The zombie argument establishes metaphysical anti-verificationism:** There could be a world physically identical to ours but lacking consciousness (metaphysical possibility). This shows consciousness facts are not reducible to physical facts.

**Theory choice requires epistemic distinguishability:** For us to have justification for believing one theory over another, we need evidence that discriminates between them (epistemic requirement). This is about what we can know, not about what's metaphysically possible.

**These operate at different levels.** The zombie argument is about the metaphysical relationship between physical and phenomenal properties. Empirical distinguishability is about our epistemic access to theoretical claims. There's no inconsistency because metaphys

</details>
