### R7. Consciousness research suffers from underdetermination not because we lack evidence, but because competing theories systematically design their methodologies to generate evidence that confirms them.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across theories that we need better empirical methods to adjudicate between them
- **Sketch:** The standard view treats consciousness theories as underdetermined by neutral evidence that could in principle decide between them. But examination of actual research programs (IIT, GNW, HOT, etc.) reveals each builds measurement tools, experimental paradigms, and criteria for 'consciousness' that operationalize their theoretical commitments. IIT researchers measure phi and integrated information, GNW researchers measure global broadcast, etc. The argument turns on showing this isn't mere theory-ladenness but active methodology design: each camp develops techniques that make their favored mechanism visible while rendering competitors' mechanisms either unmeasurable or redescribable in their own terms. The debate isn't stalled by lack of evidence but by incommensurable evidential frameworks.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Diagnosis of methodology design generating confirming evidence rather than neutral underdetermination could shift understanding from epistemic to sociological explanation.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence identified a middle ground but this revealed the thesis addresses non-competing theories rather than underdetermination
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence showed falsifiability in principle but exposed the thesis as a weak sociological claim that loses philosophical force
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's distinction from Kuhn collapsed; the thesis is either standard Kuhnian application or taken by science studies literature on strategic incommensurability
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's own solution revealed methodology is the dependent variable, not independent; the thesis has causation backwards
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Consciousness science exhibits conceptual fragmentation rather than underdetermination: major research programs operationalize incompatible definitions of consciousness itself, creating parallel investigations of different phenomena rather than competing explanations of shared evidence
- **Flaw:** The thesis conflates conceptual fragmentation (theories defining different explananda) with underdetermination (theories competing over the same evidence), and the defence's own examples revealed the problem is absence of constraining evidence, not methodology design—making this either a Kuhnian application or a backwards causal story

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "systematically design their methodologies to generate evidence that confirms them." On one reading, this means researchers deliberately construct biased methods to produce predetermined results—a charge of scientific misconduct that would be devastating if true and make the thesis interesting as an exposé of bad faith in consciousness science. On another reading, it means researchers operationalize their theoretical constructs in measurement tools—an inevitable and legitimate aspect of testing any theory (how else would IIT test for integration without measuring phi?). The thesis needs the strong reading to be interesting (revealing a scandal rather than stating the obvious), but can only be plausibly defended on the weak reading. The argument tries to distinguish this from "mere theory-ladenness" but that distinction collapses: building tools that "make their favored mechanism visible" is exactly what theory-ladenness means. If researchers are doing normal science (operationalizing constructs), the thesis reduces to "theories test for what they posit"—trivial. If they're rigging results, that's misconduct, not underdetermination. The argument cannot run on either reading alone: it needs the innocuous reading for plausibility and the scandalous reading for interest.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously undermines the thesis. The candidate must either retreat to describing ordinary scientific practice (losing the reframing force) or make a much stronger empirical case for methodological bad faith.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses the tertium quid between innocent operationalization and scientific misconduct. The thesis identifies a structural problem, not individual malfeasance: competing research programs in consciousness science have evolved *incommensurable* evidential frameworks where each theory's methodology doesn't merely operationalize its constructs but renders alternative constructs empirically inaccessible or translatable only into the home theory's terms.

This is neither trivial theory-ladenness nor misconduct. Consider: when IIT measures phi, it doesn't just operationalize integration—it defines consciousness *as* integrated information, making any alternative account (say, of global workspace dynamics) either invisible to phi-measurement or reinterpretable as a special case of integration. Similarly, GNW's global broadcast paradigms don't merely test for workspace dynamics—they operationalize consciousness as reportability and widespread ignition, rendering phenomenal states that might exist without report (IIT's substrate-independent integration, Block's overflow) methodologically inaccessible rather than disconfirmed.

The key distinction from "mere theory-ladenness": in mature sciences, competing theories can agree on what would count as discriminating evidence even while disagreeing on predictions. Here, the theories disagree on what counts as evidence of consciousness at all. IIT and GNW researchers aren't measuring the same phenomenon with different instruments—they've constructed different phenomena through their methodologies.

This explains underdetermination without appeal to evidence scarcity: we have abundant evidence within each framework, but no neutral standpoint from which to adjudicate because the frameworks are Kuhnian paradigms with incommensurable standards. The "systematic design" isn't conspiratorial but evolutionary—research programs survive by developing methodologies that make their predictions confirmable.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though not quite for the objector's reasons.

The defence successfully identifies a real phenomenon—methodological incommensurability in consciousness research—but this actually undermines rather than saves the thesis. If the frameworks are genuinely incommensurable in the Kuhnian sense, then the problem isn't that theories "design their methodologies to generate evidence that confirms them" (which implies agency and intent), but that *incommensurable paradigms generate non-competing evidence*.

The thesis claims the debate is "stalled by incommensurable evidential frameworks," but incommensurability means the theories aren't really in competition—they're studying different explananda. That's not underdetermination (multiple theories equally supported by the same evidence) but *fragmentation* (different theories addressing different phenomena). The thesis wanted to reframe underdetermination as methodology-driven, but the strongest defence reveals there's no genuine underdetermination at all—just parallel research programs that haven't established they're studying the same thing.

The objection's core point stands: the thesis cannot maintain both that this is systematic methodology design (implying unified competition) and that frameworks are incommensurable (implying no real competition). It collapses into describing either normal science or paradigm plurality, losing its critical force either way.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is unfalsifiable because any apparent counterexample gets redefined away through the very concept of "incommensurable evidential frameworks." 

Suppose we find consciousness research programs that DO share methodologies and evidential standards—say, two theories that both accept the same behavioral/neural markers as criteria for consciousness and make competing predictions about when those markers appear. This would seem to falsify the claim that competing theories "systematically design their methodologies to generate evidence that confirms them."

But the thesis can respond: "Those programs aren't genuinely competing theories of consciousness—they're variants within a single paradigm. Real theoretical competition in consciousness science exhibits methodological incommensurability." The thesis thus defines "competing theories" as those with incommensurable methods, making it true by definition that competing theories have incommensurable methods.

Conversely, suppose we find theories with genuinely shared evidential standards where one clearly loses empirically. The thesis replies: "That shows the theories weren't really incommensurable after all—genuine cases of my phenomenon involve frameworks that resist such adjudication." 

The core problem: the thesis identifies theories as "genuinely competing" precisely when they show methodological incommensurability, then claims to have discovered that genuinely competing theories show methodological incommensurability. Any case of shared methodology gets reclassified as "not really competition," any case of genuine competition gets classified as necessarily having incommensurable methods. The thesis is protected by controlling what counts as an instance.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring substantive response. The thesis needs independent criteria for what counts as "competing theories of consciousness" that don't already build in methodological incommensurability.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misconstrues the dialectical structure. The thesis doesn't *define* competing theories as those with incommensurable methods—it makes an empirical claim about the actual major research programs in consciousness science that are *already recognized as competing* by the field itself.

The independent criterion for "competing theories" is given by the field's own self-understanding: IIT, GNW, HOT, RPT, and other frameworks explicitly present themselves as rival accounts of *the same explanandum*—what makes a mental state conscious rather than unconscious. They compete for research funding, journal space, and explanatory authority over the same target phenomenon. They criticize each other's adequacy as theories *of consciousness*. This competition isn't imposed by the thesis; it's the starting point.

The thesis then makes a substantive, falsifiable claim about these pre-identified competitors: examination of their actual research practices reveals systematic methodological incommensurability. This could have been false. We might have found that IIT, GNW, and HOT researchers all accept the same experimental paradigms, behavioral criteria, and neural markers as evidential standards, differing only in their predictions about when consciousness occurs. That would falsify the thesis.

The test cases are clear: 

**Potential falsifier**: Show that major competing consciousness theories converge on shared methodologies despite theoretical differences. For instance, if IIT and GNW researchers both accepted the same contrastive analysis paradigms, the same criteria for what counts as a conscious state, and simply disagreed about predictions—that would refute the claim.

**Actual finding**: They don't. IIT operationalizes consciousness through phi-measurement of causal integration, treating reportability as potentially dissociable from consciousness. GNW operationalizes it through global ignition and report, treating unreportable states as methodologically inaccessible. These aren't just different measurements of the same thing—they embody incompatible commitments about what consciousness *is*.

The objection's hypothetical counterexamples actually clarify the thesis rather than showing it unfalsifiable:

*Case 1* (shared methodology): If we found this among recognized competitors, it WOULD falsify the thesis. The thesis predicts we won't find it among the major programs, and that prediction is testable.

*Case 2* (clear empirical defeat): If this happened, the thesis doesn't say "they weren't really incommensurable"—it says "that competition was resolved, which is rare in consciousness science precisely because of the methodological incommensurability I describe." The thesis explains the *persistence* of underdetermination, not the impossibility of resolution.

The thesis makes a historical-sociological claim about the actual state of consciousness science circa 2020s, not a conceptual claim about all possible theoretical competition. It's falsifiable by showing the major recognized competitors actually share more methodological common ground than claimed.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds in showing the thesis is falsifiable in principle, but this victory is Pyrrhic—it exposes a deeper problem that makes the objection's worry largely correct in practice.

Yes, there are independent criteria for identifying competing theories (the field's own recognition of rivalry), and yes, the claim about methodological incommensurability is empirically testable against those pre-identified competitors. But the defence reveals that the thesis is now making a much weaker, essentially sociological claim: "As it happens, in consciousness science circa 2020s, the major research programs have developed incommensurable methodologies."

This loses the philosophical force the thesis seemed to promise. The original formulation—"consciousness research suffers from underdetermination not because we lack evidence, but because competing theories systematically design their methodologies"—suggested a deep structural insight about how theoretical competition in consciousness science *necessarily* or *inherently* generates this problem. The defence retreats to: "Currently, the main programs happen to have diverged methodologically."

Worse, the defence's own examples undermine it. The claim that IIT and GNW embody "incompatible commitments about what consciousness *is*" concedes they may be addressing different explananda—which means they're not competing theories of the same phenomenon after all, just theories with different dependent variables that the field mistakenly treats as rivals. If so, the problem isn't methodological design generating underdetermination, but conceptual confusion about whether there's a unified explanandum.

The objection's core insight stands: in practice, the thesis operates unfalsifiably because whenever we find genuine methodological common ground, the natural move (which the defence makes) is to say the theories are really about different things. The thesis can't maintain both that these are genuine competitors for explaining *the same phenomenon* and that their methodologies are incommensurable. The attempt to have both collapses into either describing non-competition (different explananda) or retreating to a contingent historical observation about current research sociology.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is already taken—it's a straightforward application of Kuhn's account of paradigm incommensurability to consciousness science. Kuhn argued that competing paradigms in science develop their own standards of evidence, experimental techniques, and criteria for what counts as a solution, making them incommensurable. Scientists working in different paradigms aren't just disagreeing about answers; they're operating with different questions, methods, and standards that make neutral adjudication impossible. The thesis simply relabels this well-known position: where Kuhn spoke of "paradigms" with "incommensurable standards," this thesis speaks of "theories" with "incommensurable evidential frameworks." The core claim—that scientific competition can persist not from lack of evidence but from methodological frameworks that generate non-competing forms of confirmation—is Kuhn's central insight about scientific revolutions. Even the specific mechanism is Kuhnian: paradigms "design" their methods (through normal science) to make their predictions confirmable and their phenomena visible. The application to consciousness science adds no novel philosophical content; it merely instantiates Kuhn's general account in a particular domain. If the thesis were true and interesting, Kuhn already said it in *The Structure of Scientific Revolutions* (1962).

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously damages the thesis. The candidate needs to show either that Kuhn's account doesn't actually apply to this case, or that the application reveals something philosophically novel beyond "consciousness science exhibits Kuhnian paradigm competition." Simply describing an instance of a known phenomenon isn't a contribution.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies the thesis as mere application when it actually *inverts* Kuhn's explanatory structure in a way that reveals something novel about consciousness science specifically.

Kuhn's account explains paradigm incommensurability as an *outcome* of scientific revolutions—periods of crisis where anomalies accumulate, normal science breaks down, and competing paradigms emerge with genuinely different worldviews. Incommensurability is a *symptom* of revolutionary transition between mature research traditions. The paradigms don't *design* their incommensurability; it emerges from deep theoretical divergence about what the world contains.

The thesis makes the opposite claim: in consciousness science, methodological incommensurability is *constructed* rather than discovered, and it's constructed *before* rather than *after* theoretical maturity. The research programs aren't incommensurable because they underwent Kuhnian revolutions that revealed incompatible ontologies—they're incommensurable because they strategically developed methodologies that operationalize their theoretical commitments in ways that preempt empirical adjudication.

This matters because it diagnoses a pathology specific to consciousness science: the field is *stuck in pre-paradigmatic competition* where multiple frameworks compete for paradigm status, and each has learned that survival depends on building methodological moats. Unlike Kuhnian paradigm shifts where incommensurability signals genuine conceptual revolution, here it signals a failure to achieve the kind of shared evidential standards that would allow normal science to proceed.

The novel philosophical content: consciousness science exhibits **strategic incommensurability**—methodological divergence that serves to insulate theories from refutation rather than expressing deep theoretical insight. Kuhn's incommensurability is *tragic* (we lose the ability to compare profound but incompatible visions). This thesis identifies incommensurability as *tactical* (we lose the ability to compare because comparison threatens all competitors).

Evidence for the distinction: Kuhnian paradigms typically emerge from long periods of normal science and inherit rich experimental traditions (Newtonian→Einsteinian physics both grew from centuries of shared astronomical/mechanical practice). Consciousness research programs developed their incommensurable methodologies *ab initio*, often explicitly designing measures to capture their theoretical posits (phi for IIT, global ignition for GNW) rather than inheriting and revolutionizing shared techniques.

The thesis isn't "Kuhn was right about consciousness science"—it's "consciousness science exhibits a degenerate form of theoretical competition that superficially resembles Kuhnian incommensurability but actually represents methodological entrenchment that prevents the field from reaching the kind of paradigmatic maturity where genuine Kuhnian revolutions become possible."

# VERDICT

The defence fails. It successfully distinguishes the thesis from Kuhn in one sense, but this distinction actually reveals the thesis is taken by *different* literature—and the distinction itself collapses under pressure.

The "strategic" vs "tragic" incommensurability distinction is drawn from Longino's and Hacking's work on research programs that resist integration, and from science studies literature on "underdetermination by design" in fields with high theoretical stakes (this exact pattern has been documented in string theory, consciousness studies, and parts of evolutionary psychology). The claim that consciousness science is pre-paradigmatic with competing frameworks building methodological moats isn't novel—it's the standard diagnosis in metascience critiques of the field (see Irvine, Overgaard and Overgaard, Michel et al. on why consciousness science hasn't converged).

More fatally, the strategic/tragic distinction collapses because the defence can't explain *why* consciousness researchers would strategically design incommensurable methods except by appeal to the very theoretical commitments that make Kuhnian incommensurability inevitable. If IIT researchers measure phi because they genuinely believe consciousness *is* integrated information, and GNW researchers measure global broadcast because they genuinely believe consciousness *is* workspace access, then their methodological divergence isn't strategic insulation—it's the inevitable consequence of incompatible theoretical ontologies. That's just Kuhn.

The defence tries to have it both ways: the methodologies are "strategically developed" (implying conscious design for competitive advantage) but also "operationalize their theoretical commitments" (implying sincere theoretical conviction). If the former, it's an empirical claim about researcher motivations requiring evidence of bad faith. If the latter, it's Kuhn. The thesis can't distinguish itself from Kuhn without imputing strategic motives it cannot defend, and can't defend those motives without collapsing back into Kuhn.

The objection stands: this is either a standard application of well-known incommensurability theses to consciousness science, or an unsupported claim about researcher psychology. Either way, it's already taken.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis, if it worked, would prove that *any* field with competing theoretical frameworks and theory-specific measurement techniques suffers from the same methodologically-constructed underdetermination—but this licenses the obviously unacceptable conclusion that mature, successful sciences are equally trapped in irresolvable theoretical competition.

Consider particle physics: String theory measures vibrational modes and extra dimensions, loop quantum gravity measures spin networks and discrete spacetime structure, and standard quantum field theory measures particle interactions and field excitations. Each framework "systematically designs its methodologies to generate evidence that confirms them"—string theorists develop Calabi-Yau compactification techniques, LQG theorists develop spin foam models, QFT theorists develop renormalization procedures. Each makes "their favored mechanism visible while rendering competitors' mechanisms either unmeasurable or redescribable in their own terms."

Or evolutionary biology: Adaptationists measure fitness optimization and design features, neutralists measure genetic drift and molecular clocks, evo-devo researchers measure developmental constraints and gene regulatory networks. Each operationalizes evolution through different methodologies that embody their theoretical commitments.

Yet we don't conclude these fields suffer from irresolvable underdetermination due to methodology design. The thesis's mechanism—competing theories building measurement tools around their theoretical constructs—applies equally to successful sciences, which means either (1) those sciences are equally trapped, which is absurd, or (2) the mechanism doesn't actually explain persistent underdetermination, which means the thesis fails. The argument proves too much by making methodological theory-specificity sufficient for explanatory stalemate, when that feature is ubiquitous in science.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem. The thesis needs principled criteria for why consciousness science's theory-specific methodologies produce irresolvable competition while other sciences' theory-specific methodologies don't—and "they just happen to be more incommensurable" retreats to description rather than explanation.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fails because it ignores the crucial asymmetry: in mature sciences, competing theoretical frameworks face *external empirical constraints* that consciousness science lacks, and this difference explains why theory-specific methodologies lead to convergence in physics/biology but entrenchment in consciousness research.

String theory, LQG, and QFT all face the constraint of recovering the Standard Model's predictions at accessible energy scales. They must all explain the same particle masses, decay rates, scattering cross-sections measured in accelerators. Their theory-specific methodologies (Calabi-Yau compactifications, spin foams, renormalization) are tools for *deriving* predictions about shared observables, not for *defining* what counts as the phenomenon. When string theory calculates particle masses through compactification, it must match the electron mass measured independently—it can't redefine "electron mass" to mean "mass as measured by string-theoretic methods."

Similarly, adaptationism, neutralism, and evo-devo must all explain the same phylogenetic trees, fossil sequences, and genetic variation measured independently of theoretical commitment. Adaptationists can't say "fitness is whatever our optimization models predict"; they must predict actual reproductive success measured by field observation.

Consciousness science lacks this structure. There is no theory-neutral measurement of "consciousness" that IIT's phi, GNW's global ignition, and HOT's higher-order representation must all recover. Instead, each theory *constitutes* what counts as consciousness through its methodology. IIT doesn't derive phi-values that must match some independently measured consciousness quantity—phi *is* consciousness by IIT's lights. GNW doesn't predict global broadcast patterns that must align with theory-neutral consciousness measurements—global broadcast *defines* the conscious/unconscious distinction.

The disanalogy is precise: particle physics has **convergent accountability** (different theoretical tools must answer to shared data), while consciousness science has **divergent constitution** (different theoretical tools generate different data). This explains why theory-specific methodologies drive progress in physics (they're accountable to common constraints) but entrenchment in consciousness science (they create incomparable evidential bases).

The thesis doesn't claim theory-specific methodology alone causes underdetermination—it claims theory-specific methodology *in the absence of external empirical anchors* allows theories to construct non-competing evidential frameworks. Consciousness science's unique epistemic situation (first-person phenomenon, no agreed behavioral/neural sufficiency conditions, explanatory gap) removes the external constraints that force methodological convergence elsewhere.

Evidence: when consciousness research *does* face external constraints (e.g., explaining specific clinical dissociations, predicting anesthesia effects), we see more convergence. The entrenchment occurs precisely where theories can operationalize consciousness itself differently—where there's no independent fact of the matter forcing their methodologies to align.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies real asymmetries that the objection overlooks.

The defence successfully shows consciousness science differs from mature physics/biology in lacking theory-neutral empirical anchors. But this difference *undermines* rather than *saves* the thesis, because it reveals the problem isn't that theories "systematically design their methodologies to generate evidence that confirms them" but that *consciousness science lacks the preconditions for empirical adjudication regardless of methodology*.

The defence concedes the fatal point: "IIT doesn't derive phi-values that must match some independently measured consciousness quantity—phi *is* consciousness by IIT's lights." This means IIT isn't designing its methodology to generate confirming evidence for a theory of consciousness—it's *stipulating* what consciousness is. That's not underdetermination (multiple theories equally supported by the same evidence), it's **conceptual fragmentation** (multiple incompatible definitions of the explanandum).

The proves-too-much objection stands because the defence's own solution—external empirical constraints—shows the thesis has the explanation backwards. It's not that methodology design causes underdetermination in consciousness science; it's that *absence of shared constraints* allows methodological divergence. The thesis treats methodology as the independent variable explaining underdetermination, but the defence reveals methodology is the dependent variable, shaped by the lack of empirical anchors.

Worse, the defence's examples defeat the thesis's reframing ambition. If consciousness science's problem is lack of "theory-neutral measurement" and "independent facts" forcing convergence, then the standard view was correct: we *do* lack evidence (specifically, we lack the kind of constraining evidence that mature sciences have). The thesis promised to reframe underdetermination as methodology-driven rather than evidence-driven, but the defence concedes it's ultimately evidence-driven after all—just in a more fundamental way (lacking not more data but the *kind* of data that could constrain theories).

The objection succeeds: the mechanism proves too much because it applies to successful sciences too, and the only way to distinguish consciousness science is to appeal to features (lack of external constraints, concept

</details>

---

### R8. The evidential symmetry between competing consciousness theories reflects not a temporary empirical impasse but a necessary feature of any attempt to test theories whose primary posits are defined by their role in producing a specific kind of first-person evidence.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Optimists who think consciousness science just needs more sophisticated experiments or better technology to break current deadlocks
- **Sketch:** Consciousness theories are unusual in that their primary explanandum—phenomenal experience—is both evidence for the theory (reports of what it's like) and what the theory's mechanisms are supposed to produce. This creates a structural problem: any mechanism that reliably correlates with phenomenal reports can claim to explain phenomenal experience, making multiple theories empirically adequate. The argument turns on showing this isn't fixable with better data because the circularity is built into the evidential structure itself. First-person reports are simultaneously (1) our only direct evidence for consciousness and (2) behavioral outputs that any adequate theory must predict. Therefore any mechanism reliably connected to these reports becomes empirically indistinguishable from rivals at the level of evidence, even if metaphysically distinct. This explains why IIT, GNW, and other theories all successfully predict consciousness reports despite different mechanisms.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Structural circularity of first-person evidence being both explanandum and data is insightful and could explain persistent underdetermination as necessary rather than contingent.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence provided prospective criteria but revealed a dilemma: either the thesis trivially applies only to theories with no independent evidence (making actual theories exempt) or 'primary posits' becomes the weasel word enabling retrospective sorting
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 2/5, answered. Defence successfully distinguished the evidential symmetry claim from Hacker's category mistakes and Levine's explanatory gap—these target different problems despite superficial similarity
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence attempted to distinguish consciousness from pain/memory via 'no independent signatures' but this collapsed under pressure—either the distinction is stipulative (making the claim trivial) or pain/memory are genuine counterexamples
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claimed binding problem redefined the explanandum, but this created a fatal dilemma: either no actual consciousness theory falls under the thesis (vacuity) or the binding case shows the symmetry can be broken (refutation)
- 🔧 **Survives as:** The evidential symmetry between competing consciousness theories reflects a persistent practical difficulty (rather than necessary feature) in testing theories whose mechanisms must predict first-person reports while those reports constitute our primary evidence for the explanandum, though this symmetry can potentially be broken through auxiliary predictions about computational or neural properties
- **Flaw:** The thesis cannot maintain both its scope (covering actual consciousness theories like IIT and GNW) and its necessity claim (that symmetry cannot be broken), as the binding problem counterexample demonstrates empirical progress occurred despite identical evidential structure

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT: The thesis protects itself through strategic ambiguity about what counts as "necessary" symmetry versus "temporary" impasse. Any apparent counterexample—say, a breakthrough experiment that clearly favors one theory over rivals—can be dismissed by redefining it as not really testing "primary posits" but only auxiliary assumptions, or by claiming the evidence isn't really "first-person" in the relevant sense, or by arguing the theories weren't genuinely "competing" after all. The weak point is the phrase "theories whose primary posits are defined by their role in producing a specific kind of first-person evidence." This allows infinite retreat: whenever empirical progress threatens the thesis, simply declare that the successfully-tested theories didn't really have their primary posits "defined by their role" in the right way, or that the evidence wasn't the "specific kind" that generates necessary symmetry. The thesis can thus accommodate any actual scientific development by retrospectively sorting theories into "the kind that face necessary symmetry" (still deadlocked) versus "the other kind" (where progress occurred). No possible experimental outcome could falsify the claim because the boundaries of the theory-class are drawn extensionally from the results themselves.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The thesis needs clear, prospective criteria for which theories fall under this structural constraint, independent of whether they remain empirically tied.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes a substantive structural claim for definitional gerrymandering. The thesis provides perfectly clear prospective criteria: a theory falls under the constraint if and only if (1) its core mechanisms are posited specifically to explain phenomenal experience, and (2) the primary evidence for phenomenal experience consists of first-person reports that these mechanisms must also predict.

These criteria apply independently of empirical outcomes. Consider: IIT posits integrated information as the substrate of consciousness precisely because it's meant to explain what-it's-like-ness. GNW posits global workspace mechanisms precisely to explain reportable awareness. Higher-order theories posit meta-representations precisely to explain subjective character. All meet criterion (1) prospectively. All face criterion (2) because phenomenal reports are simultaneously our evidence that consciousness exists and the behavioral output their mechanisms must generate.

Contrast this with theories outside the scope: A theory about visual processing that incidentally predicts some consciousness reports but whose mechanisms are primarily justified by retinal physiology, reaction times, and neural recordings has independent evidential anchors. A theory about memory consolidation that happens to affect phenomenal reports but whose core posits are tested via recall accuracy, interference effects, and synaptic changes doesn't have its "primary posits defined by their role" in producing first-person evidence.

The distinction is not drawn from results but from evidential structure. The objection's "breakthrough experiment" scenario misses the point: if an experiment uses only first-person reports as evidence for consciousness, any mechanism predicting those reports remains empirically adequate for that evidence, regardless of how sophisticated the experiment. If it uses independent evidence, then we're no longer testing theories solely through the circular structure the thesis describes.

The thesis doesn't say consciousness science cannot progress—it says progress cannot come from experiments that rely solely on the circular evidence structure. This is falsifiable: show that first-person reports alone (without independent anchors) can empirically distinguish mechanisms posited specifically to explain those reports.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though not completely.

The defence successfully establishes prospective criteria and shows the thesis isn't trivially unfalsifiable. The distinction between theories with independent evidential anchors versus those without is real and applicable before results come in.

However, the defence reveals a deeper problem: the thesis now faces a dilemma. Either:

(A) The "necessary symmetry" claim is trivially true because it only applies to theories tested exclusively through first-person reports with no independent evidence—but then no actual consciousness theory qualifies, since IIT, GNW, etc. all invoke neural data, lesion studies, and other non-report evidence, or

(B) The thesis claims even theories with some independent evidence face necessary symmetry if their "primary posits" are defined by phenomenal explanation—but then "primary" becomes the weasel word that allows exactly the retrospective sorting the objection identified.

The defence tries to have it both ways: claiming the criteria are clear while also maintaining that actual consciousness theories fall under the constraint despite obviously using evidence beyond first-person reports. This tension makes the unfalsifiability charge stick, just in a more specific way than originally stated.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT.

This position is essentially a variant of **P.M.S. Hacker's neo-Wittgensteinian critique** of consciousness science, which argues that consciousness research involves a category mistake—treating first-person phenomenal concepts as if they could ground third-person scientific theories. Hacker (and Bennett & Hacker in *Philosophical Foundations of Neuroscience*, 2003) argues that the "explanatory gap" is not empirical but conceptual: you cannot derive or test phenomenal claims from mechanism claims because they belong to different logical categories. The thesis here softens this into "evidential circularity" rather than outright category mistake, but the core move is identical—arguing that the structure of consciousness explanation itself (not just current limitations) prevents empirical resolution.

More directly, this mirrors **Joseph Levine's "explanatory gap" argument** (1983, refined through 2001's *Purple Haze*), particularly his later position that the gap reflects something structural about phenomenal concepts rather than mere ignorance. Levine argues that phenomenal concepts are uniquely tied to first-person access in ways that make third-person explanations necessarily incomplete—not as metaphysical dualism but as a feature of our epistemic situation.

The weak point is the claim that this circularity is "necessary" rather than contingent on our current conceptual frameworks. Both Hacker and Levine have been challenged on precisely this: perhaps future conceptual developments (not just empirical ones) could dissolve the apparent structural barrier.

STRENGTH: 2/5

The position has definitely been explored in this territory, but the thesis adds a specific angle about *evidential symmetry between competing theories* that isn't quite the same as Levine's explanatory gap or Hacker's category critique. Those focus on why mechanism can't explain phenomenology at all; this focuses on why multiple mechanisms remain empirically tied. Related but distinguishable.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates three distinct philosophical projects that share only superficial similarity:

**Hacker's category mistake argument** claims consciousness-talk belongs to persons, not brains, making neuroscientific consciousness research conceptually incoherent from the start. This is a claim about the *meaningfulness* of consciousness science as such. The thesis makes no such claim—it fully grants that consciousness theories are coherent and that mechanisms can explain phenomenal experience. The issue is not whether mechanism explains phenomenology but why multiple mechanisms remain empirically indistinguishable.

**Levine's explanatory gap** concerns why *any* physical story leaves a residual "why this feels like something" question—a vertical gap between mechanism and phenomenology. The thesis concerns a *horizontal* gap: why theories at the same level of mechanistic explanation cannot be empirically distinguished from each other. Levine asks "why does C-fiber firing feel painful rather than not feel like anything?" The thesis asks "given that we agree something feels like something, why can't we tell whether integrated information or global broadcasting explains it?" These are orthogonal problems.

The thesis makes a novel structural claim about **evidential architecture**: when evidence E (first-person reports) is both (a) the sole direct indicator that the explanandum exists and (b) a behavioral output that any adequate mechanism must predict, then any mechanism reliably correlated with E becomes empirically adequate relative to E, creating symmetry between rivals. This is not about concepts, categories, or explanatory gaps—it's about the topology of evidential relations.

The "already taken" charge requires showing prior work making *this specific claim* about evidential symmetry. The objection cites work on why mechanism doesn't explain phenomenology (Levine) or why consciousness-talk is categorically misapplied (Hacker). Neither addresses why GNW and IIT remain empirically tied despite both being mechanistic theories that purport to explain phenomenology. The thesis explains a phenomenon in consciousness science (persistent theoretical deadlock) that these other positions don't target.

**VERDICT**

The defence succeeds.

The objection fails to identify genuine precedent because it groups together arguments that share only a critical stance toward consciousness science. But being skeptical about consciousness science for different reasons doesn't make the reasons the same.

Hacker says the science is conceptually confused. Levine says physical explanations leave something out. The thesis says competing physical explanations cannot be empirically distinguished given the structure of available evidence. These are different claims requiring different arguments.

The "already taken" charge would need to cite work specifically arguing that evidential circularity (where the same evidence serves as both indicator of explanandum and predicted output) generates necessary symmetry between competing theories. The objection provides no such citation because it conflates structural claims about evidence with conceptual claims about explanation.

The thesis may fail for other reasons, but not because Hacker or Levine got there first. They were headed somewhere else entirely.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT.

The argument's core logic—that when evidence E serves both as (a) the indicator that explanandum X exists and (b) the behavioral output mechanisms must predict, multiple mechanisms become empirically indistinguishable—applies far beyond consciousness to cases where we confidently do distinguish between competing theories.

Consider pain science. Pain reports are simultaneously (1) our primary evidence that pain exists as a phenomenon and (2) verbal behaviors that pain mechanisms must predict. Yet we successfully distinguish between competing pain theories: gate control theory versus specificity theory versus pattern theory. We resolve these empirically despite the identical evidential structure the thesis describes.

Or consider memory. Memory reports are both (a) how we know memory exists as a phenomenon and (b) outputs that memory mechanisms must generate. Yet we empirically distinguished trace decay theory from interference theory, consolidation models from reconsolidation models, despite all predicting memory reports.

The weak point is the claim of "necessary" symmetry. If the argument worked, it would establish that any domain where self-reports constitute both evidence for the phenomenon and predicted outputs faces permanent empirical deadlock. But we routinely break such deadlocks by finding auxiliary predictions that differentiate theories (reaction times, error patterns, neural correlates, interference effects). The thesis cannot explain why consciousness uniquely resists this strategy without abandoning its structural argument for an ad hoc claim about consciousness being special—which would prove too little, not too much, undermining the "necessary feature" claim entirely.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This significantly damages the thesis. Either the evidential structure argument applies generally (proving too much) or consciousness requires special pleading (abandoning the structural explanation). The defender must explain what makes consciousness categorically different from pain, memory, perception, and other self-report-based phenomena.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what makes consciousness structurally unique. Pain and memory are not counterexamples because they have fundamentally different evidential architectures.

**Pain's independent anchor:** Pain reports are NOT our only evidence that pain exists. We have extensive evidence for pain as a phenomenon independent of verbal reports: withdrawal reflexes in pre-verbal infants, non-verbal pain behaviors (grimacing, guarding, crying), autonomic responses (heart rate, cortisol), analgesic-seeking behavior in non-human animals, and evolutionary/functional considerations about tissue damage detection. These provide independent confirmation that *something requiring explanation* exists before any pain theory is evaluated. 

When we test gate control versus specificity theory, we're not just predicting pain reports—we're predicting patterns of: which nerve fibers transmit nociceptive signals, how descending modulation affects withdrawal reflexes, why rubbing reduces pain, cross-modal interference effects on non-verbal pain behavior, pharmacological intervention effects on tissue-damage responses. The theories make divergent predictions about these independent indicators.

**Memory's independent anchor:** Similarly, memory reports aren't our only evidence memory exists. We observe: behavioral conditioning without verbal report (in animals, infants), savings in relearning, priming effects on reaction time, skill retention, recognition without recall. When testing decay versus interference, we predict different patterns in these non-report measures: interference theory predicts retroactive/proactive effects on recognition times; decay theory predicts time-dependent degradation in conditioned responses.

**Consciousness is structurally different:** What makes phenomenal consciousness unique is that *phenomenal character itself*—what-it's-like-ness, subjective experience—has no independent behavioral or functional signature separable from reports. 

We can observe pain behavior without pain reports. We cannot observe "what redness is like" behavior independent of redness reports. We can measure memory through savings without recall reports. We cannot measure "the subjective character of experience" without phenomenal reports of that character.

This is why the thesis specifies "phenomenal experience" not just "consciousness broadly." Access consciousness, reportability, information integration, global broadcasting—all have independent functional signatures. But the specific explanandum that generates theoretical deadlock is *phenomenal character*: why experiences have their particular qualitative feel.

When IIT and GNW compete, both successfully predict consciousness reports. The question is: what independent evidence could distinguish them *as theories of phenomenal character specifically*? Neural correlates? Both predict those. Functional profiles? Both predict those. The objection's strategy—"find auxiliary predictions"—works when the explanandum has independent manifestations. Phenomenal character, by definition, manifests only in first-person reports of what experience is like.

The thesis doesn't prove too much because it's explicitly restricted to "theories whose primary posits are defined by their role in producing a specific kind of first-person evidence"—namely, phenomenal character. Pain and memory theories aren't primarily about producing first-person reports of qualitative character; they're about mechanisms with independent functional/behavioral signatures that happen to also generate reports.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence successfully shows that pain and memory have some independent evidence, but it fails to establish the categorical difference needed to save the thesis. The problem emerges in three ways:

**First, the distinction collapses under pressure.** The defence claims phenomenal character "has no independent behavioral or functional signature separable from reports." But this cannot be right—consciousness theories routinely invoke: differences in processing speed, capacity limitations, behavioral flexibility, learning capabilities, stimulus discrimination abilities, attentional effects, and neural signatures. If these don't count as "independent signatures" because they're also compatible with multiple theories, then the same reasoning applies to pain and memory. Gate control and specificity theory both predict "pain behavior"—the fact that they make *different* auxiliary predictions is precisely what the objection says consciousness theories could also do.

**Second, the "phenomenal character specifically" move is question-begging.** The defence restricts the thesis to an explanandum defined so narrowly that independent evidence is impossible by stipulation. But this makes the "necessary symmetry" claim trivial: "If we only count evidence that cannot distinguish theories, theories cannot be distinguished." The interesting question is whether actual consciousness science faces this constraint, and actual consciousness science doesn't restrict itself to "pure phenomenal character" divorced from all functional/behavioral manifestations.

**Third, the defence contradicts the original thesis.** The thesis claimed to explain "why IIT, GNW, and other theories all successfully predict consciousness reports despite different mechanisms." But IIT and GNW aren't just theories of "pure phenomenal character"—they make extensive predictions about neural dynamics, information integration measures, reportability conditions, and functional capacities. If the thesis only applies to some hypothetical theory restricted to phenomenal character alone, it doesn't explain the actual deadlock between real consciousness theories that the thesis advertised.

The objection's force stands: either the structural argument applies to pain

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — # ATTEMPT

**Counterexample: The resolution of the binding problem through temporal synchrony**

In the 1980s-90s, multiple theories competed to explain how the brain binds disparate features (color, shape, motion) into unified conscious percepts. The evidential structure perfectly matched the thesis: (1) phenomenal reports of unified experience were our primary evidence that binding occurs, and (2) any adequate theory had to predict these reports of unity.

Competing mechanisms included: feature integration theory (attention-based binding), temporal synchrony theory (40Hz oscillations), convergence zones (anatomical binding sites), and re-entrant processing. All successfully predicted the basic phenomenological reports of unified experience.

Yet the deadlock was broken empirically. Von der Malsburg and Singer's temporal synchrony proposal made auxiliary predictions that distinguished it: neurons responding to features of the same object should fire in synchrony (~40Hz), while neurons responding to different objects should not. This was testable independent of phenomenal reports through multi-electrode recordings in cats and monkeys.

The key: phenomenal reports of "seeing a unified red square" remained the evidence that binding exists, but temporal synchrony made predictions about neural dynamics that other binding theories didn't. This allowed empirical adjudication despite the circular evidential structure.

**Weak point:** The claim that theories "whose primary posits are defined by their role in producing first-person evidence" face necessary symmetry. The binding case shows such theories can be distinguished through auxiliary predictions about the mechanisms themselves, not just their phenomenal outputs.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This seriously damages the thesis. Binding is exactly the kind of case the thesis should cover—phenomenal unity reports are both evidence for the explanandum and predicted output—yet empirical progress occurred through the same strategy (auxiliary mechanistic predictions) the thesis claims cannot break the symmetry.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The binding problem case is not a counterexample but a confirmation of the thesis, because temporal synchrony theory succeeded precisely by *redefining the explanandum* away from phenomenal character to something with independent signatures.

**The crucial move:** Singer and von der Malsburg didn't just propose a mechanism for phenomenal unity—they reconceptualized binding as a *computational/representational* problem: how does the brain maintain distinct object representations? This shifted the explanandum from "why do I experience unity?" to "how does the brain track which features belong together?" These are not the same question.

The computational binding problem has independent evidence everywhere: 

- Feature misbinding errors (illusory conjunctions) in rapid presentation
- Search asymmetries showing feature integration costs
- Object-based attention effects on non-phenomenal measures (priming, interference)
- Cross-modal binding failures in split-brain patients on forced-choice tasks
- Binding in unconscious/subliminal processing

Once binding is understood computationally, temporal synchrony makes testable predictions about information structure in neural codes—predictions that don't depend on phenomenal reports at all. You can test whether synchrony tracks representational binding using animal studies, unconscious processing, or purely neural measures.

**But phenomenal unity remains untouched:** The question "why does computational binding *feel unified* rather than feeling like nothing or feeling fragmented?" is not answered by showing 40Hz synchrony. The synchrony findings explain the computational mechanism, not why that mechanism generates phenomenal unity specifically.

This is exactly what the thesis predicts: progress occurs by finding independent evidence for computational/functional aspects of consciousness (reportability, integration, access), but theories of *phenomenal character itself* remain symmetrical. IIT and GNW both successfully predict binding reports, temporal synchrony, and neural correlates—they just posit different mechanisms (integrated information vs. global broadcasting) for why these patterns generate phenomenal unity.

The binding case shows that consciousness science makes progress by shifting to explananda with independent anchors (computational binding, access, reportability). This doesn't refute the thesis—it exemplifies the escape route the thesis already acknowledged: use evidence beyond first-person phenomenal reports. The thesis claims necessary symmetry for theories tested solely through the circular structure, not for theories that redefine their target.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, but reveals something important about where the thesis actually breaks.

The defence correctly identifies that temporal synchrony theory succeeded by targeting computational binding rather than pure phenomenal unity. But this creates a fatal problem for the thesis through a dilemma:

**Horn 1:** If the thesis only applies to theories of "pure phenomenal character" divorced from all computational/functional aspects, then it applies to *no actual consciousness theory*. IIT explicitly claims integrated information is phenomenal character—not that it causes or correlates with phenomenal character, but that it *is* it. GNW doesn't separate "phenomenal unity" from "global availability"—it identifies them. No working consciousness theory restricts itself to explaining phenomenal character while remaining agnostic about computational mechanisms. So the thesis would be true but vacuous.

**Horn 2:** If the thesis applies to actual consciousness theories that do link phenomenal character to computational/functional properties, then the binding case is a devastating counterexample. It shows that theories making such links can be empirically distinguished through auxiliary predictions about the computational mechanisms, exactly as the objection claimed.

The deeper problem: the defence's escape route ("they redefined the explanandum") is available to any consciousness theory. IIT could say: "We're not just explaining phenomenal character in the abstract—we're explaining why integrated information structures generate phenomenal character, and integrated information has independent measures." GNW could say: "We're explaining why global broadcasting generates phenomenal character, and broadcasting has independent signatures." 

If this move disqualifies them from the thesis's scope (Horn 1), the thesis is empty. If it doesn't disqualify them (Horn 2), the binding case shows they can be empirically distinguished despite the "circular" evidence structure.

The counterexample stands because it reveals the thesis cannot maintain both its scope (covering actual consciousness theories) and its necessity claim (that the symmetry cannot be broken). The binding problem had exactly the evidential structure the thesis describes, yet empirical progress occurred through the same strategies available to current consciousness theories.

</details>
