### R11. The 'hard problem' diagnosis commits you to the claim that zombie scenarios are conceptually coherent, but zombie coherence requires that consciousness is not a functional kind—which undermines the theoretical projects of those who invoke the hard problem while pursuing functional theories.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Researchers like Tononi and some GWT proponents who accept hard problem framing while proposing computational/functional theories
- **Sketch:** Many consciousness researchers (including some IIT and GWT theorists) accept Chalmers' hard problem framing while proposing functional/computational theories. But the hard problem's force depends on conceiving consciousness as possibly absent despite functional/physical identity (zombie scenarios). If zombies are coherent, consciousness cannot be constituted by functional organization—it must be something 'extra.' Yet theories like IIT precisely claim consciousness IS a functional property (integrated information). The key premise is that you cannot consistently maintain both that (a) the explanatory gap shows consciousness transcends functional description and (b) consciousness is identical to some functional property. Researchers invoking the hard problem while proposing functional solutions are in an unstable position—they must either reject zombie conceivability or reject that their theory genuinely explains consciousness.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Exposes genuine tension in researchers who accept both hard problem and functional theories—could force clarification of widespread inconsistent commitments.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence showed IIT is organizationally defined but didn't resolve the equivocation between causal-role functionalism (which zombie arguments historically targeted) and broader organizational specification
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. The defence provided a stable criterion (organizational/structural specification) and a decisive test question that falsifies the vagueness charge
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence retreated to 'applied philosophy' but provided no empirical evidence that target researchers actually occupy the incoherent position, making the contribution a reading assignment rather than a diagnostic insight
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence embraced that zombie coherence entails anti-functionalism, which makes the thesis either tautological (if obvious) or incomplete (if it needs to argue against Type-B materialism's distinction between supervenience and a priori entailment)
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Researchers proposing theories that identify consciousness with specific organizational properties (like integrated information) while simultaneously endorsing zombie conceivability face incoherence, as organizational identity would necessitate phenomenal identity—but this claim requires textual evidence that specific theorists make both commitments and philosophical argument against Type-B materialism's supervenience-without-entailment position
- **Flaw:** The thesis either restates the well-known Type-A materialist position without showing target researchers actually occupy incoherent ground (ALREADY TAKEN), or it conflates metaphysical supervenience with a priori entailment in a way that begs the question against Type-B materialism (PROVES TOO MUCH)

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "functional kind" between two readings that make different parts of the argument work.

Reading 1 (Narrow): "Functional kind" means role-defined properties specifiable entirely in terms of causal relations and dispositions, abstracting away from implementation details—the kind of functional property a classic functionalist like Lewis or Armstrong defended. On this reading, zombie coherence does plausibly undermine functionalism, since zombies are stipulated as functional duplicates lacking consciousness.

Reading 2 (Broad): "Functional kind" includes any property defined by mathematical or structural features of a system, including intrinsic organizational properties like integrated information. IIT doesn't claim consciousness is a role-property but rather an intrinsic property of certain physical structures characterized by their information geometry.

The argument needs Reading 1 to make zombie coherence incompatible with consciousness being functional—that's where the tension lives historically. But it needs Reading 2 to catch IIT theorists in the net, since IIT explicitly rejects causal-role functionalism while still being "functional" in a broader sense of depending on organizational structure.

The weak point is the premise that "zombie coherence requires consciousness is not a functional kind." This is true only on Reading 1, but IIT theorists aren't committed to functionalism in that sense.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The argument conflates different varieties of functionalism, and IIT's position may escape through this gap.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes IIT's self-description for a genuine escape from functionalism. Consider what "intrinsic organizational properties" actually means: integrated information (Φ) is still defined entirely by the system's causal-structural organization—how information is partitioned, how mechanisms relate, which cause-effect structures are irreducible. This remains specification in organizational/relational terms, just with different mathematics than classical functionalism.

The key question is: could two systems be identical in their Φ-relevant organization yet differ in consciousness? If yes, then IIT hasn't actually explained consciousness—it's just correlated it with structure, leaving the hard problem intact. If no—if organizational identity guarantees phenomenal identity—then zombies are incoherent after all, because a functional duplicate (in the relevant organizational sense) would be a phenomenal duplicate.

IIT theorists cannot have it both ways. Either:
- Φ-identity necessitates phenomenal identity (making zombies incoherent, undermining the hard problem framing they accept), or
- Φ-identity leaves phenomenal identity open (making IIT merely correlational, not explanatory)

The "intrinsic vs. role" distinction is a red herring. What matters is whether the property is specified organizationally. If consciousness is whatever-has-integrated-information-structure-Φ, then a system with identical Φ-structure must have identical consciousness—otherwise Φ isn't doing the explanatory work. This makes it a functional kind in the relevant sense: defined by organizational features rather than by what-it's-like.

The objector's "Reading 2" doesn't actually avoid functionalism—it just uses different mathematics to describe organizational structure.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though not completely.

The defence successfully shows that IIT's "intrinsic" framing doesn't automatically escape the functionalism category—integrated information is still organizationally defined. But the objection identifies a real equivocation that the defence doesn't fully resolve.

The problem: "zombie coherence requires consciousness is not a functional kind" trades on classical functionalism's commitments. Historically, zombie arguments targeted the claim that consciousness is a causal role that could be multiply realized in different substrates. IIT explicitly rejects this—it claims consciousness requires specific *physical* implementations with the right information geometry, not just any substrate playing the right causal role.

A philosophical zombie (identical in all physical respects including information geometry) would be incoherent on IIT's terms. But a "functional zombie" (same causal role, different implementation) might not be—IIT allows that silicon couldn't support consciousness even if it played the same causal roles.

So the defence's dilemma is too quick. IIT can say: "Organizational identity at the physical level necessitates phenomenal identity (no zombies), but causal-role identity doesn't (multiple realizability fails)." This lets them maintain both hard problem sympathy (consciousness isn't just causal role) and a structural theory (it's information geometry).

The thesis needs refinement to specify which variety of functionalism creates the tension. The equivocation charge stands.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis protects itself by leaving "functional kind" sufficiently vague that any apparent counterexample gets reclassified. 

Consider IIT as a test case. If we point out that IIT theorists accept hard problem framing while proposing organizational theories, the thesis says "gotcha—tension exposed!" But if IIT theorists respond "we reject causal-role functionalism; integrated information is an intrinsic physical property," the thesis can retreat: "Ah, but that's still functional in the relevant sense—it's organizationally defined." And if they further specify "it's substrate-dependent, not multiply realizable," the response becomes "organizational specification at the physical level still counts as functional for our purposes."

The weak point is the premise that "zombie coherence requires consciousness is not a functional kind." What counts as "functional" here? The thesis needs this to be:
- Broad enough to catch IIT, GWT, and other organizational theories (otherwise the targets escape)
- Narrow enough that zombie coherence genuinely rules it out (otherwise there's no tension)

But these requirements pull in opposite directions. Make "functional" broad (any organizational/structural specification), and zombie coherence doesn't clearly rule it out—IIT can say physical organizational identity precludes zombies. Make it narrow (causal-role functionalism), and the targets escape—IIT explicitly isn't that kind of theory.

Every time a theory threatens to be a counterexample, "functional kind" can be redefined to either include or exclude it as needed to preserve the tension claim.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — A genuine problem requiring clarification. The thesis needs to specify what "functional kind" means precisely enough that we could determine whether a given theory qualifies independently of whether that would make the argument work.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses dialectical flexibility with unfalsifiability. The thesis is not protecting itself by shifting definitions—it's identifying a genuine disjunction that creates problems either way.

Here's the stable criterion: a property is functional in the relevant sense if and only if it is specified entirely by organizational/structural features of a system, whether those features are characterized causally, informationally, or mathematically. This is not vague—it's precisely what distinguishes functionalism (broadly construed) from property dualism or primitivism about consciousness.

Now apply this to IIT:

**Test question**: Does IIT specify consciousness entirely in terms of organizational/structural features (information geometry, cause-effect structures, integration)?

**Answer**: Yes. Φ is calculated from the system's causal structure. There is no additional ingredient beyond "having this organizational profile."

**Consequence**: Then IIT is committed to supervenience of consciousness on organizational structure. A perfect organizational duplicate must be a phenomenal duplicate—otherwise the organizational specification hasn't actually captured what consciousness is.

**Implication for zombies**: If organizational duplicates must be phenomenal duplicates, then zombies (understood as duplicates lacking consciousness) are incoherent.

**Implication for hard problem**: If zombies are incoherent, the hard problem loses its force. The explanatory gap was supposed to show that even complete physical/functional knowledge leaves consciousness unexplained. But if organizational facts necessitate phenomenal facts, the gap closes.

The IIT theorist faces a forced choice, not an escape route:

1. **Embrace necessitation**: Organizational identity → phenomenal identity. Then zombies are impossible, the hard problem dissolves, and you shouldn't accept Chalmers' framing.

2. **Deny necessitation**: Organizational identity leaves phenomenal identity open. Then your theory doesn't explain consciousness—it just correlates it with structure, leaving the hard problem exactly where it was.

The criterion is stable: organizational specification = functional kind. The disjunction is genuine: either you've explained consciousness (making hard problem framing inappropriate) or you haven't (making your theory inadequate). This isn't unfalsifiable—it's a fork that impales either way.

The objector mistakes precision for vagueness. "Functional" has one meaning throughout: organizationally specified. What varies is the consequence for different theories, which is exactly what an internal tension argument should show.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds.

The objection claimed "functional kind" shifts to preserve the argument, but the defence provides a stable criterion: organizational/structural specification. This isn't gerrymandered—it's the natural dividing line between functionalist and non-functionalist approaches to consciousness.

The objector's examples actually demonstrate the fork rather than exposing unfalsifiability:

- "IIT rejects causal-role functionalism" → Irrelevant. It still specifies consciousness organizationally (information geometry).
- "It's substrate-dependent" → This doesn't help. The question is whether organizational features at the physical level suffice for consciousness. If yes, zombies are impossible. If no, the theory is incomplete.
- "Physical organizational identity precludes zombies" → Exactly! Then you've rejected zombie coherence and shouldn't accept hard problem framing.

The criterion doesn't shift—the theories impale themselves on it in different ways. IIT can't escape by saying "we're not *that* kind of functionalism" because the thesis isn't about causal-role functionalism specifically. It's about whether consciousness is constituted by organizational features (however characterized) or requires something additional.

The defence's test question is decisive: *Is consciousness specified entirely by organizational/structural features?* If yes, you're functional in the relevant sense. If no, you owe an account of the non-organizational component—and then you're probably not doing the kind of scientific theory the targets claim to be doing.

The unfalsifiability charge fails. The thesis makes a precise, testable claim: theories specifying consciousness organizationally cannot coherently maintain zombie conceivability. This is falsifiable—show a theory that specifies consciousness organizationally while making zombie coherence compatible with its explanatory claims. The objection hasn't done this.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing an internal tension in contemporary consciousness research, but this diagnostic move has been extensively articulated in the literature on functionalism and the hard problem, particularly in the debate between Type-A and Type-B materialists following Chalmers' taxonomy.

Type-A materialists (like Dennett, Frankish) explicitly make this exact argument: if you accept functional/computational theories of consciousness, you must reject zombie conceivability and thus reject the hard problem as misconceived. Type-B materialists (like Chalmers, Block in some moods) accept both zombie conceivability and that consciousness might be realized in functional systems, but deny this creates tension because they reject that functional specification *explains* or *constitutes* consciousness—it merely *realizes* it, with the phenomenal properties being something additional.

The weak point is the claim that this represents a distinctive "commitment" move exposing an unrecognized tension. This is actually the central, well-mapped fault line in philosophy of mind since the 1990s. Chalmers' own work extensively discusses exactly why accepting the hard problem while pursuing functional theories creates this bind—that's precisely why he distinguishes Type-A and Type-B materialism and discusses whether functional theories can be "reductive" or merely "non-reductive."

The position is David Chalmers' own distinction between reductive and non-reductive functionalism in *The Conscious Mind* (1996) and subsequent work on the structure of the consciousness debate. The thesis repackages this existing taxonomy as a newly discovered tension.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This significantly undermines the thesis's novelty. The "unstable position" it diagnoses is the well-known Type-B materialist stance, and the argument that this position is problematic is the standard Type-A materialist objection. The thesis may still have value in *applying* this known tension to specific researchers, but it's not identifying a previously unrecognized commitment structure.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses *philosophical taxonomy* with *empirical exposure*. Yes, Chalmers mapped the logical space and identified Type-A vs Type-B positions. But that philosophical cartography doesn't dissolve the problem—it *confirms* it while showing how many working scientists occupy incoherent territory.

The thesis isn't claiming to discover new philosophy. It's claiming that actual researchers—Tononi, Dehaene, Koch at various points—occupy a position that the existing philosophical analysis shows to be unstable, yet they persist in it anyway. The contribution is *diagnostic application* to scientific practice, not philosophical novelty.

Consider the parallel: everyone knows you can't consistently hold both determinism and libertarian free will. That's Philosophy 101. But if prominent neuroscientists kept asserting both in their papers, pointing this out would still be valuable work—not because the tension is new, but because the practitioners haven't integrated the lesson.

The Type-A/Type-B distinction actually *strengthens* the thesis rather than preempting it. Chalmers himself argues Type-B materialism is coherent: you can accept zombie conceivability while proposing that functional organization *realizes* consciousness without *constituting* it. But look at what IIT claims: integrated information *is* consciousness, not just its realizer. That's a Type-A claim. Yet IIT proponents often express sympathy with hard problem framing, which commits them to Type-B. *That's* the tension.

The thesis isn't "here's a new philosophical problem." It's "here's a well-understood philosophical problem that empirical researchers keep stepping into because they haven't done the philosophical homework." The value is in showing that:

1. The Chalmers taxonomy creates forced choices
2. Actual scientific theories make claims that place them in Type-A territory (consciousness = functional property)
3. The same theorists often accept Type-B commitments (hard problem framing, zombie conceivability)
4. This combination is exactly what the philosophical analysis shows to be incoherent

The "already taken" objection would work if the thesis claimed philosophical originality. But its actual contribution is showing that the philosophical clarity hasn't penetrated scientific practice. Tononi doesn't cite Chalmers' Type-A/Type-B distinction and carefully position IIT accordingly—he proposes a theory that makes Type-A claims while sometimes invoking hard problem language that presupposes Type-B.

The thesis is applied philosophy of science: using established philosophical distinctions to diagnose confusion in empirical research programs. That Chalmers already drew the map doesn't mean researchers have stopped getting lost.

VERDICT

The defence fails.

The objection substantially undermines the thesis, and the defence doesn't rescue it. Here's why:

**The defence's strongest point**—that philosophical clarity hasn't penetrated practice—would be valuable if true, but it requires empirical evidence the thesis doesn't provide. Are Tononi, Dehaene, or Koch actually making both claims simultaneously? The thesis asserts they "accept hard problem framing while proposing functional theories" but doesn't demonstrate this with textual evidence. If they're carefully positioned as Type-A materialists who reject zombie conceivability, the tension evaporates.

**More damaging**: Chalmers doesn't just taxonomize—he explicitly discusses whether Type-B materialism is coherent and how functional theories might be "non-reductive." The thesis claims researchers are in an "unstable position," but Chalmers argues this position *can* be stable if you treat functional properties as realizers rather than constituters. The defence tries to counter this by saying IIT claims identity not realization, but this needs arguing, not asserting.

**The killer point**: if the thesis is "applied philosophy" exposing practitioners' confusion, it needs to:
- Show specific researchers making both commitments explicitly
- Demonstrate they haven't already addressed the tension using available philosophical resources
- Explain why their position is worse than Type-B materialism as Chalmers articulates it

Without this, the thesis reduces to: "Some researchers might not have read Chalmers carefully." That's not a philosophical contribution—it's a reading assignment.

**What the defence gets wrong**: It claims the value is in "diagnostic application," but diagnosis requires showing the patient is actually sick. The objection reveals that the supposed illness is a well-known condition with well-known treatment options. The thesis would need to show either:
- The treatment doesn't work (Type-B materialism is actually incoherent—but that's just arguing against Chalmers)
- Specific researchers reject the treatment while exhibiting symptoms (requires textual evidence)

The defence's analogy to determinism/libertarianism fails because in that case, the incompatibility is uncontroversial. Here, whether functional theories are compatible with hard problem acceptance is precisely what Type-A vs Type-B debate is about, and Chalmers argues they *can

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis argues that accepting hard problem framing while proposing functional theories creates an unstable position because zombie coherence (required by the hard problem) is incompatible with consciousness being a functional kind. But if this argument worked, it would prove that *any* scientific theory of consciousness that specifies organizational/structural properties is incompatible with the hard problem—which would make the hard problem simply equivalent to anti-functionalism, collapsing a supposed explanatory gap into a definitional stipulation.

The weak point is the premise that "zombie coherence requires consciousness is not a functional kind." If this were true, then the hard problem wouldn't be about an *explanatory* gap at all—it would just be the claim "consciousness is not functional, full stop." But Chalmers explicitly presents the hard problem as arising *even for those who don't antecedently reject functionalism*. The zombie argument is supposed to show that there's something left unexplained by functional accounts, not to assume it by definition.

If the thesis worked, it would prove that:
- Anyone accepting hard problem framing must reject ALL organizational theories (not just IIT/GWT but any theory specifying structural features)
- The hard problem is just disguised substance dualism or property dualism
- There's no coherent position between "consciousness is purely functional" and "consciousness is non-physical"

This is too strong. It would make Type-B materialism—a position Chalmers extensively defends as coherent—simply incoherent by definition. Type-B materialists claim consciousness is physically realized but not functionally constituted, accepting both zombie conceivability AND that physical organization matters. The thesis's logic rules this out entirely, which means it's not identifying a *tension* but rather asserting that one entire side of a major philosophical debate is conceptually impossible.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The argument, if successful, would eliminate a large swath of logical space that philosophers have carefully articulated as coherent, making the hard problem trivially equivalent to anti-functionalism rather than an independent diagnostic tool.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection misunderstands the dialectical structure. The thesis doesn't claim the hard problem *is* anti-functionalism—it claims that accepting the hard problem *commits you to* anti-functionalism, and this commitment creates problems for those who don't recognize it.

This is exactly the point. The thesis reveals that the hard problem has stronger implications than its proponents often realize. When Chalmers presents zombie scenarios as showing an explanatory gap, he's not making a neutral observation—he's smuggling in a substantial metaphysical claim: that phenomenal properties are not constituted by organizational properties.

The objection says this "collapses a supposed explanatory gap into a definitional stipulation," but that's precisely the critical move the thesis makes. The hard problem *looks* like it's about explanation, but it actually presupposes a metaphysical conclusion. If zombies are genuinely conceivable, consciousness cannot be organizational. That's not a bug—it's the feature the thesis exposes.

Consider the logical structure:

1. Zombie scenarios stipulate: organizational duplicate + no consciousness
2. If such scenarios are coherent, organizational properties don't necessitate phenomenal properties
3. If organizational properties don't necessitate phenomenal properties, consciousness isn't constituted by organizational properties
4. Therefore: zombie coherence entails consciousness is not organizational/functional

This isn't proving too much—it's proving exactly what needs proving: that the hard problem comes with heavy metaphysical baggage that many researchers haven't unpacked.

The objection claims this makes Type-B materialism "incoherent by definition," but look closer at what Type-B materialism actually requires. Type-B materialists must say:

- Physical organization *realizes* consciousness (so physical duplicates are phenomenal duplicates—no zombies at the physical level)
- But functional organization doesn't *constitute* consciousness (so there's still an explanatory gap)

This requires distinguishing physical from functional properties in a way that does the needed work. But if "functional" means "organizational/structural" (as the thesis claims), and physical properties relevant to consciousness are themselves organizational (neural architecture, information flow, causal structure), then this distinction collapses.

The thesis doesn't eliminate Type-B materialism as logical space—it challenges whether actual theories can occupy that space coherently. IIT can't say "consciousness is integrated information (organizational property) but zombies are conceivable" without contradiction. Either Φ necessitates phenomenology or it doesn't.

The "proves too much" objection actually identifies the thesis's strength: it shows that the hard problem is more radical than it appears. If you accept zombie conceivability, you're committed to consciousness being non-organizational—which means rejecting not just functionalism but any theory that specifies consciousness structurally. Many researchers want to accept hard problem framing while proposing organizational theories because they haven't recognized this implication.

The thesis doesn't make the hard problem "trivially equivalent to anti-functionalism"—it makes it *substantively* equivalent, which is an important discovery about what the hard problem actually commits you to.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails, and the objection significantly damages the thesis.

The defence tries to turn "proves too much" into a virtue: "Yes, the hard problem has radical implications researchers haven't recognized!" But this strategy backfires because it makes the thesis's central claim trivial in a different way.

**The fatal problem**: If zombie coherence straightforwardly entails anti-functionalism (as the defence now explicitly claims in its four-step argument), then there's no interesting "commitment" being exposed. The thesis reduces to: "If you accept anti-functionalism's premises, you're committed to anti-functionalism's conclusion." That's not a diagnostic insight—it's a tautology.

**What the defence concedes**: By embracing that zombie coherence *obviously* entails consciousness isn't organizational, the defence undermines the thesis's claim to reveal an *unrecognized* tension. If the implication is this direct, then either:
- Researchers who accept both are simply confused (not an interesting philosophical claim)
- They have some response the thesis hasn't engaged (making the thesis incomplete)

**The Type-B materialism problem stands**: The defence claims Type-B materialism requires distinguishing physical from functional properties, then asserts this distinction "collapses" because physical properties are organizational. But this is precisely what Type-B materialists deny—they argue that phenomenal properties are fundamental physical properties not reducible to organizational ones. The thesis needs to argue *why* this position fails, not simply assert that "organizational" covers all physical properties.

**The deeper issue**: Chalmers carefully distinguishes between:
- Logical/metaphysical supervenience (no zombie worlds)
- Conceptual/a priori entailment (no explanatory gap)

Type-B materialists accept the first, deny the second. The thesis conflates these by treating "organizational properties necess

</details>

### R12. The relevant comparison class for evaluating consciousness theories is not other consciousness theories but theories in domains that successfully resolved analogous explanatory disputes, and by that standard all current consciousness theories fail a necessary condition: they don't identify causally isolable components.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The methodological assumption that we should evaluate consciousness theories primarily by their explanatory scope and parsimony relative to each other
- **Sketch:** We evaluate consciousness theories by comparing them to each other—IIT vs GNW vs HOT—asking which better explains the data. But we should instead compare them to successful theories in biology and cognitive science that resolved what-is-it disputes (What is a gene? What is memory?). Successful theories identified components that could be causally isolated: you can knock out a gene, selectively disrupt consolidation. Current consciousness theories fail this test: you can't selectively disrupt 'integrated information' or 'global broadcasting' without disrupting other processes. The key distinction is between theories that identify manipulable natural kinds versus those that identify functional descriptions that can't be isolated from their neural substrate. Until consciousness theories clear this bar, theory comparison is premature.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Novel evaluative standard imported from successful sciences; the causal isolation criterion is concrete and potentially devastating.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence distinguished dissociable capacities from redescriptions but failed on historical timing—functional theories preceded isolation proof, and gave no principled reason consciousness can't follow the same path
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Temperature as mean kinetic energy is a statistical property inseparable from substrate, yet theory comparison was productive; defence shifted thesis from 'isolable components' to 'isolable explanandum' and consciousness meets even that weaker standard
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed resolution has independent indicators beyond causal isolation, making the pattern empirical not stipulative, though necessity claim weakened to inductive generalization
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence proposed unified reading but applied inconsistent standards—accepting weak isolation for attention while demanding strong isolation for consciousness theories, revealing the equivocation rather than resolving it
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Current consciousness theories face an evidential disadvantage compared to mature theories in other domains because they cannot yet design experiments that cleanly discriminate between competing theoretical posits, though this reflects their early developmental stage rather than a categorical difference requiring suspension of theory comparison
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates between strong isolation (discrete components) and weak isolation (functional dissociation), applying stricter standards to consciousness than to the historical cases it cites, while the temperature counterexample shows successful theory comparison proceeded without component-level causal isolation

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT: If this argument worked, it would invalidate essentially all functional theories in cognitive science and neuroscience during their development phases. Consider theories of attention, decision-making, or executive control. We cannot "selectively disrupt attention" without disrupting working memory, perception, and motor control—attention is not a causally isolable component but a functional description inseparable from neural substrates. The same applies to "decision-making," "prediction error," or "cognitive control." Yet these frameworks have been scientifically productive and progressive. The thesis would force us to reject Marr's levels of analysis, which explicitly separates computational descriptions from implementational details. It would delegitimize computational neuroscience's practice of identifying functional processes before (or without) finding discrete neural components. The argument proves too much because the standard it sets—causal isolability of components—would have prohibited the very functional theorizing that enabled discoveries in memory, vision, and language. The weak premise is the implicit claim that successful "what-is-it" disputes were resolved by identifying causally isolable components rather than by converging functional evidence, computational models, and eventually implementational details in whatever order historical contingency provided.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs to explain why consciousness is special in requiring causal isolability upfront, when other cognitive functions advanced through functional theorizing without it.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objector conflates two different kinds of functional description, and the thesis survives on the distinction between them.

Attention, working memory, and decision-making ARE causally isolable in the relevant sense—not as anatomically discrete modules, but as dissociable causal capacities. You can selectively impair attention while sparing other functions: unilateral neglect patients fail to attend to contralesional space while their perception, memory, and motor control remain intact. You can pharmacologically enhance working memory (dopamine agonists in prefrontal cortex) with measurable specificity. You can lesion orbitofrontal cortex and produce selective decision-making deficits. These interventions aren't perfectly clean, but they demonstrate that attention and decision-making come apart from other processes in systematic, reproducible ways.

The gene case is even clearer: before molecular biology, geneticists could already isolate genes functionally through breeding experiments—knocking out "the gene for eye color" without eliminating "the gene for wing shape." That's causal isolation at the functional level, and it's what made the question "what is a gene?" answerable.

Consciousness theories fail this test completely. There is no intervention that disrupts "integrated information" while sparing reportability, attention, and memory, because integrated information isn't a dissociable capacity—it's a mathematical property of neural dynamics. There's no lesion that eliminates "global broadcasting" but leaves local processing intact, because global broadcasting just is certain patterns of widespread neural activity. These aren't isolable causal capacities; they're redescriptions of neural activity that can't be teased apart from the very processes (attention, working memory, executive control) that the objector mentions.

The thesis doesn't require anatomical modularity or implementation-free functional descriptions. It requires that the proposed explanandum be dissociable—that you can have or lack it while other functions vary independently. Attention passes this test. "Integrated information" does not.

VERDICT

The defence fails, but not completely.

The distinction between dissociable causal capacities and mere redescriptions has real force, and it does separate some of the objector's examples (attention, working memory) from consciousness theories. We CAN selectively impair attention; we CANNOT selectively impair integrated information.

But the defence fails on two grounds:

First, the objector's historical claim stands: functional theorizing about attention and memory preceded and enabled the discovery of their dissociability. Researchers didn't wait to confirm causal isolation before developing functional theories—they developed functional theories, and causal isolation emerged as those theories matured. The thesis demands consciousness theories meet a standard upfront that other successful theories met only retrospectively.

Second, and fatally: even if integrated information and global broadcasting aren't currently isolable, the thesis gives no principled reason why they couldn't become so. Perhaps future interventions (optogenetic, pharmacological, computational) will selectively modulate integration or broadcasting. The gene analogy actually undermines the thesis here—genes weren't causally isolable in Mendel's time either, yet the functional theory was correct and productive. The thesis conflates "not yet isolated" with "not isolable in principle," and offers no argument for why consciousness must be different.

The defence shows consciousness theories face a real problem, but not that theory comparison is "premature." It's a challenge to meet, not a reason to stop comparing.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT

The thesis claims theories resolving "what-is-it" disputes succeeded by identifying causally isolable components. Consider temperature in thermodynamics (19th century). 

Scientists asked "What is temperature?" and developed kinetic theory: temperature is mean molecular kinetic energy. This was the correct answer and resolved the dispute. But you cannot causally isolate "mean molecular kinetic energy" as a component. There's no intervention that changes the average kinetic energy of molecules in a gas without changing the kinetic energy of individual molecules—because the average just IS a mathematical property of the distribution. You cannot "knock out" mean kinetic energy while leaving individual molecular motions intact, any more than you can knock out integrated information while leaving neural activity intact.

Yet kinetic theory succeeded spectacularly. It made novel predictions (specific heat ratios, diffusion rates), unified disparate phenomena (pressure, temperature, entropy), and enabled technological applications. The theory comparison that led to its acceptance over caloric theory was entirely appropriate and productive, even though temperature wasn't a causally isolable component.

The weak premise is the universal claim that successful "what-is-it" theories identified causally isolable components. Temperature is a counterexample: a successful theoretical identification of a higher-level property with an underlying statistical/mathematical property of lower-level components, without causal isolability.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This seriously damages the thesis. If theory comparison was appropriate for temperature despite non-isolability, the thesis needs to explain why consciousness is relevantly different. The historical standard it proposes appears falsified.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objector has misidentified what was causally isolated in the temperature case, and when that identification is corrected, kinetic theory fits the thesis perfectly.

The relevant causal isolation wasn't of "mean kinetic energy as a mathematical property"—it was of **heat itself as a distinct physical quantity**. Before kinetic theory, scientists could already manipulate heat selectively: add heat to a gas without changing its volume (raising temperature), add heat while allowing expansion (doing work), transfer heat between systems while keeping their masses constant. Calorimetry worked. Heat capacity was measurable. The First Law already distinguished heat from work as separate energy transfers. Heat was causally isolable from other physical quantities in systematic, reproducible ways.

What kinetic theory did was *identify* this already-isolated phenomenon (heat/temperature) with an underlying microstructure (molecular motion). The theory succeeded precisely because there was already a well-defined explanandum with clear causal boundaries. Scientists weren't asking "what is this mathematical property of molecular distributions?"—they were asking "what is this independently manipulable physical quantity we've been measuring for a century?"

This is exactly what's missing in consciousness theory. We don't have an independently isolable phenomenon called "consciousness" that we're trying to identify with neural processes. Instead, consciousness theories are proposing the identification and the individuation simultaneously. IIT doesn't say "here's this independently measurable thing called consciousness, and it turns out to be integrated information"—it says "integrated information IS what consciousness is, and here's how to measure it." That's backwards from the temperature case.

The thesis holds: kinetic theory succeeded because temperature was already causally isolated as a physical quantity before the theoretical identification. Consciousness theories fail because they're trying to do the individuation and identification in a single step, without first establishing what's being explained as a causally isolable target.

Moreover, the objector's own description reveals the difference: kinetic theory "made novel predictions, unified disparate phenomena, enabled technological applications." It could do this because temperature was already well-defined enough to test predictions against. Current consciousness theories can't make comparably testable predictions precisely because the explanandum isn't causally isolated—we can't distinguish "disrupting consciousness" from "disrupting attention/memory/reportability."

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The historical reconstruction is partially correct—heat was indeed manipulable before kinetic theory—but it doesn't save the thesis for two decisive reasons.

First, the defence concedes too much. If the thesis now requires only that "the explanandum be causally isolated" rather than "the theory identify causally isolable components," it has abandoned its original claim. The thesis explicitly stated theories must "identify causally isolable components" and contrasted this with "functional descriptions that can't be isolated." But temperature-as-mean-kinetic-energy is precisely a functional description (a statistical property) that can't be isolated from its substrate (individual molecular motions). The defence has quietly shifted from requiring isolable components in the theory to requiring an isolable explanandum before theorizing—a different and weaker claim.

Second, and fatally: consciousness IS already isolated as an explanandum in exactly the way heat was. We can manipulate consciousness selectively—anesthetics reduce consciousness while sparing reflexes and homeostatic regulation; sleep stages vary in conscious content while basic physiology continues; lesions produce specific alterations in conscious experience (blindsight, anosognosia) while other cognitive functions persist. We have psychophysics, report-based measures, behavioral dissociations. The explanandum has clear causal boundaries in the same practical sense that heat did in 1850.

The defence tries to raise the bar by demanding we "distinguish disrupting consciousness from disrupting attention/memory/reportability," but this same problem plagued early thermodynamics: you couldn't add heat without changing temperature, pressure, or volume—these were causally entangled. Yet theory comparison proceeded productively.

The temperature case stands as a counterexample. The thesis is false.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis appears to make a substantive methodological claim about theory evaluation, but it actually reduces to a trivial stipulation about what counts as "resolving" an explanatory dispute. 

The core move is definitional: the thesis selects historical cases where theories "successfully resolved" disputes, observes they identified causally isolable components, then proposes this as a necessary condition. But this is circular—the thesis has simply defined "successful resolution" to mean "identified isolable components," then discovered (unsurprisingly) that successfully resolved disputes meet this definition.

Consider: if kinetic theory had failed to gain acceptance, the thesis would simply exclude temperature from "successfully resolved disputes." If geneticists had never isolated genes causally, the thesis would pick different examples. The thesis cherry-picks concluded disputes, extracts a common feature, and presents it as a discovery about what made them successful—but it's just stipulating that disputes aren't "resolved" until this feature appears.

The supposedly substantive claim "consciousness theories fail a necessary condition" is therefore trivial: it just means "we shouldn't say consciousness disputes are resolved until we can isolate components," which is merely defining "resolved" in a particular way. The thesis dresses up this verbal stipulation—"let's reserve 'resolved' for theories with isolable components"—as if it were a methodological discovery about what makes theories successful.

The weak premise is the implicit claim that "successful resolution" is an objective historical category rather than a term being stipulatively defined to exclude consciousness theories.

# STRENGTH: 2/5

This attack has some force but ultimately doesn't seriously damage the thesis. While there's a grain of truth—the thesis does select favorable historical cases—it's not merely stipulative. The thesis makes a substantive empirical claim: that disputes we independently recognize as resolved (gene identity, memory consolidation) share the feature of causal isolability, and this isn't arbitrary cherry-picking. We don't think the gene question is resolved *because* genes are isolable; rather, isolability enabled the resolution we recognize on independent grounds (consensus, predictive success, technological application). The thesis identifies a pattern in scientific history, not just a verbal preference.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objector conflates stipulative definition with empirical generalization from independently identifiable cases, and the thesis survives this confusion.

The thesis is not cherry-picking concluded disputes and then discovering they share a feature—it's identifying disputes that the scientific community independently recognizes as resolved (through consensus, predictive success, technological application, textbook codification) and discovering they share a common methodological structure. "What is a gene?" is resolved not because we stipulate it so, but because molecular biology achieved consensus, made novel predictions, and enabled CRISPR. "What is memory consolidation?" is resolved because we have a mechanistic account that unifies behavioral, pharmacological, and molecular evidence. These are objective indicators of resolution, not verbal stipulations.

The pattern the thesis identifies—that these independently-resolved disputes involved isolating causally manipulable components—is an empirical discovery about successful science, not a definition. It's like observing that successful vaccines historically involved exposing the immune system to weakened pathogens, then proposing this as a methodological lesson. You're not stipulating what "successful vaccine" means; you're generalizing from cases everyone already recognizes as successful.

The thesis then makes a substantive prediction: consciousness theories will not achieve the kind of resolution we see in genetics or memory research until they identify causally isolable components. This is falsifiable—if a consciousness theory achieved consensus and technological application without isolable components, the thesis would be wrong. That's not how stipulations work.

Moreover, the objector's own framing reveals the substantive content: they say "if geneticists had never isolated genes causally, the thesis would pick different examples." But this is precisely the point! The thesis claims that IF geneticists hadn't isolated genes, the dispute WOULDN'T have been resolved in the robust way it was. That's a substantive counterfactual claim about what enables scientific resolution, not a verbal trick.

The circularity charge fails because the thesis has independent criteria for "resolved" (scientific consensus, predictive success, practical application) and discovers that causal isolability is common to cases meeting those criteria. It's an inductive generalization, not a stipulation.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds, but only partially, and the thesis survives in weakened form.

The defence is correct that "resolved dispute" isn't purely stipulative—there are independent indicators (consensus, prediction, application) that mark the gene and memory questions as resolved in ways the consciousness question is not. The thesis does identify a real pattern: these resolved cases involved causal isolation. That's an empirical observation, not a definition.

However, the defence overclaims in two ways that limit the thesis's force:

First, the defence weakens the necessity claim. By admitting the thesis makes an "empirical generalization" from historical cases, it concedes these are inductive grounds, not a priori requirements. The thesis can no longer claim causal isolability is a *necessary condition* for resolution—only that it's been present in past resolutions. This leaves open that consciousness might be resolved differently, making "theory comparison is premature" too strong a conclusion. The thesis becomes: "History suggests consciousness theories will likely struggle until they isolate components," which is weaker but defensible.

Second, the defence doesn't address the objector's deeper point about circularity in case selection. The thesis claims to identify cases "the scientific community independently recognizes as resolved," but this recognition itself may be tracking causal isolability. We might recognize gene theory as resolved *because* we can manipulate genes, making the pattern the thesis "discovers" partly constitutive of what we mean by resolution rather than an independent feature of it. The defence needs to show causal isolability is genuinely independent of our resolution-judgments, not partly constitutive of them.

The defence saves the thesis from being *trivially* stipulative, but it remains vulnerable to being *partly* circular. The thesis survives as a substantive but weakened methodological suggestion rather than a necessary condition. Theory comparison may be premature not by logical necessity, but by historical pattern—a real but modest claim.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "causally isolable components" between two incompatible readings, needing both to make its argument work.

**Reading 1 (Strong)**: Components are anatomically or mechanistically discrete entities that can be knocked out while leaving other system functions intact—like deleting a gene, lesioning a brain region, or blocking a molecular pathway. On this reading, the historical claim is plausible: genes, memory consolidation mechanisms, and neurotransmitter systems are isolable in this robust sense.

**Reading 2 (Weak)**: Components are functionally dissociable capacities that can be selectively impaired or enhanced, even if they're not anatomically discrete—like attention, working memory, or heat transfer. On this reading, consciousness IS already isolable (anesthesia, sleep stages, blindsight), so the thesis's critical claim fails.

The argument needs both readings: It needs the strong reading to make consciousness theories look deficient (they don't identify discrete knockout-able mechanisms), but it needs the weak reading to make the historical examples work (attention and decision-making, cited in the defense, aren't anatomically discrete either). The thesis slides between these: using "you can knock out a gene" (strong) to set the standard, then defending with "attention is dissociable" (weak) when pressed.

The weak premise is the unargued assumption that "causally isolable" has a univocal meaning across the historical cases and consciousness theories. It doesn't, and the argument exploits this ambiguity.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis must either adopt the strong reading (making its historical examples fail) or the weak reading (making consciousness already pass the test). It cannot have both, but the argument's force depends on switching between them.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objector has invented a false dilemma by misdescribing what the thesis requires, and the unified reading they miss is precisely what distinguishes successful theories from current consciousness theories.

The thesis requires neither "anatomically discrete modules" (strong reading) nor merely "any functional dissociation whatsoever" (weak reading). It requires something specific: **the ability to selectively manipulate the proposed theoretical entity in ways that test the theory's predictions**. This is a single, consistent standard that applies uniformly across all cases.

Consider how this works for genes: The causal isolation isn't about anatomical discreteness—genes aren't spatially separate little beads. It's about *selective manipulability for theory testing*. You can knock out the gene-for-eye-color and test whether eye color changes while wing shape doesn't, thereby confirming that your theoretical posit (this gene codes for eye color) is correct. The isolation is *functional and theory-relative*: you can manipulate what the theory says is the relevant causal factor while controlling for alternatives.

The same applies to memory consolidation: You can selectively disrupt consolidation (protein synthesis inhibitors during the consolidation window) and test whether this prevents long-term memory while sparing short-term memory and other cognitive functions. This confirms your theoretical claim about what consolidation is and when it operates.

Now apply this same standard to consciousness theories and attention:

**Attention passes**: You can selectively manipulate what attention theories say is attention (spatial cueing, endogenous vs. exogenous orienting, feature-based selection) and test specific predictions (reaction time benefits, neural enhancement in visual cortex, binding effects). The theory says "attention is X mechanism," and you can manipulate X to test whether attention behaves as predicted.

**Consciousness theories fail**: IIT says consciousness is integrated information (Φ). But you cannot selectively manipulate Φ to test IIT's predictions, because every intervention that changes Φ also changes attention, arousal, reportability, and memory in ways that are equally predicted by competing theories. There's no manipulation that isolates *what IIT specifically claims* from what other theories claim. GNW has the same problem: you cannot manipulate "global broadcasting" independently of the workspace mechanisms, attention systems, and report pathways that other theories cite.

This isn't about anatomical discreteness versus functional dissociation—it's about whether you can design experiments that discriminate your theory from competitors by selectively manipulating what your theory uniquely posits. Genes, consolidation mechanisms, and attention allow this. Current consciousness theories don't.

The objector's "weak reading" (anesthesia, sleep, blindsight) doesn't show consciousness is isolable in the relevant sense—it shows consciousness *as a phenomenon* can be manipulated. But that's not what the thesis requires. The thesis requires that *the theoretical posits* (integrated information, global broadcasting, higher-order representation) be manipulable in ways that test those specific theories. We can manipulate consciousness, but we cannot manipulate "integrated information as distinct from attention and memory" or "global broadcasting as distinct from working memory and report."

There is no equivocation. The standard is consistent: successful theories identify entities that can be selectively manipulated to test the theory's specific claims against competitors. One reading, uniformly applied.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though it makes a valiant attempt to find unified ground.

The defence proposes "selective manipulability for theory testing" as the unified reading, but this actually concedes the objector's point while pretending not to. Here's why:

**First problem**: The defence admits that genes "aren't spatially separate little beads" and that the isolation is "functional and theory-relative." But this is exactly the weak reading the objector identified—genes are isolable only in the sense that we can dissociate their effects, not that they're anatomically discrete components. The defence has accepted the weak reading while claiming to transcend the dilemma.

**Second and fatal problem**: The defence's own standard—"manipulate what the theory uniquely posits to discriminate it from competitors"—actually shows the equivocation rather than resolving it. 

When applied to genes, this standard works with robust causal isolation: knock out a gene, observe phenotype change, done. The manipulation is clean enough that theory discrimination is straightforward.

When applied to attention, the standard requires much weaker isolation: you manipulate spatial cueing and look for reaction time benefits, but these manipulations also affect perception, decision-making, and motor preparation. The theory discrimination isn't clean—it relies on patterns of effects across multiple measures, not knockout-level isolation. Yet the defence says attention "passes."

When applied to consciousness theories, the defence suddenly demands the stronger standard again: you must manipulate integrated information "independently of attention, arousal, reportability, and memory." But attention manipulations aren't independent of perception and decision-making either! The defence is applying stricter standards to consciousness theories

</details>
