### O2. Any theory of consciousness that claims to be empirically testable is actually unstable—it must either retreat to being unfalsifiable metaphysics or expand to trivialize into a theory of information processing generally.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Theorists like Tononi, Dehaene, and Dennett who present their theories as empirically grounded and distinct from philosophical speculation
- **Sketch:** The argument shows that empirically testable theories face a dilemma. Either they make precise, testable predictions about neural correlates, in which case they become vulnerable to the objection that correlation isn't constitution (and thus retreat to claiming the correlations merely track consciousness, not explain it—becoming unfalsifiable metaphysics). Or they avoid this by identifying consciousness with the observable processes themselves, in which case they must either engage in arbitrary restriction (why these processes and not those?) or admit that their account applies to any information-processing system (thermostats, bacteria, etc.), which is widely regarded as unacceptable. The key premise is that the explanatory gap prevents any smooth transition from empirical observation to metaphysical conclusion.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies a genuine structural dilemma for empirical consciousness research that could reframe debates about scientific testability.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence invoked epistemic asymmetry but this itself proves too much—would bar any science of subjective experience, not just show instability
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. IIT occupies stable middle ground making falsifiable predictions without trivializing; defence confused having hard questions with being unstable
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence equivocated between theories oscillating between positions versus facing pressure; only established the latter, which is trivial
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's attempt to make separating empirical and metaphysical components count as instability applies to all successful science, proving too much
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates on what counts as 'instability'—it needs theories to literally oscillate between positions but only shows they face pressure and hard questions, which is true of all scientific theories with metaphysical dimensions

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would equally undermine all scientific theories that bridge observable phenomena and theoretical entities. Consider: Any empirically testable theory of genes must either retreat to unfalsifiable metaphysics or expand to trivialize into a theory of molecular structures generally. The theory faces the same dilemma: either it makes precise, testable predictions about DNA sequences, in which case it becomes vulnerable to the objection that correlation isn't constitution (the DNA merely tracks inheritance, not explains it—becoming unfalsifiable metaphysics), or it identifies genes with observable molecular processes themselves, in which case it must either engage in arbitrary restriction (why these molecules and not those?) or admit that any molecular information-storage system counts as genetic (prions, RNA, etc.). The "explanatory gap" between observing base-pair sequences and concluding we've found genes is structurally identical to the consciousness case. Yet we don't regard gene theory as unstable. The same pattern applies to theories of memory, disease, temperature, or any theoretical posit initially known only through its effects. If the consciousness argument worked, virtually all successful scientific reduction would be impossible—we could never move from correlations to identifications without either "retreating to metaphysics" or "trivializing." This makes the argument self-defeating: it proves scientific theorizing itself is unstable.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The argument's logic, if sound, would delegitimize standard scientific methodology for theoretical identification across domains where we clearly have successful theories.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The gene analogy fails because it ignores the unique epistemic asymmetry in consciousness. The gene case involves third-person observable phenomena on both sides: we observe inheritance patterns, then observe molecular structures, then identify them. Any competent observer can verify both the explanandum (inheritance) and the explanans (DNA sequences) through public, repeatable observation.

Consciousness is fundamentally different: the explanandum is first-person phenomenal experience, accessible only to the subject, while the explanans is third-person neural activity, publicly observable. This creates an explanatory gap with no parallel in genetics.

When we identify genes with DNA, we can point to functional integration: DNA does what genes were posited to do (encode, replicate, vary, etc.) in ways publicly verifiable. The identification succeeds because both sides occupy the same epistemic space—third-person observation.

But when IIT or GNW identifies consciousness with certain information-processing patterns, we cannot verify the identification in the same way. We cannot observe whether integrated information *feels like something* except through the subject's report. And subject reports are precisely what generated the correlation we're now trying to explain away.

This means consciousness theories face a genuine dilemma absent in gene theory:
- If they rely on subject reports to verify the theory, they remain at the correlation level (reports correlate with neural patterns)
- If they dismiss reports and identify consciousness with the neural pattern alone, they've changed the subject—they're now theorizing about information processing, not about what it's like to be conscious

The gene theorist can say "we found the thing that does the inheriting." The consciousness theorist cannot similarly say "we found the thing that does the experiencing" without either relying on the very reports that keep them at correlation-level, or stipulating an identification that abandons the original explanandum.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies something real.

There IS an epistemic asymmetry in consciousness—the first-person/third-person divide is genuine. But this doesn't save the thesis from proving too much; it just relocates where it proves too much.

The defence essentially concedes that consciousness cannot be studied like other scientific posits because of this asymmetry. But this concession itself proves too much: it would equally apply to pain, emotion, or any phenomenal state. If the argument works, then *any* science of subjective experience is impossible—not just unstable theories, but any empirically testable theory whatsoever.

Moreover, the defence's own logic undermines the thesis's instability claim. If the epistemic asymmetry is insurmountable, then theories aren't *unstable* (shifting between positions)—they're simply *impossible*. The thesis claims theories oscillate between metaphysics and trivialization. The defence claims they cannot get started at all. These are different claims.

Finally, the gene analogy still bites: early gene theory faced exactly the "correlation vs. constitution" challenge the defence describes. Critics asked whether DNA merely *correlated* with inheritance or *constituted* it. The identification succeeded not by eliminating this question but by making it scientifically productive. The defence provides no principle explaining why consciousness must be different in kind rather than merely harder.

The defence fails to block the proves-too-much objection.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider Integrated Information Theory (IIT) as applied to split-brain patients. IIT makes the precise, empirically testable prediction that severing the corpus callosum should dramatically reduce phi (integrated information) and thus split consciousness into two separate conscious entities, each with lower phi than the original unified consciousness.

This prediction is:
1. Empirically testable (we can measure neural integration before and after commissurotomy)
2. Makes specific claims about consciousness (predicts phenomenological changes)
3. Has been partially confirmed (split-brain patients do show evidence of divided awareness in certain experimental conditions)

Yet IIT does NOT become unstable in the way the thesis predicts. It doesn't retreat to unfalsifiable metaphysics when challenged—Tononi and colleagues continue refining quantitative measures of phi and making additional testable predictions. Nor does it trivialize to apply to all information processing—IIT explicitly excludes feed-forward systems and systems with low integration, maintaining principled boundaries.

The theory remains stable at precisely the middle ground the thesis claims is impossible: making empirical predictions about neural correlates while maintaining that these correlates constitute (not merely track) consciousness, without expanding to cover thermostats or bacteria. The split-brain case demonstrates that at least one theory resists both horns of the supposed dilemma.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious counterexample. IIT appears to occupy exactly the stable middle ground the thesis claims cannot exist, making testable empirical predictions while avoiding both metaphysical retreat and trivializing expansion.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The IIT example actually *demonstrates* rather than refutes the instability. Look at what happens when we examine IIT's treatment of split-brain patients more carefully.

IIT makes a testable prediction about phi values dropping after commissurotomy—this is the "empirical" horn. But when pressed on what this *means* for consciousness, the theory faces exactly the dilemma described:

**First horn (retreat to metaphysics):** When critics ask "does lower phi mean *less* consciousness or *two* consciousnesses?", IIT must either:
- Rely on phenomenological reports from split-brain patients (but these reports are inconsistent and interpretation-dependent—patients mostly report feeling unified), which keeps us at correlation-level, OR
- Stipulate that phi *is* consciousness regardless of what subjects report, which makes the theory unfalsifiable—no possible report could refute it

**Second horn (trivialization):** When IIT tries to avoid this by identifying consciousness directly with integrated information, it faces the boundary problem. The theory's "principled boundaries" (excluding feed-forward systems, requiring high integration) are actually arbitrary restrictions imposed to avoid counterintuitive results. 

Consider: Why does IIT exclude feed-forward networks? Not because of any principled connection between feedback and phenomenology, but because including them would be embarrassing. A purely feed-forward image classifier would have some phi > 0 under the mathematical definition. IIT restricts this *ad hoc* to avoid the implication.

Similarly with "low integration" exclusions—where's the threshold? IIT cannot provide a non-arbitrary cutoff between "enough integration for consciousness" and "too little." Any line drawn is drawn to match our pre-theoretic intuitions about what should count as conscious, not derived from the theory itself.

The split-brain case actually shows the instability in action: IIT oscillates between making the empirical claim (phi drops) and making metaphysical stipulations about what this means for consciousness (whether it splits, diminishes, or remains unified). When pressed on the metaphysical question, it either defers to reports (correlation) or insists on the identification regardless of reports (unfalsifiable). When pressed on why its boundaries aren't arbitrary, it either admits they're somewhat stipulative or expands the theory to cover cases we find absurd.

The appearance of stability is maintained only by not pressing too hard on either horn—but the instability is there, latent in the theory's structure.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence tries to show IIT exhibits instability by pointing to interpretive flexibility and boundary problems. But this confuses *having difficult questions* with *being unstable*. Every scientific theory has interpretive challenges and boundary cases—this doesn't make them unstable in the sense the thesis requires.

The thesis claims theories must either "retreat to unfalsifiable metaphysics" OR "expand to trivialize." IIT does neither:

**On metaphysical retreat:** IIT doesn't become unfalsifiable when split-brain reports are messy. It makes quantitative predictions about phi that can be measured independently of phenomenological reports. The theory predicts specific changes in neural integration patterns. These predictions can be wrong—if commissurotomy didn't reduce measured integration, IIT would be in trouble. That's falsifiability.

**On trivialization:** The defence claims IIT's boundaries are "ad hoc" and "arbitrary," but this is false. The exclusion of feed-forward systems follows from the mathematical definition of integration—feed-forward systems have zero or near-zero phi by IIT's own measure, not by stipulation. The theory *derives* its boundaries from its formal framework rather than imposing them to avoid embarrassment.

The defence's claim that "any line drawn is drawn to match pre-theoretic intuitions" applies equally to temperature (where's the exact boundary between hot and cold?), life (where's the exact threshold for living vs. non-living?), or any scientific category with gradual boundaries. This doesn't make thermodynamics or biology unstable.

Most critically, the defence equivocates on "instability." The thesis claims theories oscillate *between positions*—retreating when pressed one way, expanding when pressed another. But IIT maintains consistent commitments: phi is consciousness, integration is measured thus, these are the predictions. Having interpretive questions about edge cases (do split-brain patients have one consciousness or two?) is not the same as oscillating between metaphysical and trivial versions of the theory.

The counterexample stands: IIT occupies stable middle ground, making falsifiable predictions without trivializing. The defence has not shown otherwise.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis presents itself as a substantive discovery about the structure of consciousness theories, but it's actually a trivial consequence of how we've stipulatively defined "unstable" combined with an uncontested verbal point about explanatory gaps.

The weak point is the claim that theories "must either retreat to being unfalsifiable metaphysics or expand to trivialize." This sounds like an empirical discovery about consciousness theories, but it's actually just unpacking what we mean by "the explanatory gap" in a particular rhetorical register.

Everyone already agrees there's an explanatory gap between physical processes and phenomenal experience—this is Levine's uncontested point from 1983. The thesis merely restates this as: "therefore theories will face pressure from two directions." But facing pressure isn't instability—it's just having a hard problem.

The thesis then stipulatively defines any response to this pressure as "retreat" or "expansion," ensuring theories count as "unstable" by definition. If a theory maintains that correlations are constitutive, that's labeled "retreat to metaphysics." If it identifies consciousness with physical processes, that's labeled "trivialization." But these are just verbal redescriptions of the two standard positions in philosophy of mind (dualism vs. reductionism), now wearing the costume of "instability."

The supposed discovery is: "Hard problems remain hard and theorists take different positions on them." This is true but completely uncontested. Calling this "instability" rather than "disagreement" or "difficulty" is a verbal stipulation, not a finding. The thesis mistakes a dramatized restatement of the explanatory gap for a novel structural insight about theory dynamics.

# STRENGTH: 3/5

This attack has genuine force. The thesis does rely heavily on the uncontested existence of the explanatory gap and adds primarily rhetorical packaging ("instability," "retreat," "trivialize"). However, it's not entirely trivial—there IS a structural claim about theories being unable to occupy stable middle ground, which goes beyond merely noting disagreement. The attack succeeds in showing the thesis is more verbal stipulation than discovery, but doesn't quite reduce it to pure tautology.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes a structural diagnosis for mere relabeling. The thesis is not simply restating "the explanatory gap exists" or "people disagree"—it's making a specific claim about theory *dynamics*: that empirically testable theories cannot maintain a stable position but must oscillate or collapse.

Consider the difference between these claims:
- "Consciousness is hard to explain" (trivial, uncontested)
- "Theorists disagree about consciousness" (trivial, uncontested)  
- "Any empirically testable theory is forced to shift between incompatible positions or abandon its original explanatory target" (substantive, contested)

The thesis makes the third claim. This is not verbal stipulation—it's a prediction about what happens when theories try to do specific work.

The key is the *empirically testable* constraint. The thesis doesn't claim all theories face this instability—pure metaphysical theories can remain stable (property dualism, panpsychism, etc.). The instability arises specifically when theories try to be empirically grounded while explaining phenomenal consciousness.

Here's why this is non-trivial: It predicts a specific failure mode. When Dehaene's Global Neuronal Workspace theory is pressed on whether broadcast mechanisms *are* consciousness or merely *correlate* with it, the theory must either:
1. Make the identification (broadcast = consciousness), which forces expansion to cover any broadcast system, OR
2. Maintain the distinction (broadcast correlates with consciousness), which removes empirical testability—we can only test broadcast, not consciousness itself

This isn't just "facing pressure" or "disagreement"—it's a structural bind. The theory cannot simultaneously maintain empirical testability AND explanatory adequacy AND principled boundaries. Something must give.

The objection claims this is just "dualism vs. reductionism" relabeled. But that's wrong. Both dualists and reductionists can maintain stable positions. The thesis claims that *empirically testable* theories cannot—they're forced to either become non-empirical (dualism) or abandon the phenomenal target (reductionism about information processing generally). The instability is the inability to remain in the middle.

The non-triviality is demonstrated by the fact that major theorists resist this diagnosis. Tononi insists IIT is both empirically testable AND specifically about phenomenal consciousness AND doesn't apply to thermostats. The thesis says this combination is unstable. That's a substantive, falsifiable claim about theory structure, not mere verbal redescription.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though it fights hard.

The defence tries to establish non-triviality by pointing to a "structural bind" and "specific failure mode." But examine what this amounts to: when theories are pressed on the metaphysical question (constitution vs. correlation), they must take a position. Taking a position is then labeled "instability."

This is still essentially trivial. The defence's own example reveals this: "When Dehaene is pressed on whether broadcast mechanisms ARE consciousness or merely CORRELATE with it, the theory must either..." Yes—the theory must take a position on a metaphysical question. That's not instability; that's having to answer a question.

The defence claims "both dualists and reductionists can maintain stable positions" but "empirically testable theories cannot." This is confused. Dehaene IS a reductionist—he thinks broadcast mechanisms constitute consciousness. That's a stable position. The defence wants to say this position "forces expansion to cover any broadcast system," but as the IIT discussion showed, theories can maintain principled boundaries without trivializing.

The core problem: the defence equivocates between two meanings of "instability":
1. A theory that literally oscillates between positions over time (genuine instability)
2. A theory that faces pressure and must make difficult choices (just having hard questions)

The thesis needs meaning (1) to be interesting, but only establishes meaning (2), which is trivial. Every theory in every domain faces pressure and must make choices about boundary cases and metaphysical interpretation. Calling this "instability" is indeed mere relabeling.

The defence's claim that "major theorists resist this diagnosis" doesn't help. Theorists like Tononi don't resist because they're confused about their theory's structure—they resist because "instability" misdescribes what's actually happening. They maintain consistent positions while working on hard problems. That's stability.

The objection succeeds: the thesis is largely trivial, restating the explanatory gap in dramatic language and labeling the standard theory-building challenges as "instability." The structural claim dissolves into verbal stipulation.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "empirically testable" between a weak reading (makes some empirical predictions) and a strong reading (is fully empirically verifiable), and the argument needs both readings to generate the dilemma.

The weak point is the opening claim: "Any theory of consciousness that claims to be empirically testable is actually unstable."

On the WEAK reading: "empirically testable" means the theory makes some empirical predictions about observable correlates (neural activity, behavior, etc.). On this reading, the thesis is interesting—it would be surprising if theories making any empirical predictions were inherently unstable.

On the STRONG reading: "empirically testable" means the theory claims phenomenal consciousness itself can be fully verified through empirical observation. On this reading, the thesis is plausible—of course such theories face problems, since phenomenal properties aren't publicly observable.

The argument needs BOTH readings. It needs the weak reading to have interesting scope (capturing theories like IIT, GNW, HOT that make empirical predictions). But it needs the strong reading to generate the instability—the dilemma only works if we demand that theories empirically verify the phenomenal properties themselves, not just their correlates.

The sketch reveals this: "they make precise, testable predictions about neural correlates" (weak reading) but then "correlation isn't constitution" becomes a problem only if we expected empirical tests to establish constitution of phenomenal properties (strong reading). Theories making empirical predictions about correlates (weak) don't claim to empirically verify qualia themselves (strong), so they don't face the dilemma. The argument slides between these readings.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem. The thesis generates its dilemma only by equivocating on what "empirically testable" means. Theories that make empirical predictions about neural correlates while remaining agnostic or metaphysical about the constitution question don't face the instability described—they're stable precisely by separating empirical and metaphysical components.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies where the equivocation allegedly occurs. The thesis uses "empirically testable" consistently in the weak sense throughout—theories that make empirical predictions about observable correlates. The instability arises not from smuggling in a strong reading, but from the internal pressure within theories that make only weak empirical claims.

Here's why: A theory that merely predicts neural correlates while remaining "agnostic or metaphysical about the constitution question" is not actually a theory of consciousness at all—it's a theory of neural activity that happens to correlate with consciousness reports. This is the first horn of the dilemma (retreat to metaphysics), not an escape from it.

The objection claims theories can be "stable precisely by separating empirical and metaphysical components." But this separation IS the instability the thesis diagnoses. Consider what this means in practice:

When GNW predicts that conscious access requires global broadcast, is this:
- An empirical claim about what consciousness IS (broadcast = access consciousness), OR
- An empirical claim about what merely CORRELATES with consciousness (broadcast tracks consciousness but doesn't constitute it)?

If the first: the theory must defend why broadcast specifically constitutes consciousness rather than any information-processing pattern (expansion pressure toward trivialization).

If the second: the theory has "separated" its empirical component (broadcast) from its metaphysical component (consciousness), which means the empirical predictions no longer test the theory of consciousness—they test only the theory of broadcast. The consciousness part has become metaphysical speculation untethered from the empirical work.

This separation is not stability—it's the retreat to metaphysics the thesis describes. The theory presents itself as empirically testing claims about consciousness, but under pressure reveals that it's only testing claims about neural activity, with consciousness as metaphysical addendum.

The objection's proposed stable position—"making empirical predictions about correlates while remaining agnostic about constitution"—is precisely the unstable middle ground. Such theories face constant pressure: Why should we accept this as a theory of consciousness rather than just neuroscience? Either make the constitutive claim (and face expansion pressure) or admit the empirical work doesn't actually bear on consciousness (metaphysical retreat).

The thesis doesn't equivocate on "empirically testable." It maintains the weak reading throughout and shows that even this weak empirical commitment generates instability when combined with the explanatory gap. The instability comes not from demanding strong empirical verification of qualia, but from the tension between making any empirical predictions and explaining phenomenal consciousness.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence claims theories that "separate empirical and metaphysical components" exhibit instability rather than escaping it. But this radically overextends what counts as "instability" to the point of absurdity.

Consider the defence's key claim: a theory that predicts neural correlates while remaining agnostic about constitution "is not actually a theory of consciousness at all—it's a theory of neural activity that happens to correlate with consciousness reports."

This is false. Such a theory IS a theory of consciousness in the perfectly respectable scientific sense—it identifies the neural basis of consciousness without settling metaphysical questions about the nature of phenomenal properties. This is how most successful science works: we identify what realizes a phenomenon without resolving every philosophical question about it.

The defence tries to make "facing pressure" equivalent to "being unstable." It says theories face "constant pressure: Why should we accept this as a theory of consciousness?" But facing questions is not instability. Evolutionary theory faces constant pressure ("Why should we accept common descent?"), but it's not unstable—it maintains consistent commitments while answering challenges.

More critically, the defence's own logic proves too much in exactly the way earlier objections identified. By the defence's standard, ANY theory that makes empirical predictions while leaving metaphysical questions open is "unstable." But this describes virtually all successful science:

- Genetic theory predicts DNA correlates with inheritance while remaining agnostic about whether genes are "really" DNA or DNA merely realizes genetic function
- Thermodynamics predicts temperature correlates with molecular motion while remaining agnostic about whether temperature IS mean kinetic energy or merely correlates with it
- Memory theory predicts neural patterns correlate with recall while remaining agnostic about constitutive questions

These theories are stable despite "separating empirical and metaphysical components." The defence provides no principle for why consciousness must be different.

The equivocation objection succeeds. The thesis needs theories to claim strong empirical verification of phenomenal properties to generate genuine instability, but actual theories (IIT, GNW, etc.) make only weak empirical claims about correlates. These theories can remain stable by doing what all successful science does: making empirical predictions while leaving some metaphysical questions open or underspecified.

The defence's attempt to classify this standard scientific practice as "instability" or "metaphysical retreat" is verbal legislation that makes the thesis trivial: "theories that

### O3. Accepting that unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated commits you to accepting that no functional profile, no matter how complex, could ever confirm a consciousness theory.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Functionalist approaches to testing consciousness theories, including many versions of GNW that rely on reportability and behavioral measures
- **Sketch:** Most researchers now accept cases like blindsight, subliminal priming of complex decisions, and unconscious skill learning—showing that unconscious processing can match conscious processing in sophistication. But this acceptance has a devastating implication: if unconscious systems can mimic any functional profile of conscious systems, then functional evidence cannot discriminate between theories. Any behavioral test could in principle be passed by an unconscious system. IIT theorists and functionalists both accept the premise about unconscious sophistication, yet still propose functional tests. They're committed to either (a) consciousness making no functional difference, or (b) there being something functionally detectable that they haven't yet specified—but they've already ruled out all functional candidates.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Powerful internal tension argument: accepting unconscious sophistication undermines the functional testing both camps actually employ.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claimed a novel reductio targeting empirical acceptance, but the judge found this was already theorized as the 'calibration problem' and that GNW theorists specify functional differences unconscious processing lacks
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence argued consciousness is unique in lacking independent access, but the judge found this fails because we have the same inferential access to life-in-bacteria and pain-in-others, where functional confirmation demonstrably works
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claimed anesthesiologists don't accept the premise, but the judge found they accept both that unconscious processing can be sophisticated and that specific profiles reliably indicate consciousness in context—no contradiction
  - TRIVIAL — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence reframed as pragmatic inconsistency, but the judge found this abandons the original claim and reduces to normal scientific refinement—researchers aren't contradicting themselves by continuing to propose tests
- **Flaw:** The thesis either overgeneralizes to all scientific domains where functional confirmation demonstrably works (life, pain, anesthesia levels) or, when narrowed to avoid this, reduces to the unremarkable observation that refining criteria after failures is normal science, not a devastating commitment

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This thesis articulates the "functional indiscriminability argument" that has been central to debates about consciousness since at least Block's 1995 distinction between phenomenal and access consciousness. Block explicitly argued that P-consciousness could be functionally identical to states lacking phenomenal character, making functional profiles insufficient for consciousness detection. Chalmers' "hard problem" (1995) makes essentially the same point: zombie thought experiments show that any functional organization could in principle exist without consciousness, so functional tests cannot confirm consciousness theories. More recently, Irvine (2013) and Cohen & Dennett (2011) debated precisely whether unconscious processing can match conscious processing in sophistication while remaining unconscious. The thesis's specific focus on "arbitrarily sophisticated" unconscious processing as undermining functional confirmation is the core of what Overgaard (2017) calls the "consciousness-function dissociation problem." The weak point is the phrase "accepting that unconscious processing **can be** arbitrarily sophisticated" - the thesis equivocates between logical possibility and actual empirical cases, but the existing positions already occupy both versions.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is seriously damaging. The thesis appears to be a restatement of well-established positions in the consciousness literature rather than a novel commitment-revealing move. The argument structure is Block and Chalmers's, just applied specifically to contemporary empirical acceptance of unconscious sophistication.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The thesis is not a restatement of Block or Chalmers—it's a *reductio* targeted at researchers who reject their conclusions while accepting empirical premises that entail them.

Block and Chalmers argued from *logical possibility*: zombies are conceivable, therefore functional profiles cannot metaphysically guarantee consciousness. Most functionalists and GNW theorists reject this—they deny zombies are genuinely possible, or they accept only "weak" metaphysical conclusions while maintaining that functional profiles can still *empirically confirm* theories in our world.

This thesis operates differently. It says: *You already accept, on empirical grounds, that actual unconscious systems in actual brains perform arbitrarily sophisticated processing*. You accept blindsight patients navigating obstacles, unconscious semantic priming, procedural learning matching declarative performance. You're not dealing with philosophical zombies—you're dealing with empirically documented cases where the functional work gets done without consciousness.

The commitment-revealing move is this: if you accept these empirical cases while still proposing that reportability, global availability, or flexible control can *confirm* a consciousness theory, you face a dilemma the zombie argument never posed. Either (a) you must specify what functional difference consciousness makes that these unconscious cases somehow lack—but you've already accepted they lack nothing functional—or (b) you must admit your proposed tests cannot actually confirm your theory, since unconscious systems pass them.

This isn't Block's metaphysical point. It's an *internal* inconsistency between accepting the neuroscience of unconscious sophistication and proposing functional confirmation methods. The thesis shows that empirical liberalism about unconscious processing is incompatible with methodological functionalism about theory testing—a tension that doesn't exist in Block's framework because he never accepted the empirical premise in the first place.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, but not completely.

The thesis does identify a genuine tension that differs from Block's argument: researchers who accept rich unconscious processing while proposing functional tests face a burden Block's opponents don't. But this tension was already explicitly theorized as the "calibration problem" in consciousness science (Sandberg et al. 2010, Overgaard & Overgaard 2010). The question "how do we calibrate our measures when unconscious processing is so sophisticated?" is the central methodological challenge these researchers acknowledge.

More fatally: the defence's dilemma is false. Option (c) exists: consciousness makes a *specific* functional difference that unconscious processing, despite its sophistication, systematically lacks. GNW theorists explicitly propose this—global broadcast to a workspace, enabling a specific kind of flexible integration. They don't claim unconscious processing lacks sophistication; they claim it lacks *this particular functional architecture*. Whether they're right is open, but they're not committed to the contradiction the thesis alleges.

The thesis survives as a challenge to poorly-specified functionalism, but dies against positions that specify the functional difference clearly.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would prove that no empirical evidence could ever confirm any theory about any unobservable process that has functional effects. Consider: We accept that non-living chemical systems can be arbitrarily sophisticated (autocatalytic networks, self-organizing systems, complex molecular computation). Therefore, by this thesis's logic, accepting that non-living chemistry can be arbitrarily sophisticated commits you to accepting that no functional profile could ever confirm a theory of life. Any metabolic test could in principle be passed by a non-living system. But this is absurd—we do successfully use functional profiles (metabolism, reproduction, homeostasis) to confirm theories of life and distinguish living from non-living systems, despite non-living chemistry being arbitrarily complex. Similarly for: computation vs. non-computation (accepting physical systems can be arbitrarily complex doesn't prevent functional confirmation of computation theories), agency vs. mechanism, memory vs. mere information storage. The weak point is the move from "unconscious systems can be arbitrarily sophisticated" to "therefore no functional profile could discriminate." This conflates sophistication-in-general with possession-of-the-specific-functional-profile-that-consciousness-theories-propose. The thesis would, if valid, prove that functional discrimination is impossible for any phenomenon, which is obviously false.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The argument structure genuinely does overgeneralize to cases where we clearly can use functional profiles for confirmation despite underlying processes being arbitrarily sophisticated. The thesis needs to explain why consciousness is special in a way that life, computation, and agency are not.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The consciousness case is disanalogous in a way that saves the thesis from overgeneralization.

For life, computation, and agency, we have *independent access* to the phenomenon that lets us calibrate functional profiles against ground truth. We can observe metabolism, reproduction, and homeostasis in paradigm living systems, then extract the functional profile that distinguishes them from non-living chemistry. We build computers, know which physical systems are computing, and reverse-engineer the functional architecture. We are agents, experience our own agency, and can map its functional signature.

But consciousness is unique: we have *no* independent access to it in other systems. I know I'm conscious through first-person acquaintance. For others—including other humans—I have only functional profiles: reports, behaviors, neural activity. I cannot independently verify that these correlate with consciousness the way I can verify that metabolism correlates with life by checking both independently.

This asymmetry is fatal for functional confirmation. With life, the method is: (1) identify living things independently, (2) find their common functional profile, (3) use that profile to test theories and detect new instances. With consciousness, we cannot do step (1) for other systems. We're trying to use functional profiles to *establish* the presence of consciousness, not just to characterize something we've already identified.

Now add the empirical premise: unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated. This means that for any functional profile F you propose as consciousness-indicating, I can point to empirically documented cases of F occurring without consciousness (or at least, without the consciousness you're trying to detect). Blindsight shows sophisticated visual processing without visual experience. Subliminal priming shows semantic processing without awareness. You cannot reply "but living systems have metabolism" because we independently verified that metabolism goes with life. You have no such verification for F and consciousness.

The thesis doesn't claim functional discrimination is *always* impossible. It claims functional discrimination is impossible when (a) you lack independent access to the target phenomenon, and (b) you accept that systems lacking the phenomenon can exhibit arbitrary functional sophistication. Consciousness uniquely satisfies both conditions.

The life analogy fails because life violates condition (a)—we have independent access through multiple converging observations that don't rely solely on functional profiles.

## VERDICT

The defence fails.

The "independent access" move is the standard response, but it doesn't save the thesis because the objection's examples can be reconstructed to match consciousness's epistemic situation exactly.

Consider: I am a scientist who has never experienced pain (congenital insensitivity to pain). I'm trying to confirm theories about pain in others. I have no first-person access to pain in other systems—only functional profiles (withdrawal behaviors, reports, neural activity). Non-pain states can be arbitrarily sophisticated (actors feigning pain, reflexive withdrawal without pain experience, neural activity in anesthetized patients). By the thesis's logic, I'm committed to accepting that no functional profile could confirm a pain theory.

But this is false. Pain scientists in this position *can* and *do* confirm theories by finding functional profiles that systematically co-occur with pain reports in calibration cases, then using those profiles for detection. The method works despite lacking "independent access" to others' pain.

More decisively: the defence claims we have independent access to life but not consciousness. This is wrong. We have *exactly the same kind* of access to both in other systems—namely, inference from functional profiles. I don't directly observe metabolism in a bacterium any more than I directly observe consciousness in you. I observe functional indicators (movement, growth, chemical exchange) and infer life. The asymmetry the defence needs doesn't exist.

The thesis proves too much. If it worked for consciousness, it would work for pain-in-others, life-in-bacteria, and any phenomenon where we lack direct access and must infer from functional profiles. Since functional confirmation clearly works in these cases, the argument structure is flawed.

The thesis is dead.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider the case of distinguishing genuine understanding from mere symbol manipulation in language systems—the Chinese Room problem. We accept that unconscious/non-understanding symbol manipulation can be arbitrarily sophisticated (it can pass Turing tests, generate coherent novels, solve complex problems). Yet we successfully use functional profiles to confirm theories about understanding. Linguists and cognitive scientists propose that understanding requires specific functional architectures: grounded symbol systems connected to perception and action, integration across multiple modalities, certain patterns of generalization and error. These theories ARE empirically testable and confirmable despite sophisticated non-understanding systems existing. 

Similarly, a concrete consciousness case: We accept that unconscious processing during certain anesthesia states can be highly sophisticated (the brain still processes speech, maintains homeostasis, performs complex regulation). Yet anesthesiologists successfully use functional profiles (response to command, purposeful movement, explicit recall) to confirm theories about consciousness levels and distinguish conscious from unconscious states. The functional tests work for theory confirmation despite sophisticated unconscious processing existing. The thesis predicts this should be impossible, but it demonstrably occurs. The weak point is the universal quantifier "no functional profile... could ever confirm"—counterexamples where functional confirmation succeeds despite sophisticated unconscious alternatives show this is too strong.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — These counterexamples seriously damage the thesis. They show that functional confirmation can work even when we accept sophisticated unconscious/non-target processing, contradicting the thesis's central claim.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The counterexamples fail because they equivocate on "sophisticated" and miss the dialectical structure of the commitment.

**On the Chinese Room case:** The thesis doesn't claim that *we* cannot distinguish understanding from non-understanding using functional profiles. It claims that *if you accept* that non-understanding systems can replicate any functional profile of understanding systems, *then* you're committed to functional profiles being insufficient. Searle's Chinese Room argument is precisely an attempt to show this commitment—that functionalists who accept the room's functional equivalence must either deny it understands (contra functionalism) or accept it understands (contra intuition). The thesis identifies the same structure for consciousness. The counterexample actually *supports* the thesis by showing the parallel commitment problem.

**On anesthesiology:** This case reveals the crucial distinction the thesis exploits. Anesthesiologists succeed because they *don't* accept that unconscious processing during anesthesia can match *every* functional profile of conscious processing. They specifically deny that unconscious anesthetized patients show purposeful movement in response to command or form explicit recall. The functional tests work precisely because there *are* functional differences—the sophisticated unconscious processing during anesthesia is not "arbitrarily" sophisticated in the relevant sense.

Here's the commitment-revealing move the thesis makes: Many consciousness researchers accept cases like blindsight, subliminal priming, and unconscious skill learning as showing that unconscious processing can match conscious processing *on the very dimensions they propose as consciousness-indicating*. Blindsight patients navigate obstacles (spatial processing without visual consciousness). Subliminal primes affect complex decisions (semantic processing without awareness). Unconscious skill learning achieves expert performance (flexible control without conscious access).

The anesthesia case differs crucially: we calibrate the functional tests by finding profiles that unconscious states *don't* match. But if you've already accepted that unconscious processing can match those profiles in other contexts (blindsight patients DO navigate, unconscious processing DOES guide flexible behavior), you've undermined your own calibration.

The thesis doesn't claim functional confirmation is always impossible. It claims: *If you accept the empirical premise that unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated—meaning it can match any functional profile you might propose—then you cannot coherently propose functional tests*. The anesthesiologist doesn't accept this premise; they maintain specific functional boundaries. Researchers who accept rich unconscious processing while proposing the same functional profiles as tests are in a different position.

**The universal quantifier "no functional profile... could ever" is not too strong—it's conditional.** The thesis says: *Given that you accept unconscious sophistication, no functional profile could confirm your theory*. It's a reductio of the combination of commitments, not a universal claim about functional confirmation.

## VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence tries to save the thesis by claiming anesthesiologists "don't accept" that unconscious processing can match the relevant functional profiles. But this misunderstands how empirical acceptance works and reveals the thesis's fatal flaw.

Anesthesiologists accept that unconscious processing *can in principle* be arbitrarily sophisticated—they know about blindsight, subliminal priming, complex unconscious regulation. They also know that unconscious processing *in anesthetized patients specifically* doesn't produce purposeful movement to command. These are not contradictory positions. They're accepting both:
1. Unconscious processing can be functionally sophisticated in various ways
2. Specific functional profiles reliably correlate with consciousness in specific contexts

The thesis demands that accepting (1) commits you to rejecting (2). But the anesthesia case shows this is false. You can accept that unconscious systems *somewhere* exhibit sophisticated processing while maintaining that *in your experimental context*, specific functional profiles reliably indicate consciousness.

More fatally: the defence's interpretation of "arbitrarily sophisticated" as meaning "can match ANY functional profile you might propose" makes the thesis trivial. If unconscious processing can literally match any functional profile, then by definition nothing functionally distinguishes conscious from unconscious processing, and of course functional tests fail. But this is just stipulating the conclusion. 

The interesting question is whether accepting *actual documented cases* of sophisticated unconscious processing (blindsight, priming, etc.) commits you to functional testing being impossible. The answer is no, because those cases show sophistication in *some* dimensions while potentially lacking it in others. GNW theorists accept blindsight but claim it lacks global broadcast. IIT theorists accept subliminal priming but claim it lacks integrated information. They're not contradicting themselves—they're proposing that consciousness involves a *specific* functional architecture that documented unconscious cases lack.

The thesis dies because it conflates "unconscious processing can be sophisticated in many ways" with "unconscious processing can match every functional profile," and the counterexamples show this conflation is unjustified.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing a hidden commitment, but it's actually just restating the definitional relationship between functionalism and the possibility of functional zombies—a relationship that's been explicit in the literature since functionalism was first articulated. The weak point is that this isn't a *discovery* about what researchers are "committed to"—it's a verbal unpacking of what "unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated" means. 

If "arbitrarily sophisticated" means "can match any functional profile," then "no functional profile could discriminate" is true by definition—you've just stipulated that functional profiles don't discriminate. If it means something weaker (like "can be sophisticated in many documented ways"), then the thesis is false, as the previous exchanges showed. 

The thesis tries to sound like it's exposing a contradiction between accepting empirical cases (blindsight, priming) and proposing functional tests. But there's no contradiction—researchers who accept those cases simply claim consciousness involves a *specific* functional profile those cases lack. The thesis either reduces to "if you define unconscious processing as functionally identical to conscious processing, they're functionally identical" (trivial) or "accepting some sophisticated unconscious processing means accepting all functional profiles can be unconscious" (false). Either way, it's not revealing a hidden commitment; it's either stipulating an equivalence or making an invalid inference that researchers explicitly reject.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is seriously damaging. The thesis collapses into either a tautology (if we grant its interpretation of "arbitrarily sophisticated") or a non-sequitur (if we use the empirical interpretation). It's not revealing what researchers are committed to—it's either restating what they explicitly deny or playing with definitions.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The thesis is neither tautological nor invalid—it identifies a *pragmatic* inconsistency between methodological commitments that researchers actually hold simultaneously.

The objection claims the thesis either stipulates its conclusion or makes an invalid inference. But this misses the dialectical target. The thesis isn't a deductive proof; it's a challenge to a *research program's coherence*. Here's the actual commitment structure:

**What researchers actually accept:**
1. Empirical liberalism: Unconscious processing can perform tasks previously thought to require consciousness—navigation (blindsight), semantic processing (subliminal priming), skill learning, complex decision-making
2. Methodological functionalism: We can test consciousness theories by identifying functional profiles (reportability, global availability, flexible control) that distinguish conscious from unconscious processing
3. Progressive discovery: As we find more sophisticated unconscious processing, we refine our functional criteria but maintain that some functional profile will work

The thesis reveals these three commitments are in tension. Each time you accept a new case of sophisticated unconscious processing, you're accepting that *yet another* functional profile you might have proposed doesn't actually track consciousness. The pattern shows an empirical trend: the space of "functional profiles that could distinguish consciousness" keeps shrinking as unconscious sophistication grows.

This isn't stipulating that unconscious processing matches *all* profiles (tautology), nor claiming that accepting *some* sophisticated cases entails accepting *all* profiles (invalid inference). It's observing that the research program's own discoveries are systematically undermining its methodology. The commitment is to *both* discovering ever-more-sophisticated unconscious processing *and* believing that functional profiles will ultimately confirm theories—but each discovery makes the latter less credible.

**The real bite:** Researchers cannot specify in advance which functional profile consciousness requires, because they keep discovering unconscious systems that have the profiles they previously proposed. GNW theorists said "global availability"—but then accepted that unconscious processing can be globally available in certain senses (widespread neural activation, influence on multiple systems). They refined to "broadcast to a specific workspace"—but cannot independently verify this workspace exists without assuming the functional profile they're trying to establish.

This is not trivial. It's showing that the research program has a *methodological regress*: you need functional criteria to test theories, but you keep finding that unconscious processing meets your criteria, so you refine your criteria, but you have no independent way to verify the refined criteria except by assuming they track consciousness, which is what you're trying to establish.

The commitment revealed is: *believing that functional testing will work despite systematically discovering it hasn't worked for the profiles you've tried so far*. That's not a tautology or an invalid inference—it's a justified challenge to methodological optimism.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, and the thesis is dead.

The defence tries to reframe the thesis as identifying a "pragmatic inconsistency" or "methodological regress," but this reformulation abandons what made the thesis seem powerful. The original thesis claimed researchers are *committed to* accepting that no functional profile could *ever* confirm a theory. The defence retreats to claiming researchers face *diminishing credibility* for their methodological optimism.

These are completely different claims. The first is a logical commitment that would, if true, show researchers are contradicting themselves. The second is a sociological observation about research program dynamics that researchers can acknowledge without contradiction.

More fatally, the defence's "methodological regress" is exactly how science normally works. Consider immunology: researchers kept discovering that processes they thought required adaptive immunity could be performed by innate immunity (pattern recognition, memory-like responses, specificity). This didn't show that "no functional profile could ever distinguish adaptive from innate immunity"—it showed they were refining their understanding of what the distinction actually is. The progressive refinement the defence describes is *normal scientific progress*, not a fatal commitment.

The thesis claimed to reveal that accepting sophisticated unconscious processing *commits you to* accepting that functional confirmation is impossible. But the defence shows only that it commits you to *continuing to refine your functional criteria*—which is what researchers explicitly say they're doing. There's no hidden commitment, no contradiction, no regress that doesn't apply equally to every scientific research program that refines its concepts.

The thesis is trivial after all, but in a different way than the objection claimed: it's trivially true that if you keep finding your criteria don't work, you have less reason to think new criteria will work. But this is just Bayesian updating, not a devastating commitment. Researchers who continue proposing functional tests after each refinement aren't contradicting themselves—they're betting that the next refinement will succeed where previous ones failed. That bet might be wrong, but it's not incoherent.

**The thesis is dead.** It either overgeneralizes to all science (if taken as a logical commitment) or reduces to an unremarkable observation about research program dynamics (if taken as the defence suggests).

</details>

### U1. Consciousness theories are actually testable, but only through second-person methods where the theorist undergoes systematic phenomenological training and first-personally verifies the purported structures—making consciousness science methodologically closer to pure mathematics than to physics.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both sides of the debate about whether consciousness science is possible: eliminativists who say it isn't, and neural correlate researchers who say it requires third-person methods
- **Sketch:** The dispute about theory-testing assumes the model is something like physics: third-person observations adjudicate hypotheses about unobservable structures. But consciousness is directly observable—just not third-personally. The real methodological model should be pure mathematics, where claims about abstract structures are verified through trained first-person investigation of those structures. Just as you cannot test claims about higher-dimensional topology without learning the mathematics, you cannot test claims about phenomenal structures without learning to attend to your experience in the theoretically-specified way. IIT, HOT, GWT make different predictions about discoverable first-person structures (integration vs. higher-order awareness vs. global availability). The key premise is that intersubjective verification through trained phenomenological practice is as rigorous as mathematical proof verification, making the science empirical but not third-person.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Genuinely distinctive methodological proposal; mathematics analogy is fresh and the phenomenological training requirement would reframe the field.

### U2. The real barrier to testing consciousness theories is not empirical but mathematical: we lack formal tools to compare information-theoretic, representational, and dynamical-systems explanations on a common scale, which means we cannot tell whether theories make the same predictions in different vocabularies or genuinely different predictions.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Both experimentalists who think more data will decide the issue and philosophers who think the problem is that consciousness is empirically inaccessible
- **Sketch:** Methodologists assume the problem is getting better data, but consider: IIT makes predictions in terms of phi (integrated information), GWT in terms of ignition patterns, predictive processing in terms of prediction error minimization. Even when we have all the neural data, we cannot tell whether 'high phi' and 'global ignition' are picking out the same phenomenon described differently or distinct phenomena that happen to correlate. The key premise: without translation functions between these mathematical frameworks, we cannot determine whether apparent theoretical disagreements are substantive or merely notational. This is a counterexample to the widespread assumption that empirical underdetermination is the problem—actually, we have mathematical incommensurability. What we need is not better experiments but better formal tools for relating different kinds of theoretical quantities.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Shifts focus from empirical to mathematical incommensurability in a novel way; identifies a concrete gap (translation functions) that's underexplored.

### U3. Any empirical test of consciousness theories requires that we already know whether the test subject is conscious, making the enterprise circular unless we can find cases where we're genuinely uncertain.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Neuroscientific approaches that assume we can use paradigm cases (like anesthesia studies) to test theories
- **Sketch:** Consider how we'd test IIT versus Global Workspace Theory: we'd look at neural signatures during conscious versus unconscious states. But this requires already knowing which states are conscious. If we use self-report, we've assumed linguistic creatures can report consciousness accurately. If we use behavior, we've assumed consciousness correlates with certain behaviors. The only way forward is to find cases where we're genuinely uncertain whether consciousness is present (locked-in patients, unresponsive patients, or novel systems) and see which theory's predictions match our eventual verdict. But this commits us to the view that empirical progress is possible only at the boundaries of our existing concept, not in its core. The key premise is that empirical confirmation requires independent access to both the predictor (neural activity) and the predicted (consciousness), but for consciousness we lack independent access.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies a genuine methodological circularity that could reframe how we understand empirical progress in consciousness science, though the boundary-cases solution may be too narrow.

### U4. The criterion dispute about consciousness theories can only be resolved by recognizing that different theories are actually answering different questions about distinct explanatory targets that we've mistakenly bundled under one concept.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption that theories like IIT, GWT, and HOT are genuine competitors about the same explanandum
- **Sketch:** The argument would show that what look like competing empirical criteria (neural correlates, functional organization, information integration) actually track different phenomena: reportability, behavioral flexibility, and subjective presence. The key premise is that these come apart in cases like blindsight, anesthesia awareness, and split-brain patients in ways that suggest they're measuring different things. Current debates assume theories compete to explain 'consciousness,' but they're actually compatible accounts of distinct but related capacities. The reframing dissolves the criterion problem by showing there's no single right answer because there's no single question.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Dissolving apparent competition by revealing distinct explanatory targets is a powerful move if the empirical dissociations can be demonstrated convincingly.

### U5. Accepting that consciousness theories can be tested empirically commits you to eliminativism about phenomenal consciousness, because empirical testability requires public accessibility that phenomenal properties by definition lack.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Researchers who think consciousness science can vindicate both scientific methodology and traditional phenomenal realism
- **Sketch:** The argument would show that treating consciousness theories as empirically testable requires treating consciousness as occupying a functional role that could be multiply realized and publicly verified. But phenomenal consciousness is traditionally defined precisely by its intrinsic, private, qualitative character—features that make it empirically inaccessible. The key premise is that you cannot both claim phenomenal properties are irreducibly subjective (only accessible from first-person perspective) AND claim we can empirically test which physical systems have them. Either consciousness is whatever plays a certain empirically identifiable functional role (making traditional phenomenal consciousness a myth), or it's genuinely phenomenal but empirically untestable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Forces a stark choice between empirical testability and phenomenal realism that could clarify what's at stake, though the dilemma may admit more positions than presented.

### U6. The philosophical zombie argument inadvertently proves that consciousness theories are empirically indistinguishable: if p-zombies are conceivable, then any evidence that confirms theory T for conscious beings would equally confirm T for p-zombies.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Philosophers and scientists who both (a) think p-zombies are conceivable and (b) believe empirical evidence can discriminate between consciousness theories.
- **Sketch:** If p-zombies are conceivable (as many philosophers grant for sake of argument), then there exists a possible world physically identical to ours but lacking consciousness. In that world, whatever empirical evidence we gather here (neural correlates, behavioral reports, information integration measures) would be identical. Therefore, any evidence-based argument for a consciousness theory in our world would be equally compelling in the p-zombie world—but wrongly so, since ex hypothesi there's no consciousness there. This means proponents of conceivable p-zombies are committed to accepting that empirical evidence cannot adjudicate consciousness theories. The only escape is denying p-zombie conceivability, but many consciousness researchers want to remain neutral on this metaphysical question.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Cleverly turns the zombie argument against empirical testability in a way that forces zombie-sympathizers to confront an uncomfortable commitment.

### U7. Consciousness theories are empirically testable right now, but only through their predictions about when conscious systems will deny having certain experiences.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across the debate that empirical testability requires verifying the presence of consciousness rather than its reported absence
- **Sketch:** The testability problem assumes we need to verify positive reports of consciousness, but we've overlooked that theories make differential predictions about when systems will report absence or confusion about experience. Higher-order theories predict metacognitive failures should eliminate reportability of experiences that still exist; first-order theories predict no such dissociation. Global workspace theories predict that unbroadcast information cannot be reported even if processed; recurrent processing theories make different predictions about reportability during masking. The key premise is that negative reports and performance dissociations constrain theory space as much as positive reports, and we've been ignoring half the data by focusing only on what systems claim to experience rather than what they claim not to experience or cannot report despite processing.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Genuinely constructive proposal that negative evidence has been underutilized; could shift experimental focus if the differential predictions hold up.

### U8. The consciousness theory debate is actually unstable because testability demands converge on illusionism—any theory genuinely constrained by third-person evidence must explain why consciousness seems to exist rather than why it exists.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Realist consciousness theorists who think Global Workspace, Higher-Order Thought, or Recurrent Processing theories can be empirically confirmed while preserving real consciousness
- **Sketch:** Realists about consciousness want theories tested by neural correlates, behavior, and cognitive architecture. But these only constrain theories of how consciousness seems to subjects and what functional roles drive reports about consciousness. The very demand for empirical constraint forces theorists to explain the cognitive mechanisms producing consciousness-claims and consciousness-seemings. Once you've explained those mechanisms completely (as an empirically adequate theory must), positing actual consciousness becomes explanatorily idle. The key premise is that empirical adequacy requires explaining the full causal chain from stimulus to report, and once that explanation is complete, consciousness itself drops out as epiphenomenal or eliminable. Therefore, pursuing empirically testable theories of consciousness leads inexorably to illusionism, whether theorists realize it or not.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Argues that empirical constraint itself drives toward illusionism in a way that could expose hidden commitments, though the inference may be resistible.

### U9. All proposed empirical tests of consciousness theories actually presuppose a first-person/third-person bridge principle that itself depends on which theory is correct, making the testing question circular.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Proponents of empirical methods in consciousness science who believe we can straightforwardly test competing theories
- **Sketch:** Consider any proposed test: adversarial collaboration, neural dissociation studies, predictions about unusual cases. Each requires assuming some principle about when subjective reports reliably track phenomenology, or when neural patterns reliably indicate experience. But these bridge principles are themselves theory-laden. IIT says experience occurs where phi is high even without reports; GWT says reportability is constitutive; HOT says experience requires higher-order representation of the state. The circularity isn't vicious, but it means empirical testing alone cannot adjudicate between theories—we need to first resolve the bridge principle question, which requires non-empirical philosophical argument about the nature of the evidence relation itself.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Bridge-principle circularity is real but widely recognized; doesn't offer novel way forward.

### U10. The right question is not 'which theory is true' but 'at what grain of analysis does consciousness admit of a unified theory,' and the answer may be 'none'—different theories may capture real patterns at incompatible scales.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across the debate that consciousness theories are competing explanations of the same explanandum
- **Sketch:** We assume consciousness is a natural kind with a unified explanation, but this may be a category error. Consider how 'computation' works: there are gate-level, algorithm-level, and functional-level descriptions that don't reduce to each other but aren't competing either. Similarly, phenomenal concepts might pick out patterns that cohere at the millisecond timescale (GWT), others at the structural level (IIT), others at the representational level (HOT). The theories aren't false—they're addressing different grains of the phenomenon. The error is asking 'which is right' rather than 'what is the cross-scale relationship.' This means the epistemological question dissolves: we don't adjudicate between them; we map their domains and bridge principles.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The grain-of-analysis point is interesting but the multi-level computation analogy is familiar; publishable but unlikely to shift the debate.

### U11. The position that we should run adversarial collaborations to test consciousness theories is unstable: it collapses either into stipulating one theory's evidential standards as canonical or into an unworkable pluralism where nothing counts as a definitive test.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Proponents of adversarial collaboration methodology like Dehaene and Tononi who assume we can test theories while remaining neutral about evidential standards
- **Sketch:** Adversarial collaborations require pre-agreed success conditions. But in consciousness, theories disagree about what counts as evidence. If we stipulate behavioral report as the gold standard, we've already favored access-based theories; if we privilege neural markers, we've favored certain neural theories. The collaborator might respond: 'We'll negotiate evidence standards case by case.' But then each 'test' just reveals whether both theories happen to agree about this case—it doesn't adjudicate the deeper disagreement. The position tries to remain neutral about evidential standards while running empirical tests, but you can't do both: you either impose standards (thus begging the question) or accept all standards (thus testing nothing). The apparently moderate methodological stance is actually unstable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Adversarial collaboration critique is sharp but the dilemma may be resolvable; doesn't definitively close the door.

### U12. The fact that consciousness theories make identical predictions about first-person reports proves they are addressing different explananda, not that they are empirically equivalent.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The widespread view that consciousness theories face an empirical underdetermination problem (Chalmers, Dennett, Schwitzgebel, etc.)
- **Sketch:** Most philosophers treat convergence on behavioral/report predictions as showing consciousness theories are empirically indistinguishable. But this reasoning assumes that first-person reports are theory-neutral data that any correct theory must accommodate. Instead, what counts as the relevant first-person datum is itself theory-laden: a higher-order theory treats 'I am in pain' as data about meta-representations, while first-order theories treat it as data about pain states plus language. The theories predict the same utterances while addressing different questions about what those utterances are evidence of. This explains why no amount of report-based testing resolves the debate—the theories slice up the explanandum space differently.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The theory-ladenness of data is a recognized issue; this application is competent but the basic move is familiar from philosophy of science.

### U13. Testing consciousness theories requires accepting that some conscious experiences are undetectable in principle, which means the demand for empirical testability is self-undermining.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Philosophers demanding empirical criteria for theory choice (Schwitzgebel, Irvine, Farrell) and consciousness science programs claiming measurable signatures
- **Sketch:** Any empirically adequate consciousness theory must account for cases like locked-in syndrome, anesthesia awareness, and potential AI consciousness—cases where we worry about consciousness existing without behavioral markers. This means the theory must allow that consciousness could be present without any evidence we can access. But if consciousness can exist without evidence, then any theory compatible with all our evidence might be false (consciousness might be distributed differently than the theory predicts). Therefore, the very condition that makes a consciousness theory adequate (accounting for evidence-transcendent consciousness) is what makes it untestable. The demand for testable consciousness theories collapses because testability requires evidence-response coupling that consciousness cases specifically problematize.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Points to a real tension between testability and accounting for undetectable consciousness, though the self-undermining claim may overreach.

For instance, Integrated Information Theory predicts subjects should report discrete phenomenal 'atoms' that can't be further decomposed, while Global Workspace Theory predicts subjects should report a unified broadcast state. Higher-order theories predict subjects can't reliably distinguish between having an experience and having a thought about that experience, while first-order theories predict this distinction should be reportable.
