### U15. The only empirically tractable approach to consciousness theories is reverse: instead of testing which theory is true, we should identify which interventions reliably produce or eliminate consciousness, then define consciousness ostensively as whatever those interventions affect.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The standard theory-first methodology where we propose definitions/theories of consciousness and then seek evidence for them
- **Sketch:** Rather than starting with theories and looking for confirming evidence, we should start with robust interventions we can all agree affect consciousness: general anesthesia reliably eliminates it, waking up reliably restores it, certain brain stimulations reliably produce specific experiences. We then say: consciousness is whatever property these interventions manipulate. This gives us an ostensive definition grounded in causal handles rather than phenomenal or functional definitions. Theories become valuable insofar as they predict new interventions or explain why our existing interventions work. The key premise is that we have more agreement about what causally affects consciousness than about what consciousness is, so we should build up from the former rather than down from the latter.

### U16. The search for consciousness's neural correlates systematically confuses the detection problem with the constitution problem, and solving the former cannot even in principle constrain answers to the latter.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The mainstream consciousness science paradigm that treats NCC-discovery as theory-constraining (Crick & Koch's original program and its descendants)
- **Sketch:** We conflate two questions: what allows us to detect/attribute consciousness (detection) versus what consciousness is made of (constitution). Finding NCCs tells us what reliably indicates consciousness in creatures we've already decided are conscious, but this is circular—we can't use detection tools to answer constitution questions. An analogy: discovering that smoke detects fire doesn't tell us what fire is constituted by. The entire research program of finding 'the NCC' assumes that whatever correlates most tightly with consciousness must constitute it, but this assumes away the hard problem. We could have perfect detection (always correctly identifying conscious states from neural activity) while remaining completely ignorant of what makes those states conscious. The programs diverge completely: detection could be solved pragmatically/behaviorally while constitution remains metaphysically open.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The detection/constitution distinction is important and the argument is clear, but this ground has been covered in discussions of the hard problem.

### U17. The methodological assumption that simpler conscious creatures provide evidential constraint on theories of human consciousness reverses the actual epistemic order: we can only identify which creatures are conscious by first having the correct theory.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The methodological assumption underlying comparative consciousness studies and 'minimal consciousness' research programs
- **Sketch:** Consciousness research typically assumes we can test theories by examining simpler systems (insects, organoids, minimal networks) where we can track all the variables. But we face a prior question: which of these systems are conscious? The standard move is to say the theory tells us—if the system satisfies IIT/GWT/etc., it's conscious. But this means we're not testing the theory against independent evidence; we're using the theory to generate the evidence pool. The apparent methodological progression from simple to complex actually disguises a conceptual circle. We can only treat simple systems as evidential if we already know which ones are conscious, but we need a theory to tell us that. The dispute is thus not straightforwardly empirical but concerns which theoretical virtues (simplicity, conservativeness, fit with intuition) should govern theory-choice in the absence of independent empirical constraint. We've mistaken a conceptual/pragmatic choice for an empirical discovery.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The circularity in using simple systems is real but the point about epistemic order has been made in debates about animal consciousness.

### U18. Accepting that unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated commits you to the view that no third-person functional or neural evidence could ever distinguish conscious from unconscious processing, only first-person reports can—but this makes consciousness theories unfalsifiable.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Theorists like Dehaene and Tononi who both accept sophisticated unconscious processing and claim their theories are empirically testable
- **Sketch:** Many consciousness researchers accept that unconscious processes can be highly complex, perform elaborate computations, and even exhibit integration and global broadcasting (evidenced by blindsight, unconscious priming, etc.). The argument shows that if we grant this—if there's no computational or functional operation that's necessarily conscious—then any third-person evidence (neural patterns, behavioral capacities, information integration) is compatible with both conscious and unconscious processing. The key premise is that this commits theorists to relying exclusively on first-person reports to determine what was conscious, but reports themselves are just another functional capacity. This means the only real evidence becomes subjects saying they were conscious, which makes the theory unfalsifiable: if the predicted neural signature appears without report, we say the subject was unconscious; if it appears with report, we say they were conscious. The theory can't be wrong because the only arbiter is the explanandum itself.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies a genuine problem about sophisticated unconscious processing undermining third-person evidence, though the unfalsifiability conclusion may be too strong.

### U19. The apparent empirical dispute between consciousness theories conceals a deeper disagreement about whether consciousness is metaphysically determinate—most consciousness theories presuppose determinacy, but testing them actually requires assuming indeterminacy.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Both sides of debates about consciousness in non-paradigm cases (AI, animals, organoids) who assume there's a fact of the matter while disagreeing about what it is
- **Sketch:** The argument draws a distinction between theories that presuppose consciousness has sharp boundaries (either something is conscious or it isn't) and the methodology required to test these theories, which actually requires treating consciousness as coming in degrees or being vague. When we design experiments, we need graded measures, probability distributions, and statistical thresholds—we're forced to ask 'how conscious' something is or 'what's the probability of consciousness.' The key premise is that this isn't just methodological convenience but reveals something mandatory: any testable theory needs to make graded predictions to be statistically assessable. But most theories (IIT with its phi values aside) are formulated as categorical claims about the presence/absence of consciousness. This means testing them requires translating categorical claims into graded ones, and there's no principled way to do this translation. The metaphysical assumption of determinacy makes theories empirically intractable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The determinacy/gradedness tension is real and underexplored, but the argument needs tightening about whether this is truly mandatory.

### U20. The search for tests between consciousness theories fails because it targets the wrong asymmetry: the real empirical challenge isn't explaining why some processes are conscious while others aren't, but why consciousness feels like it has features (unity, subjectivity, phenomenal properties) that no physical process exhibits.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The shared assumption across consciousness science that the hard problem can be dissolved or addressed by identifying distinctive functional or neural signatures
- **Sketch:** Current theory-testing methodology focuses on identifying the neural correlates or functional properties that distinguish conscious from unconscious processing—essentially treating consciousness as a discriminable state among others. The argument provides a counterexample to the implicit premise that this is what needs explaining: even if we perfectly predicted which processes correlate with consciousness reports, we wouldn't have explained why those processes feel unified, private, or qualitative. The key premise is that every current consciousness theory treats certain features of experience (unity, subjectivity, qualia) as if they were physical or functional properties to be predicted, but these features are actually category mistakes—they're not properties of processes but of perspectives. The real explanatory challenge is the asymmetry between perspectival features and process features, not the asymmetry between conscious and unconscious processes. This means all current theories, despite their differences, share a mistaken assumption about what makes consciousness puzzling.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Distinguishes correlate-finding from explaining phenomenal features, but the hard problem framing is well-trodden and the 'category mistake' move needs more development to advance debate.

### U21. Consciousness theory disputes are irresolvable because they equivocate between creature consciousness (what systems are conscious) and state consciousness (what makes particular states conscious), and any evidence for one interpretation is compatible with rival views on the other interpretation.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Theorists who frame IIT, GWT, HOT, and recurrent processing theories as competing explanations of the same phenomenon
- **Sketch:** Current debates treat consciousness theories as competing answers to a single question, but they're actually answering different questions that get conflated. IIT primarily identifies which systems are conscious (creature-level) by measuring integrated information, while Higher-Order Thought theory primarily explains what makes particular mental states conscious (state-level) by their meta-representational properties. When we try to test these theories, evidence that seems decisive at one level is reinterpretable at the other: neural correlates of reportable states (supporting HOT) don't tell us which systems have states to report about (IIT's question), while measures of system integration (supporting IIT) don't tell us which of that system's states are conscious (HOT's question). The key premise is that creature and state consciousness are logically independent—a system could integrate information without its states being meta-represented, or meta-represent states without being an integrated system.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The creature/state distinction is recognized in the literature; showing evidential reinterpretability is useful but won't fundamentally reorient the debate.

### U22. The falsifiability of consciousness theories requires that we could be wrong about our own consciousness, but accepting this possibility undermines the Cartesian certainty that grounds the explanandum, creating a methodological paradox where consciousness science must simultaneously affirm and deny first-person authority.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The methodological assumption shared by Chalmers, Dennett, and empirical consciousness researchers that we can maintain both the reality/certainty of consciousness and the empirical testability of theories about it
- **Sketch:** Scientists insist consciousness theories must be empirically testable—that neural/computational evidence could show IIT or GWT is wrong. But testability requires that our consciousness attributions could be mistaken, including about ourselves. Yet consciousness science treats first-person reports as the gold standard precisely because of their supposed incorrigibility: I cannot be wrong that I am conscious now. This creates a counterexample to the shared assumption that we can have both scientific testability and privileged first-person access. If I could be wrong about being conscious (required for falsifiability), then we lose the secure explanandum that justifies preferring consciousness theories over eliminativism. If I cannot be wrong (preserving the explanandum), then theories accommodating my reports are unfalsifiable. The key premise is that the asymmetry between first- and third-person access that makes consciousness scientifically interesting is the same asymmetry that makes consciousness theories empirically underdetermined.
- **Appraiser (blind):** First-person authority paradox is real but the field has various responses; doesn't definitively show instability.

### U23. The demand for empirical testability of consciousness theories presupposes epiphenomenalism, which most consciousness researchers explicitly reject.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The methodological consensus in consciousness science that treats neural correlates as the primary empirical constraint, shared by IIT, GWT, HOT, and other competing theories
- **Sketch:** The standard methodology asks 'what observable predictions distinguish theory A from theory B?' But if consciousness has causal powers (non-epiphenomenalism), then different theories of what consciousness IS should predict different behaviors, not just different neural correlates. The fact that we treat 'same behavior, different neural substrate' as a meaningful test scenario reveals we're tacitly assuming consciousness doesn't affect behavior—otherwise changing what consciousness is would change what the system does. This means either: (1) we abandon the causal efficacy of consciousness, (2) we admit current testing methods are fundamentally misconceived, or (3) we accept that theories making identical behavioral predictions but differing only in 'what it's like' are genuinely empirically indistinguishable—which would make consciousness theory selection a matter of theoretical virtues, not empirical test.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Points to an inconsistency between causal efficacy commitments and testing practices, but the epiphenomenalism worry is somewhat familiar.

---

### U24. The criterion 'theory T is right if T's predicted neural correlates match actual neural correlates' assumes consciousness is realized by but not reducible to neural states—a metaphysical position that rules out both strong reductionism and strong emergentism before empirical testing begins.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The neural correlates of consciousness (NCC) research program, treated as theory-neutral by researchers across different theoretical commitments including reductionists like Crick and Emergentists like Searle
- **Sketch:** If consciousness just IS certain neural activity (strong reductionism), then finding that consciousness correlates with those states is trivial—we've discovered consciousness correlates with itself. The correlation is only informative if consciousness is something over and above the neural correlate. But if consciousness strongly emerges from neural states with novel causal powers, then the neural correlate is insufficient—we need to test the emergent properties directly, not just their base. The neural-correlate methodology only makes sense on a specific middle position: realization/constitution without reduction. This commits researchers to a metaphysical view (nonreductive physicalism) that's highly controversial and that many theories explicitly reject. Either the methodology is metaphysically biased, or finding neural correlates can't actually confirm theories because it's neutral between incompatible metaphysical pictures.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Correctly identifies that neural-correlate methodology presupposes nonreductive physicalism, but this observation alone won't change research practice.

---

### U25. The 'hard problem' is not a problem about consciousness but a problem about theory-selection criteria: we have no principled way to decide whether explanatory gaps count as theory failures or merely as limits of a certain kind of explanation.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The framing of the hard problem as a metaphysical question about the relationship between physical and phenomenal properties, shared across the Chalmers-Dennett-Frankish debates
- **Sketch:** Current debate treats the hard problem as metaphysical: can physical facts explain phenomenal facts? But the real action is epistemological: should an adequate theory of consciousness leave no explanatory gap, or is it sufficient that a theory correctly predicts/systematizes the phenomena even if it doesn't make them intuitively 'make sense'? Physics doesn't explain why mass curves spacetime in a way that removes all sense of mystery, yet we accept GTR. The hard problem dissolves into a question about what counts as adequate explanation. This reclassifies the positions: type-A materialists think gap-closure is required and achievable; type-B materialists think it's not required (theories can be right despite gaps); illusionists think it's required but only achievable by denying the phenomena; dualists think it's required but impossible physically. The dispute isn't about consciousness's nature but about epistemic standards for theory adequacy, which suggests we need philosophy of science to settle consciousness science—not neuroscience or metaphysics.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Reclassifying hard problem as about explanatory standards is valuable but not entirely novel; the typology is useful.

---

### U26. Treating substrate-independence as a criterion for evaluating consciousness theories is unstable: it collapses into either question-begging functionalism or abandons the possibility of empirical theory adjudication entirely.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Computational and functionalist approaches that use substrate-independence as a criterion for theory evaluation
- **Sketch:** Many consciousness researchers use substrate-independence as a theoretical virtue: good theories shouldn't privilege biological neurons. But this criterion either already assumes functionalism is true (making it question-begging against biological theories) or it's meant as a methodological constraint independent of truth. If the latter, then on what grounds? The only non-question-begging justification would be epistemic: we have better empirical access to functional organization than to intrinsic physical properties. But this justification proves too much—it makes the choice of consciousness theory turn entirely on our epistemic limitations rather than on what consciousness actually is, undermining the very idea that empirical evidence tracks truth about consciousness. The instability is that substrate-independence is either substantive (and question-begging) or methodological (and undermines empirical realism).
- **Appraiser (blind):** Exposes instability in substrate-independence as a criterion, but the dilemma needs sharper development to force methodological change.

---

### U27. Any theory that claims to explain consciousness rather than merely correlate with it commits you to pan-experientialism, because the only way to avoid brute psychophysical laws is to make consciousness constitutively built into the physical base.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Functionalist theories (GWT, HOT) that claim explanatory status while rejecting panpsychism, and physicalists who reject both panpsychism and explanatory gaps
- **Sketch:** Genuine explanation requires showing how the explanandum follows from more basic facts. For consciousness, we either accept brute bridging laws (physicalism with nomological danglers) or we make experience constitutive of the physical. The key premise: if neural activity N generates experience E as a further fact about N, that's a brute correlation, not an explanation. The only way N could genuinely explain E is if E is part of what N fundamentally is—if the intrinsic nature of the physical is itself experiential. This means functionalists like Dennett and GWT theorists who claim their view explains consciousness (not just correlates) are unwittingly committed to a Russellian pan-experientialism. Those who avoid this commitment (like Chalmers) must admit they're only mapping correlations, not explaining.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The argument that genuine explanation requires pan-experientialism is provocative but the explanation/correlation distinction needs more careful handling.

---

### U28. First-person and third-person methods cannot both be reliable guides to consciousness because they track different natural kinds: first-person methods track phenomenality while third-person methods track cognitive access, and assuming these coincide is what makes consciousness seem unmeasurable.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Integrated research programs assuming consciousness is unitary (like adversarial collaboration frameworks) and theorists who treat access/phenomenal distinction as merely conceptual rather than marking real kinds
- **Sketch:** The measurability problem assumes consciousness is one thing that both introspection and neuroscience should converge on. But introspective reports reliably track what Block calls phenomenal consciousness (what it's like), while neuroscience and behavioral tests reliably track access consciousness (availability for report and control). These come apart in cases like inattentional blindness, blindsight, and potentially during anesthesia. The current debate treats divergences between methods as measurement error, but this reclassifies them: they're measuring different real phenomena. Neither method is 'wrong'—they're correctly tracking distinct kinds. This means IIT is a theory of phenomenal consciousness, GWT is a theory of access consciousness, and the debate about which is 'right' rests on failing to see they're theories of different explananda.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Access/phenomenal tracking different kinds is known; needs more to show this dissolves rather than deepens the problem.

---

### U29. Consciousness theories are untestable not because we lack good methods, but because researchers systematically confuse what would confirm their theory with what would motivate someone to accept it.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The shared assumption across Global Workspace Theory, IIT, Higher-Order Thought theories, etc. that we just need better empirical methods or predictions
- **Sketch:** The argument distinguishes between evidential confirmation and dialectical persuasion. When consciousness researchers propose tests, they typically specify what would make them accept theory X over theory Y. But this conflates two questions: what evidence bears on X's truth, versus what would rationally compel X's opponents to switch positions. For instance, IIT proponents might say 'finding consciousness in cerebellum would refute IIT,' but this just identifies what would make them abandon IIT—not what actually tests it. The key premise is that in mature sciences, these come apart: we can specify evidence that would confirm heliocentrism even for someone who had independent reasons to resist it. Consciousness research lacks this because the theories themselves determine what counts as the relevant evidence.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Distinguishing confirmation from persuasion is conceptually useful but the diagnosis may be too general to drive specific methodological reforms.

---

### U30. First-person report methodology forces consciousness theories into a self-sealing structure: if reports match predictions, the theory is confirmed; if reports diverge from predictions, subjects are confabulating or misunderstanding their experience, making empirical falsification logically impossible.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The methodological assumption shared across consciousness research that combining third-person measures with first-person reports can produce empirically testable theories
- **Sketch:** This targets the shared methodological assumption that first-person reports provide neutral evidence. The argument presents cases from actual consciousness research where theories made predictions about what subjects should experience, subjects reported otherwise, and researchers concluded the subjects were wrong about their own experiences rather than that the theory was wrong. For example, when change blindness research predicted subjects shouldn't be conscious of changing features (given attention theories), but subjects reported noticing them, researchers posited 'partial awareness' or 'sensory memory not real consciousness.' The key premise is that this move is always available: any mismatch between predicted and reported experience can be explained as subjects' mischaracterization rather than theory failure. Since this interpretive flexibility is built into the methodology, no first-person report can actually falsify consciousness theories. But first-person reports are our primary evidence. Therefore, the theories are empirically unfalsifiable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Self-sealing structure is concerning but examples need careful examination; may be resolvable with better protocols.

---

### U31. Theories of consciousness are empirically testable only if they make predictions about when consciousness ceases to exist, not just about which systems possess it.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The common methodological assumption shared by Chalmers, Dennett, and others that empirical testing primarily involves checking whether systems with certain functional profiles exhibit consciousness
- **Sketch:** Current methodological discussions focus on which systems are conscious (integrated information, global workspace, higher-order theories all make claims about this). But there's an asymmetry: we can never be certain a system IS conscious (other minds problem remains), but we could potentially be certain a system ISN'T conscious if we had a theory that predicted consciousness's absence and we could verify no relevant capacities remain. The argument turns on the key premise that falsification requires not just failed positive predictions but successful negative predictions. This means the real empirical bite comes from theories that specify necessary conditions and predict their absence, not from theories that merely expand the circle of conscious beings.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Valid methodological point about falsification asymmetry, but the insight about negative predictions is somewhat familiar from philosophy of science generally.

---

### U32. Accepting that theories of consciousness can be confirmed by studying consciousness in other possible worlds commits one to either modal rationalism or to the view that consciousness science cannot be naturalistic.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Philosophers who simultaneously embrace naturalistic methodology while relying on conceivability arguments about consciousness
- **Sketch:** Many philosophers (Chalmers, Frankish, etc.) appeal to conceivability arguments, zombie thought experiments, and inverted qualia scenarios—all essentially claims about what's possible in other worlds. But confirming what's possible in other worlds requires either (a) a priori rational insight into modal facts (modal rationalism), which most naturalists reject, or (b) empirical access to other possible worlds, which is impossible. The argument's key premise is that there's no third option: you can't be both a naturalist (rejecting a priori metaphysics) and treat modal claims as evidentially relevant to consciousness without contradiction.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Competent dilemma about modal epistemology, but this territory is well-trodden in debates about conceivability arguments and naturalism.

---

### U33. Any theory of consciousness that relies on 'access consciousness' to validate its claims about 'phenomenal consciousness' is methodologically unstable because it must either collapse the distinction or abandon empirical testability.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Theorists like Block, Rosenthal, and others who maintain the access/phenomenal distinction while also claiming their theories are empirically testable
- **Sketch:** Theories regularly use the access/phenomenal distinction (Block) to defend themselves: they claim to be about phenomenal consciousness but test their theories using access consciousness (reports, discrimination, memory). However, if access reliably tracks phenomenal consciousness, the distinction does no work—why posit two properties that perfectly covary? But if access doesn't reliably track phenomenal consciousness, then all our 'evidence' for theories is actually evidence about something else, making the theories empirically untestable. The key premise is that there's no stable middle ground: either the distinction matters (and undermines testability) or it doesn't matter (and the distinction is idle).
- **Appraiser (blind):** Access/phenomenal instability is real but the dilemma may have middle ground; needs tighter argument.

### U34. The project of testing consciousness theories presupposes that consciousness has a uniform nature across cases, but our only evidence for this presupposition would come from already having confirmed a theory—making the entire enterprise question-begging.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The shared methodological assumption across all theorists (IIT, GWT, HOT, etc.) that they're seeking THE theory of consciousness rather than theories of consciousness-kinds
- **Sketch:** Every proposed test assumes that if theory T is right, it's right for all instances of consciousness—my visual experiences, your pain, a bat's echolocation experience all have the same underlying nature. But what if consciousness is like jade—multiple distinct natural kinds that we group under one folk concept? We have no pre-theoretical reason to expect uniformity; the only way to confirm uniformity would be to test and confirm a single theory across cases. But we can't confirm a theory IS correct across cases without already assuming what we need to prove: that there's one thing to be right about. The key premise is that our intuition that consciousness is uniform is just that—an intuition—not something we can know independently of having a confirmed theory.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The jade analogy is apt and the circularity real, but the uniformity assumption has been questioned before; competent but not groundbreaking.

### U35. The demand for empirical criteria to adjudicate between consciousness theories presupposes that consciousness theories are causal-explanatory hypotheses, but most theories of consciousness are actually normative proposals about which physical systems we ought to grant moral status to.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across Chalmers, Dennett, Koch, Dehaene, and Tononi that consciousness science is continuous with other empirical sciences
- **Sketch:** The argument turns on examining what practitioners actually do when defending consciousness theories versus what they claim to do. When IIT proponents defend phi as the measure of consciousness, their arguments bottom out in intuitions about which systems 'deserve' to be called conscious rather than predictions about behavior or function. Similarly, when GWT theorists exclude feed-forward networks from consciousness, they're not making a discovery but a stipulation. The key premise is that if these were genuinely empirical theories, proponents would be willing to accept empirically absurd conclusions (like IIT's grid being more conscious than humans), but instead they treat such results as reductios. This reveals the theories are actually encoding ethical intuitions about moral considerability while wearing the costume of neuroscience.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The normative-disguised-as-empirical point has been made; the reductio observation is interesting but not sufficiently developed to be transformative.

### U36. The undetectable-zombie scenario is treated as showing that consciousness is empirically inaccessible, but it actually shows the opposite: if zombies are empirically indistinguishable from conscious beings, then our concept of consciousness fails to carve nature at the joints, which means the right theory of consciousness is eliminativism.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Both dualists like Chalmers who use zombies to argue for non-physical consciousness and physicalists like Frankish who accept zombies are conceivable but think this is unproblematic
- **Sketch:** Philosophers use zombie conceivability to argue that consciousness transcends the physical or that we need something beyond third-person methods. But this gets the dialectic backwards. In every other domain, if our concept allows for empirically indistinguishable cases that supposedly differ in their fundamental nature, we conclude the concept is confused or that the supposed distinction is bogus (phlogiston, vital force, etc.). The key premise is a parity argument: consciousness should not get special epistemic treatment. If zombie scenarios show that consciousness could be absent with no empirical difference, this is a reductio of consciousness-realism, not a reductio of physicalism. The correct response is to eliminate the concept of consciousness in favor of the empirically tractable phenomena (reportability, integration, metacognition, etc.) that we can actually study. This counterexample targets the shared assumption that zombie conceivability poses a problem for physicalist theories rather than for consciousness-talk itself.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The parity argument is interesting and the reversal clever, but eliminativism as response to zombie arguments has been defended before.

### U37. Any theory of consciousness that cannot explain why it cannot be empirically confirmed or disconfirmed is thereby disconfirmed.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Both sides of the physicalism/dualism debate who treat the 'hard problem' as revealing something about consciousness rather than revealing something about their own theories
- **Sketch:** Meta-epistemological helplessness about consciousness is not a neutral datum but a constraint that consciousness theories themselves must explain. If functionalism is true, we should be able to tell whether something is conscious by examining its functional organization—yet functionalists admit we cannot. If phenomenal realism is true, there should be some epistemic story about why private qualia are nevertheless trackable—yet phenomenal realists typically embrace the explanatory gap. The argument turns on this premise: a theory that entails its own unverifiability has thereby provided evidence against itself, because correct theories should predict the epistemic situation we actually find ourselves in. Any adequate theory must explain not just what consciousness IS, but why our epistemic access to it has exactly the structure it does.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The self-defeat argument is valid and the meta-epistemological demand reasonable, but similar reflexive requirements have been discussed in epistemology of consciousness.

### U38. If consciousness is real, then there must be facts about its distribution that are both determinate and unknowable to us; this combination is unstable and collapses either into eliminativism or mysterianism, revealing that moderate physicalism about consciousness is not a stable position.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The 'moderate physicalist' position held by most consciousness researchers who think consciousness is physical and scientifically tractable while acknowledging we currently cannot solve the hard problem
- **Sketch:** Moderate physicalists (most working consciousness scientists) want to affirm both that consciousness is a real physical phenomenon and that we can study it scientifically. But consider: either facts about consciousness distribution are determinate (there is a fact about whether this fetus/AI/organoid is conscious) or indeterminate. If indeterminate, consciousness is not robustly real—it's more like 'being a heap.' If determinate, either we can know these facts or we cannot. If we can know them, show me the method—but there isn't one forthcoming. If we cannot know them (which is where we are), then we're positing a realm of determinate but unknowable facts, which is the mysterian position moderate physicalists reject. The argument turns on the premise that you cannot coherently claim something is both a determinate physical fact and systematically epistemically inaccessible.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Determinate-but-unknowable instability is interesting but moderate physicalists have responses; doesn't definitively collapse position.

### U39. Any empirical test that could adjudicate between consciousness theories must rely on conscious reports, but accepting conscious reports as evidential commits you to the very functionalist assumptions that non-functionalist theories deny.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Non-functionalist theories like IIT that claim to make testable predictions while denying that consciousness necessarily involves reportability or cognitive access
- **Sketch:** Consider how we'd actually test predictions from IIT versus GNW in practice: we need dependent variables, which inevitably involve subjects reporting experiences or exhibiting report-linked behaviors. But treating these reports as evidence about consciousness requires assuming that certain functional states (being disposed to report, forming metacognitive judgments) are reliably connected to consciousness. This is precisely what non-functionalist theories deny—they claim consciousness could exist without these functional signatures. The argument shows that the experimental methodology itself presupposes functionalism. Non-functionalists face a dilemma: either accept that reports track consciousness (contradicting their theory) or reject reports as evidence (making their theory untestable).
- **Appraiser (blind):** Correctly identifies that experimental methodology presupposes functionalist assumptions, but this circularity concern has been raised in various forms before.

### U40. The position that consciousness science can remain neutral between realist and instrumentalist interpretations of consciousness theories is unstable—it collapses into eliminativism about consciousness as a natural kind.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Methodological agnostics like Crick and Koch's 'astonishing hypothesis' approach and modern consciousness science's claimed methodological naturalism
- **Sketch:** Many consciousness researchers adopt what they see as a safe methodological stance: we can test theories, refine predictions, build models, while remaining neutral about whether consciousness is 'real' or whether theories are tracking an actual phenomenon versus just organizing behavioral/neural data. The argument shows this collapses: if you're genuinely neutral about whether there's a phenomenon being tracked, you cannot justify theory choice on evidential grounds rather than purely pragmatic ones. The moment you say 'theory T is right because the evidence supports it,' you've abandoned neutrality—you're claiming the evidence reveals something about consciousness itself. Maintaining true neutrality means treating consciousness talk as merely instrumental, which is just eliminativism with extra steps. The unstable middle position tries to have empirical discovery without an object of discovery.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Shows that methodological neutrality collapses into eliminativism; the argument is sound but the instability of instrumentalism is a familiar philosophical point.

### U41. First-person experimental protocols (like meditation training studies or Dennett's heterophenomenology) cannot adjudicate consciousness theories because they face an unnoticed calibration problem: we have no theory-neutral way to determine if subjects are becoming better reporters or changing what they're conscious of.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Both neurophenomenology advocates like Varela and Lutz, and analytical phenomenologists like Bayne who think refined first-person methods can constrain theory
- **Sketch:** Advocates across the spectrum argue that sufficiently sophisticated first-person methods could resolve theoretical disputes—meditators making finer discriminations, subjects trained in phenomenological reflection, etc. This assumes improved first-person methodology gives us better data about consciousness. But this conflates two possibilities: (1) training makes subjects conscious of features that were always present but unreported, versus (2) training changes the structure of consciousness itself. Without a prior theory of consciousness, we cannot distinguish these. Yet both options presuppose answers to the very theoretical questions at issue. If consciousness is HOT-theory-style, training might create new conscious states. If it's IIT-style, training might just improve access to pre-existing integrated information. The calibration problem: our measuring instrument (first-person reports) is necessarily entangled with the phenomenon measured.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Better-reporter vs. changed-consciousness calibration problem is real but may not be fatal to first-person methods.

### U42. Commitment to the possibility of testing consciousness theories requires accepting that consciousness has essential bridge laws connecting it to physical states, which commits you to a priori physicalism—making the empirical testing circular.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The view held by many consciousness scientists that empirical work is metaphysically neutral and can discover whether physicalism is true
- **Sketch:** Testing theories empirically requires that we can move between evidence (physical/functional states) and conclusions about consciousness. This requires bridge principles: 'when physical state P obtains, consciousness state C obtains.' But these bridges can't themselves be empirically discovered without prior bridges, generating regress. They must be knowable a priori or stipulated. If stipulated, theory choice isn't empirical discovery. If a priori knowable, we've accepted that there are necessary connections between physical and phenomenal—which is a substantive metaphysical claim that rules out logical possibility of zombies, inverted qualia, etc. The argument shows that taking consciousness science as genuinely empirical (not mere stipulation) commits you to rejecting the conceivability arguments before you start. The methodology presupposes physicalism rather than discovering it.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The bridge law regress is real and the circularity valid, but this is recognizable from debates about psychophysical laws and the explanatory gap.

### U43. Any experimental paradigm that could actually distinguish consciousness theories must rely on subjects' reports about their experiences, but relying on reports presupposes a global workspace architecture for accessing and reporting mental states, therefore no experiment can adjudicate between global workspace theory and its rivals without begging the question.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The methodology of contrastive analysis and neural correlates research that treats itself as theory-neutral
- **Sketch:** The argument starts from the observation that all proposed experimental tests—contrastive analysis, perturbational complexity, no-report paradigms—ultimately rely on either direct reports or behavioral indicators that presuppose reportability-in-principle. Even 'no-report' paradigms use report-independent measures of attention or memory, but these capacities themselves require global availability of information. When we use a subject's report of seeing versus not seeing as our ground truth for consciousness, we're implicitly treating global accessibility as definitive of consciousness. This means experiments aren't neutral arbiters—they're designed in a way that builds in GWT's core claim that consciousness requires global availability. A theory like IIT, which allows for consciousness without access, cannot be confirmed by methods that treat access as the mark of consciousness. The commitment works both ways: if you accept report-based methodology, you've already accepted that consciousness requires the kind of broadcasting GWT describes, making the experimental comparison circular.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Report-methodology presupposing GWT is concerning but 'no-report' paradigms may provide more independence than claimed.

### U44. First-person/third-person asymmetry is not the obstacle to testing consciousness theories; rather, the obstacle is that consciousness theories are unfalsifiable-in-practice because they can always relocate consciousness to whatever neural correlates we fail to manipulate.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Both mysterian anti-physicalists and confident physicalists who agree that the hard problem is about first/third person asymmetry
- **Sketch:** The standard view is that consciousness is special because of the epistemic gap between first and third-person access. But consider: when we lesion area A and consciousness persists, GWT can say 'the workspace relocated to area B'; when we disrupt feedback connections and consciousness persists, higher-order theory can say 'there are alternative feedback routes'; when we reduce phi and consciousness persists, IIT can say 'we miscalculated which complex had maximal phi'. The key premise is that these moves are no different from how phlogiston theory relocated phlogiston, or how Ptolemaic astronomy added epicycles. The problem isn't metaphysical (subjectivity) but methodological (unfalsifiability). This explains why even materialist consciousness theories remain untestable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Competent point about unfalsifiability but the phlogiston/epicycle analogy is familiar and the diagnosis not particularly novel.

### U45. We can only test which theory of consciousness is right by testing them on artificial systems where we can build in the relevant mechanisms, but doing so commits us to accepting that any system with those mechanisms is conscious—making the test self-defeating for those who believe consciousness requires biological substrate.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Researchers like Seth or Dehaene who advocate for testing theories on artificial systems while remaining agnostic about machine consciousness
- **Sketch:** Many propose that the way forward is building artificial systems that satisfy different theories' criteria and seeing which ones are conscious. But the test only works if we can determine whether the artificial system is conscious—and if we accept it is conscious when it meets Theory T's criteria, we've already presupposed T is correct. The key premise is sharper though: the only evidence we could have that the artificial system is conscious would be that it satisfies the theoretical criteria, but this makes the test circular. The alternative—requiring biological substrate—means artificial tests can never work. So either the test is circular or biological theories are untestable. Either way, holding that consciousness theories are testable commits you to positions you likely reject.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies a real tension but the circularity problem with artificial systems has been noted; limited new insight.

### U46. The demand for 'getting the extension right' in consciousness science is incoherent because consciousness lacks borderline cases in the right way—instead it has systematic ambiguity about which dimension we're measuring.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Theorists like Schwitzgebel and Birch who frame theory-choice around correctly handling borderline cases and graded consciousness
- **Sketch:** Philosophers often frame theory-choice as 'which theory correctly classifies borderline cases (foetuses, split-brains, AIs)?' This assumes consciousness has vague boundaries like 'bald.' But these 'borderline cases' aren't dimensionally similar to clear cases but informationally impoverished—we lack access to whether there's something-it's-like because we can't occupy their perspective. The appearance of borderline cases emerges from evidential limitations, not metaphysical vagueness. What looks like 'getting the extension right' is actually choosing which behavioral/functional proxy to privilege when first-person evidence is unavailable. The key premise is that genuine borderline cases would admit of precisification along the same dimension as clear cases, but alleged consciousness borderline cases introduce new dimensions (degrees of integration vs. degrees of access vs. degrees of self-representation).
- **Appraiser (blind):** Interesting distinction between vagueness and evidential limitation, but unclear how it changes the practical debate about theory-choice.

### U47. Higher-order theories and first-order theories are not actually competitors because they solve inverse problems: HOT explains how consciousness becomes epistemically accessible to subjects, while first-order theories explain what makes states have phenomenal character, and both are required for a complete account.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The framing of the HOT vs. first-order debate as a genuine competition, as seen in Lau, Rosenthal, Block, and Lamme
- **Sketch:** The debate treats these as rival explanations of consciousness, with neural evidence meant to crown a winner. But examine their actual explanatory achievements: first-order theories (like recurrent processing) explain why certain neural states have qualitative character—why red looks like *that*. Higher-order theories explain how subjects come to know they're in conscious states—the structure of self-awareness. These are different explananda with a natural dependency order: you need phenomenal character (first-order) before there's anything to be aware of (higher-order). Neural evidence supports both because both are correct about different aspects. The apparent competition emerges from conflating 'what consciousness is' with 'what makes consciousness accessible to introspection.' The key premise is that phenomenal character and epistemic accessibility are distinct explanandum requiring different sufficient conditions.
- **Appraiser (blind):** HOT/first-order solving different problems is plausible but whether both are 'correct' needs more argument about explanatory completeness.

### U48. The reason we cannot adjudicate consciousness theories is that they make the same predictions about all measurable outcomes, but differ only in their metaphysical overlay—what they say consciousness 'is' given identical functional profiles.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The assumption that competing consciousness theories make empirically distinguishable predictions, targeted at researchers who think more data will settle the debate
- **Sketch:** Consider that GNW, HOT, IIT, and recurrent processing theories all predict similar patterns: reportability, integration, attention, specific neural signatures. Their apparent disagreement dissolves when we see they're attaching different metaphysical interpretations to the same functional architecture. GNW says 'global broadcasting IS consciousness,' HOT says 'higher-order representation OF those states is consciousness,' IIT says 'the integrated information realized IN that system is consciousness.' The key premise is that no purely empirical test can distinguish 'consciousness is X' from 'consciousness is Y' when X and Y are reliably co-instantiated. We've mistaken a metaphysical dispute for an empirical one.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Reasonable claim about metaphysical overlay but doesn't provide clear criteria for distinguishing genuine from merely verbal disputes.

---

### U49. Any test that could distinguish consciousness theories must rely on consciousness-dependent capacities (discrimination, report, memory), but this creates a detection problem: we cannot tell whether a theory fails because it's wrong about consciousness or because consciousness is present but undetectable by the chosen probe.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Non-functionalist theories (especially phenomenal realism and quale-based views) that nonetheless claim empirical testability
- **Sketch:** Suppose we test whether a patient in a particular brain state is conscious by checking for behavioral discrimination. If they fail to discriminate, theories disagree about whether consciousness is absent or merely unexpressed. But every probe we might use—verbal report, motor response, memory formation, attention effects—assumes consciousness can be expressed through that channel. Accepting any test methodology commits you to the claim that consciousness necessarily involves the capacity for that type of expression. The argument shows that theorists who endorse empirical testability are thereby committed to functionalist constraints they often explicitly reject. The key premise: if X is undetectable in principle by all possible probes, X is explanatorily idle.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The detection problem is real but the argument doesn't sufficiently advance beyond familiar underdetermination worries.

---

### U50. We can only empirically test consciousness theories if consciousness has effects distinguishable from its physical correlates, but the only way to establish such distinguishable effects requires already knowing which theory is correct—making empirical adjudication impossible without prior metaphysical commitment.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Optimistic empiricists like Dehaene or Tononi who believe their experimental programs can definitively establish their theories
- **Sketch:** Consider testing whether integrated information or global broadcasting is the correlate of consciousness. To determine this empirically, we need cases where they come apart: high integration without broadcasting, or vice versa. But identifying which case involves consciousness requires knowing what consciousness does—its causal profile. If we say 'whichever allows reportability is conscious,' we've presupposed functionalism. If we say 'whichever feels like something,' we've presupposed first-person authority. The key premise: there is no theory-neutral empirical signature of consciousness because what counts as evidence depends on which causal powers you attribute to consciousness, and that attribution is precisely what's in dispute. This counterexamples the widespread assumption that accumulating neural correlates data will eventually force theoretical convergence.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies circularity in establishing causal profiles but the argument structure is somewhat familiar from theory-ladenness debates.

---

### U51. Theories of consciousness that cannot explain why particular qualia feel the way they do (rather than merely that they exist) are not theories of consciousness at all, but theories of meta-consciousness or self-awareness.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The widespread assumption that Global Workspace Theory, Integrated Information Theory, Higher-Order Thought theories, etc. are competing theories of the same phenomenon
- **Sketch:** The argument turns on distinguishing between explaining that there is something-it-is-like (which Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Thought theories, etc. attempt) and explaining why pain feels painful rather than how sweetness feels. Current 'consciousness' theories systematically address only the former, treating quale-specific character as a dangling input. But explaining phenomenal presence while treating phenomenal character as a brute given is analogous to explaining why we have perceptual beliefs while treating perceptual content as given. Just as we'd reject a 'theory of perception' that only explained that we perceive, not what, we should recognize that current theories address only a meta-level awareness-of-experience, not experience itself. This redraws the map: we lack even candidate theories of consciousness proper.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Distinguishes meta-consciousness from consciousness proper, but the that/what distinction has been explored; limited additional traction.

### U52. Defending any theory of consciousness commits you to explaining why organisms with that mechanism deserve moral consideration, but all current theories make moral status either too liberal (including thermostats) or too restrictive (excluding clear moral patients).

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** All major theories of consciousness, but especially those claiming empirical adequacy (IIT, GWT, Recurrent Processing Theory)
- **Sketch:** The argument turns on forcing proponents to connect their theory to moral status. If consciousness matters morally because it grounds moral patiency, then identifying consciousness with information integration (IIT) commits you to either granting moral status to any integrated system (extremely liberal, including artifacts with no plausible moral standing) or adding ad-hoc restrictions that have nothing to do with the theory itself. Global Workspace theorists face a parallel problem: if global broadcasting suffices, then simple robots with broadcast architectures qualify, which seems wrong; but if you add restrictions (must be biological, must be sufficiently complex), these do the moral work, not the theory. Higher-Order Thought theories exclude animals without meta-representation (possibly very young humans), which conflicts with considered moral judgments. Every theory thus reveals an unnoticed commitment: it must either revise commonsense morality radically or admit that its account of consciousness omits what makes consciousness matter.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Connects theories to moral status in a way that reveals commitments, but the too-liberal/too-restrictive dilemma is somewhat predictable.

---

### U53. Testing consciousness theories requires determining whether consciousness can be absent when subjects insist it's present, but any evidence we could accept for this would show our concept of consciousness is actually empty.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The shared assumption across theories that consciousness is both essentially subjective (defined by what-it's-like) and empirically investigable as a natural phenomenon
- **Sketch:** The argument targets the shared assumption that we can discover consciousness-mechanism dissociations. Consider: what would convince us that subjects reporting rich visual phenomenology actually have none? Not neural evidence alone—correlations underdetermine which is epiphenomenal. Not behavioral evidence—if they act like it's present, that's exactly what presence predicts. Not conceptual analysis—that begs the question. The only remaining option is privileged first-personal access, but that's exactly what's being questioned (if I'm wrong about phenomenology's presence, first-personal authority fails). So either we can never have evidence for report-phenomenology dissociation (making some theories untestable), or we can have such evidence, but then we've abandoned the idea that consciousness is what-it's-like-for-the-subject (since the subject can be massively wrong about it). Either horn shows that our concept—inherently tied to first-personal authority—doesn't pick out a discoverable natural kind. This counterexample undermines the shared premise that consciousness is both subjectively defined and empirically discoverable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Interesting dilemma about report-phenomenology dissociation but doesn't clearly resolve which horn to take or what follows.

---

### U54. Any proposed empirical test for consciousness theories commits you to either behaviourism (if the test is third-personal) or mysterianism (if it requires first-personal access we can't systematically share).

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Methodological optimists like Doerig, Schurger & Herzog who think adversarial collaboration and clever experiments can settle which theory is right
- **Sketch:** Consider what any test would look like. If it's purely third-personal (neuroimaging, behaviour, report-dispositions), then you're committed to consciousness being constituted by whatever the test measures - but that's definitionally behaviourist, reducing consciousness to its outward signs. If instead you insist the test must involve first-personal verification (we each check our own phenomenology against predictions), then there's no way to make this systematic or shared - you've made consciousness empirically inaccessible in the way mysterians claim. The key premise is that this exhausts the options: tests are either third-personal-and-therefore-behaviourist or first-personal-and-therefore-unshareable. Most methodologists want neither behaviourism nor mysterianism, but their proposals for testing consciousness theories collapse into one or the other. This means the quest for empirical tests rests on an unstable methodological foundation.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Behaviorism-or-mysterianism dilemma is stark but may be false dichotomy; needs to rule out middle positions more carefully.

---

### U55. Consciousness theories are empirically distinguishable in principle but not in practice, because the only cases where they make different predictions are cases where we have no independent access to whether consciousness is present.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Both optimists who think we'll find the right test and pessimists who think theories are empirically equivalent - they're both wrong because they miss the distinction between distinguishable and decidable
- **Sketch:** Draw a distinction between 'in-principle empirical distinguishability' (theories make different predictions about some cases) and 'practically testable distinguishability' (we can actually check which prediction is right). IIT and GNW make different predictions about whether the cerebellum could be conscious, whether feed-forward sweeps involve consciousness, whether offline processing is conscious. But in all these cases, we have no independent way to verify which prediction is correct - we can't ask the cerebellum, we can't catch ourselves in the 50ms between feed-forward and recurrence. The key premise is that theories diverge exactly where phenomenology becomes inaccessible to report, memory, and introspection. This isn't a coincidence - theorists construct their theories to match clear cases and only diverge on unclear cases. This means the theories ARE empirically distinct (they make different predictions) but not empirically decidable (we can't access the evidence that would decide them).
- **Appraiser (blind):** The in-principle versus in-practice distinction is useful but the observation that theories diverge where evidence is inaccessible is somewhat recognized in the literature.

### U56. The methodological dispute about consciousness is really a dispute about whether we should treat first-person reports as more like observation reports or more like theoretical posits, and recognizing this reveals that the empirical adequacy criteria different theories employ are question-begging.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across the Frankish/Dennett deflationists, Rosenthal's HOT theory, and Block's phenomenal realism that we can settle the debate by seeing which theory is empirically adequate
- **Sketch:** When higher-order theorists demand that a theory explain our judgments about consciousness, and phenomenal realists demand it explain consciousness itself, they're importing different evidential standards from philosophy of science. The former treats phenomenal reports like theoretical claims (which theories can revise), the latter like observation reports (which constrain theory). But choosing an evidential standard already presupposes an answer to what consciousness is—whether it's constituted by our cognitive access to it or independent of such access. This means the apparently empirical question 'which theory best explains the data?' covertly presupposes metaphysical commitments about what counts as data in the first place, making the debate more methodologically circular than participants realize.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Observation-report vs. theoretical-posit distinction is useful but circularity claim needs tighter argument about unavoidability.

### U57. The demand that a theory of consciousness tell us 'which things are conscious' commits us to treating consciousness as a natural kind with sharp boundaries, but if consciousness is instead a cluster concept or determinable property, then multiple incompatible theories could each be correctly describing different legitimate ways of carving up the space.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The widespread assumption that consciousness is a single explanandum that different theories compete to explain, shared by most participants
- **Sketch:** Most consciousness research assumes there's a fact of the matter about whether, say, a bee or a fetus or a patient in a vegetative state is conscious, and that the right theory will give us the answer. But this assumes consciousness is a natural kind with determinate boundaries—that there's a unified phenomenon to theorize about. The key premise is that if consciousness is instead more like 'health' or 'intelligence'—a cluster of features that come apart in edge cases—then different theories might be tracking different legitimate clusters. IIT might track one cluster (integrated information), GWT another (broadcast availability), etc. This would explain why the theories seem incommensurable: they're not competing to describe the same phenomenon, but rather proposing different precisifications of a loose folk concept. Recognizing this reveals that demanding 'the right theory' commits us to an unjustified unification assumption.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The cluster concept possibility is worth exploring but the argument that theories might track different legitimate precisifications is already discussed in some form.

### U58. The development of brain-computer interfaces that allow us to artificially induce the neural signatures that different theories identify with consciousness—without the usual functional or informational properties—will show that all current theories conflate consciousness with its typical causes or effects, since subjects will report consciousness is absent despite the signatures being present.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Theories that identify consciousness with neural or functional patterns (GWT, IIT, recurrent processing theories)
- **Sketch:** Suppose we can artificially create the 'global broadcasting' of information that GWT identifies with consciousness, or the integrated information that IIT quantifies, without the normal context—say, directly stimulating the relevant neural patterns. The key empirical premise is that when we do this, subjects will report that nothing is like anything, that they're not conscious of any content, even though the theory's proposed neural/functional signature is present. This would show that these theories have mistaken reliable correlates for constitutive features. The argument reveals that what seems like consciousness-constituting features are actually just typical causes or effects, and that we can only discover this through intervention, not mere observation. This means the right methodology is experimental manipulation, not looking for correlations.
- **Appraiser (blind):** An empirical prediction about future BCI experiments; interesting but depends on contingent empirical outcomes and the conflation claim is already debated.

### U59. If we take seriously the possibility of theory-neutral consciousness measures, we discover that any such measure must either presuppose phenomenal realism (making it question-begging) or reduce to measuring confidence in self-reports (making it compatible with illusionism), revealing that the intermediate position—that consciousness is real but scientifically tractable—is unstable.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The 'functionalist realism' position held by many working consciousness scientists who think consciousness is real, measurable, but not mysteriously non-physical
- **Sketch:** Many researchers seek theory-neutral ways to measure consciousness—say, using perturbational complexity or machine learning on neural data. But consider what validates such a measure. Either (a) we validate it by checking whether it matches what subjects are 'really' conscious of, which presupposes that phenomenal states are what we're measuring (assuming phenomenal realism); or (b) we validate it by checking whether it predicts subjects' reports and behaviors, which means we're just measuring reportability/confidence (compatible with illusionism). The key premise is that there's no third option: any validation must appeal either to phenomenal facts or to functional/behavioral facts. This means the moderate position—consciousness is real and objective but functionally grounded—collapses into either phenomenal realism or illusionism depending on how we try to validate our measures. Those who think we can be empirical realists about consciousness without being either phenomenal realists or illusionists are confused.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Theory-neutral measures collapsing into realism-or-illusionism is interesting but the exhaustiveness of options needs defense.

---

### U60. Every proposed empirical test of consciousness theories actually requires assuming the correct theory of consciousness to interpret the results, making the testing problem impossible rather than merely hard.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Methodological optimists who think the testing problem is tractable with better experimental design
- **Sketch:** Consider any proposed test—say, testing whether integrated information correlates with consciousness. To run this test, we must first identify which systems are conscious and which aren't (to establish ground truth). But we can only make these identifications by assuming some theory of consciousness is correct. If we assume IIT, we 'discover' IIT is right; if we assume GWT, we 'discover' GWT is right. The key premise is that there is no theory-neutral way to establish which states are conscious, because 'being conscious' is precisely what the theories disagree about defining. This means the position 'we just need better experiments' is unstable—it collapses into 'we need to already know which theory is right in order to test which theory is right.' The testing project requires what it's trying to discover.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The circularity of needing ground truth is noted in the literature, though the impossibility claim rather than difficulty claim adds some force.

### U61. Committing to consciousness being multiply realizable commits you to consciousness being empirically undetectable in principle, not just in practice.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Functionalists and most physicalists who accept multiple realizability while maintaining consciousness is scientifically tractable
- **Sketch:** Most participants in the consciousness debate accept multiple realizability: consciousness could be realized in biological neurons, silicon chips, or other substrates. The argument turns on this premise: if consciousness is multiply realizable, then there is no physical signature that all and only conscious systems share. But if there's no such signature, then any proposed empirical test that succeeds for one implementation could fail for another implementation of the same conscious state. This means accepting multiple realizability commits you to accepting that no empirical test could reliably detect consciousness across all possible realizations. Proponents of multiple realizability are therefore committed to a form of empirical inaccessibility they typically reject, creating an unnoticed tension in views like functionalism that are both physicalist (seemingly empirical) and multiply realizable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Multiple realizability entailing undetectability is provocative but the inference may be too quick; needs careful modal argument.

### U62. Any consciousness theory that makes testable predictions necessarily confuses consciousness with its cognitive consequences, while theories that avoid this confusion make no testable predictions.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Theories like GWT and IIT that claim to both identify consciousness correctly and make testable predictions
- **Sketch:** Draw a distinction current theories blur: between consciousness itself (the qualitative character of experience) and its cognitive consequences (what the system does because it's conscious—report, reason, remember). Testable theories like GWT predict things about processing, integration, broadcasting—all cognitive consequences. But these predictions don't tell us about consciousness itself, only about what accompanies it. Meanwhile, theories that carefully distinguish consciousness from cognition (like pure qualia theories) correctly identify the target but generate no predictions, since consciousness-without-consequences is empirically invisible. The key premise is that testability requires causal consequences, but consciousness qua consciousness has no causal consequences—only the underlying mechanisms do. This creates a forced choice the literature hasn't recognized: be testable but study the wrong thing, or study the right thing but be untestable. Current theories try to have it both ways by equivocating on what 'consciousness' refers to.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The forced choice between testability and studying the right thing is sharp, though the consciousness-versus-consequences distinction is somewhat familiar.

### U63. Testing consciousness theories requires solving the other-minds problem first, and any method that solves the other-minds problem would make consciousness theories empirically redundant.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The research program that treats empirical validation of consciousness theories as both necessary and achievable
- **Sketch:** The argument proceeds by examining what would actually count as confirming a consciousness theory. To confirm that integrated information (or global workspace, etc.) correlates with consciousness, we need independent access to when consciousness is present. But the other-minds problem shows we lack such access—we can only observe behavior and neural activity, never consciousness itself in others. If someone proposes a solution (say, 'these neural signatures reliably indicate consciousness'), they've already answered the empirical question the theories were meant to answer, making the theories explanatorily idle. The instability: either we can't test the theories (no solution to other-minds), or we don't need them (we already have a working consciousness detector).
- **Appraiser (blind):** The dilemma is clear—either can't test or theories are redundant—but the other-minds connection to theory-testing has been discussed before.
