### U64. First-person report reliability creates an experimenter's regress: any evidence that subjects accurately report their phenomenology already presupposes a theory about which neural states constitute consciousness.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Methodologies that rely on first-person reports as neutral evidence, particularly in contrastive analysis paradigms
- **Sketch:** When researchers use subjective reports to validate consciousness theories, they must decide when to trust these reports. If a theory predicts consciousness is present but the subject reports none (or vice versa), researchers face a choice: reject the theory or doubt the report. This choice requires criteria for report reliability—but these criteria inevitably smuggle in commitments about what consciousness is and when it occurs. For instance, treating reports as reliable only when subjects are 'paying attention' commits you to a view where attention is constitutive of consciousness. The regress: to validate theory T, you need reliable reports, but determining reliability requires already accepting some theory T*, which itself needs validation. Proponents of empirical validation are committed to either circular reasoning or an unargued first theory.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The regress about report reliability is worth articulating but the circularity concern about validating theories through reports is recognized.

### U65. Consciousness theories are empirically distinguishable only to the extent they make architectural claims rather than consciousness claims, revealing that the theories are actually competing models of cognitive architecture that merely use 'consciousness' as a marker.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The framing that treats these as theories of consciousness rather than theories of information-processing that stipulate what counts as consciousness
- **Sketch:** The key distinction: between a theory's architectural claims (information is integrated in this way, broadcasting occurs through these networks) and its consciousness claim (this architecture constitutes/produces consciousness). The architectural claims are testable—we can measure integration, track broadcasts, map predictions. But once we confirm the architecture, the consciousness claim remains an interpretive addition. IIT's integrated information is measurable, but 'Φ > 0 means consciousness' is not additionally tested. This reveals that calling something a 'consciousness theory' is really staking a claim about which measurable architectural features deserve the label 'consciousness.' The theories differ empirically only in their architecture, not in their account of consciousness per se.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The architecture versus consciousness distinction is useful but the observation that architectural claims are what's actually tested is somewhat acknowledged.

### U66. Accepting that consciousness could be multiply realizable commits you to accepting that no evidence could distinguish between substrate-neutral theories, making empirical testing impossible in principle.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Functionalists and computational theorists who accept multiple realizability but propose empirical testing programs based on neuroscience
- **Sketch:** Multiple realizability means that consciousness doesn't require specific physical implementation—silicon could work as well as neurons. But if substrate doesn't matter, then any functional isomorphs share phenomenology. This commits multiple-realizability theorists to the view that structurally identical systems must have identical consciousness regardless of substrate. However, this means we can never test whether artificial systems are conscious by comparing them to biological ones—any difference in verdict must stem from structural differences, not substrate. The key premise is that multiple realizability entails that substrate-level evidence is definitionally irrelevant, yet substrate-level evidence (neuroimaging, neurotransmitter studies) is our primary empirical tool.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Competent argument about multiple realizability's epistemic consequences, but the tension between substrate-neutrality and substrate-based evidence is already recognized in the literature.

### U67. The distinction between 'access consciousness' and 'phenomenal consciousness' makes theories empirically testable by the same methods that distinguish them, but only if we abandon verbal report as privileged evidence.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Both deflationists who reject the distinction and phenomenal realists who use it to argue that P-consciousness is empirically inaccessible
- **Sketch:** Block's A-consciousness/P-consciousness distinction is typically used to show why certain experiments fail (they test access, not phenomenology). But flip this: the distinction itself suggests a testing methodology. If A and P come apart, then cases where subjects cannot report their phenomenology (due to access limitations, not absence of phenomenology) become crucial test cases. The key premise is that theories make different predictions about P-consciousness in the absence of A-consciousness (overflow in inattention, unconscious perception, locked-in states). The methodological upshot: abandon the assumption that verbal report is our epistemic ground, and instead triangulate from behavior, neural dynamics, and structural features in cases where report is impossible. This reclassifies the debate from 'epistemically impossible' to 'methodologically demanding.'
- **Appraiser (blind):** Interesting methodological flip on Block's distinction, but the proposal to triangulate without verbal report faces well-known challenges already discussed.

### U68. Every current theory of consciousness assumes that facts about consciousness supervene on facts capturable in a completed neuroscience, but this assumption is a counterexample to itself—if true, it would be a fact about consciousness that no neuroscientific evidence could establish.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The shared naturalistic assumption across Dennett, Churchland, higher-order theories, and global workspace theory that empirical neuroscience suffices for theory choice
- **Sketch:** Both physicalist and functionalist theories grant that consciousness supervenes on physical/functional organization. This supervenience claim is itself a claim about the nature of consciousness. But establishing supervenience requires showing that varying the base necessitates varying consciousness. The key premise: demonstrating necessity requires access to metaphysically possible variations (zombie worlds, inverted spectra), not just actual correlations. No amount of neuroscientific evidence—which only reveals actual correlations—can establish that the correlation is necessary rather than contingent. Therefore, the supervenience assumption that grounds the testability of consciousness theories is itself empirically untestable. This is self-undermining: the meta-claim that makes theories testable is itself not testable by the methods it authorizes.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Supervenience assumption being untestable is philosophically interesting but may not undermine practice as much as claimed.

### U69. Any empirical test that could definitively adjudicate between consciousness theories would require solving the other minds problem for systems we currently treat as test cases.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Theorists who claim their consciousness theory is empirically testable (Tononi, Dehaene, Rosenthal)
- **Sketch:** Consider testing IIT against GWT using brain organoids or split-brain patients. To know which theory correctly predicts consciousness in these cases, we'd need independent grounds for knowing whether the system is conscious—but this is precisely what's in dispute. We can't bootstrap from behavioral criteria (organoids don't behave) or introspective reports (split-brain patients give conflicting reports across hemispheres). The commitment to empirical testability thus commits theorists to either (a) already solved other minds problems for edge cases, or (b) restricting tests to cases where we have independent certainty, which circularly validates theories against our prior intuitions rather than testing them. The demand for empirical testability therefore collapses into either question-begging or requires achieving what most consider impossible.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The other minds problem for edge cases is recognized; this formulation is clear but doesn't add substantially new considerations.

### U70. Every proposed empirical test of consciousness theories presupposes functionalism, making it impossible for such tests to vindicate non-functionalist theories.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The widespread methodological assumption that empirical neuroscience can adjudicate between consciousness theories including non-functionalist ones
- **Sketch:** Empirical tests necessarily rely on observable inputs and outputs—brain states, behaviors, reports, or computational processes. But non-functionalist theories (like certain qualia realist views or biological naturalist positions) claim consciousness depends on intrinsic properties that don't supervene on functional organization. Any test showing that system X with functional profile F is conscious while system Y with different profile G is not would vindicate functionalism itself, not any particular functionalist theory. This undermines proposals to test between theories empirically: we can only test functionalist theories against each other, while non-functionalist alternatives are empirically untestable by design. The methodological naturalist consensus thus builds in a substantive theoretical commitment.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The point that empirical methods presuppose functionalism is known; competent articulation but limited novelty in consciousness literature.

### U71. Consciousness theories are stable only when theorists equivocate between type-identity and token-identity with neural states, but enforcing consistency on this point makes every major theory collapse into either eliminativism or panpsychism.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Functionalist consciousness theories that claim to avoid both eliminativism and panpsychism
- **Sketch:** Consider what GWT or HOT theorists claim when they say 'consciousness is global broadcast' or 'consciousness is higher-order representation'. Type-identity (conscious states as types are identical to functional types) leads to liberalism problems—thermostats have global broadcast, simple feedback systems have higher-order representation. To avoid this, theorists retreat to token-identity (this particular conscious state is this particular neural token). But token-identity provides no theoretical generalization—it's just eliminativism about consciousness-as-a-kind. The middle position requires saying consciousness is multiply realizable but only in systems 'sufficiently like us', which is either unprincipled anthropocentrism or sneaks in panpsychism (all matter has proto-conscious properties that reach threshold in complex systems). Every attempt to characterize the restriction on multiple realizability either becomes arbitrary or expands to everything.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Type/token equivocation forcing eliminativism-or-panpsychism is sharp but the dilemma may have escape routes needing examination.

### U72. Any theory of consciousness that can be empirically confirmed must fail to be a theory of consciousness, because empirical confirmation requires operational definitions that eliminate the very subjectivity consciousness theories claim to explain.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The entire project of empirical consciousness science as currently conceived, especially naturalistic theories claiming both scientific status and explanatory adequacy for phenomenal consciousness
- **Sketch:** For a consciousness theory to make testable predictions, it must specify observable conditions that correlate with consciousness. But any such specification requires treating consciousness as identical to some functional/behavioral/neural profile, which converts phenomenal properties into functional ones. The argument shows that theories either (a) remain unconfirmable by refusing operational definitions, preserving subjectivity but losing empirical traction, or (b) adopt operational definitions that make them confirmable but thereby eliminate subjectivity in favor of objective proxies. The key premise: operational definitions work by substituting intersubjectively accessible properties for private ones, which is precisely what makes them inadequate for genuinely subjective properties. Attempts to have both confirmability and genuine subjectivity therefore reveal internal tension in the project itself.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Operational definition eliminating subjectivity is a familiar tension in philosophy of mind; competent but well-trodden ground.

### U73. The search for 'the' right theory of consciousness presupposes a false uniformity: different aspects of consciousness (access, phenomenal character, unity, subjectivity) may require different and mutually irreducible explanatory frameworks.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The assumption shared by competing theories that they're offering rival accounts of the same unified explanandum
- **Sketch:** Current debates assume consciousness is a natural kind requiring a unified theory. But examining what makes access consciousness puzzling versus what makes phenomenal character puzzling reveals these target different explananda requiring different resources. Access consciousness poses a cognitive architecture problem answerable by information-processing theories like GNW. Phenomenal character poses a metaphysical problem about intrinsic qualities requiring different resources (perhaps Russellian monism). Unity raises mereological questions. Subjectivity raises perspective-dependence issues. The argument turns on showing these aren't separable aspects of one phenomenon but genuinely distinct phenomena that happen to co-occur. Looking for 'the theory of consciousness' is like looking for 'the theory of living things' when we need separate theories of metabolism, reproduction, and evolution.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Pluralism about consciousness aspects is already advocated by several theorists; this articulates it clearly but isn't breaking new ground.

### U74. Holding that consciousness science requires solving the hard problem commits you to rejecting that we can currently know anyone else is conscious—a commitment most consciousness researchers implicitly deny when designing experiments.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Hard problem theorists who also engage in empirical consciousness research, particularly Chalmers and those who take the hard problem seriously while still doing consciousness science
- **Sketch:** Many consciousness researchers (especially those skeptical of functionalism) maintain that solving the hard problem is necessary for a complete theory of consciousness. But experimental consciousness research requires assuming certain beings (awake humans, not anesthetized ones) are conscious to establish ground truth for training classifiers, validating measures, or testing predictions. This assumption requires already having reliable indicators of consciousness—precisely what solving the hard problem would provide. The argument shows that researchers cannot coherently both (a) claim we need to solve the hard problem to have a theory of consciousness and (b) design experiments that depend on already knowing who's conscious. The key premise: if phenomenal consciousness can't be read off functional/behavioral/neural profiles, then our experimental attributions of consciousness to ground-truth cases are epistemically unjustified.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Tension between hard problem commitment and experimental practice is recognized; solid point but doesn't substantially advance the debate.

### U75. Theories of consciousness are empirically testable only when paired with specific bridge principles about reportability, but the choice of bridge principle already presupposes a theory of consciousness, making the entire testing enterprise circular.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The shared assumption across empirical consciousness research (Dehaene, Tononi, Prinz, Block) that we can test theories by finding their distinctive empirical predictions
- **Sketch:** Arguments proceed by showing that every proposed way to test consciousness theories (neural correlates studies, dissociation paradigms, lesion studies) requires assuming what counts as evidence of consciousness—whether subjects must be able to report experiences, whether unconscious processing counts, whether attention is necessary. For instance, testing whether phenomenal consciousness overflows access consciousness requires deciding whether unreportable experiences exist, but this is precisely what overflow theories claim and anti-overflow theories deny. The key premise is that bridge principles aren't theoretically neutral auxiliary hypotheses but are themselves substantive claims about consciousness that different theories reject. This means the apparently stable research program of 'testing theories against data' collapses into theory-choice based on prior commitments about what would count as data.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Bridge principle circularity is discussed in consciousness methodology; this is clear but doesn't offer new resolution or insight.

### U76. Every proposed test for consciousness theories actually tests our concept of consciousness rather than consciousness itself, which means what appears to be theory comparison is actually conceptual negotiation about what 'consciousness' should mean.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The realist assumption shared across debates that there's a fact of the matter about what's conscious, which our theories approximate
- **Sketch:** When researchers debate whether a patient in a vegetative state with covert task-related fMRI activity is conscious, they're not discovering a fact but negotiating whether 'consciousness' should be tied to reportability, behavioral responsiveness, or neural signatures. Similarly, debates over whether integrated information theory makes the cerebellum massively conscious don't turn on empirical discovery but on whether we should bite that bullet or revise the theory. The key premise is that consciousness has no theory-independent essence to anchor these disputes—our pre-theoretic concept underdetermines extension to novel cases (AI, organoids, split-brain, locked-in). What looks like testing theories against reality is actually testing conceptual proposals against intuitions, making this closer to stipulative definition than empirical discovery.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Conceptual negotiation versus empirical discovery is a recognized issue; competent but the insight is familiar from debates about natural kinds.

### U77. Commitment to finding 'the' correct theory of consciousness commits you to epiphenomenalism, because only epiphenomenal properties can have multiple realizability space large enough to adjudicate between current theories.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Researchers who both reject epiphenomenalism and claim we can empirically determine which neural/computational substrate realizes consciousness
- **Sketch:** Current theories disagree about what consciousness is realized in (integrated information vs. global broadcasting vs. higher-order states, etc.). For there to be a fact about which is correct, consciousness must be multiply realizable across these different substrates—otherwise the question is empirically empty. But the key premise is that the only properties with this degree of multiple realizability are those that don't constrain their physical realizers—i.e., epiphenomenal properties. If consciousness actually did causal work, it would constrain its realization narrowly enough that we couldn't meaningfully test between such diverse physical theories. Therefore, those seeking to empirically adjudicate between theories are committed to consciousness being epiphenomenal, even though most explicitly reject epiphenomenalism.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Interesting connection between multiple realizability and epiphenomenalism, but the argument that causal efficacy constrains realization narrowly is contestable.

### U78. The assumption that consciousness admits of degrees commits us to the conclusion that no discrete theory can be correct, which eliminates all current leading theories.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The shared assumption that current leading discrete-mechanism theories are viable candidates
- **Sketch:** Nearly everyone in the field accepts that consciousness admits of degrees (partial awareness, degrees of clarity, vegetative states, etc.). But all leading theories—IIT, GWT, HOT, recurrent processing theory—specify discrete architectural or computational features as necessary and sufficient for consciousness (either you have integrated information above phi-min, or global broadcasting, or higher-order representation, or you don't). The key premise is that discrete on-off mechanisms cannot smoothly generate graded phenomena without arbitrary threshold-setting. If consciousness genuinely comes in degrees, then theories specifying discrete mechanisms fail as explanations, leaving only continuous theories (perhaps something like degree of information integration without a threshold). But this counterexample eliminates the very theories that are supposed to be empirically testable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Points to real tension between gradedness and discrete mechanisms, though the 'eliminates all current theories' conclusion overstates the case.

### U79. Theories of consciousness are empirically distinguishable in principle but only at the surgical moment of creating or destroying consciousness, which our ethical commitments make permanently inaccessible to us.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Pessimists who think consciousness theories are empirically indistinguishable even in principle, and optimists who think current neuroscience can resolve the debate
- **Sketch:** Against the shared assumption that we can't ethically test consciousness theories, consider that we actually could distinguish them empirically: theories differ on whether consciousness requires global broadcasting (GWT), integrated information above threshold (IIT), or certain recursive structures (HOT). These predict different conditions are sufficient/necessary for consciousness. We could test them by systematically creating minimal systems that satisfy one theory's requirements but not others, or by surgically lesioning/stimulating to remove specific capacities, then checking for presence/absence of consciousness. The key premise is that consciousness is either present or absent in these cases—there's a fact of the matter. The problem isn't epistemic or methodological but purely ethical: the experiments that would distinguish theories require creating conscious entities to test them, destroying consciousness in existing subjects, or both. We've chosen not to know the answer.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Novel to frame the problem as purely ethical rather than epistemic, though the claim that theories are distinguishable 'in principle' needs more support.

### U80. Any empirical test sufficient to adjudicate between consciousness theories would require solving the grain problem for neural correlates—but solving the grain problem already presupposes a theory of consciousness.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Researchers like Crick, Koch, and Chalmers who treat finding NCCs as an empirically tractable path to theory confirmation
- **Sketch:** The grain problem asks: which spatiotemporal scale of neural activity constitutes the NCC—individual neurons, columns, hemispheres, or some other level? Every consciousness theory makes claims about NCCs at some grain, but we need independent evidence about the right grain to test theories against neural data. However, our only evidence for the correct grain comes from which grain best correlates with consciousness—creating a tight circle. IIT says integrated complexes at one scale matter; GWT says global broadcasts at another. The key premise is that 'finding the NCC' requires already knowing what size/duration events to look for, but this is precisely what competing theories disagree about, so empirical NCC evidence cannot be theory-neutral.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies real circularity in grain problem, though this has been noted before; the systematic application to theory-testing adds some value.

### U81. First-person reports can only adjudicate consciousness theories if we already know which cognitive processes subjects have introspective access to—but this is precisely what consciousness theories disagree about.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Methodological assumptions shared across camps that first-person reports provide theory-neutral data (assumed by both Schwitzgebel and mainstream consciousness science)
- **Sketch:** Higher-order theories claim subjects can only report states they have higher-order thoughts about; first-order theories claim direct access to first-order states; illusionist theories claim reports are unreliable confabulations. Each theory thus predicts different reliability patterns for introspective reports. If we use reports to test theories, we must already assume some theory of introspective access to interpret whether a report's presence/absence confirms or disconfirms a prediction. The key premise is that treating reports as evidence commits you to a specific theory of reportability—you cannot neutrally collect 'what subjects say' without presupposing an account of what their saying it indicates. This means researchers who think phenomenology can constrain theory choice are already smuggling in theoretical commitments through their interpretation of reports.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Solid point about circularity in using reports, but this concern is familiar in debates about introspection and higher-order theories.

### U82. The standard methodology of comparing conscious versus unconscious conditions commits researchers to treating consciousness as an on/off switch, but this commitment is incompatible with gradualist theories that most researchers explicitly endorse.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The methodological consensus in consciousness science exemplified by studies using binocular rivalry, masking, and attentional paradigms
- **Sketch:** The dominant experimental paradigm uses contrastive analysis: compare trials where subjects report seeing a stimulus versus trials where they don't (masking, suppression, inattention). This methodology assumes consciousness comes in discrete present/absent states for a given content at a given time. However, most theorists explicitly endorse gradualism—consciousness varies continuously in degree, clarity, or richness. IIT assigns consciousness a continuous phi value; GWT allows partial broadcasts; most researchers accept that consciousness fades gradually in sleep onset. The key premise is that if consciousness is truly gradual, then comparing 'conscious' versus 'unconscious' trials is like comparing 'tall' versus 'short' people to study height—it imposes artificial categories on continuous variation, potentially finding correlates of the arbitrary threshold rather than consciousness itself.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Valid observation about methodology/theory mismatch, though the problem is somewhat recognized; systematic articulation has value but limited novelty.

### U83. The zombie argument's conceivability premise commits functionalists to the conclusion that no empirical evidence could ever distinguish between their theory and epiphenomenalism, because any evidence that could settle this question would require consciousness to play a causal role that functionalists must attribute to physical function alone.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Functionalists who grant zombie conceivability while maintaining that consciousness can be empirically studied
- **Sketch:** Functionalists typically grant that zombies are conceivable but not metaphysically possible, while epiphenomenalists grant both conceivability and possibility. But this disagreement cannot be empirically settled. Any behavioral or neural evidence we gather will be evidence about functional organization, not about whether that organization is accompanied by consciousness. To empirically distinguish functionalism from epiphenomenalism, we'd need consciousness itself to make a causal difference independent of its functional role—but functionalists are committed to denying this. If consciousness just IS functional organization, then all our evidence is equally compatible with epiphenomenalism (same functions plus causally idle qualia). The functionalist who grants zombie conceivability has thus committed themselves to their theory being empirically indistinguishable from a view they reject.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Interesting that granting zombie conceivability commits functionalists to empirical indistinguishability from epiphenomenalism, but argument needs tightening.

### U84. The search for neural correlates of consciousness (NCCs) covertly assumes that consciousness is a brain-local property, but if extended or relational theories of consciousness are correct, then the entire NCC research program is looking for correlates in the wrong location space—it's a category mistake, not a hard empirical problem.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The mainstream neuroscience assumption that consciousness is realized in or by the brain
- **Sketch:** The NCC framework assumes consciousness correlates with something inside the skull. But this assumption is contradicted by extended theories (where consciousness includes loops through environment/body) and relational theories (where consciousness is constituted by relations to external objects). These aren't just exotic positions—they're motivated by cases like tool-use integration and transparent perception. If I'm consciously seeing a tree, and the relationalist is right that this state is partly constituted by the tree itself, then looking for the NCC entirely within my brain is like looking for the correlate of 'being married' entirely in one person's body. The argument's key premise: research programs can fail not because they lack sophisticated enough tools, but because they're searching in an ontologically inappropriate domain. This means current methodology doesn't just need better technology—it needs reconceptualization of what location means for consciousness.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Extended/relational theories challenging NCC location assumptions is valuable, though the 'category mistake' framing may overreach.

- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The shared assumption across IIT, GNW, HOT, and other theories that there is a unified phenomenon of consciousness to be explained by a single theory
- **Sketch:** We assume that the correct theory of consciousness will correctly classify all actual conscious systems and correctly predict what interventions would create/destroy consciousness. But if consciousness has multiple independent realizations (say, recurrent-processing-based and quantum-coherence-based), then any unified theory that correctly predicts one kind will necessarily misclassify the other. This explains why every current theory seems to get some cases right and others wrong—not because they're all incomplete approximations of one true theory, but because unified theories of consciousness are impossible. The empirical data already contains incompatible signatures, forcing a choice between false positives and false negatives.

### U86. First-person methods could disconfirm but never confirm consciousness theories, because phenomenological mismatch is evidentially asymmetric with phenomenological match.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Neurophenomenology programs (Varela, Lutz, Thompson) and anyone who thinks first-person methods provide confirmatory evidence for consciousness theories
- **Sketch:** Many philosophers (especially phenomenologists and neurophenomenologists) argue that first-person reports should constrain consciousness theories—theories must match the phenomenology. But there's a crucial asymmetry: if a theory predicts experience X and subjects report not-X, this is strong disconfirmation. But if subjects report X when the theory predicts X, this confirms nothing, because subjects might confabulate, might lack concepts for what they're experiencing, or might be biased by knowing the theory. This counterexample targets the idea that first-person data provides positive evidence. The premise: testimonial evidence has epistemic force only when the testifier has independent access to the facts, but in consciousness research, subjects' concepts for their experiences are contaminated by the very theories being tested.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The asymmetry point is valid but relatively familiar from testimony literature; moderate impact on neurophenomenology debates.

- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both mysterian positions (like Chalmers' epistemic gap arguments) and optimistic empiricists who think we just need better instruments
- **Sketch:** Most discussions assume we face an epistemic barrier to discovering which theory is correct. But the real problem is that we cannot articulate what metaphysical difference remains once all functional, behavioral, and structural facts are settled. If we could specify this residual difference, we could design tests for it. The inability to do so suggests the dispute is either (a) verbal—different theories stipulating different meanings for 'consciousness', or (b) substantive but the substance lies in functional/structural facts we already have methods for investigating. The key premise is that any genuine metaphysical difference must cash out in some specifiable way beyond complete functional descriptions, even if that difference isn't directly observable.

If we accept this principle, then we're committed to treating certain AI systems as conscious once they produce such reports, even if our current theories say they shouldn't be. - **Sketch:** Arguments about how to empirically test theories of consciousness (IIT vs GWT vs HOT, etc.) assume we're trying to discover what consciousness 'really is' independently of our methods of detection. The key premise is that our actual practices of consciousness-attribution (in medicine, law, animal ethics, AI ethics) are revisable in light of pragmatic considerations, not discoveries about consciousness's hidden nature. This explains why seemingly empirical disputes resist resolution: the disputants are unknowingly trying to regiment different evolving practices.

### U90. The search for tests to distinguish consciousness theories fails because these theories are actually downstream specifications of prior normative commitments about moral status, not competing hypotheses about a pre-theoretically fixed domain.

- **Sketch:** We think the order of explanation is: discover what consciousness is, then derive moral implications. But the actual order is reversed. Theorists are drawn to IIT, GWT, HOT, etc. based on prior commitments about which beings matter morally—do insects matter (IIT says yes, GWT says no)? Do we matter when dreamlessly sleeping (IIT says yes, HOT says no)? The theories don't disagree about observations but about which beings to include in the moral community, then work backwards to consciousness criteria that yield those inclusions. Evidence: theory preferences correlate with prior moral intuitions; 'problem cases' (split-brain, locked-in syndrome, anesthesia, AI) are precisely cases where moral status is contested; theorists resist 'empirical findings' that would expand or contract moral communities in unwelcome ways. The key premise is that consciousness theory choice is underdetermined by phenomenological and neural data but determined by ethical commitments.

### U91. All current proposals for testing consciousness theories equivocate between the property consciousness (phenomenal character) and the state consciousness (being conscious vs. unconscious), but these require completely different testing methods—solving one won't solve the other.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Testing proposals that treat consciousness as a single target phenomenon, particularly neural correlate approaches
- **Sketch:** Debates about testing consciousness theories slide between two questions: 'What makes an experience feel like something specific?' (property) and 'What makes something conscious rather than unconscious?' (state). IIT's phi measure, GWT's global broadcasting, HOT's meta-representation might track state-consciousness (on/off) but this tells us nothing about property-consciousness (why red feels red-ish). Conversely, explaining property-consciousness doesn't explain state-consciousness—explaining why experiences have specific qualities doesn't explain why there are experiences at all at certain times. Current testing proposals fail because they try to adjudicate both questions simultaneously with single measures. The key premise is that property and state are metaphysically distinct: state-consciousness involves a threshold or transition, property-consciousness involves determination of character. Evidence: theories make different relative claims on these dimensions (HOT is better for state, IIT for property).
- **Appraiser (blind):** Property/state distinction is known but claim that current tests equivocate and require different methods is a useful clarification.

### U92. Any test capable of distinguishing between current consciousness theories would require subjects who can be conscious in ways humans cannot, which means the debate cannot be settled by any evidence actually available to us.

But the only systems where these actually come apart are either hypothetical (artificial systems not yet built) or biological systems whose phenomenology we cannot access (other species, brain lesion patients who cannot report). The key premise: human subjects necessarily instantiate these properties in a coupled way due to how our brains evolved, so normal human consciousness always confounds the variables. Every actual consciousness theorist is committed to predictions that require evidence from subjects whose consciousness we cannot access, making their theories empirically idle with respect to available human data.

### U93. Consciousness theories are actually answering different questions that happen to use the same word 'consciousness,' but we can tell which question is the legitimate one by testing whether eliminating consciousness (on that definition) would matter to the subject.

- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The pluralist consensus that different theories might be tracking different equally legitimate aspects of consciousness, held by Prinz, Block, and others
- **Sketch:** IIT is answering 'what has intrinsic existence?', GWT is answering 'what information is accessible for decision-making?', higher-order theory is answering 'what mental states are represented?'. We can test this by asking: if you could have all the integration (IIT) but no global accessibility (GWT), which would you choose to keep? The real consciousness property is the one subjects would pay to preserve. This provides an empirical tie-breaker: the correct theory is the one whose presence/absence tracks what matters from the inside.

### U94. Consciousness theories that predict consciousness in systems we're unwilling to grant it (like thermostats under panpsychism, or inactive brains under IIT) don't fail empirically—they fail because they make the concept of consciousness explanatorily useless.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Both sides of debates about whether counterintuitive predictions count as refutations or as consciousness-expanding discoveries
- **Sketch:** We typically treat predictions about non-paradigm cases as empirical failures (IIT predicting high phi in inactive systems is seen as a bug). But these aren't empirical failures—we have no independent evidence that inactive cerebellums lack consciousness. Rather, they're pragmatic failures: a theory that attributes consciousness too liberally makes consciousness explanatorily idle. We invoke consciousness to explain reportability, behavioral flexibility, attention, memory formation, etc. A theory that says consciousness is present without these functions makes consciousness epiphenomenal in a new way—not causally inert, but explanatorily inert. The distinction is between empirical adequacy (matching observations) and pragmatic adequacy (preserving consciousness's role in our explanatory practices). The key premise is that our resistance to certain theories isn't based on evidence they're false, but on evidence they'd make consciousness a useless posit.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Pragmatic-not-empirical-failure distinction is useful but the explanatory-idleness criterion needs more work to be compelling.

### U95. Testing consciousness theories requires solving the inverse problem: since many physical implementations could produce the same conscious experience, finding neural correlates tells us nothing about which theory correctly identifies the minimal sufficient conditions.

- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The widespread research program of testing theories by finding neural correlates of consciousness
- **Sketch:** Suppose we find that global workspace activation correlates perfectly with reported consciousness. This doesn't vindicate Global Workspace Theory over IIT, because IIT could be right about what consciousness IS (integrated information) while global workspace happens to be how human brains implement high integration. Similarly, finding high phi in conscious states doesn't vindicate IIT if global broadcasting is what phi measures in biological systems. This is an inverse problem: multiple theories at different levels could all be 'correct' about their own level. The discovery of neural correlates of consciousness systematically underdetermines theory choice. The key premise is that correlation studies identify sufficient conditions in actual human brains, but consciousness theories make claims about necessary and sufficient conditions across all possible implementations, which requires counterfactual knowledge we can't obtain empirically.
