- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The methodological naturalism shared by most consciousness scientists, particularly those who model their approach on discovering the nature of water or heat.
- **Sketch:** The natural kind strategy requires that pre-theoretical phenomenology picks out a unified phenomenon that scientific investigation then reveals the nature of. But consciousness isn't presented to us with clear boundaries—we're uncertain about animal consciousness, unconscious perception, partially attended states, etc. If we say 'investigate the clear cases first,' we face a dilemma: either the 'clear cases' have nothing theoretically interesting in common (eliminativism), or we define 'consciousness' as whatever our investigation of those cases reveals (stipulation). The middle position—that there's a pre-theoretical unity waiting to be discovered—requires the improbable coincidence that folk phenomenology happens to carve at the joints. Most consciousness theorists try to occupy this unstable middle ground.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The natural kind instability is a real problem but the dilemma may be overstated; middle positions exist in other sciences with vague boundaries.

---

### U100. First-person methodology cannot adjudicate between consciousness theories because any phenomenological report compatible with theory T is also compatible with 'I'm wrong about my phenomenology in exactly the way that would make T seem true.'

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Methodologies that privilege careful first-person reports as neutral data for theory confirmation, including Varela's neurophenomenology and Chalmers's phenomenal realism.
- **Sketch:** When subjects report their experience to test consciousness theories (e.g., reporting what it's like during inattentional blindness, or introspecting the grain of visual experience), we assume these reports provide theory-neutral data. But for any report R that supports theory T, the alternative hypothesis 'T is false and subjects systematically misreport their phenomenology in T-supporting ways' is empirically equivalent. We cannot distinguish genuine phenomenology from systematic introspective error without already knowing which theory is true. This undermines neurophenomenology, Chalmersian careful phenomenology, and any methodology that treats first-person reports as foundational evidence rather than mere behavioral data. The key premise is that introspective access doesn't come with built-in veridicality markers.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Systematic introspective error hypothesis is worth exploring but the empirical equivalence claim may be too strong given cross-validation possibilities.

---

### U101. Adversarial collaboration between theories reveals that IIT, Global Workspace Theory, and Higher-Order Thought theory make identical predictions about all behaviorally accessible states, proving they are notational variants rather than genuine competitors.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The assumption driving adversarial collaborations that Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Thought theory, and Integrated Information Theory are testing distinct empirical hypotheses
- **Sketch:** Recent adversarial collaborations claim to test between theories, but examination of their actual predictions shows they differ only in terminology. IIT's 'integrated information', GWT's 'global broadcast', and HOT's 'metacognitive representation' all predict the same reportable/unreportable divisions in humans. Where they seem to differ—regarding simple systems or unusual architectures—neither behavior nor report can adjudicate because these systems can't tell us what they experience. The theories diverge only in what they say about unobservable facts: whether a system we all agree shows no consciousness-behavior has 'integrated information' is a metaphysical dispute dressed as empirical science. The key premise is that theoretical terms that play identical roles in predicting all possible observations are notational variants, not substantive alternatives.
- **Appraiser (blind):** If true that predictions are identical this would be important, but the empirical claim is contestable and adversarial collaborations do find some differences.

---

### U102. Any theory that makes the presence of consciousness depend on extrinsic relational properties cannot be empirically distinguished from one that makes it depend on intrinsic properties, because all our evidence is itself extrinsically constituted.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Both internalist theories (like some versions of IIT) and externalist theories (like some functionalist accounts) who assume their dispute is empirically tractable
- **Sketch:** Consider theories making consciousness depend on functional role, evolutionary history, or social embedding versus theories making it depend on intrinsic neural dynamics. Our evidence—behavioral, neural, reportable—is all relationally constituted: it exists only in a web of external relations. Even 'direct' first-person evidence is mediated by memory, attention, and judgment systems that are themselves extrinsically characterized. This means no possible evidence could favor intrinsic over extrinsic theories: any datum an intrinsic theory explains by pointing to internal states, an extrinsic theory can explain by pointing to those states' relational properties. The theories are empirically equivalent but metaphysically incompatible, suggesting empirical methods alone cannot resolve the question.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The intrinsic/extrinsic empirical equivalence claim has been explored in various forms in the literature on functionalism and qualia.

---

### U103. Any consciousness theory that relies on 'seeming' evidence is committed to accepting that consciousness could have arisen through pure random variation with zero selective pressure.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Property dualists, illusionists, and any view that treats phenomenal intuitions as evidence while allowing consciousness could be causally inert
- **Sketch:** Theories that defend themselves by appeal to how things seem to us (phenomenal concepts, introspection, intuition pumps) must grant epistemic authority to these seemings. But our seemings about consciousness could only be evolutionarily reliable if consciousness contributed to fitness. If consciousness is epiphenomenal or if the theory allows that consciousness could have emerged without functional role, then our seemings about it are fitness-irrelevant and could have stabilized through pure drift. Therefore, such theories face a dilemma: either abandon seeming-based evidence (leaving them with no special evidence) or accept that consciousness must have been selected for (contradicting their allowance of epiphenomenal or functionless consciousness).
- **Appraiser (blind):** The evolutionary reliability of introspection has been discussed; the dilemma is interesting but not novel enough.

---

### U104. Consciousness theories that appeal to neural correlates are committed to the view that consciousness research in 2100 will look like phlogiston theory looks to us now.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Empirically-minded consciousness researchers and philosophers who think neuroscience will resolve theoretical disputes (Crick, Koch, Tononi, Dehaene)
- **Sketch:** Neural correlate research assumes that consciousness theories can be tested by finding which neural patterns correlate with consciousness. But correlations only support a theory if we have independent access to both relata. We only 'access' consciousness through reports and behaviors—the same evidence available for the neural side. This means neural correlates can never distinguish between 'patterns that realize consciousness' and 'patterns that merely cause consciousness-reports.' Any pattern we identify is equally compatible with illusionism (these patterns cause consciousness-talk without consciousness) and realism (these patterns are consciousness). Therefore, neural correlate research cannot make progress on the theory question. If consciousness scientists are making genuine discoveries, it must be because consciousness was a confused concept being replaced by precise functional categories—exactly how oxygen replaced phlogiston.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The illusionism point about neural correlates has been made; the phlogiston analogy adds rhetorical force but little new substance.

---

### U105. Committing to any scientific theory of consciousness requires accepting that there are facts about consciousness in systems that cannot in principle verify those facts themselves, which means accepting philosophical zombies are possible in the relevant sense.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Functionalists and deflationists who reject zombie scenarios while simultaneously advocating for scientific theories that must make determinate claims about non-reportive systems
- **Sketch:** Consider a scientific theory that identifies consciousness with physical property P. Such a theory must make verdicts about edge cases: thermostat-like systems, patient in vegetative states, cerebral organoids. In some of these cases, the system cannot possibly tell us whether it's conscious. But if the theory is to be action-guiding (for medical decisions, AI safety, etc.), it must nonetheless deliver a verdict. This commits the theorist to holding that facts about consciousness can obtain even when no verification is possible from the subject's perspective. But this is precisely what makes philosophical zombies conceptually coherent—the possibility of consciousness facts that float free of functional verification. The key premise is that scientific adequacy requires determinate verdicts about cases that cannot self-verify.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The connection between scientific theories and zombie conceivability has been explored in the literature on functionalism and phenomenal concepts.

---

### U106. Empirical tests can distinguish consciousness theories only if we first solve the distribution question—whether consciousness admits of degrees or multiple independent realizations—which cannot itself be settled empirically.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Empirical consciousness researchers who believe theory-choice can be driven by experimental results (Tononi, Dehaene, Seth, etc.)
- **Sketch:** Current consciousness science assumes we can test theories (IIT, GWT, HOT, etc.) by finding neural correlates or running dissociation experiments. But any such test presupposes an answer to whether consciousness is all-or-nothing or gradable, and whether the same conscious state can be multiply realized. For instance, if we find that neural pattern X correlates with report of experience Y in humans but not in octopuses, this could mean: (1) the theory is wrong, (2) octopuses lack that experience, (3) octopuses have it but realize it differently, or (4) octopuses have a degraded version of it. Which interpretation we choose depends on prior commitments about distribution that no amount of correlation data can adjudicate. The empirical project thus inherits an unsolvable metaphysical debt.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Distribution question matters but the unsolvability claim is overstated; some empirical traction seems possible.

---

### U107. Any empirical test that could distinguish between consciousness theories would necessarily either create new conscious subjects or destroy existing ones, meaning genuine theory-testing faces an insurmountable ethical barrier.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The assumption shared by both theorists and critics that this is fundamentally an epistemic or methodological problem rather than an ethical one
- **Sketch:** Consider what it would actually take to distinguish Global Workspace from Integrated Information Theory: we would need to create systems with high phi but no global broadcast, or vice versa, then determine if they're conscious. The key premise is that discriminating tests require building edge cases—systems specifically designed to satisfy one theory's criteria but not another's. But if either theory might be correct, we're either creating conscious beings to be experimental subjects or building systems we cannot know are non-conscious and treating them as mere apparatus. The dispute is therefore not primarily epistemological but ethical: we cannot get the evidence needed because getting it would require unacceptable moral risks.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The ethical barrier to testing is worth noting but the claim that it's insurmountable overstates the case; edge cases exist naturally.

---

### U108. Consciousness theories are empirically testable in principle but only across evolutionary timescales—we can tell which theory is right by seeing which one successfully predicts the convergent evolution of consciousness-like phenomena in independent lineages.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The synchronic, neuroscience-focused methodology dominant in both consciousness science and philosophy of mind
- **Sketch:** The standard debate assumes we test theories synchronically (scanning brains, behavioral tests, comparing systems now). But the key premise is that consciousness exists because it solves specific evolutionary problems, and true theories should predict that similar solutions evolve independently when those problems recur. If IIT is right, we should find high-phi systems evolving in any lineage facing certain environmental pressures; if Global Workspace is right, we should find convergent evolution of broadcasting architectures. The actual evidence lies in paleobiology and comparative evolution—cephalopod consciousness, for instance, is a natural experiment. We're asking the wrong organisms at the wrong timescale; the answer requires waiting for or reconstructing evolutionary convergence.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Evolutionary convergence as evidence is an interesting angle but the timescale makes it impractical and the approach has been suggested before.

---

### U109. The question 'which theory of consciousness is right?' presupposes that consciousness picks out a natural kind, but our inability to settle the question empirically is evidence that it doesn't—consciousness is instead a functional role term that different physical systems fill in different ways.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across IIT, Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Thought theories, and their critics that there is a fact of the matter about what physically constitutes consciousness
- **Sketch:** The argument proceeds by analogy to 'gene': before molecular biology, there were competing theories (Mendelian factors, developmental determinants, etc.), but the discovery that multiple molecular mechanisms implement genetic functions revealed that 'gene' was never naming a single natural kind. Similarly, our persistent inability to decide between competing consciousness theories despite accumulating neuroscience suggests we're not discovering which substrate realizes consciousness-as-natural-kind, but rather that different neural (or computational) architectures implement the functional role we associate with consciousness. The key premise is that persistent empirical underdetermination, despite relevant data accumulation, is diagnostic of role-terms rather than kind-terms.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Gene analogy is familiar; persistent underdetermination as diagnostic of role-terms is interesting but needs stronger argument.

---

### U110. Any test that could definitively adjudicate between consciousness theories would require conscious-to-unconscious transitions that violate the continuity of actual neural processes, making the question empirically underdetermined in principle rather than merely in practice.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Proponents of empirical methodology for consciousness science who believe we just need better experiments (Dehaene, Koch, Tononi)
- **Sketch:** Theories like IIT and GWT make different predictions about which systems are conscious, but testing these requires creating systems that one theory says are conscious and another says are not. However, the argument shows that any physical implementation lies on continuous dimensions (integration level, global broadcasting degree, etc.), and conscious-unconscious boundaries on these continua are precisely what's in dispute. To test the theories, we'd need sharp boundaries in nature where the theories disagree, but the commitment to physicalism that all major theories share entails that the underlying processes are continuous. The key premise is that theoretical disputes about categorical boundaries cannot be resolved by observations of continuous underlying processes.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The continuity argument has been made in various forms; showing that categorical boundaries can't be tested on continuous processes is familiar territory.

---

### U111. Disagreement about which beings are conscious reveals that competing theories are actually answering different questions about different target phenomena.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The framing of consciousness theories as competing explanations of a single phenomenon, assumed by meta-theorists and researchers attempting to adjudicate between theories
- **Sketch:** When IIT says a thermostat might be minimally conscious while GWT says it clearly isn't, this looks like empirical disagreement. But the arguments reveal they're tracking different phenomena: IIT tracks 'what it's like-ness' as an intrinsic property of information integration, while GWT tracks reportability and cognitive access. The theories diverge on edge cases (simple systems, unconscious processing) precisely where these two phenomena come apart. The illusion of genuine disagreement comes from both using 'consciousness' as the label, but if we asked 'which theory better explains subjective experience?' versus 'which better explains the cognitive phenomenon we access through report?', we'd see these aren't rival answers to one question but decent answers to two questions. The real dispute is which question deserves the term 'consciousness,' which is terminological.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The 'different questions' diagnosis is common in meta-debates; calling it terminological rather than substantive doesn't add much leverage.

---

### U112. The demand that empirical evidence settle which theory of consciousness is correct presupposes that consciousness has a discoverable essence, but if consciousness is instead a homeostatic property cluster, different theories could be tracking different stable patterns in the same phenomena without any being uniquely correct.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The shared methodological assumption across consciousness science that empirical evidence should ultimately adjudicate between theories
- **Sketch:** Arguments about how to empirically test consciousness theories typically assume there's a fact of the matter about what consciousness really is that evidence could reveal. But this commits these arguments to essentialism about consciousness. If we accept Boyd's homeostatic property cluster account of natural kinds, consciousness might be held together by causal mechanisms rather than necessary features. Different theories (HOT, GWT, IIT) might be tracking different stable clusters of properties that reliably co-occur without any single theory capturing the essence. The argument turns on showing that the evidential demand itself smuggles in a metaphysical assumption that consciousness theorists officially reject when doing metaphysics of mind.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Applying homeostatic property clusters to consciousness is interesting but the payoff—that multiple theories could be partially right—is somewhat obvious.

---

### U113. The intractability of consciousness theory adjudication demonstrates that 'consciousness' functions linguistically as a success term rather than a natural kind term, which is why every theory can claim to explain consciousness while seeming to explain different things.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The assumption shared by both sides of methodology debates that consciousness picks out a natural phenomenon about which we could be wrong
- **Sketch:** When we examine theory disputes, proponents talk past each other because 'consciousness' works like 'knowledge' or 'justice'—it marks whatever satisfies certain normative/success conditions rather than picking out a unified phenomenon. IIT identifies consciousness with integrated information because consciousness is supposed to be unified. HOT identifies it with meta-representation because consciousness is supposed to involve self-awareness. Each theory stipulates that consciousness is whatever meets the criterion they privilege. This explains why theories seem incommensurable and why empirical evidence doesn't settle disputes—we're not discovering what consciousness is but negotiating what should count as consciousness. The key premise is that linguistic analysis of how theorists actually deploy the term reveals success-term grammar.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The success-term analysis has been applied elsewhere; showing consciousness works this way clarifies but doesn't dramatically advance the debate.

---

### U114. Consciousness theories are actually testable, but only diachronically: the right theory is whichever one still generates novel, confirmed predictions about what feels like what after 50 years of normal science, making current 'crucial experiments' premature.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both Chalmers-style pessimists about ever settling consciousness theories empirically and those seeking 'adversarial collaboration' crucial experiments now
- **Sketch:** The debate treats theory choice in consciousness as requiring synchronic adjudication through present evidence, leading to pessimism about decidability. But this misunderstands how theoretical maturity works in science. Early quantum mechanics and evolution both faced similar complaints about testability. The key premise is that theories of consciousness, like other scientific theories, should be evaluated by their long-term fertility—whether they continue opening new predictive domains. IIT, GWT, HOT, etc. should be assessed by whether they do what good theories do: survive attempts at falsification while generating unexpected correct predictions about cross-modal binding, metacognitive feelings, developmental trajectories, edge cases in split-brain patients, etc. The theory that's still standing after decades of this process, rather than winning a single decisive test, is the right one.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The diachronic fertility criterion is standard philosophy of science; applying it here is sensible but doesn't illuminate what makes consciousness special.

---

### U115. The fact that we cannot currently adjudicate between consciousness theories is itself evidence against higher-order theories, because if consciousness required meta-representation, we should be able to determine what's conscious by checking for the meta-state, but we can't.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Higher-order thought theories and representationalist theories more broadly, which claim functional organization constitutes consciousness
- **Sketch:** Higher-order theories claim that a mental state is conscious iff there's a higher-order representation of it. This makes consciousness a relational property—the presence of a specific cognitive architecture. If this were true, determining what's conscious should be easier than for competitor theories: just check whether the higher-order representation exists. We have good third-person methods for detecting representations and their contents. But we manifestly cannot reliably detect consciousness this way—hence the whole debate about measurability. The key premise: if consciousness were constituted by a functionally-specifiable relation, detecting that relation should be more tractable than the hard problem suggests. Our inability to settle consciousness questions is evidence that consciousness is not a matter of functional organization or meta-representation, undermining HOT, but consistent with IIT (where integration is much harder to measure) or even dualism.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Uses measurability difficulty as evidence against HOT, but the inference is too quick—many factors could explain measurement difficulty.

---

### U116. The dispute about which neural correlates of consciousness are constitutive appears empirical but is actually settled by one's prior commitment to whether consciousness could exist in radically different substrates.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The NCC research program's assumption that empirical investigation can determine which correlates are constitutive versus merely correlated
- **Sketch:** Debates about whether the NCC is in prefrontal cortex vs. posterior cortex, or whether recurrent processing vs. feedforward processing matters, appear to be straightforwardly empirical. But these debates implicitly assume an answer to substrate-independence: if you think consciousness requires specifically biological neurons, you'll interpret the same data as showing constitution; if you think consciousness could exist in silicon, you'll interpret identical data as merely correlational. The key premise is that no amount of correlation data, even perfect correlation, can settle whether the correlation is constitutive without first settling the substrate question—because constitution requires necessity, and we can only judge necessity by our prior modal commitments.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Constitution/correlation distinction depending on substrate-independence is familiar; doesn't advance debate significantly.

---

### U117. Any empirical test that could distinguish between consciousness theories would require that consciousness makes a causal difference beyond its functional correlates, but accepting that consciousness has such causal powers commits functionalists to epiphenomenalism being false, which commits them to either dualism or strong emergence—both of which they explicitly reject.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Functionalist philosophers who participate in consciousness science (Dennett, Frankish, Humphrey) while maintaining consciousness is 'just' functional organization
- **Sketch:** Functionalists like Dennett claim consciousness is identical to certain functional states, which means consciousness has whatever causal powers those functional states have—no more, no less. But to empirically test between theories, we need consciousness to leave some distinctive causal trace that its functional correlates alone wouldn't produce. If we find such a trace, it proves consciousness has causal powers beyond the functional, contradicting functionalism. If we don't find one, we have no way to test theories empirically. The key premise is that 'causal difference beyond functional correlates' is the only way to generate differential empirical predictions. This means functionalists who want testable theories of consciousness are committed to denying functionalism.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The dilemma for functionalists has been extensively discussed; the causal exclusion problem and epiphenomenalism worries are familiar terrain.

---

### U118. The correct theory of consciousness is whichever one best predicts the boundary conditions where consciousness disappears, not the one that best explains paradigm cases of consciousness.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared methodology in consciousness studies that treats theoretical virtues (parsimony, explanatory power for clear cases, scientific naturalism) as primary evidence
- **Sketch:** Philosophers treat consciousness theories as competing explanations of clear cases (wakeful humans, perhaps some animals), to be evaluated by theoretical virtues like parsimony and integration with science. But this gets the epistemology backwards: we already know consciousness exists in paradigm cases regardless of which theory is right. The real discriminatory power lies at the margins—determining whether octopuses are conscious, whether split-brain patients have one or two streams of consciousness, whether locked-in patients without reportability are conscious, whether progressively simplified neural networks remain conscious. A theory earns its keep by getting these boundary conditions right, and we can check its predictions against our evolving intuitive verdicts in edge cases far more reliably than we can adjudicate abstract theoretical considerations.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Prioritizing boundary conditions over paradigm cases is methodologically sound but not particularly novel; this is how many already approach theory testing.

---

### U119. We can determine which consciousness theory is correct by examining which theory's proponents update their views most rationally when presented with borderline cases they initially got wrong.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The assumption shared across debates that we must evaluate theories synchronically rather than diachronically
- **Sketch:** Instead of trying to identify which theory best fits the data, we should examine the meta-level question: which theoretical framework generates a research program with better error-correction mechanisms? The key premise is that theories are not static but evolve, and we can evaluate their epistemic credentials by tracking how they respond to recalcitrant cases. When Global Workspace Theory faced evidence of unconscious working memory, or when Higher-Order Theories confronted cases of phenomenology without meta-awareness, did the frameworks accommodate these findings through principled modifications or ad hoc patches? This shifts the question from 'which theory is right now?' to 'which theory-tradition has the methodological resources to become right?'—and that question is empirically tractable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Meta-level evaluation of research programs is Lakatosian territory; applying it to consciousness is reasonable but the methodological move is established.

---

### U120. The correct theory of consciousness is whichever one makes the fewest predictions about consciousness distribution, because any prediction about whether X is conscious that goes beyond what we can verify is unfalsifiable metaphysics masquerading as science.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The shared assumption that theories of consciousness should be evaluated like standard scientific theories, where broader scope and more predictions count in favor of a theory
- **Sketch:** Consciousness researchers typically prefer theories that make bold, determinate predictions (IIT says this photodiode has non-zero consciousness, GWT says this patient in minimally conscious state lacks consciousness, etc.). But this is a counterexample to the standard scientific virtue of preferring more predictive theories. The key premise: in domains where we have no independent verification method, making more predictions is a vice not a virtue—it simply multiplies your unfalsifiable commitments. A theory that says 'consciousness exists in paradigm cases and I cannot tell you about edge cases' is more epistemically honest than one that confidently pronounces on every edge case. Therefore, consciousness researchers should prefer the most conservative theory—perhaps a restricted HOT or attention schema theory that makes minimal commitments—rather than ambitious frameworks like IIT or Global Workspace Theory.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The conservatism recommendation is defensible but the argument that predictiveness is a vice in unverifiable domains, while interesting, goes against scientific intuitions without sufficient payoff.

---

### U121. Testing consciousness theories is impossible in principle because any behavioral or neural evidence that could confirm a theory would equally confirm that the theory identifies the functional role that evolution selected for playing the consciousness-broadcasting role in social contexts.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Non-functionalist consciousness researchers (like Chalmers or IIT theorists) who think their theories are empirically testable
- **Sketch:** The argument turns on the premise that consciousness theories generate predictions only via their claims about functional/computational organization. But any functional organization that makes a system genuinely conscious would ALSO make it adaptive for that system to behave as if conscious in social settings (report experiences, act on the basis of them, etc.). This means evolution would select for the same functional organization whether it produces consciousness or merely plays the consciousness-role in the social game. Therefore, holding that we can empirically test consciousness theories commits you to holding that consciousness is whatever plays the consciousness-role—a form of functionalism that non-functionalists explicitly reject.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The evolutionary argument has been made in various forms; the specific formulation adds little new leverage.

---

### U122. Any empirical test that could distinguish consciousness theories must rely on behavioral reports, but the reliability of such reports is itself theory-laden in a way that makes crucial experiments impossible: theories that predict consciousness without report can always dismiss negative behavioral evidence, while theories that tie consciousness to report cannot be challenged by behavioral data.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Philosophers like Dennett and Frankish who think careful behavioral experiments can be theory-neutral arbiters, and scientists who design experiments assuming reportability is neutral evidence
- **Sketch:** Consider testing whether phenomenal consciousness requires global workspace broadcasting. If subjects don't report experiences in cases of local processing, GWT claims no consciousness occurred, while first-order theories claim consciousness occurred but wasn't reportable. If subjects do report, both theories are compatible with it. The asymmetry: GWT makes behavioral evidence decisive (no report = no consciousness), immunizing it against behavioral counterexamples. First-order theories allow consciousness without report, but then what behavioral evidence could challenge them? They become empirically unfalsifiable by the very methods consciousness science employs. The key premise is that behavioral report is our only window into consciousness in others, but theories differ on whether report is constitutive of or merely evidential for consciousness, and this difference makes the evidential relation itself theory-dependent in a vicious way.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The theory-ladenness of behavioral evidence is well-established; this application to consciousness adds little conceptual novelty.

---

### U123. Testing consciousness theories requires first-person access to phenomenology, yet this very requirement guarantees that any empirical test will be question-begging against theories that deny the evidential status of first-person reports.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The shared assumption across IIT, GNW, HOT, and other empirical programs that we can adjudicate theories through prediction-testing against known conscious/unconscious cases
- **Sketch:** The standard methodology assumes we validate consciousness theories against paradigm cases of conscious/unconscious states identified via first-person report. But theories like illusionism or strong representationalism reject that first-person reports reliably track consciousness itself—they track only functionally-defined states that seem conscious. So any empirical test must either: (a) presuppose that first-person reports are veridical about consciousness (begging the question against illusionism), or (b) use only third-person measures (but then we've abandoned the explanandum). The argument turns on showing that the calibration problem—how we know what counts as a paradigm conscious state—cannot be solved neutrally between these theories.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The calibration problem against illusionism has been discussed; this formulation doesn't add sufficient new structure.

---

### U124. The debate over testing consciousness theories falsely assumes a single target phenomenon, when 'consciousness' refers to at least three empirically dissociable processes (arousal/wakefulness, attention/selection, and phenomenal presence), and theories are actually correctly describing different phenomena.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The framing of the consciousness theory landscape as competing explanations of a unified explanandum
- **Sketch:** GNW excels at explaining reportability and access; IIT captures something about intrinsic integration present even in unconscious states; HOT theories track a metacognitive capacity that comes apart from both. Rather than competing explanations of one thing, they're adequate explanations of different things we've confused under one label. The argument turns on showing empirical dissociations: preserved integration without access (vegetative states), access without integration (split attention), phenomenal states without either (unattended experiences). The inability to test which theory is 'right' is a category mistake—it's like asking whether theories of vision or memory are 'right' about perception.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Multiple-phenomena diagnosis is familiar; empirical dissociations are known; doesn't sufficiently advance understanding.

---

### U125. Any empirical test designed to adjudicate between consciousness theories will presuppose the correctness of one of them, because consciousness is only observable through behavioral or neural proxies whose connection to consciousness is precisely what's disputed.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Methodological optimists like those proposing adversarial collaborations (Mudrik, Koch, et al.) and 'consciousness science' as empirically tractable
- **Sketch:** The argument proceeds from a circularity diagnosis: To test whether theory T correctly identifies consciousness with process P, we need independent access to when consciousness occurs. But if we use reports, we presuppose that reportability tracks consciousness (favoring Global Workspace Theory). If we use attention, we presuppose attention suffices (favoring Attention Schema Theory). If we use integration measures, we presuppose IIT. The seemingly stable methodological position—'we should design empirical tests'—collapses because every test design smuggles in the conclusion. This affects even sophisticated proposals like adversarial collaboration or critical experiments, since choosing which dissociations matter (report without phenomenology? phenomenology without report?) already takes sides. The position 'we can empirically adjudicate this debate' is thus unstable: it necessarily assumes what it claims to test.
- **Appraiser (blind):** General circularity diagnosis that's been made before; this version doesn't add sufficient new structure or examples.

### U126. The debate over consciousness theories is actually a debate over whether subjective reports are authoritative about their own causes, disguised as a debate about mechanisms.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Participants who think this is a straightforwardly empirical debate about which brain processes constitute consciousness (Koch, Dehaene, Lau, etc.)
- **Sketch:** The argument reframes by showing that methodological disagreements do all the work. Higher-Order theorists treat subjective reports as potentially mistaken about what makes them conscious (you might report phenomenology without having it). Global Workspace and first-order theorists treat reports as authoritative (if you report it, it's conscious). These commitments about report-authority determine which dissociations count as evidence: whether unconscious information processing plus HOT yields phenomenology (HOT says yes, first-order says no) just is the question of whether reports can be mistaken about phenomenology's presence. The apparent dispute about mechanisms (workspace vs. hierarchy vs. recurrence) is downstream from this methodological disagreement. Once we see this, we recognize the debate is about epistemic authority, not metaphysics. This explains why evidence never settles it: each side interprets dissociations through their prior about report-reliability.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Report-authority as fundamental disagreement is insightful but needs development to show it's really doing all the work.

### U127. Theories of consciousness become empirically distinguishable only when they make mistakes about the extension of consciousness, so the correct theory must be the one that gets phenomenology right everywhere but is maximally conservative about novel cases.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared methodological assumption that we need some revolutionary new empirical test or that current theories are empirically equivalent pending future neuroscience
- **Sketch:** Most debate assumes we need some 'special test' to distinguish theories. But actually, theories already make empirical predictions - they predict what has consciousness. Higher-order theories say the right-order representations suffice; IIT says the right information integration suffices; GNW says the right broadcasting suffices. The theories only seem empirically equivalent because proponents keep adjusting their theories when faced with counterexamples (refusing to bite bullets about edge cases). The key premise is that our actual evidence IS the full range of what does and doesn't have phenomenology - including our judgments about whether foetuses, dreamless sleep, split-brain patients, etc. are conscious. The theory that's correct is the one that wouldn't need post-hoc adjustment when we get clearer on these cases. This shifts the question from 'what future test could help?' to 'which theory is already accommodating vs. predicting our full phenomenological landscape?'
- **Appraiser (blind):** The conservative extension-fitting approach has been proposed; this doesn't add enough to the existing methodological discussion.

### U128. If a theory of consciousness can't be wrong about p-zombies being metaphysically possible, then it can't be the kind of thing empirical evidence could show to be right.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Both physicalist theories (IIT, GNW, HOT) that claim to be empirical while remaining neutral on metaphysical zombie scenarios, and debates that treat empirical evidence as relevant while maintaining zombie intuitions
- **Sketch:** Most scientific theories can in principle be empirically wrong about modal questions (evolution could have been wrong about whether life could arise from non-life; physics could be wrong about whether perpetual motion is possible). But consciousness theories are typically formulated to be compatible with either position on zombie possibility - physicalists say their theory shows zombies are impossible, dualists say it shows they're possible, but the empirical content is meant to be neutral. The key premise is that empirical confirmability requires modal commitments - a theory must stake out positions on what's possible that could turn out to be false. If your 'theory' is compatible with zombies being possible and with them being impossible, it's not making the kind of claim that evidence can bear on; it's more like a conceptual framework than a scientific hypothesis. This means most consciousness theories, as currently formulated, aren't in the game of being empirically testable at all.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The connection between modal commitments and empirical testability has been explored; this restates familiar worries about consciousness theories' metaphysical flexibility.

### U129. Any empirical test that could distinguish consciousness theories must rely on behavioral or neural patterns that one theory predicts and another doesn't, but every major theory can be trivially modified to predict any such pattern by adjusting what it says about the relationship between consciousness and its signatures, making the theories empirically equivalent in principle.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Empirically-oriented consciousness researchers (Koch, Tononi, Dehaene) who believe their frameworks make distinctive empirical predictions
- **Sketch:** Consider any proposed test—say, showing that certain neural patterns correlate with reports of consciousness. IIT could say 'those patterns indicate high phi'; GWT could say 'those patterns indicate global broadcast'; HOT could say 'those patterns indicate higher-order representation.' Each theory has enough theoretical degrees of freedom to accommodate any correlation we find. The key premise is that consciousness theories necessarily include bridge principles connecting their posited conscious states to measurable outputs, and these bridge principles can always be adjusted without changing the core theory. This means what look like distinct empirical theories are actually empirically equivalent cores plus adjustable auxiliary hypotheses—the debates are therefore primarily conceptual, not empirical, and those who claim we can empirically test our way to the answer are confused about the logical structure of consciousness theories.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The Duhem-Quine problem applied to consciousness theories; the adjustable bridge principles point is familiar from philosophy of science generally.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption among IIT, GWT, HOT theorists that their disagreement is straightforwardly empirical
- **Sketch:** The argument turns on the premise that any empirical test requires a measurable dependent variable, and the only measurable variables we have access to are behavioral/neural markers of reportability. When theories make predictions about 'what it's like' independently of any reportability measure, they've left the domain of empirical testability. The debate about testing theories of consciousness is actually a debate about whether unreportable consciousness exists—if it doesn't, all testable theories are really theories of access/report, not phenomenal consciousness. This reframes the testing problem: we're not trying to find better experiments to adjudicate between theories; we're facing a conceptual issue about whether the target phenomenon is even the kind of thing that could distinguish competing theories.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The point that we only measure reportability is recognized; reframing as about whether unreportable consciousness exists doesn't add sufficient novelty.

### U131. The assumption that consciousness is a natural kind is what makes theory-testing seem tractable, but consciousness is actually a motley class unified only by historical linguistic practice.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The natural kind assumption shared by both eliminativists who deny consciousness exists and realists who affirm it
- **Sketch:** The shared premise among theory-testing optimists is that consciousness is a natural kind—a unified phenomenon carved at nature's joints—such that discovering the right theory is like discovering the nature of water or heat. The counterexample: consider that 'consciousness' groups together phenomenal states as diverse as color qualia, pain, thoughts about mathematics, and the sense of self. These may have nothing scientifically important in common beyond our labeling them 'conscious.' Just as 'jade' turned out to be two different minerals (jadeite and nephrite), consciousness might be multiple unrelated phenomena. If so, there is no single correct theory to test for—IIT might be right about some cases, GWT about others, and HOT about still others. The testing problem is intractable not because consciousness is mysterious, but because we're trying to find a unified explanation for a disunified phenomenon.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The jade analogy and disunity claim have been made; suggesting consciousness isn't a natural kind is already a live position in the debate.

### U132. If consciousness theories cannot currently be empirically distinguished, this shows they are addressing different phenomena rather than competing explanations of the same thing.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The widespread assumption that consciousness theories are competitors in a race that empirical evidence will eventually decide
- **Sketch:** The argument turns on the premise that genuine scientific theories about the same phenomenon must eventually make divergent predictions, even if we currently lack the technology to test them. When putative theories remain empirically indistinguishable in principle (not just in practice), this is diagnostic of them implicitly operationalizing different dependent variables. For instance, IIT's focus on integrated information, HOT's focus on metacognitive access, and GWT's focus on broadcast availability likely track distinct (though correlated) features of mental life. The persistence of the 'hard problem' reflects our treating these as competing when they're actually orthogonal dimensions requiring a multi-factor account.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The claim that persistent empirical equivalence indicates different phenomena is interesting but the multi-factor conclusion is already suggested by some researchers.

### U133. The empirical tractability of consciousness theories depends on consciousness being modular rather than holistic, but consciousness theories themselves generally entail holism.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The shared assumption across neuroscience and philosophy that consciousness theories make testable predictions via contrastive analysis
- **Sketch:** Scientific testing requires isolating variables and identifying minimal sufficient conditions—the modular assumption that you can have consciousness-of-X without the full cognitive system. Yet major theories imply holism: IIT requires system-wide integration, global workspace requires broadcast to multiple subsystems, predictive processing makes consciousness emerge from whole-brain hierarchical prediction. This generates a counterexample to the shared assumption that these theories are empirically testable: testing requires decomposition, but the theories deny decomposability. You cannot test whether 'just' the global workspace suffices because removing other systems changes what the workspace is. The commitment to empirical methods presupposes modularity, while the theories' own logic entails holism.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Modularity/holism tension is real but theories may have resources to handle it; doesn't definitively show untestability.

### U134. Theories of consciousness are empirically distinguishable only if they make different predictions about borderline cases we haven't built yet, not about normal human consciousness.

- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across Chalmers, Dennett, and Tononi that existing neuroscience or introspection could adjudicate between theories
- **Sketch:** The debate assumes theories can be tested against paradigm cases (wakeful humans, dreamless sleep, etc.), but all major theories were constructed to accommodate exactly these cases. The argument turns on the premise that theories constructed to fit the same data necessarily make the same predictions about that data. What distinguishes them are predictions about edge cases: partial brain emulations, split-brain patients with one hemisphere anesthetized, systems with structural but not biological similarity to brains, or artificial systems we haven't built. The dispute about 'how to tell which theory is right' has been misconstrued as epistemological when it's actually about which experimental programs to prioritize.

### U135. The position that 'we need better measures of consciousness' is unstable because improving a measure requires already knowing which theory is correct.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The IIT research program and other measurement-focused approaches that claim to remain neutral between theories while developing metrics
- **Sketch:** Many researchers propose developing better measures (phi, global workspace metrics, etc.) to test theories. But measure-improvement requires validation: checking the measure against ground truth. In consciousness science, what serves as ground truth? Either verbal reports (but theories disagree on when reports track consciousness) or theoretical commitments about what has consciousness. The key premise is that measure validation is inherently circular in consciousness science—you calibrate your measure against cases where you already know the answer, but knowing the answer presupposes a theory. This position collapses into either 'assume a theory to test theories' (incoherent) or 'accept verbal report as definitive' (which most theorists explicitly reject for cases like locked-in syndrome, attention, or animal consciousness).
- **Appraiser (blind):** Measure-improvement circularity is concerning but may be manageable through iterative refinement; not clearly fatal.

- **Sketch:** The apparent empirical underdetermination between theories like Global Workspace, Higher-Order Thought, and Integrated Information Theory dissolves when we recognize they're answering different questions disguised as the same question. GWT asks 'what computational architecture enables broadcast?', HOT asks 'what makes a state a conscious state rather than an unconscious one?', and IIT asks 'what physical systems have irreducible causation?'. Each theory specifies different adequacy conditions for explanation—computational sufficiency, conceptual necessity, or mathematical optimality. The disagreement is meta-theoretical: they dispute what philosophical work a theory of consciousness must do. This explains why they agree on much phenomenological data while seeming incompatible.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The 'different questions' diagnosis is familiar; doesn't sufficiently explain why this matters for methodology or what follows.

### U137. The appearance of competing consciousness theories is an artifact of conflating what consciousness is with what consciousness does, and no theory can be wrong about both.

- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The assumption that theories like IIT, GWT, and PP are mutually exclusive alternatives
- **Sketch:** Current theories equivocate between constitutive claims (what consciousness is made of) and functional claims (what role consciousness plays). IIT makes primarily constitutive claims about integrated information; GWT makes primarily functional claims about cognitive access; Predictive Processing makes claims about computational role. When theories seem to conflict, they're actually answering different questions: IIT specifies the substrate, GWT specifies the function, PP specifies the algorithm. A theory can be wrong about substrate while right about function (perhaps consciousness requires integrated information but doesn't serve global broadcast) or vice versa. This reclassifies the debate: instead of competing theories, we have partial specifications along different dimensions that could be combined or independently evaluated.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Constitution/function/algorithm distinction is useful but whether theories are really compatible along these dimensions needs work.

The key premise is that no empirical finding could force someone to extend moral status to machines if they're determined not to—they'll always reinterpret the data through a biological lens. The debate looks like disagreement about neural mechanisms but functions to coordinate moral boundaries.

### U139. Commitment to testing consciousness theories through third-person methods commits you to the claim that consciousness could have been absent from a world physically identical to ours, making consciousness epiphenomenal—yet researchers who accept the methods deny epiphenomenalism.

But if it supervenes on the physical and is still a separate property requiring theory (not just identical to physical states), then we can conceive of the physical substrate without the consciousness—making consciousness causally idle. The key premise is that you can't have it both ways: either consciousness is nothing over and above the measurable physical facts (eliminativism), or it's something additional that makes no difference to those facts (epiphenomenalism). Researchers like Tononi and Dehaene reject both options but their methodology commits them to one or the other.

The key premise is that there's no theory-neutral way to identify the explanandum: what we're trying to explain (reportability? subjective character? unity?) determines which theory will fit. ### U141. Testing consciousness theories requires solving the inverse problem in neuroscience, which is provably unsolvable for consciousness specifically because we lack the direct measurement that makes inverse problems tractable in other domains.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Methodological assumptions underlying adversarial collaboration approaches and neuroscience-based testing protocols
- **Sketch:** In neuroscience, inverse problems involve inferring hidden causes from observed effects (e.g., inferring neural sources from EEG). These are mathematically underdetermined but practically tractable when we can validate inferences through direct measurement of the hidden causes. The key distinction is between inverse problems where the hidden variable is eventually directly measurable (like recording from the inferred neural population) and those where it is not. Consciousness occupies a unique position: we can never directly measure another system's phenomenal experience to validate our inverse inferences from behavioral/neural data to conscious states. This means the inverse problem for consciousness lacks the validation loop that makes such problems scientifically tractable elsewhere. Therefore, any empirical testing program is structurally different from normal scientific inference.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The inverse problem point has been made in various forms; the uniqueness claim about consciousness lacking validation loops is not sufficiently novel.

Evidence about brain function underdetermines theory choice because theories differ in their criteria for ascription: one counts only reportable states, another counts richly structured experience, another counts nothing but dispositions to judge. Option (1) abandons the explanandum; option (2) makes testing impossible. Any middle path that tries to use third-person evidence to confirm first-person claims requires a bridge principle (e.g., 'reports track phenomenology'), but that bridge principle is itself what the theories disagree about, returning us to circularity.

- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across Chalmers, Dehaene, Tononi, and methodological reformers like Kanai that we can settle the empirical methodology question independently of metaphysical commitments
- **Sketch:** Current debates about methodology (neural correlates vs. function vs. reportability) appear to be about experimental design, but they actually inherit their structure from incompatible starting assumptions about explanatory direction. Those who think phenomenal properties are basic (like IIT proponents) demand different evidence than those who think functional/computational properties are basic (like higher-order theorists), not because they disagree about experimental rigor, but because they disagree about what grounds what. The key premise: evidential standards are not theory-neutral but depend on which properties you take to be doing the explaining versus which are being explained. This means the methodological impasse is actually a metaphysical one in disguise, and appeals to 'just do better experiments' miss the point entirely.
- **Appraiser (blind):** That methodological disputes reflect metaphysical ones is familiar; the explanatory priority point doesn't add much novelty.

---

### U153. The standard demand that consciousness theories make 'testable predictions' requires a background theory of the relationship between consciousness and behavior that is itself one of the contested theories of consciousness.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Methodological naturalists like Dennett and mainstream cognitive neuroscientists who treat 'makes testable predictions' as a neutral constraint on acceptable theories
- **Sketch:** Philosophers and scientists demand that consciousness theories generate testable predictions, but testing requires behavioral or neural measures as proxies for consciousness. Which measures count as valid proxies depends on your theory of how consciousness relates to its effects—that is, on a background theory of consciousness itself. For instance, if you think consciousness requires reportability (a theoretical claim), you'll accept verbal report as a test; if you don't, you won't. The key premise: there is no theory-neutral translation manual from 'subject is conscious of X' to observable predictions. Therefore, the methodological standard of testability is unstable—it appears to be a neutral criterion but actually presupposes answers to the very questions it's meant to help resolve.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Theory-ladenness of testing is well-known; application to consciousness-behavior proxies is competent but expected.

---

### U154. Theories of consciousness are empirically distinguishable only when they disagree about borderline cases (simple organisms, organoids, future AI), not about paradigm cases (humans, mammals).

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption that we're discovering facts about what consciousness fundamentally is, rather than making pragmatic decisions about conceptual boundaries
- **Sketch:** The debate appears to be about what consciousness IS, but empirically it can only be about where to draw boundaries. IIT, Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Thought theories, etc. all agree that normal human adults are conscious and rocks aren't. The actual empirical content lies entirely in their verdicts about edge cases—whether bees are conscious, whether split-brain patients have two consciousnesses, whether feed-forward networks could be conscious. This reframes the dispute: we're not discovering what consciousness is in paradigm cases (we already agree), but rather negotiating how to extend a concept from clear cases to unclear ones. The key premise is that theories making identical predictions about all possible cases are empirically equivalent regardless of their underlying mechanisms.
- **Appraiser (blind):** That theories agree on paradigm cases and differ on borderlines is widely recognized; the reframing as boundary negotiation is familiar.

---

### U155. The methodological commitment to physicalism in consciousness science makes all empirical consciousness research question-begging against dualist theories, revealing that 'empirical testability' already presupposes a metaphysical position.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The widespread framing that treats consciousness science as straightforwardly empirical while treating dualism as unscientific
- **Sketch:** All proposed empirical tests of consciousness theories look at physical systems (brains, computers, organoids) and ask whether they're conscious. But this methodology already assumes that facts about physical structure determine facts about consciousness—which is precisely what dualists deny. A Cartesian or property dualist could accept all the neural data and say 'yes, these physical conditions correlate with consciousness, but they don't constitute it or explain it.' The commitment to finding empirical tests commits us to the view that consciousness facts supervene on physical facts. This means the debate between physicalist consciousness theories can be empirical, but the debate between physicalism and dualism about consciousness cannot be—it's metaphysical all the way down. The key premise is that empirical testability requires that the phenomenon supervenes on observable properties, which begs the question against non-supervenience views.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The observation that physicalist methodology begs questions against dualism is already widely recognized in the literature and doesn't advance the discussion.

---

### U156. We can only tell which consciousness theory is right by identifying which theory best explains consciousness's biological function, but both sides of the hard problem debate are committed to denying that consciousness has any such function.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Both physicalists who treat consciousness as a spandrel and dualists/mysterians who deny physical efficacy
- **Sketch:** The argument draws a distinction between causal role (what consciousness does) and biological function (what consciousness is for—what it was selected for). Physicalists typically say consciousness plays causal roles but avoid function-talk because it suggests consciousness is an adaptation rather than inevitable byproduct. Dualists deny consciousness has biological function because that would make it physically efficacious in ways that threaten their position. The key premise is that only functional analysis—asking what problem consciousness solves—can adjudicate between theories, because it connects first-person and third-person evidence. But this path is blocked by commitments both sides share for different reasons.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The claim that both sides avoid function-talk is questionable empirically, and functional analysis is already widely discussed as a methodology.

---

### U157. Any empirical test that could adjudicate between consciousness theories would presuppose an already-settled answer to what counts as consciousness, making theory-neutral evidence logically impossible.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The widespread assumption that consciousness science can proceed like other sciences through theory-neutral observation, held by most empirical consciousness researchers including IIT and GWT proponents.
- **Sketch:** Every proposed test (measuring phi, detecting global workspace signatures, identifying higher-order representations) requires specifying which systems to measure. But selecting which systems are candidates for consciousness (humans yes, thermostats no, worms maybe) already assumes an answer to what consciousness is. This isn't merely an epistemic limitation but a logical one: the very act of designing an experiment requires drawing boundaries around the explanandum, but those boundaries are what's theoretically contested. Defenders might claim they're only assuming 'clear cases' everyone agrees on, but agreement on cases without agreement on what makes them cases is exactly the problematic circularity.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Restates the circularity problem from thesis 0 without adding substantive new insight about theory-neutral evidence.

---

### U158. The demand that consciousness theories be empirically discriminable commits us to eliminativism, because any genuinely empirical test would have to work on systems we're certain lack consciousness, which requires that consciousness itself be eliminable.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Philosophers who demand empirical testability while maintaining that consciousness has irreducible first-person features or could be present in unexpected substrates
- **Sketch:** To empirically validate a consciousness theory, we need cases where the theory predicts presence/absence and we can check the prediction. But checking requires independent access to ground truth about consciousness. If we had such access, we wouldn't need theories. The only way to have ground truth is to test on systems where consciousness is guaranteed absent (simple thermostats, rocks). But this only works if consciousness can be guaranteed absent—which requires that facts about physical organization alone can settle consciousness facts. This commits you to strong functionalism or eliminativism: consciousness must be the kind of thing that can be settled by third-person physical facts. The key premise is that empirical testability requires calibration cases, and calibration requires the possibility of verified negatives, which requires consciousness be a purely structural property.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The argument conflates needing verified negatives with eliminativism; calibration doesn't require what's claimed and the conclusion is too strong.

---

### U159. We can tell which theory is right by testing which one artificial systems designed according to each theory would themselves endorse after philosophical reflection on their own cognitive architecture.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The assumption that consciousness theories are third-person descriptions rather than first-person-accessible architectures
- **Sketch:** Current approaches ask which theory correctly describes systems we already think are conscious. But this draws the wrong distinction—between theories-as-descriptions versus theories-as-designs. Instead, build systems satisfying each theory's criteria (high phi for IIT, global broadcast for GWT, metacognitive loops for HOT), then expose them to philosophy of mind and ask which theory they argue for based on introspection. The correct theory is the one whose implementation produces systems that, upon reflection, endorse that very theory. A GWT-based system that introspects and concludes 'I seem to have higher-order thoughts but no global workspace' falsifies GWT. The key premise is that consciousness theories should be self-intimating: a system genuinely conscious according to theory T should be able to recognize its own architecture as fitting T upon sufficient reflection.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Creative but the self-intimation criterion is questionable (why should correct theories be self-recognizing?) and the proposal may be circular.
